ACE Professional · Data Center · AN-DC-PR01 (or AN-DC-OP01 + AN-DC-EN01)

L5 Data Center lab guide

Seven domain labs and five capstones on one containerlab fabric. Each lab follows the published exam domains, uses the same objective numbers as the study guide, and ends with checks you can run yourself.

Exam format
Hands-on, open book
Time limit
4 h (Ops alone 2 h)
Lab platform
containerlab + cEOS-lab
Fabric
2 spines · 4 leaves · EVPN
Hands-on time
About 30 h

How to use this guide

Each domain lab opens with the objectives it covers, a time estimate, the start state, and the Arista Warrior chapters to read first. Tasks are written as requirements, the way the exam phrases them. Try each one before you open the solution. Then run the Verify block and compare your output. Every objective ends with a Break / fix exercise, because the exam grades the final state of the devices and you need to find your own mistakes fast.

Tick the box on a task when you finish it. Your ticks are saved in this browser only, and the counts in the side menu show progress per lab. The objective numbers (for example 6.2) match the study guide; the study guide link beside each objective heading takes you to the theory for it.

Suggested order

The domain numbers follow Arista's exam layout, but the fabric gets built in the engineering labs. Pick one of these paths.

Build first (recommended)

  1. D4 L2LS, then the move to L3LS
  2. D5 eBGP underlay
  3. D6 VXLAN and EVPN
  4. D7 rebuild the same fabric with AVD, validate with ANTA
  5. D1 and D3 on the finished fabric
  6. D2 CloudVision (Academy lab, ATD, or CVaaS)
  7. Capstones C2 to C5, then C1 as a timed mock exam

Exam is soon

  1. Deploy the finished fabric from labs/avd/intended/configs
  2. D6 and D5 break / fix exercises
  3. C3 troubleshooting ticket queue
  4. D2 and D3 CloudVision tasks
  5. C1 mock exam, timed

The exam and this lab

"L5 Data Center" here means ACE Professional, Data Center. You earn it by passing the 4-hour Professional exam (AN-DC-PR01), or both Specialist exams: Operations (AN-DC-OP01, 2 h) and Engineering (AN-DC-EN01, 4 h). All of them are live, hands-on tasks in a virtual lab of EOS switches with CloudVision Portal, opened from a browser topology page. They are open book (documentation allowed, people not), proctored online, and each sitting uses one of several scenarios. Arista does not publish weights or pass marks. Configurations are collected and graded automatically when time runs out.

The older Arista level names used "ACE: L5" for a CloudVision, Ansible and Python automation course. Lab D7 covers that ground too, so you are prepared under either reading.

How this lab maps to the exam environment

Exam environmentThis labGap and how to close it
vEOS switches, browser topology page, SSH to each devicecEOS-lab containers, SSH from your shellSame EOS CLI. cEOS has no real hardware, so LANZ, optics and some counters are missing. Those labs say so.
Spine, leaf, host layout like the Arista Test Drive (ATD) data centerspine1-2, leaf1-4 as two MLAG pairs, EOS hosts with a VRF per VLAN, an external routerClose match. Device names in your exam will differ.
CloudVision Portal for provisioning, Studios and monitoringNot free. Each CloudVision task has Path A (CloudVision) and Path B (EOS, eAPI, AVD)Get CloudVision time through the Arista Academy course labs, an ATD session from your Arista contact, or CVaaS. Do not skip D2.
Graded on final device stateANTA test catalogs generated from the AVD designRun the catalogs after every lab and in the C1 mock exam.

Exam habits to practise in every lab

  • Read the whole scenario first and list the devices each task touches.
  • Use configure session for any change that could cut you off, and check show session-config diffs before you commit.
  • Verify after each task, not at the end. A wrong VNI found at minute 30 costs 2 minutes; at minute 230 it costs the task.
  • Save with write memory (or copy running-config startup-config) on every device you touched.
  • Know where things live in the EOS manual and the AVD docs so open-book time is spent reading, not searching.

Build the lab

You need a Linux host (or a Linux VM, or WSL2) with Docker, about 12 GB of free RAM and 4 or more CPU cores. Everything here is free. cEOS-lab needs a free arista.com account to download.

  1. Install Docker and containerlab.

    $ curl -sL https://containerlab.dev/setup | sudo -E bash -s "all"
$ containerlab version

  2. Download cEOS-lab from arista.com (Support, Software Download, EOS, cEOS-lab; pick a 4.32 or later 64-bit image) and import it with the tag the topology file expects.

    $ docker import cEOS64-lab-4.34.2F.tar.xz ceos:4.34.2F
$ docker images | grep ceos

    If you use a different release, change image: in labs/topology/l5dc.clab.yml to match.

  3. Clone the repository and deploy the topology. Each node boots with a day-0 config: hostname, management IP, admin/admin, eAPI, gNMI and LLDP. The two hosts and core1 come pre-built because you never change them.

    $ git clone https://github.com/T-Frizz/arista-l5-dc-training.git
$ cd arista-l5-dc-training/labs/topology
$ sudo containerlab deploy -t l5dc.clab.yml
$ sudo containerlab inspect -t l5dc.clab.yml

  4. Log in to a switch. Use SSH, or docker exec straight into the CLI.

    $ ssh admin@172.100.100.21
$ docker exec -it clab-l5dc-leaf1 Cli

  5. Optional: install pyavd and ANTA for Lab D7 and the capstones. They also let you jump to the finished fabric at any time.

    $ python3 -m venv ~/l5dc-venv && . ~/l5dc-venv/bin/activate
$ pip install pyavd anta
$ python3 labs/avd/build.py        # writes labs/avd/intended/configs/*.cfg

What containerlab does for you

You can start cEOS with plain docker create, and AW Ch 31 · Containers walks through it (pp. 175-177). containerlab's ceos node kind sets the same options for you, which is why the topology file is so short.

OptionWhat it does
--privilegedEOS needs to manage interfaces, routes and kernel settings inside the container.
-e CEOS=1, -e container=dockerTell EOS it is cEOS running under Docker, not EOS on hardware.
-e EOS_PLATFORM=ceoslabSelects the lab platform; the right value is in the readme for your cEOS release.
-e ETBA=1Use the software data plane, since there is no switching ASIC.
-e INTFTYPE=ethMap Linux ethN interfaces to EOS EthernetN.
-e SKIP_ZEROTOUCH_BARRIER_IN_SYSDBINIT=1Skip ZTP at boot so the startup config is used.
/sbin/initThe command that boots EOS inside the container.

containerlab also maps eth0 to Management0 and loads the startup config. The bigger difference is the links. Plain Docker networks are Linux bridges, which swallow link-local multicast such as LLDP and LACP frames, and when a container joins more than three networks the order of its interfaces is not guaranteed (p. 177). containerlab wires each link as a direct veth pair, so Ethernet1 is always the link you drew and LLDP, LACP and MLAG work. That is why this lab uses containerlab rather than hand-made Docker networks. Lab D1 has an optional drill that shows the bridge problem.

Running this lab on EVE-NG

The lab is defined for containerlab, but nothing in it depends on containerlab. On EVE-NG you build the same nine nodes and twenty links by hand once, then use the same startup configs, AVD inputs, ANTA catalogs and fault script. Two choices to make first:

  • vEOS-lab (QEMU) works on every EVE-NG edition. It boots like a real switch (Aboot, boot system, reloads), so the Lab D1 upgrade and Aboot recovery tasks work for real, which cEOS cannot do. Each node needs about 2 GB of RAM (4 GB is more comfortable with EVPN) and 1 to 2 vCPUs, so plan for 20 to 36 GB for all nine. vEOS is the same EOS code built as a VM; anything that needs a switching ASIC (LANZ, TAP aggregation, hardware counters) is missing, but everything in software works, including MLAG, LLDP, ZTP, eAPI, Event Manager and event monitor (AW Ch 32 · vEOS, p. 181).
  • cEOS needs Docker node support in your EVE-NG edition (check your version's documentation). It uses much less RAM, keeps Management0, and matches the guide exactly.

  1. Install the vEOS image (64-bit combined image, so no separate Aboot ISO is needed), then fix permissions:

    root@eve:~# mkdir -p /opt/unetlab/addons/qemu/veos-4.34.2F
root@eve:~# cp vEOS64-lab-4.34.2F.qcow2 /opt/unetlab/addons/qemu/veos-4.34.2F/hda.qcow2
root@eve:~# /opt/unetlab/wrappers/unl_wrapper -a fixpermissions

    The folder name must start with veos- so EVE-NG uses its vEOS template.

  2. Add the nine nodes with the names in the table below. In EVE-NG's vEOS template the first port (eth0 / Mgmt1) is Management1 and port ethN is EthernetN, the same numbering containerlab uses.

  3. Draw the links:

    PurposeLinks (A side to B side)
    Leaf uplinksleaf1 Eth1-spine1 Eth1, leaf1 Eth2-spine2 Eth1, leaf2 Eth1-spine1 Eth2, leaf2 Eth2-spine2 Eth2, leaf3 Eth1-spine1 Eth3, leaf3 Eth2-spine2 Eth3, leaf4 Eth1-spine1 Eth4, leaf4 Eth2-spine2 Eth4
    MLAG peer linksleaf1 Eth3-leaf2 Eth3, leaf1 Eth4-leaf2 Eth4, leaf3 Eth3-leaf4 Eth3, leaf3 Eth4-leaf4 Eth4, spine1 Eth5-spine2 Eth5, spine1 Eth6-spine2 Eth6 (the spine pair is only used in the L2LS stage of Lab D4)
    Hostshost1 Eth1-leaf1 Eth5, host1 Eth2-leaf2 Eth5, host2 Eth1-leaf3 Eth5, host2 Eth2-leaf4 Eth5
    Bordercore1 Eth1-leaf3 Eth6, core1 Eth2-leaf4 Eth6

  4. Management: connect every node's first port to one EVE-NG network bridged to the segment your Ubuntu automation server is on (a Cloud / pnet network). Easiest is to keep the lab's 172.100.100.0/24 addresses and add one address from that range to the automation server (sudo ip addr add 172.100.100.5/24 dev <nic>; use netplan to make it permanent). If you re-address instead, change the IPs in labs/topology/startup/*.cfg, labs/avd/inventory.yml, labs/avd/anta-inventory.yml and mgmt_gateway in labs/avd/group_vars/L5DC.yml.

  5. vEOS only: rename the management interface in the configs, then rebuild the AVD output. Wherever this guide says Management0, read Management1.

    $ cd arista-l5-dc-training/labs
$ sed -i 's/Management0/Management1/g' topology/startup/*.cfg avd/group_vars/L5DC.yml avd/group_vars/L5DC_FABRIC.yml
$ python3 avd/build.py

  6. First boot: vEOS starts in Zero Touch Provisioning. On each console, log in as admin with no password, run zerotouch cancel (the node reloads), then log in again, enter configure, paste that node's file from labs/topology/startup/, and write memory. After that you can reach every node over SSH and eAPI and never need the console again. Once you have built the ZTP lab, you can let the nodes fetch these files by ZTP instead.

  7. Check the links with show lldp neighbors on leaf1: you should see spine1, spine2, leaf2 and host1. If LLDP is empty, LACP and MLAG will also fail; EVE-NG's bridges normally pass these frames, so check your EVE-NG version notes.

Everything else in the guide stays the same, with two swaps. Where a command uses docker exec clab-l5dc-<node> Cli, use SSH or the EVE-NG console. Run the fault script with MODE=eapi labs/capstone/inject-faults.sh .... To reset a node, copy the AVD config over with scp and run configure replace flash:target.cfg, or wipe the node in EVE-NG and repeat the first-boot step. If the first configure replace to the AVD config says the routing protocol model change needs a reload, run write memory and reload once.

Reset or skip ahead

Two configs act as save points. The day-0 configs are in labs/topology/startup/. The finished EVPN fabric that AVD generates is in labs/avd/intended/configs/. Copy either onto a switch and replace the running config in one step.

$ docker cp labs/avd/intended/configs/leaf1.cfg clab-l5dc-leaf1:/mnt/flash/target.cfg
$ docker exec clab-l5dc-leaf1 Cli -p 15 -c "configure replace flash:target.cfg"

# whole fabric
$ for n in spine1 spine2 leaf1 leaf2 leaf3 leaf4; do
    docker cp labs/avd/intended/configs/$n.cfg clab-l5dc-$n:/mnt/flash/target.cfg
    docker exec clab-l5dc-$n Cli -p 15 -c "configure replace flash:target.cfg"
  done

To start completely fresh, run sudo containerlab destroy -t l5dc.clab.yml --cleanup and deploy again.

Watch out. The AVD configs use MTU 1500 on fabric links because cEOS runs on Linux veth interfaces. On real hardware you would set 9214 on the underlay. Lab D4 explains the VXLAN overhead math.

Topology and addressing

The layout follows the ATD data center labs: two spines, two MLAG leaf pairs, EOS "servers" dual-homed on port-channels, and a border pair facing an external router. Every lab uses these values, so you can copy an output from one lab and compare it with another.

MAC-VRFs use RD <Loopback0>:<VNI> and RT <VNI>:<VNI>. The anycast gateway MAC is 00:1c:73:00:dc:01. Each host is one EOS box with VRFs H10, H20 and H30, one per VLAN, so it behaves like three servers. Always ping with vrf.

Files: labs/topology/l5dc.clab.yml (topology), labs/topology/startup/ (day-0), labs/avd/ (AVD inputs, build.py, generated configs and ANTA catalogs), labs/capstone/ (fault injection for C3).

Domain 1 · Operations exam

Lab D1 · EOS Management

You run a live leaf-spine fabric and practice changing it safely: configuration sessions with commit timers, checkpoints and configure replace, a drained and staged upgrade of an MLAG pair, and extension, recovery and storage health tasks. The exam grades the final state of your switches, so the habits here (save a restore point, review the diff, verify, then write memory) are what keep you from losing points to a typo.

Objectives
1.1, 1.2, 1.3
Time
3 h
Start state
Fabric in its final AVD state (configs in labs/avd/intended/configs). Use the "Load the AVD end state" steps below.
Devices
leaf1, leaf2 (MLAG pair POD_A) for most tasks; spine1 for verification; host1 for traffic.

Read first: AW Ch 8 · Introduction to EOS · AW Ch 9 · Configuration Management · AW Ch 10 · Upgrading EOS · AW Ch 14 · Aboot · AW Ch 16 · The EOS Extension System · AW Ch 18 · MLAG (ISSU, pp. 89, 94, 96-97) · EOS manual: Maintenance Mode · EOS manual: service commands

Load the AVD end state

D1 and D3 assume the finished EVPN fabric. If you completed the Engineering labs you may already be there. If not, or if your fabric has drifted, load the AVD intended configs with configure replace. Run this from the repo root on the containerlab host. It copies each config into the switch flash and replaces the running config with it.

# from the repo root, lab already deployed with labs/topology/l5dc.clab.yml
for n in spine1 spine2 leaf1 leaf2 leaf3 leaf4; do
  docker cp labs/avd/intended/configs/$n.cfg clab-l5dc-$n:/mnt/flash/avd.cfg
  docker exec clab-l5dc-$n Cli -p 15 -c "configure replace flash:avd.cfg"
  docker exec clab-l5dc-$n Cli -p 15 -c "write memory"
done

If you use the AVD Ansible collection instead of build.py, deploying the same intended configs with its eAPI deploy role gets you to the same place. Either way, give MLAG about five minutes (reload-delay 300 s only matters after a reboot, but BGP and EVPN need a minute to converge) and then check:

leaf1# show mlag | include state
leaf1# show ip bgp summary
leaf1# show bgp evpn summary
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 repeat 3
Watch out. configure replace removes everything that is not in the file, including the management plane you are using. The AVD configs in this repo keep eAPI and gNMI on because the AVD inputs add them through structured_config (see labs/avd/group_vars/L5DC_FABRIC.yml). If you write your own replace file, check that management api http-commands, the management IP and the default route are in it before you run the replace.

SysDB and agents: why an EOS process can crash without an outage

This section follows AW Ch 7 · SysDB (pp. 35-37). SysDB is the idea everything else in EOS is built on: hitless agent restarts, configure replace, ISSU and SSU, and the state CloudVision streams. Expect it in "explain why" questions and as the reason behind many EOS behaviors you will see in the lab exam.

This is the first part of the foundations unit, built from AW Ch 7, 8, 9, 12 and 16: SysDB (how EOS works inside), CLI power tools (how to drive it fast), objective 1.1 and its traps (how to change it safely), Bash on the switch (the Linux underneath) and the extension system (how new software gets on). Aboot and ZTP, which act before EOS is running, are in Aboot hands-on and Lab D2.

Key ideas from the chapter. Older network operating systems were one large program, so a bug in one feature could take down the whole switch, and a restarted process had to relearn everything (p. 35). EOS runs each feature as a separate Linux process, called an agent (Stp, Lldp, Bgp, Lacp, and so on), each in its own protected memory. Agents do not keep their state privately: they write it to SysDB, a central in-memory database, and read other agents' state from it (p. 35). When an agent starts, it first asks SysDB for its state. If it finds none (a fresh boot) it starts from scratch; if it finds state (a restart) it carries on from there. So if Stp crashes, the process manager restarts it, Stp reads the current topology back from SysDB, and the network never reconverges (p. 35). SysDB lives in memory, so it does not survive a reboot; that is the job of startup-config (p. 37).
EventWhat happensNetwork impact
One agent crashes or is killed (Stp, Lldp)The process manager restarts it within about a second; it reloads its state from SysDB and logs AGENT-6-INITIALIZED (p. 36).None. No STP topology change, no lost LLDP neighbors.
Feature turned off and on (spanning-tree mode none, then mstp)The state is deleted, so the agent starts from nothing (p. 36).Full reconvergence: ports go discarding, learning, forwarding.
SysDB itself is killedEvery agent loses its state and restarts; running-config is rebuilt from startup-config (p. 36).The same as a reload, only faster. Unsaved changes are lost.
Forwarding agent restarted twice in quick successionEOS gives up on a hitless restart and reprograms the hardware tables (HARDWARE-3-DISRUPTIVE_RESTART, p. 37).A brief forwarding outage.
configure replaceOnly the agents whose config changed are touched (p. 37).Only what the config change itself causes.
Watch out. Killing agents is for a lab only. The book says the same in plain words: never do it on a production switch unless Arista TAC asks you to (pp. 35-36). Everything below runs on cEOS, but expect agent names and timings to differ a little from hardware.

  1. On leaf1, list the running agents and their restart counts. Find the Stp and Lldp agents and their Linux process IDs.

  2. Record the STP state of Port-Channel5 (toward host1) on leaf1 and on host1. Put a marker in the log, kill the Stp agent with kill -9, and show that it restarted, that STP state did not change, and that the log has no port state transitions.

  3. Do the same with the Lldp agent and check that show lldp neighbors is full immediately after the restart.

  4. Now compare a real reconvergence: put another marker in the log, set spanning-tree mode none on host1, then spanning-tree mode mstp again, and read the log on host1. Use host1, not a leaf, so the fabric is not disturbed.

  5. Lab only: on host2, save the config, then kill SysDB and watch what happens. Time how long until you can log in again.

  6. Explain why configure replace is the right way to load a completely different config, and why the book's trick of killing SysDB to load a new startup-config is not.

Solution · agents and processes 
leaf1# show agent uptime
leaf1# show agent uptime | include Stp|Lldp
leaf1# show processes | include Stp|Lldp|Sysdb
leaf1# bash ps -eo pid,etime,cmd | grep -E "Stp|Lldp|Sysdb" | grep -v grep

show agent uptime lists each agent with how long it has run and how many times it has restarted; a non-zero restart count on a healthy switch is worth a TAC case. Several processes may match Stp (for example Stp and StpTopology); the book kills everything matching the name (p. 36). Agent crash details, when there are any, go to /var/log/agents/ and appear in show tech-support.

Solution · kill Stp and Lldp, no reconvergence 
leaf1# show spanning-tree interface Port-Channel5
host1# show spanning-tree
leaf1# send log message KILLING-STP
leaf1# bash sudo killall -9 Stp
leaf1# show logging last 2 minutes
leaf1# show agent uptime | include Stp
leaf1# show spanning-tree interface Port-Channel5

leaf1# send log message KILLING-LLDP
leaf1# bash sudo killall -9 Lldp
leaf1# show lldp neighbors
leaf1# show logging last 2 minutes | include Lldp|AGENT
Oct 25 14:02:11 leaf1 ConfigAgent: %SYS-6-LOGMSG_INFO: Message from admin on vty3: KILLING-STP
Oct 25 14:02:13 leaf1 Stp: %AGENT-6-INITIALIZED: Agent 'Stp' initialized; pid=8812
Oct 25 14:02:13 leaf1 Stp: %SPANTREE-6-INTERFACE_ADD: Interface Port-Channel5 has been added to instance MST0

Representative output. If send log message is not accepted, check send log ? on your release. The interface is added back to MST0, but there is no "moving from discarding to learning" or "learning to forwarding" line, because Stp found the state already in SysDB and did not have to run the protocol from scratch (p. 36). The restart count in show agent uptime went up by one. LLDP neighbors are there straight away for the same reason. The book also shows an undocumented agent ... terminate command; leave it, and the agent shutdown and environment options, alone unless TAC asks (p. 36).

Solution · a real reconvergence, for comparison 
host1# send log message STP-OFF-ON
host1(config)# spanning-tree mode none
host1(config)# spanning-tree mode mstp
host1# show logging last 2 minutes | include SPANTREE
host1 StpTopology: %SPANTREE-6-DISABLED: STP is disabled.
host1 Stp: %SPANTREE-6-INTERFACE_DEL: Interface Port-Channel1 has been removed from instance MST0
host1 StpTopology: %SPANTREE-6-MODE_CHANGE: Spanning tree mode is now mstp.
host1 Stp: %SPANTREE-6-INTERFACE_STATE: Interface Port-Channel1 instance MST0 moving from discarding to learning
host1 Stp: %SPANTREE-6-INTERFACE_STATE: Interface Port-Channel1 instance MST0 moving from learning to forwarding

This time the state was deleted when STP was turned off, so the agent started from nothing and the port walked through discarding, learning and forwarding (p. 36). host1's pings to its gateway stall for that time. Killing the process caused no outage; turning the feature off and on did.

Solution · kill SysDB (lab only, host2) 
host2# write memory
host2# bash sudo killall -9 Sysdb
! the session drops; log in again after a short wait
$ ssh admin@172.100.100.32
host2# show logging | include AGENT-6-INITIALIZED
host2# show agent uptime

Your session closes, every agent loses its connection to SysDB and restarts, and the switch comes back running startup-config, which is the same result as a reload but much faster (the book saw about 30 seconds against more than 4 minutes for a full reload on a 7280R, p. 36). Anything not saved is gone, with no warning (p. 37). On cEOS the whole container may restart instead; if host2 does not come back, run docker restart clab-l5dc-host2 (or restart the node in EVE-NG).

The book then uses this to load a new config: copy a different file to startup-config and kill SysDB (pp. 36-37). It works, but it is a reload in disguise, and it can trigger the disruptive hardware restart shown above. configure replace flash:FILE does the same job in seconds with no reload, touches only what changed, and with a configuration session you can review the diff and use commit timer for an automatic rollback (objective 1.1). That is the only method to use on a real network (p. 37).

Exam tip. Know the chain: agents keep state in SysDB, so an agent restart is hitless; SysDB is in memory, so a reboot needs startup-config; and the same separation is what makes configure replace, configuration sessions and SSU/ISSU possible. If a question asks why EOS can restart a protocol process without a topology change, the answer is state in SysDB, not "fast timers".
Real world. The SysDB model is also why CloudVision sees so much: TerminAttr subscribes to state changes on the switch and streams them, so CloudVision gets the same live state the agents use, not a five-minute SNMP poll. And because a single crashing agent rarely causes an outage, watch for it explicitly: alert on AGENT-6-INITIALIZED outside boot time and on rising restart counts in show agent uptime, and open a TAC case with show tech-support when you see one.

EOS CLI power tools

This section follows AW Ch 8 · Introduction to EOS (pp. 39-45). It is the middle of the foundations unit: SysDB explains how EOS works, this section is how you drive it fast, and objective 1.1 is how you change it safely. On a timed, hands-on exam the CLI tricks below are worth real minutes.

Key ideas from the chapter. EOS runs on an unmodified Linux kernel and you can reach the shell, but the CLI is deliberately close to the industry standard (p. 39). Agents keep their state in SysDB, so features are isolated and restartable (p. 39). There is one EOS image for every Arista switch: the image loads the right driver for the ASIC it finds, so the same .swi file runs on a small fixed switch and a large chassis, and support for old ASICs is dropped from newer images over time (p. 39). Out of the box only SSH is allowed (telnet is off), passwords are stored as SHA-512 with secret, and there is no service password-encryption because clear text is not an option (p. 39). EOS answers a typo with a plain "% Invalid input" and no caret (pp. 39-40).
ToolExampleNotes
Help and completionro?, rou<Tab>? lists matches, Tab completes the longest unique match (p. 40).
Line editingCtrl-A, Ctrl-E, Ctrl-B, Ctrl-F, Esc-B, Esc-FStart and end of line, back and forward one character, back and forward one word (p. 40).
Mode changesType interface Ethernet2 from inside router bgpNo need to exit first; EOS moves to the right mode. end or Ctrl-Z leaves configuration (p. 40).
Exec commands in config modeshow ip bgp summary at a config promptNo do needed (p. 41).
Interface rangesinterface Ethernet1-3,5, interface Ethernet1-$No range keyword; $ means the last port. On switches with lanes, use Ethernet1-$/$ (p. 41).
Number expansionip address 10.1.{1,4}.1/24, description ROW-{1,7,2,2}{start,end,step,pad} gives one value per interface in the range: 1 to 4, or 01, 03, 05, 07 (pp. 40-41).
Current sectionshow activeSame as show running-config section for the mode you are in (p. 41).
Defaultsshow running-config all interfaces Ethernet1Shows every setting, including defaults that the normal view hides (p. 42).
Filters| include, | exclude, | begin, | section, | nz, | json, | no-moreshow ... | ? lists them. nz hides all-zero counter rows (pp. 41-42).
Linux tools| grep -i, | egrep, | awk, | tail, | wc -lWork after any show command, even though most are not in the | ? list (pp. 42-44).
Save output| redirect flash:out.txt, | append ..., | tee ..., or | tail > /mnt/flash/out.txtEOS filters take EOS paths; a Linux redirect needs the full Linux path (p. 44).
Live logshow logging followLike tail -f; Ctrl-C to stop (p. 44).
Aliasesalias senz show interfaces counters errors | nzAliases can take arguments (%1, %2) and can run several lines (p. 42).

  1. On leaf1, enter interface Ethernet1-$ and run show active. Which ports did $ select, and why only those on cEOS?

  2. Create Loopback101 to Loopback104 in one command. Give them addresses 192.0.2.1 to 192.0.2.4 (/32) and descriptions LAB-01, LAB-02 and so on, each with one command. Then do it again with only odd numbers (192.0.2.1, .3, .5, .7). Remove the loopbacks.

  3. Find every line of leaf1's config that mentions BGP, in any case, and then the same with context (which section each line belongs to). Compare | include bgp, | include BGP, | grep -i bgp and | section bgp.

  4. List every interface on leaf1 with its MAC address, one line each. Then make it an alias shmc.

  5. Create an alias senz that shows only interfaces with errors, and a multi-line alias intdesc that takes an interface and a description and sets it. Test both, then remove them.

  6. Save the last 20 log lines that mention BGP to flash:bgp-log.txt, once with an EOS filter and once with a Linux redirect. Check both files with dir flash:.

  7. Show Ethernet1 with and without defaults and name two settings you only see with all.

Solution · ranges, expansion, show active 
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet1-$
leaf1(config-if-Et1-5)# show active
leaf1(config-if-Et1-5)# exit

leaf1(config)# interface Loopback101-104
leaf1(config-if-Lo101-104)# ip address 192.0.2.{1,4}/32
leaf1(config-if-Lo101-104)# description LAB-{1,4,1,2}
leaf1(config-if-Lo101-104)# show active
leaf1(config-if-Lo101-104)# ip address 192.0.2.{1,7,2}/32
leaf1(config-if-Lo101-104)# show active
leaf1(config)# no interface Loopback101-104

On cEOS, $ stops at Ethernet5 because containerlab only created the ports that are wired. The expansion gives one value per interface in order: {1,4} counts 1 to 4, {1,7,2} counts 1, 3, 5, 7, and the fourth field pads with zeros to that width (pp. 40-41). If your release rejects a range of loopbacks, do the same on a range of Ethernet ports in a lab-only node such as host2 and restore it with configure replace afterwards.

Solution · finding config, include vs grep vs section 
leaf1# show running-config | include bgp
leaf1# show running-config | include BGP
leaf1# show running-config | grep -i bgp
leaf1# show running-config | section bgp
leaf1# show running-config section mlag

include and grep are case-sensitive, so bgp and BGP each find only part of the lines; grep -i finds both, but a matching description line does not tell you which interface it is under. section ignores case and prints each match with its whole section, so you see the interface and the full router bgp block (pp. 44-45). For any feature, show running-config section NAME is the fastest way to read its whole config.

Solution · MAC list and aliases 
leaf1# show interfaces | awk '/^[A-Z]/ {intf=$1} /, address is/ {print intf, $6}'
leaf1(config)# alias shmc show interfaces | awk '/^[A-Z]/ {intf=$1} /, address is/ {print intf, $6}'
leaf1# shmc

leaf1(config)# alias senz show interfaces counters errors | nz
leaf1(config)# alias intdesc
leaf1(config-alias-intdesc)# 10 configure
leaf1(config-alias-intdesc)# 20 interface %1
leaf1(config-alias-intdesc)# 30 description %2
leaf1(config-alias-intdesc)# 40 end
leaf1(config-alias-intdesc)# exit
leaf1# senz
leaf1# intdesc Loopback0 TEST-ALIAS
leaf1# show running-config interfaces Loopback0

leaf1(config)# no alias shmc
leaf1(config)# no alias senz
leaf1(config)# no alias intdesc
leaf1(config)# interface Loopback0
leaf1(config-if-Lo0)# description ROUTER_ID

The awk program remembers the interface name from each line that starts with a capital letter and prints it with the MAC from the "address is" line (p. 42). An alias must be entered on one line (p. 42). The multi-line alias form and its prompt can differ slightly by release; check alias intdesc ?. Put back Loopback0's original description (check leaf2 or the AVD config for the exact text) or restore with configure replace.

Solution · saving output, defaults 
leaf1# show logging | include BGP | redirect flash:bgp-log.txt
leaf1# show logging | grep BGP | tail -20 > /mnt/flash/bgp-log2.txt
leaf1# dir flash: | include bgp-log

leaf1# show running-config interfaces Ethernet1
leaf1# show running-config all interfaces Ethernet1

redirect, append and tee are EOS filters and take an EOS path such as flash:. After a Linux command such as tail, the output goes through the Linux shell, so you need the Linux path /mnt/flash/ (p. 44). The all view adds defaults such as no shutdown, default load-interval, no mac-address and flow-control settings (p. 42). Delete the two files when you are done.

Exam tip. Three habits save the most time in the lab: show running-config section NAME to read one feature, show active to check what you just typed in the current mode, and | nz on counters. Use | grep, not a second | include, whenever you chain two filters.
Real world. Keep a small, shared set of aliases in your base config (AVD and CloudVision configlets can push them) so every engineer has the same shortcuts. Avoid an alias with the same name as a real command, and remember that a script run by someone else will not have your aliases.

Break / fix

Inject: a colleague's audit runbook says to check that every interface has a description with show interfaces | include Ethernet | include Description. Run it on leaf1.

Symptom: the output does not show interface names next to their descriptions, and changes in an unexpected way when you swap the two filters. The colleague concludes that descriptions are missing.

Root cause and fix

include is part of the EOS CLI, not a Linux pipe, so a second include does not simply filter the output of the first (pp. 41-42). Chain Linux tools instead, or use a command built for the job:

leaf1# show interfaces | grep -E "^Ethernet|Description"
leaf1# show interfaces description
leaf1# show running-config | section interface Ethernet

The descriptions are all there. Fix the runbook, and test any filtered command against the full output once before you trust it.

1.1 EOS operations: sessions, checkpoints, rollback, configure replace

A change ticket asks you to add a new tenant VLAN on POD_A. You must stage it, show the reviewer the exact diff, apply it with an automatic safety net, and be able to return to the pre-change state in one command. Work on leaf1 unless a task says otherwise.

  1. Before touching anything, create a named restore point called pre-chg1001 on leaf1 and confirm it exists. Also note which automatic checkpoints EOS has already created.

  2. Open a configuration session called CHG1001. In it, create VLAN 40 named WEB, add it to the host-facing trunk Port-Channel5 (keep VLANs 10, 20 and 30), and add the command alias nz for show interfaces counters | nz. Do not commit yet.

  3. Show the reviewer the pending diff from inside the session and again from exec mode (after leaving the session without committing). Prove that the running config does not yet contain VLAN 40.

  4. The reviewer rejects the alias. Remove it from the session, then commit the session with a 5-minute commit timer. Confirm the commit before the timer expires.

  5. Start a second session CHG1002 that shuts Ethernet1 (the uplink to spine1) and commit it with a 2-minute timer. Do not confirm. Watch the change roll back on its own and check BGP to spine1 recovers.

  6. Leave the change in place but compare running to startup config, then save. Compare the running config with checkpoint pre-chg1001.

  7. Back out the whole change by replacing the running config with checkpoint pre-chg1001. Verify VLAN 40 is gone and the trunk is back to 10,20,30.

  8. Practice the "replace inside a session" pattern that CloudVision and AVD use: open session FULL-REPLACE, clear it to an empty config, load flash:avd.cfg into it, review the diff (it should be empty or very small), and commit.

  9. Set up a simple configuration archive: keep 20 checkpoints and 10 completed sessions, and schedule an hourly copy of the running config to flash with 48 versions kept.

Verify

Outputs are representative and trimmed; your timers, MACs and counters will differ.

leaf1(config-s-CHG1001)# show session-config diffs
--- system:/running-config
+++ session:/CHG1001-session-config
@@ -1,4 +1,5 @@
+alias nz show interfaces counters | nz
 !
 no enable password
@@ -24,6 +25,9 @@
 vlan 30
    name L2_STRETCH
 !
+vlan 40
+   name WEB
+!
 vlan 3001
@@ -38,7 +42,7 @@
 interface Port-Channel5
    description SERVER_host1
    no shutdown
-   switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30
+   switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30,40
    switchport mode trunk
leaf1# show configuration sessions detail
Maximum number of completed sessions: 10
Maximum number of pending sessions: 5

  Name        State                 User      Terminal    PID
  ----------- --------------------- --------- ----------- -----
  CHG1001     pendingCommitTimer    admin     vty3        2214
                (commit timer expires in 0:04:31)
leaf1# show configuration checkpoints
Maximum number of checkpoints: 20
Filename                   Date                     User
-------------------------- ------------------------ ------
ckp-20261025-0             2026-10-25 09:02:11      admin
ckp-20261025-1             2026-10-25 09:31:40      admin

+++ system:/running-config
@@ -24,6 +24,9 @@

After task 4 the session is shown as committed (no timer). After task 5 Ethernet1 is back up without you touching it and show ip bgp summary shows 10.255.255.0 Established again. After task 7 show vlan 40 returns nothing and show running-config interfaces Port-Channel5 shows 10,20,30.

Solution · tasks 1 to 5 (leaf1) 
leaf1# configure checkpoint save pre-chg1001
leaf1# show configuration checkpoints
leaf1# dir checkpoint:

leaf1# configure session CHG1001
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001)# vlan 40
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001-vlan-40)# name WEB
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001-vlan-40)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001-if-Po5)# switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001-if-Po5)# exit
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001)# alias nz show interfaces counters | nz
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001)# show session-config diffs
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001)# end
! "end" or "exit" leaves the session pending; it is NOT committed
leaf1# show configuration sessions
leaf1# show session-config named CHG1001 diffs
leaf1# show running-config section vlan 40
! (no output: not applied yet)

leaf1# configure session CHG1001
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001)# no alias nz
leaf1(config-s-CHG1001)# commit timer 00:05:00
leaf1# configure session CHG1001 commit
! confirms the commit and stops the timer

leaf1# configure session CHG1002
leaf1(config-s-CHG1002)# interface Ethernet1
leaf1(config-s-CHG1002-if-Et1)# shutdown
leaf1(config-s-CHG1002-if-Et1)# commit timer 00:02:00
leaf1# show ip bgp summary
! wait 2 minutes without confirming
leaf1# show logging last 5 minutes
leaf1# show interfaces Ethernet1 | include line protocol
Solution · tasks 6 to 9 (leaf1) 
leaf1# show running-config diffs
leaf1# write memory
leaf1# diff checkpoint:pre-chg1001 running-config
! if your release lacks "diff", copy the checkpoint to flash and compare in bash:
leaf1# copy checkpoint:pre-chg1001 flash:pre-chg1001.cfg
leaf1# bash diff /mnt/flash/pre-chg1001.cfg /mnt/flash/startup-config

leaf1# configure replace checkpoint:pre-chg1001
! equivalent: configure checkpoint restore pre-chg1001
leaf1# show vlan 40
leaf1# show running-config interfaces Port-Channel5
leaf1# write memory

leaf1# configure session FULL-REPLACE
leaf1(config-s-FULL-R)# rollback clean-config
leaf1(config-s-FULL-R)# copy flash:avd.cfg session-config
leaf1(config-s-FULL-R)# show session-config diffs
leaf1(config-s-FULL-R)# commit

leaf1(config)# service configuration checkpoint max saved 20
leaf1(config)# service configuration session max completed 10
leaf1(config)# schedule cfg-archive interval 60 max-log-files 48 command show running-config
leaf1# show schedule cfg-archive
leaf1# dir flash:schedule/cfg-archive/

Check the exact keywords with ?: some releases spell the checkpoint limit as service configuration checkpoint max saved, and diff may not exist on every release. Each scheduled run saves a gzipped copy of the command output under flash:schedule/<name>/, which is a cheap local archive. In production you would also send configs off-box (CloudVision keeps every config version automatically; AVD keeps them in Git).

NeedEOS toolKey behavior
Stage and review before applyingconfigure session NAME, show session-config diffsChanges live in a copy of running-config until commit. Leaving the session keeps it pending.
Automatic undo if you lose accesscommit timer hh:mm:ss then configure session NAME commitIf not confirmed, EOS rolls back to the pre-commit config.
Throw away a staged changeabort inside the sessionNothing reaches running-config.
Named restore pointconfigure checkpoint save NAMEStored in checkpoint:. EOS also auto-creates ckp-YYYYMMDD-N checkpoints.
Return to a known configconfigure replace <file|checkpoint:NAME>Full replace, computed as a diff. Anything not in the file is removed.
What is unsavedshow running-config diffsRunning vs startup.
Force sessions for everyoneservice configuration terminal disabledPlain configure is refused; users must use sessions.
Exam tip. Grading reads the device state at the end. A session you forgot to commit, or a commit timer you forgot to confirm, silently loses your work. Before you move on from any task run show configuration sessions and make sure nothing is pending, then write memory. Save a checkpoint at the start of every task so you can undo a bad path in one command instead of untangling it by hand.
Real world. CloudVision and AVD both push changes this way: they open a session, run rollback clean-config, load the full designed config, check the diff and commit. That is why an out-of-band CLI change disappears on the next push. Use commit timers for any change that could cut your own access (management ACLs, AAA, uplinks, routing on the management path).

Break / fix

Inject: on leaf2, a colleague "restores" the config with the wrong file: configure replace flash:avd.cfg after copying leaf1's file over it (docker cp /tmp/l5dc/leaf1.cfg clab-l5dc-leaf2:/mnt/flash/avd.cfg).

Symptom: the prompt on leaf2 now says leaf1. show mlag shows the peer link up but MLAG inactive or disabled, BGP to the spines is down, and host1 has lost half its links.

Root cause and fix

The replaced config carries leaf1's identity: Loopback0 10.255.0.3, Vlan4094 10.255.252.0 (the same IP as the real leaf1, so peer-address 10.255.252.1 points at itself), and P2P addresses .1 and .3 that clash with leaf1. Fix it by restoring the right config. If you saved a checkpoint before, configure replace checkpoint:<name>. Otherwise copy the correct file back (docker cp /tmp/l5dc/leaf2.cfg clab-l5dc-leaf2:/mnt/flash/avd.cfg) and run configure replace flash:avd.cfg. Verify with show hostname, show mlag, show ip bgp summary. Lesson: before any replace, look at the file (more flash:avd.cfg | include hostname) and do it inside a session so you see the diff first.

Configuration management traps: merge, replace and the session snapshot

This section follows AW Ch 9 · Configuration Management (pp. 45-49) and closes the foundations unit. Objective 1.1 above taught the tools; this part is about the ways they surprise people. The chapter's one big message: in EOS, only copy merges. configure replace, checkpoint restore and session commit all replace the whole running config, which is what makes them powerful and what makes them dangerous (pp. 45, 49).

Key ideas from the chapter. The CLI is additive: to remove a line you must type its no form, so before configure replace the only way to load a completely different config was to negate everything by hand or overwrite startup-config and reload (p. 45). copy FILE running-config merges: it adds the file's lines and removes nothing. configure replace FILE makes running-config equal to the file, so a two-line file leaves a two-line switch, with the hostname back to localhost and no management address (p. 45). Sections that do not change are not touched at all, so replacing a config that keeps the same BGP section does not disturb BGP (p. 45). A checkpoint restore is a replace from the checkpoint file, so it undoes anything done after the checkpoint (p. 47). A configuration session is not a list of commands: it is a copy of running-config taken when the session starts, with your commands applied to it, and commit replaces running-config with that copy (pp. 47-48).
OperationMerge or replaceWhere it livesSurvives reload
copy flash:FILE running-configMerge: adds lines, removes nothing (p. 45)The file on flashYes (the file); the result only after write memory
configure replace flash:FILEReplace: running-config becomes the file (p. 45)The file on flashYes (the file)
configure checkpoint save NAME / restore NAMERestore is a replace (p. 47)Plain text in /mnt/flash/.checkpoints/, also reachable as checkpoint:; default limit 20, set with service configuration checkpoint max saved (up to 100); the space they use is not shown in show configuration checkpoints (p. 47)Yes
configure session NAME then commitReplace with the session's copy (p. 48)Memory only; default 5 pending and 1 completed, set with service configuration session max pending | completed (up to 20, p. 49)No (p. 48)
commit timer that expiresReplace back to an automatic checkpoint taken at commit (p. 48)A system session named like cfg-NNNN--NNNNNNNNN-0, described as "config replace of commitTimerCheckPointConfig" (p. 48)No
rollback clean-config inside a sessionReplaces the session's copy with factory defaults (p. 49)The sessionNo
Watch out. Tasks 1 and 4 deliberately wreck a switch. Use host2 only, never a leaf or spine, and run write memory on host2 first so startup-config is a good copy to come back to. Keep a console open: on containerlab that is docker exec -it clab-l5dc-host2 Cli, on EVE-NG the node's console.

  1. On host2, create a two-line file flash:snippet.cfg that sets a description MERGE-TEST on Ethernet1. Load it with copy and confirm the rest of the config is untouched. Then preview what configure replace flash:snippet.cfg would do, without actually doing it, and count what would be removed.

  2. On host2, save checkpoint CK-A. Then add the description LATER-CHANGE to Ethernet2. Restore CK-A and check Ethernet2. Find the checkpoint file in Linux and restore it a second way with configure replace.

  3. The lost-change trap. On host2, start session S1 and add the description FROM-SESSION to Ethernet1, then leave it pending. In plain configuration mode, add the description DIRECT to Ethernet2. Before committing S1, predict what happens to DIRECT, prove it with the session diff, then commit and check. Then repeat, but this time keep both changes.

  4. In a session on host2, run rollback clean-config, read the session config, and abort. Then check the session limits and how many pending and completed sessions are kept.

  5. After the 2-minute commit timer in objective 1.1 (task 5) expires on leaf1, run show configuration sessions detail and find the session EOS made for the rollback.

Solution · merge vs replace preview (host2) 
host2# write memory
host2# bash printf 'interface Ethernet1\n   description MERGE-TEST\n' > /mnt/flash/snippet.cfg
host2# more flash:snippet.cfg
host2# copy flash:snippet.cfg running-config
! Missing final 'end' command, adding automatically.
host2# show running-config interfaces Ethernet1
host2# show running-config | include hostname|Management0

host2# configure session PREVIEW
host2(config-s-PREVIE)# rollback clean-config
host2(config-s-PREVIE)# copy flash:snippet.cfg session-config
host2(config-s-PREVIE)# show session-config diffs | grep -c "^-"
host2(config-s-PREVIE)# show session-config diffs | include hostname|ip address|management
host2(config-s-PREVIE)# abort

After the copy, Ethernet1 has the description and keeps channel-group 1 mode active; nothing else changed (p. 45). The session preview is the safe way to see a replace before it happens: rollback clean-config plus copy ... session-config builds exactly what configure replace would build, and the diff shows dozens of minus lines, including the hostname, the Management0 address, the default route, eAPI, the VRFs and the port-channel. abort throws it away. The break/fix below does the replace for real.

Solution · checkpoint restore loses later work (host2) 
host2# configure checkpoint save CK-A
host2(config)# interface Ethernet2
host2(config-if-Et2)# description LATER-CHANGE
host2(config-if-Et2)# show active
host2(config-if-Et2)# configure checkpoint restore CK-A
host2(config-if-Et2)# show active
! description LATER-CHANGE is gone
host2# bash ls -al /mnt/flash/.checkpoints/
host2# configure replace checkpoint:CK-A
host2# show configuration checkpoints

The restore is a replace, so a change made after the checkpoint disappears, even one that has nothing to do with the problem you were undoing (p. 47). The checkpoint files are plain text on flash, so they survive a reload and use flash space (p. 47). Before restoring on a real switch, run diff checkpoint:CK-A running-config (or compare the two files in bash) so you know what you are about to lose.

Solution · the session snapshot trap (host2) 
host2# configure session S1
host2(config-s-S1)# interface Ethernet1
host2(config-s-S1-if-Et1)# description FROM-SESSION
host2(config-s-S1-if-Et1)# end
host2# configure
host2(config)# interface Ethernet2
host2(config-if-Et2)# description DIRECT
host2(config-if-Et2)# end
host2# show session-config named S1 diffs
 interface Ethernet1
+   description FROM-SESSION
    channel-group 1 mode active
 !
 interface Ethernet2
-   description DIRECT
    channel-group 1 mode active
host2# configure session S1 commit
host2# show running-config interfaces Ethernet1-2

! the safe way: put the direct change in the session too, or start a fresh session
host2# configure session S2
host2(config-s-S2)# interface Ethernet1
host2(config-s-S2-if-Et1)# description FROM-SESSION
host2(config-s-S2-if-Et1)# interface Ethernet2
host2(config-s-S2-if-Et2)# description DIRECT
host2(config-s-S2-if-Et2)# show session-config diffs
host2(config-s-S2-if-Et2)# commit

Representative output. The session holds the running-config as it was when S1 started, so DIRECT shows as a line to be removed, and the commit removes it (pp. 47-48). The rule: read the session diff just before you commit, and if it shows minus lines you did not type, someone (maybe you) changed the switch in the meantime. abort and start again, or add the missing change to the session. Clean up with default interface descriptions or configure replace flash:startup-config.

Solution · clean-config, limits and the timer rollback 
host2# configure session BLANK
host2(config-s-BLANK)# rollback clean-config
host2(config-s-BLANK)# show session-config
host2(config-s-BLANK)# abort
host2(config)# service configuration session max ?
host2# show configuration sessions

leaf1# show configuration sessions detail

rollback clean-config leaves only defaults in the session: the same result as write erase and a reload, but nothing happens until you commit (p. 49). It is the first step of every full-config push that CloudVision makes. Leave the session limits at their defaults unless you have a reason: sessions are kept uncompressed in memory, and a large config multiplied by 20 adds up (p. 49). After the timer expires on leaf1, the list shows a session with a generated name and the description "config replace of commitTimerCheckPointConfig": EOS saved a checkpoint at commit time and replaced back to it (p. 48). The User and Terminal columns are only filled in while someone is inside a session (p. 48).

Exam tip. If a task says "apply this config" and gives you a partial file or snippet, it means merge: paste it, or copy it to running-config. Use configure replace only with a complete config. And never mix a pending session with direct edits: commit or abort the session before you type in plain configure again.
Real world. The session snapshot is exactly why an out-of-band CLI fix vanishes on the next CloudVision or AVD push: the push replaces the whole config with the designed one. Put the fix into the source of truth (configlet, Studio or AVD inputs) first. Use the commit timer for anything that touches your own path to the switch; the book's author learned reload in the hard way after an empty ACL cut off a whole country, and a commit timer does the same job without a reboot (p. 48).

Break / fix

Inject: a change ticket says "apply flash:snippet.cfg to host2", and a colleague runs configure replace flash:snippet.cfg over SSH.

Symptom: the SSH session freezes, host2 no longer answers on 172.100.100.32, eAPI and gNMI are gone, and host2's pings into the fabric fail. leaf3 and leaf4 show Port-Channel5 down.

Root cause and fix

A replace with a two-line file removed everything else, including the management address, the default route and the port-channel; the hostname is now localhost (p. 45). There is no SSH, so use the console, and replace back to the saved config (p. 45):

$ docker exec -it clab-l5dc-host2 Cli
localhost>enable
localhost# show running-config
localhost# configure replace flash:startup-config
host2# show hostname
host2# show port-channel summary
host2# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.1 repeat 3

No reload was needed. The ticket meant a merge: copy flash:snippet.cfg running-config. If startup-config had not been saved, a checkpoint or the AVD file (labs/topology/startup/host2.cfg copied back in) would do the same job.

Bash on the switch (optional)

This section follows AW Ch 12 · Bash (pp. 55-57). It is not an exam objective on its own, but it ties the foundations unit together: SysDB agents are Linux processes, the CLI is a Linux program, and the EOS Linux filters in CLI power tools work because there is a real shell underneath. It is also the basis for the on-box scripts, Event Manager actions and extensions later in the guide.

Key ideas from the chapter. An Arista switch is a Linux server built to be a switch, and bash from enable mode drops you into a real Bash shell, not an emulation (p. 55). You log in to Linux as your EOS user with an empty home directory and normal user rights, so system changes need sudo (p. 55). Every front-panel port appears to the kernel as a network interface, and normal tools such as ifconfig and vmstat work (p. 55). The CLI itself is a process: Cli from Bash starts a CLI session, and Cli -c "COMMAND" runs one command and prints the result, so you can pipe it into any Linux tool (p. 55). In current EOS, Cli points to FastCli, which hands the command to an agent and is fast (p. 56). The other way round, bash COMMAND at the EOS prompt runs one Linux command without leaving the CLI, in your home directory (p. 56). Most of the file system is rebuilt at every boot: only /mnt/flash, /mnt/usb1, /mnt/drive (SSD models) and /persist (a link into flash) survive a reload (p. 56). If you don't want junior engineers in Bash, restrict it with AAA (p. 55).
NeedCommandNotes
EOS version from a scriptcat /etc/Eos-releaseOne line, easy to parse (p. 56). Cli -c "show version | json" is better when you need more fields.
Which agent started which processpstreeShows ProcMgr starting the agents, and ConfigAgent starting your CLI and Bash (pp. 56-57).
Kernel view of the portsifconfig -s, ip -br linkFront-panel ports appear as et1, et2 and so on, next to internal interfaces such as cpu, txfwd and vxlan (p. 57).
Memory and CPU over timevmstat 1 5Five one-second samples (p. 55).
Run one EOS command from BashCli -p 15 -c "show ip bgp summary"-p 15 runs at privilege 15. Cli --help lists the other options (p. 55).
Run one Linux command from EOSbash uname -a, bash pwdRuns in your home directory (p. 56).
Storageflash: = /mnt/flash, drive: = /mnt/driveOn SSD models, drive: keeps the var_archive log archives (p. 56).
Linux network namespace of a VRFip netns list, sudo ip netns exec ns-VRF ...Each VRF is a kernel namespace; the [netns] entries in pstree are those (p. 57).
Watch out. cEOS is a container, so its root file system is the container's writable layer, not a RAM disk rebuilt at boot. A docker restart may keep files in your home directory that a hardware switch would lose, while containerlab destroy and deploy wipe them. flash: survives both because containerlab maps it to a folder on the host. For a faithful persistence test, use a vEOS node in EVE-NG, where reload behaves like hardware.

  1. On leaf1, enter Bash. Find out who you are, where you are, and whether you can read /var/log/messages with and without sudo. Leave Bash, then run pwd and uname -a without entering it.

  2. From Bash on leaf1, read the EOS version from a file, show the process tree up to the first 30 lines, list the kernel interfaces with their counters, and take five one-second vmstat samples. Match the kernel interfaces to show interfaces status.

  3. From Bash, print only the image version line of show version, save leaf1's running config to a file in flash with a timestamp in the name, and set the description FROM-BASH on Loopback0 in a single Cli call. Put the original description back.

  4. On host2 (vEOS in EVE-NG if you have it), create a test file in your home directory, in /mnt/flash and in /persist. Reload the node and see which files are still there. Find out where /persist really points on your release.

  5. On host2, list the network namespaces and find the one for VRF H10. Capture five ICMP packets on its vlan10 interface while you ping the anycast gateway from VRF H10 in a second session.

  6. Write a small backup script in flash that saves the running config to flash:backup/ with the hostname and a timestamp in the file name and keeps only the newest 30 files. Run it every 24 hours with the EOS schedule command, run it once by hand, and then remove the schedule.

Solution · getting around, and the Linux view of the switch 
leaf1# bash
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ whoami; id; pwd; ls -al
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ tail -3 /var/log/messages
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ sudo tail -3 /var/log/messages
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ cat /etc/Eos-release
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ pstree | head -30
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ ifconfig -s
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ ip -br link
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ vmstat 1 5
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ exit
leaf1# bash pwd
leaf1# bash uname -a
leaf1# show interfaces status

You are admin in /home/admin, with no superuser rights until you add sudo (p. 55). On hardware the ports show up as et1, et2 and so on (p. 57). On cEOS you will also see the eth1 to eth5 veth links that containerlab created (because of INTFTYPE=eth); EOS maps them to Ethernet1 to Ethernet5. In pstree, find ProcMgr and the agents from the SysDB section (Stp, Lldp, Bgp, Sysdb), and your own bash under ConfigAgent (pp. 56-57).

Solution · Cli from Bash 
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ Cli --help
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ Cli -c "show version" | grep -i image
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ Cli -p 15 -c "show running-config" > /mnt/flash/leaf1-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M).cfg
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ ls -l /mnt/flash/*.cfg
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ Cli -p 15 -c $'configure\ninterface Loopback0\ndescription FROM-BASH\nend'
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ Cli -c "show running-config interfaces Loopback0"
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ Cli -p 15 -c $'configure\ninterface Loopback0\ndescription ROUTER_ID\nend'

The book saves its output to the home directory and then warns that the file will not survive a reboot (p. 56), so write anything you want to keep to /mnt/flash. $'...' is Bash syntax for a string with \n line breaks, which lets one Cli call enter several lines of configuration. Check the original Loopback0 description on leaf2 or in the AVD config before you restore it. For structured data, use | json and a JSON tool rather than grep; that is the step from here to eAPI (Lab D7).

Solution · what survives a reload (host2) 
host2# bash
[admin@host2 ~]$ echo home > ~/t-home.txt
[admin@host2 ~]$ echo flash > /mnt/flash/t-flash.txt
[admin@host2 ~]$ ls -ld /persist /persist/*
[admin@host2 ~]$ sudo sh -c 'echo persist > /persist/local/t-persist.txt'
[admin@host2 ~]$ exit
host2# write memory
host2# reload now
! log in again after the reload
host2# bash ls -l ~/t-home.txt /mnt/flash/t-flash.txt /persist/local/t-persist.txt

On vEOS and hardware, the home file is gone and the flash and persist files remain (p. 56). The book describes /persist as a link to /mnt/flash/persist/; on newer releases it is a directory with subfolders such as local, sys and secure, so check with ls -ld and write into a subfolder that exists. On cEOS, see the warning above. Delete the test files afterwards.

Solution · tcpdump inside a VRF (host2) 
host2# bash ip netns list
host2# bash sudo ip netns exec ns-H10 ip -br addr
host2# bash sudo ip netns exec ns-H10 tcpdump -n -i vlan10 -c 5 icmp
! in a second session
host2# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.1 repeat 5
13:42:07.118 IP 10.10.10.102 > 10.10.10.1: ICMP echo request, id 512, seq 1, length 80
13:42:07.121 IP 10.10.10.1 > 10.10.10.102: ICMP echo reply, id 512, seq 1, length 80

Representative output. Each EOS VRF is a Linux namespace named ns-VRF; the default VRF is the main namespace. Without ip netns exec, tcpdump -i vlan10 fails because that interface lives in the H10 namespace. EOS also has its own tcpdump command (try tcpdump ?), which saves you the namespace step for interfaces in the default VRF. On hardware, a capture on the switch sees only traffic to and from the CPU (control plane, ARP, pings to the switch), not the traffic the ASIC forwards; use a monitor session or TAP for that. On cEOS forwarding is done in software, so you may see more.

Solution · timestamped backup with schedule 
host2# bash
[admin@host2 ~]$ mkdir -p /mnt/flash/backup
[admin@host2 ~]$ cat > /mnt/flash/backup.sh <<'SCRIPT'
#!/bin/bash
# Save running-config to flash with hostname and timestamp; keep the newest 30.
d=/mnt/flash/backup
mkdir -p "$d"
FastCli -p 15 -c "show running-config" > "$d/$(hostname)-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S).cfg"
ls -1t "$d"/*.cfg | tail -n +31 | xargs -r rm -f
SCRIPT
[admin@host2 ~]$ chmod +x /mnt/flash/backup.sh
[admin@host2 ~]$ /mnt/flash/backup.sh && ls -l /mnt/flash/backup/
[admin@host2 ~]$ exit
host2(config)# schedule cfg-backup interval 1440 max-log-files 5 command bash /mnt/flash/backup.sh
host2# show schedule cfg-backup
host2(config)# no schedule cfg-backup

The script and its output live in flash, so both survive a reload (p. 56). schedule also keeps its own gzipped log of each run under flash:schedule/cfg-backup/, which is where to look if the backup folder stays empty. A simpler built-in alternative is the plain scheduled show running-config from objective 1.1, task 9. Keep an eye on flash space (objective 1.3): 30 copies of a large config add up.

Real world. On-box backups are a safety net, not a backup strategy: if the switch dies, so do they. Pull configs off the box from your Ubuntu automation server instead (the arista.eos.eos_config module with backup: true, or the AVD and CloudVision config history), and keep on-box copies for the "I just broke it and SSH still works" moment. Bash is very useful for one-off troubleshooting (namespaces, captures, ss and netstat for listening sockets, top for a busy agent), but anything you do there is invisible to the config and to CloudVision; if you find yourself repeating it, turn it into an Event Manager action, an alias or an extension. To keep juniors out of Bash, give them a role that denies the bash command (for example role NO-BASH with 10 deny mode exec command bash and a final permit command .*; check the exact syntax with ?) or use TACACS+ command authorization (p. 55).

Break / fix

Inject: a colleague's cron-style backup on a vEOS or hardware switch writes Cli -p 15 -c "show running-config" > ~/backups/run.cfg every night. After a planned reload the change board asks for last night's config.

Symptom: ~/backups no longer exists, and the scheduled job's log shows "No such file or directory" on every run since the reload.

Root cause and fix

The home directory is part of the file system that is rebuilt at boot, so the folder and every backup in it were lost, and the script does not recreate it (p. 56). Move the script and its output to /mnt/flash (or /persist), add mkdir -p to the script, and test it across one reload. Better still, collect the configs off-box from the automation server.

The EOS extension system

This section follows AW Ch 16 · The EOS Extension System (pp. 76-78) and is the last foundations part: SysDB agents, the CLI, configuration files, the Linux shell, and now how new software gets onto the switch. The graded version of this skill is in objective 1.3 (install, persist, remove, and a broken boot-extensions break/fix); this part explains the model and adds the reload test.

Key ideas from the chapter. An extension (.swix, SoftWare Image eXtension) is one or more RPM packages zipped together with a manifest (p. 76). EOS runs on Linux but deliberately has no access to the normal package repositories, because an unplanned Linux upgrade could break EOS; the extension system is the controlled way to add packages, together with their dependencies, in one file (p. 76). Arista also ships security patches this way (pp. 76-77). The life cycle has four steps (pp. 76-77): copy the file to the extension: file system (a hidden .extensions folder on flash), install it with extension NAME, check show extensions (A or NA for available, I or NI for installed, F for forced), and make it persistent with copy installed-extensions boot-extensions, because the installed state does not survive a reboot on its own (p. 77). boot-extensions is just a text file on flash listing what to load at boot (p. 77). no extension NAME only uninstalls; delete extension:NAME removes the file (p. 77). Extensions load early in boot, so startup-config can contain commands that only exist once the extension is loaded (p. 78). Some extensions add CLI commands or aliases that appear only after you log out and back in (p. 76).
StepCommandState after a reload
Fetchcopy http://SERVER/FILE.swix extension: (also https:, scp:, sftp:, ftp:, tftp:, flash:, p. 76)The file stays on flash.
Installextension FILE.swixLost, unless it is in boot-extensions (p. 77).
Persistcopy installed-extensions boot-extensionsInstalled again at every boot.
Uninstallno extension FILE.swix, then copy installed-extensions boot-extensions againWithout the copy, it comes back at the next boot.
Removedelete extension:FILE.swixGone. A stale line may stay in boot-extensions (p. 77).

  1. On the Ubuntu automation server, put an extension file in nginx's web root (a TerminAttr .swix from Arista Software Downloads is the realistic choice). On leaf1, copy it straight from the server into extension: over HTTP, install it, and read show extensions before and after the install.

  2. Without updating boot-extensions, reload the node and check show extensions again. Then install it again, make it persistent, look at the boot-extensions file in EOS and in Bash, reload once more and check.

  3. Remove the extension completely: uninstall, delete the file, and leave boot-extensions in the state a careless engineer would. Read show boot-extensions and the file in Bash, and clean up the stale entry.

Solution · fetch and install from the Ubuntu server 
$ sudo mkdir -p /var/www/html/swix
$ sudo cp TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix /var/www/html/swix/

leaf1# show extensions
leaf1# copy http://172.100.100.5/swix/TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix extension:
leaf1# dir extension:
leaf1# show extensions
leaf1# extension TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix
leaf1# show extensions
leaf1# show extensions detail

Use the address of your automation server and the file name you downloaded. Before the install, the status shows A, NI; afterwards A, I, and the last column counts the RPMs inside (pp. 76-77). On containerlab, if leaf1 cannot reach 172.100.100.5, copy the file in with docker cp to /mnt/flash/ and use copy flash:FILE extension:. If the install is refused because of signature checking, see the note in the objective 1.3 solution. Without an Arista account, the steps are the same with any .swix your team builds; Arista's open-source swi-tools can package an RPM as a .swix.

Solution · the reload test 
leaf1# write memory
leaf1# reload now
leaf1# show extensions
! A, NI: the file is there but no longer installed

leaf1# extension TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix
leaf1# copy installed-extensions boot-extensions
leaf1# show boot-extensions
leaf1# more flash:boot-extensions
leaf1# bash cat /mnt/flash/boot-extensions
leaf1# reload now
leaf1# show extensions
! A, I: installed again at boot

Installation is a run-time state, so it is lost on reload unless the extension is listed in boot-extensions (p. 77). On cEOS, reload restarts EOS inside the container and should show the same behavior; if your cEOS release does not, repeat the test on a vEOS node, which reloads like hardware. On a chassis with two supervisors, the file must be on both (p. 76).

Solution · removal and the stale boot-extensions line 
leaf1# no extension TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix
leaf1# delete extension:TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix
leaf1# show extensions
leaf1# show boot-extensions
! still lists TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix

! the clean fix: rewrite the list from what is installed now
leaf1# copy installed-extensions boot-extensions
leaf1# show boot-extensions

! or edit the file in Bash
leaf1# bash sudo sed -i '/TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix/d' /mnt/flash/boot-extensions

There is no delete boot-extensions command; the book's author ended up removing the entry from the file in Bash (p. 77). Running copy installed-extensions boot-extensions after every change avoids the problem, because it rewrites the whole list from the current state. A stale entry makes EOS try to load a missing file at every boot and log an error, which is the break/fix in objective 1.3.

Exam tip. Three words to remember: copy, install, persist. A task that says "install the extension so that it survives a reload" is only complete when show boot-extensions lists it, and a removal is only complete when it does not.
Real world. In production, extensions are mostly security patches, bug-fix hotfixes and agents such as TerminAttr; patches for Arista security advisories are often shipped as extensions, so check the advisory for a .swix before you plan a full upgrade (p. 77). Keep the files on an internal server, roll them out with CloudVision image bundles or Ansible (copy, install, persist, verify with show extensions), and remove a hotfix once you upgrade to a release that includes the fix. Large operators go further and write their own agents with the EOS SDK, shipped as extensions (p. 78).

1.2 EOS upgrades: standard, SSU, MLAG ISSU, chassis, CLI and CloudVision

Change window: upgrade POD_A (leaf1 and leaf2) to a new EOS release with no host outage. host1 is dual-homed with an LACP port-channel, so one leaf at a time can be out of service. You will run the real procedure up to the reload. cEOS cannot boot a .swi image, so the reload step uses a container image swap as the analog. The basic image steps follow AW Ch 10 (pp. 51-52); to do a real reload into a new image, use A real image upgrade on vEOS after this objective.

MethodWhat it doesTraffic impactWhen
Standard upgradeboot system (or install source) then reload. Full reboot.Device down for several minutes. Hidden by redundancy (MLAG peer, ECMP).Any fixed or modular switch.
Smart System Upgrade (SSU)reload fast-boot: the data plane keeps forwarding with the last programmed state while the control plane restarts into the new image.Sub-second loss on supported platforms.Supported fixed platforms, typically single-homed leaf. Check release notes and show reload fast-boot warnings.
MLAG ISSUUpgrade one MLAG peer at a time while the other forwards. Peers run different versions briefly.Near zero for dual-homed hosts. Single-homed hosts on the reloading peer go down.MLAG pairs. Versions must be ISSU-compatible (show mlag issu warnings, release notes).
Chassis with dual supervisorsUpgrade standby supervisor, redundancy switchover (SSO), then upgrade the old active.Hitless for the control plane with SSO; line card reloads depend on release.Modular 7500R/7800R class switches.
From CloudVisionImage bundle (Network Provisioning) or Software Management Studio, applied through change control with optional MLAG-aware ordering and BGP maintenance actions.Same as the method CV executes.Fleet upgrades with approvals and audit.

  1. Pre-checks. On leaf1 and leaf2 record the current version, boot configuration, flash free space, MLAG state, MLAG config-sanity, reload-delay values and the ISSU warnings. On spine1 record the BGP summary and the number of paths to the POD_A VTEP 10.255.1.3. Save a checkpoint pre-upgrade on both leaves. Find out which peer is MLAG primary and which is secondary with show mlag detail | include State. The steps below assume leaf2 is the secondary; if leaf1 is, swap the names.

  2. Stage the image on the secondary (leaf2) first; the primary is upgraded last. Before you go further, check that the new image is MLAG ISSU compatible with the running image. Copy the new image to flash and verify its checksum against the value published on arista.com. In this lab, create a placeholder file to practice the commands.

  3. Point the next boot at the new image with boot system (classic) and know the newer install source form. Check show boot-config.

  4. Drain leaf2 gracefully: put the built-in System maintenance unit into quiesce, confirm leaf2 is under maintenance, and confirm on spine1 that traffic to 10.255.1.3 now prefers leaf1.

  5. Save the config and reload leaf2 with plain reload (in cEOS: do the image swap analog described in the solution). Read every warning EOS prints before confirming; if one applies, answer no and fix the cause first. While it is down, ping from host1 continuously and confirm no loss beyond a second or two.

  6. Post-checks on leaf2: version, MLAG active and both ports of Po5 up after the reload-delay, BGP and EVPN sessions back. Take leaf2 out of maintenance. Only then repeat steps 2 to 6 on leaf1.

Verify

leaf2# show version
Arista cEOSLab
Hardware version:
Serial number: 5B3C6F0A1E2D
System MAC address: 001c.7300.0002

Software image version: 4.34.2F-...
Architecture: x86_64
Internal build version: 4.34.2F-...
Uptime: 2 hours and 11 minutes
Total memory: 32867200 kB
Free memory: 21550212 kB
leaf2# show mlag issu warnings
If you are performing an upgrade, and the Release Notes for the new version of
EOS indicate that MLAG is not backwards-compatible with the currently installed
version (4.34.2F), the upgrade will result in packet loss.

Stateful Switchover is not supported in MLAG and the reload of the switch will
cause traffic loss on the MLAG ports ... (text varies by release)
leaf2(config-maintenance)# show maintenance
Flags:
o - On-boot maintenance
v - Violations present
...
Unit Name           Status                         Flags
------------------- ------------------------------ ------
System              Under Maintenance
spine1# show ip bgp 10.255.1.3/32
BGP routing table information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.1, local AS number 65100
BGP routing table entry for 10.255.1.3/32
 Paths: 2 available
  65101
    10.255.255.1 from 10.255.255.1 (10.255.0.3)
      Origin IGP, metric 0, localpref 100, IGP metric 0, weight 0, tag 0
      Received 02:11:40 ago, valid, external, best
  65101 ...
    10.255.255.5 from 10.255.255.5 (10.255.0.4)
      ... valid, external   (leaf2 path now less preferred or withdrawn)

Before the reload, leaf2 is Under Maintenance, spine1 no longer uses leaf2 as an ECMP next hop for 10.255.1.3, and host1 pings still succeed. After the reload, show mlag shows state: Active, show port-channel 5 detailed shows both members in the bundle (once reload-delay has passed), and the unit is back to active.

Solution · pre-checks and staging 
leaf2# show version
leaf2# show boot-config
leaf2# dir flash:
leaf2# show file systems
leaf2# show mlag
leaf2# show mlag config-sanity
leaf2# show mlag detail | include reload
leaf2# show mlag detail | include State
leaf2# show mlag issu warnings
leaf2# show spanning-tree bridge detail | include agent
leaf2# configure checkpoint save pre-upgrade
spine1# show ip bgp summary
spine1# show ip route 10.255.1.3

! real switch: copy from an SCP/HTTP server, then verify the hash
leaf2# copy scp:admin@172.100.100.1/images/EOS-4.34.3M.swi flash:
leaf2# verify /sha512 flash:EOS-4.34.3M.swi
! lab: practice with a placeholder (cEOS cannot boot a .swi)
leaf2# bash dd if=/dev/zero of=/mnt/flash/EOS-4.34.3M.swi bs=1M count=10
leaf2# verify /md5 flash:EOS-4.34.3M.swi

leaf2(config)# boot system flash:EOS-4.34.3M.swi
! newer releases: install source flash:EOS-4.34.3M.swi  (validates the image and sets boot-config)
leaf2# show boot-config

On cEOS boot system and install source may be rejected or have no effect, and install source will refuse the placeholder because it is not a valid image. That is expected; the point is knowing the order of operations. Check show mlag issu compatibility ? on your release; some releases can check a staged image directly.

Solution · drain, reload, return to service 
leaf2# show maintenance units System
leaf2# configure
leaf2(config)# maintenance
leaf2(config-maintenance)# unit System
leaf2(config-builtin-unit-System)# quiesce
leaf2(config-builtin-unit-System)# end
leaf2# show maintenance
leaf2# show ip bgp summary
! neighbors in maintenance are flagged; spines see leaf2 routes deprioritized
leaf2# write memory
leaf2# reload
! read the MLAG warnings; answer no and fix if any apply. Never "reload now" here.
! real switch only. On cEOS use the image-swap analog below.

! --- containerlab analog of "reload into new EOS" ---
! 1. import a second cEOS image, for example ceos:4.34.3M
! 2. in a copy of l5dc.clab.yml set leaf2's image to ceos:4.34.3M
! 3. host$ sudo containerlab destroy -t l5dc.clab.yml --node-filter leaf2
!    host$ sudo containerlab deploy  -t l5dc.clab.yml --node-filter leaf2
!    (check "containerlab deploy --help" for node-filter on your version;
!     do NOT use --reconfigure, it discards the saved flash config)
! meanwhile on host1:
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 repeat 1000

leaf2# show version | include image
leaf2# show mlag
leaf2# show mlag interfaces 5
leaf2# show bgp evpn summary
leaf2# configure
leaf2(config)# maintenance
leaf2(config-maintenance)# unit System
leaf2(config-builtin-unit-System)# no quiesce
leaf2(config-builtin-unit-System)# end
leaf2# show maintenance
leaf2# write memory
Watch out: MLAG ISSU order and reload warnings. AW Ch 18 lays out the procedure (the book is written for EOS 4.21, so the syntax here is current EOS; confirm with ?). Before you start, check the new image's MLAG ISSU compatibility against the running image (release notes compatibility table, show mlag issu warnings; AW Ch 18, p. 89). The order (AW Ch 18, pp. 96-97): find primary and secondary with show mlag detail | include State, confirm show mlag config-sanity is clean, resolve any reload warnings, upgrade the secondary first, monitor until it is fully back, then upgrade the primary and confirm again. When you type reload on an MLAG peer, EOS warns about conditions that would cause an outage, for example MLAGs that are only active-partial (one side down), a reload-delay set below the recommended value, a peer that still has errdisabled ports because it is inside its own reload-delay, and spanning tree that cannot restart gracefully (check show spanning-tree bridge detail | include agent). Never use reload now during an MLAG upgrade: it skips these warnings. After the upgraded peer boots, its MLAG ports stay errdisabled for the reload-delay (300 s by default on fixed switches; AW Ch 18, p. 94), so wait for that peer to finish before you touch the other one.
Exam tip. The exam environment cannot really reload into a new image for you either, so upgrade questions are likely to test the steps and state: correct boot system or image bundle, the right peer drained first, maintenance mode entered and exited, ISSU warnings checked. Leaving a unit in maintenance is a classic way to "finish" a task in a broken state. Always end with show maintenance and show boot-config.
Real world. On an MLAG pair the reload-delay mlag (300 s here) and reload-delay non-mlag (330 s) timers keep the rebooted peer's MLAG ports in errdisabled state until MLAG state and routing have converged, so it does not black-hole traffic as it boots.
