Wait for the timer and for show mlag to report Active before you touch the second peer. Read the release notes for the ISSU compatibility table before every upgrade.

Break / fix

Inject: after "upgrading" leaf2, leave the System unit quiesced and proceed to drain leaf1 as well.

Symptom: both leaves show Under Maintenance. On spine1 the routes to 10.255.1.3 are still present but every path carries the maintenance policy; the network appears to work, but leaf2 never returns to full ECMP and any later failure of leaf1 is not protected as you expect. In a real fabric with other paths, all traffic avoids POD_A.

Root cause and fix

Maintenance mode is persistent configuration, not a one-shot action. Exit it on leaf2 first (maintenance, unit System, no quiesce), verify with show maintenance and on spine1 that both next hops are back for 10.255.1.3 (show ip route 10.255.1.3 shows two ECMP paths), then continue with leaf1. Add "exit maintenance" to your post-check list.

A real image upgrade on vEOS

This section follows AW Ch 10 · Upgrading EOS (pp. 51-52). Objective 1.2 practices the MLAG-safe procedure on cEOS, which cannot boot a .swi. Here you do the part cEOS cannot: copy an image, point boot-config at it and reload into the new release, on a vEOS node in EVE-NG. Use ztp1 or any vEOS node outside the fabric, and download a second vEOS-lab .swi release from Arista (newer or older than the one you run; both directions work the same way).

Key ideas from the chapter. An upgrade is three steps: get the image onto the switch, tell the switch to boot it, reload (p. 51). show version gives the release; show version detail adds every installed package with its version and the firmware components such as Aboot, which is mostly useful to TAC (p. 51). The letter in the release name shows its life-cycle stage: F for a feature release, M for a maintenance release (p. 51). dir ? lists the file systems, including drive: on SSD models and usb1: when a USB stick is inserted (p. 51). EOS does not boot the first image it finds; boot system chooses it (p. 51). That command does not change running-config: it writes SWI= in flash:boot-config, which is why show running-config | include boot shows it as a comment and why reload does not ask you to save after it (pp. 51-52). show boot reads boot-config (p. 52). Without boot-config, Aboot has no image to load and stops at its prompt; current EOS warns you at reload instead (p. 52). Some upgrades also update FPGA firmware, which adds minutes and sometimes extra reboots; do not power off during it (p. 52).

  1. On the vEOS node, record show version and show version detail (just the first page and the components at the end), dir ?, dir flash:, show boot and show running-config | include boot. Explain why the boot line is a comment.

  2. Put the second .swi on the Ubuntu automation server's web root. Copy it to flash over HTTP, check free space first, and verify its checksum against the value on Arista's download page.

  3. Point the next boot at the new image and check show boot and dir flash: (look at boot-config's time stamp). Reload, and note whether EOS asks you to save. After the reload, confirm the new release.

  4. Go back to the original release the same way. Keep both images on flash when you are done.

Solution · before the upgrade 
ztp1# show version
ztp1# show version detail | no-more
ztp1# show version detail | begin Component
ztp1# dir ?
ztp1# dir flash:
ztp1# show boot
ztp1# show running-config | include boot
! boot system flash:/vEOS-lab.swi

Representative output. The line begins with ! because it is not running-config; EOS only shows what boot-config says (pp. 51-52). On a hardware switch, show inventory tells you whether an SSD is fitted (and so whether drive: exists), and the model name itself encodes airflow, memory and port types (p. 51). The packages in show version detail are the RPMs that make up EOS, the same format that extensions use.

Solution · copy, verify, boot system, reload 
$ sudo mkdir -p /var/www/html/images
$ sudo cp vEOS-lab-4.35.0F.swi /var/www/html/images/
$ sha512sum /var/www/html/images/vEOS-lab-4.35.0F.swi

ztp1# dir flash: | include bytes
ztp1# copy http://172.100.100.5/images/vEOS-lab-4.35.0F.swi flash:
ztp1# verify /sha512 flash:vEOS-lab-4.35.0F.swi
ztp1(config)# boot system flash:vEOS-lab-4.35.0F.swi
ztp1# show boot
ztp1# dir flash:
ztp1# reload
! no "save?" prompt if nothing else changed: boot system did not touch running-config
ztp1# show version

Use the real file names and compare the checksum with the one Arista publishes for that file; check verify ? for the hash types your release offers (/md5, /sha512). copy also accepts https:, scp:, sftp:, ftp: and tftp: sources (p. 51). Tab completion saves typing the file name (p. 51). After boot system, the boot-config time stamp in dir flash: changes and show boot names the new image (p. 52). The first boot of a new release takes longer than a normal reload; on hardware, watch the console for FPGA messages and let them finish (p. 52). For MLAG pairs, wrap these steps in the drain and ISSU checks of objective 1.2 (AW Ch 18).

Exam tip. If a task says "make the switch boot release X", the graded state is show boot, not running-config, and write memory alone does not change it. Check that the file named in SWI= exists in dir flash: before you leave the task.
Real world. Copy images from an internal server, verify the checksum every time (a truncated copy is the classic failed upgrade), and keep exactly two images on flash. Choose releases with Arista's life-cycle policy in mind: M releases for production, F releases only when you need a new feature (p. 51). For more than a handful of switches, let CloudVision image bundles or your Ansible playbooks do the copy, verify, boot system and reload, with the post-checks in the same run (p. 52).

Break / fix

Inject (on the vEOS node): delete flash:boot-config, then reload.

Symptom: current EOS stops you with a warning that the boot image is not set or not present and asks whether you really want to reload. On older releases, or if you confirm anyway, the node stops at an Aboot# prompt with a message that no SWI is specified in /mnt/flash/boot-config.

Root cause and fix

Without boot-config, Aboot does not know which image to load (p. 52). If you are still in EOS, answer no and run boot system flash:FILE, then show boot (p. 52). If you are already in Aboot, boot the image by hand and fix boot-config from EOS:

Aboot# ls /mnt/flash
Aboot# boot /mnt/flash/vEOS-lab.swi
ztp1(config)# boot system flash:vEOS-lab.swi
ztp1# show boot

Or create the file in Aboot with echo SWI=flash:vEOS-lab.swi > /mnt/flash/boot-config and reboot. More Aboot recovery: Aboot hands-on. On hardware, a switch sitting in Aboot also runs its fans at full speed until you fix it (p. 52).

1.3 EOS extensions, Aboot recovery, flash and memory health

Operations wants a streaming agent extension installed on every leaf so it survives reboots, a documented password-recovery procedure, and a monthly health check of storage and memory.

  1. Inventory: list installed extensions and boot extensions on leaf1. cEOS ships TerminAttr built in, so the list may be empty; that is fine.

  2. Install an extension package (SWIX or RPM). If you have an Arista account, download the TerminAttr SWIX from Software Downloads and copy it to leaf1. Install it, confirm it is installed, and make it persistent across reboot.

  3. Remove it again cleanly: uninstall, update boot extensions, delete the file.

  4. Health check on leaf1: show file systems and free space, the biggest consumers on flash, CPU and memory top processes, uptime and reload cause, and any agent crashes or core files. Clean up the placeholder image from 1.2.

  5. Write down (in your notes, no lab action) the Aboot steps to recover a switch with a lost password and a switch that fails to boot its image.

Verify

leaf1# show extensions
Name                          Version/Release       Status      Extension
----------------------------- --------------------- ----------- ---------
TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix      v1.35.0/1.el7         A, I        1

A: available | NA: not available | I: installed | F: forced | S: valid signature
leaf1# show boot-extensions
TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix
leaf1# show file systems
File Systems:

     Size(b)      Free(b)  Type     Flags  Prefixes
 62725623808  40125337600  ext4     rw     flash:
     1931920      1930000  tmpfs    rw     extension:
           -            -  system   rw     system:
           -            -  checkpoint rw   checkpoint:
           -            -  network  rw     scp:, http:, https:, ftp:, tftp:
leaf1# show processes top once
top - 10:14:01 up  2:31,  0 users,  load average: 0.61, 0.70, 0.66
Tasks: 312 total,   1 running, 311 sleeping
%Cpu(s):  4.1 us,  2.0 sy,  0.0 ni, 93.6 id
KiB Mem : 32867200 total, 21550212 free,  6120340 used
  PID USER      PR  NI    VIRT    RES  %CPU %MEM     TIME+ COMMAND
 1876 root      20   0  690m   310m    3.0  1.0   4:12.33 Sysdb
 2310 root      20   0  780m   290m    2.3  0.9   3:40.10 Bgp
 2290 root      20   0  560m   190m    1.0  0.6   1:02.44 Strata (absent on cEOS)

The extension shows A, I and appears in show boot-extensions. After removal it is in neither list and dir extension: is empty. On cEOS, flash is a directory on the host disk, so the free space you see is the host's free space.

Solution · extensions 
leaf1# show extensions
leaf1# show boot-extensions
leaf1# copy scp:admin@172.100.100.1/swix/TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix extension:
! from the host you can also: docker cp TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix clab-l5dc-leaf1:/mnt/flash/
!   then: copy flash:TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix extension:
leaf1# dir extension:
leaf1# extension TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix
leaf1# show extensions detail
leaf1# copy installed-extensions boot-extensions
leaf1# show boot-extensions

! removal
leaf1# no extension TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix
leaf1# copy installed-extensions boot-extensions
leaf1# delete extension:TerminAttr-1.35.0-1.swix

Extension signature checking is controlled under management security (signature-verification extension); an unsigned package needs extension NAME force or verification turned off. Use ? to confirm on your release.

Solution · health check 
leaf1# show file systems
leaf1# dir flash:
leaf1# bash df -h /mnt/flash
leaf1# bash sudo du -sh /mnt/flash/* | sort -h | tail
leaf1# show processes top once
leaf1# show processes top memory once
leaf1# show version | include memory
leaf1# show uptime
leaf1# show reload cause
leaf1# show agent uptime
leaf1# show logging | include (CRASH|core|AGENT)
leaf1# show system coredump
leaf1# delete flash:EOS-4.34.3M.swi

On hardware also add show system environment all, show inventory and show hardware capacity. cEOS has no fans, PSUs, temperature sensors or forwarding ASIC, so those commands return little or nothing.

SituationAboot procedure (concept)
Enter AbootConsole access, reboot, press Ctrl-C when prompted ("Press Control-C now to enter Aboot shell"). If an Aboot password is set (boot secret in EOS config, shown in show boot), you need it. Hands-on steps on vEOS: Aboot hands-on.
Lost admin passwordIn the Aboot shell: cd /mnt/flash, rename startup-config (for example mv startup-config startup-config.old), boot or reboot. EOS boots with an empty config and starts ZTP (zerotouch cancel). Log in as admin without a password, copy the old file back into running config, fix the user, write memory.
Image will not bootIn Aboot: ls /mnt/flash to find another .swi, edit boot-config (SWI=flash:EOS-old.swi) or run boot flash:EOS-old.swi. Keep the previous image on flash until the new one is proven.
Factory recoveryfullrecover in Aboot restores the factory image and erases flash. Last resort; you lose configs and images.
Boot loops into ZTPNo startup-config means ZTP. zerotouch cancel for this boot, zerotouch disable to stop it permanently (this reloads).
Exam tip. Know the extension life cycle by heart: copy ... extension:, extension NAME, copy installed-extensions boot-extensions. Installing without updating boot-extensions works until the next reload, and a graded reload or a check of show boot-extensions will catch it.
Real world. Full flash breaks upgrades (the copy fails or the image is truncated) and can stop configuration saves. Old .swi files, tech-support archives under flash:schedule/ and core files are the usual culprits. Keep exactly two images on flash: the running one and the previous one.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf1): bash dd if=/dev/zero of=/mnt/flash/.hidden-dump.bin bs=1M count=500, and append a file that does not exist to the boot extensions list: bash echo missing-agent.swix | sudo tee -a /mnt/flash/boot-extensions.

Symptom: flash free space dropped by 500 MB with nothing obvious in dir flash:; show boot-extensions lists an extension that show extensions does not know. After a reboot the log reports a boot extension that could not be installed.

Root cause and fix

Hidden files do not show in a plain dir. Use dir /all flash: (shows hidden files) or bash sudo du -ah /mnt/flash | sort -h | tail to find the big file and delete it (delete flash:.hidden-dump.bin). For boot extensions, rebuild the list from what is really installed: copy installed-extensions boot-extensions, then check show boot-extensions. On cEOS do not fill flash completely: it is your host disk.

Aboot hands-on (vEOS only)

This section follows AW Ch 14 · Aboot (pp. 63-67) and turns the Aboot table in objective 1.3 into real practice. cEOS has no bootloader, so everything here needs a vEOS node in EVE-NG with console access. Use the spare node ztp1 from the ZTP lab (or any vEOS node that is not part of the fabric), and before you start, export its config or note how to wipe it in EVE-NG, because some steps erase it.

Key ideas from the chapter. Aboot is the second-stage bootloader that loads EOS (p. 63). It reads /mnt/flash/boot-config to find the image, and halts at an Aboot# prompt if boot-config is missing or corrupt, if the image is missing or corrupt, or if someone presses Ctrl-C on the console at the "Press Control-C now to enter Aboot shell" message (p. 63). Aboot is a tiny Linux built on BusyBox, so ls, cd, more, vi, cp, wget, scp and ifconfig work, plus Aboot's own boot, swiinfo, recover, netconf, udhcpc and showtech (p. 63). flash: is an EOS name; in Aboot you go to /mnt/flash, and anything outside flash, such as your home directory, does not exist there (p. 64). The ASIC driver is not loaded, so the only network port Aboot can use is the management port, ma1 (pp. 64, 66). On a switch in Aboot the fans run at full speed (p. 63).
boot-config lineMeaningSet it from
SWI=The image to boot: flash:, /mnt/flash/, usb1:, or a network URL with http://, ftp:// (with optional user:pass@), tftp:// or nfs:// (p. 64)EOS boot system, Aboot or Bash
CONSOLESPEED=Console speed: 1200 to 38400, default 9600 (p. 64)EOS boot console speed
PASSWORD=Aboot shell password, stored as a SHA-512 hash (p. 65)EOS boot secret only; never type it in by hand (p. 65)
NETDEV=, NETAUTO=, NETIP=, NETMASK=, NETGW=, NETDOMAIN=, NETDNS=Aboot's own network settings, used to fetch a network image: device (ma1), dhcp or static, dotted-decimal mask only, IPv4 only (p. 66)Aboot or Bash only; EOS cannot set them (p. 66)
Watch out. On vEOS, some Aboot features behave differently from a hardware switch: the management device name, the recovery partition behind fullrecover, and the FPGA steps do not exist on a VM. Treat vEOS as the place to learn the shell and the boot-config file, and check anything you plan to do on hardware against the platform's documentation. Know your EVE-NG console well: Ctrl-C must reach the node, so use the console window, not SSH.

  1. From EOS on ztp1, read the boot-config three ways: show boot, more flash:boot-config and bash cat /mnt/flash/boot-config. Note the image name. Then reload, press Ctrl-C at the Aboot prompt, and explore: help, pwd, ls -al, cd /mnt/flash, more boot-config, swiinfo on the image, ifconfig -a. Boot EOS from the prompt.

  2. Set an Aboot password LabAboot1 from EOS, check how it appears in show boot, and test it at the Aboot prompt, including a wrong password three times (but do not accept what Aboot offers). Then remove it.

  3. Password recovery: on ztp1, add a user admin with a password you then "forget" (type a random one), save, and log out. Recover access through Aboot twice: once by commenting out the username lines, once by renaming startup-config. Explain what happens to ZTP the second time and get back to the original config.

  4. Network boot: copy the vEOS image from ztp1 to the Ubuntu server's web root, then set boot-config so Aboot boots the image over HTTP from 172.100.100.5 using a static management address. Reboot without touching the keyboard, and confirm in show version and show boot where it booted from. Put boot-config back to flash.

  5. Manual network fetch: in Aboot, give ma1 an address yourself and download a file from the Ubuntu server with wget, the way you would pull an image onto a switch whose flash was wiped.

  6. Lab only: read the fullrecover solution. If you want to see it, run it on ztp1 and rebuild the node afterwards (or wipe it in EVE-NG).

Solution · exploring Aboot 
ztp1# show boot
ztp1# more flash:boot-config
ztp1# reload now
! console: press Ctrl-C at "Press Control-C now to enter Aboot shell"
Aboot# help
Aboot# pwd
Aboot# ls -al
Aboot# cd /mnt/flash
Aboot# ls -l
Aboot# more boot-config
Aboot# swiinfo vEOS-lab.swi
Aboot# ifconfig -a
Aboot# boot /mnt/flash/vEOS-lab.swi

Use your image name from show boot. pwd shows /, not flash, and /mnt holds the mounted file systems (flash, and drive on SSD models) and their config files (p. 63-64). ifconfig -a lists only the loopback and the management port, because front-panel ports need the ASIC driver that EOS loads later (p. 64). boot with a path starts that image directly without changing boot-config, which is the quickest way out of Aboot when boot-config is wrong.

Solution · Aboot password (boot secret) 
ztp1(config)# boot secret LabAboot1
ztp1# show boot
! Aboot password (encrypted): $6$... (SHA-512 hash)
ztp1# reload now
! Ctrl-C at the prompt
Aboot password:
! three wrong entries end with an offer to run fullrecover; just press Enter to reboot
! enter the right password to get Aboot#
Aboot# boot /mnt/flash/vEOS-lab.swi
ztp1(config)# no boot secret
ztp1# show boot

The password is stored as a hash in PASSWORD= in boot-config, so set and remove it only through boot secret (p. 65). Older releases used MD5; current ones use SHA-512 (p. 65). After three wrong passwords, Aboot offers fullrecover, and that is the only way in if both the Aboot and EOS passwords are lost (p. 65). Pressing Enter just reboots. Removing the PASSWORD= line from boot-config in Bash also clears it (p. 65). Real world: set a boot secret on switches in shared or unattended spaces, keep it in your password vault with the EOS credentials, and remember it does nothing against someone who is allowed to run fullrecover.

Solution · password recovery, two ways 
! way 1: comment out the users (the book's method)
Aboot# cd /mnt/flash
Aboot# cp startup-config startup-config.orig
Aboot# vi startup-config
! put "!" in front of every line that starts with "username" and save (:wq)
!   or, without vi: sed -i 's/^username/!username/' startup-config
Aboot# reboot
! log in as admin with no password
ztp1# configure
ztp1(config)# username admin privilege 15 role network-admin secret NewPass123
ztp1(config)# end
ztp1# write memory

! way 2: rename startup-config
Aboot# cd /mnt/flash
Aboot# mv startup-config startup-config.bak
Aboot# reboot
! EOS boots with no startup-config, so ZTP starts
localhost# zerotouch cancel
! reloads; log in as admin, no password
localhost# copy flash:startup-config.bak running-config
ztp1(config)# username admin privilege 15 role network-admin secret NewPass123
ztp1# write memory
ztp1# delete flash:startup-config.bak

Both work the same way on every Arista switch, because the procedure is just "find startup-config in /mnt/flash and edit it" (p. 67). With no username lines left, admin logs in on the console without a password (p. 67). Renaming the file leaves the switch with no startup-config, so it boots into ZTP (p. 67); cancel ZTP, then merge the old file back with copy, not configure replace, so you can fix the user before anything else (see the merge vs replace traps). If your release uses aaa root or other local accounts, check those lines too. Keep a copy of the original file until you have logged in with the new password.

Solution · booting an image over HTTP 
ztp1# bash scp /mnt/flash/vEOS-lab.swi admin@172.100.100.5:/tmp/
$ sudo mkdir -p /var/www/html/images && sudo mv /tmp/vEOS-lab.swi /var/www/html/images/

ztp1# bash sudo cp /mnt/flash/boot-config /mnt/flash/boot-config.flash
ztp1# bash sudo vi /mnt/flash/boot-config
SWI=http://172.100.100.5/images/vEOS-lab.swi
NETDEV=ma1
NETIP=172.100.100.52
NETMASK=255.255.255.0
NETGW=172.100.100.1
ztp1# reload now
! do NOT press Ctrl-C: let Aboot bring up ma1 and fetch the image
ztp1# show version
ztp1# show boot
$ tail /var/log/nginx/access.log

ztp1# bash sudo cp /mnt/flash/boot-config.flash /mnt/flash/boot-config
ztp1# show boot

The NET lines can only be written from Bash or Aboot (p. 66). The mask must be dotted decimal, and only IPv4 is supported (p. 66); use NETAUTO=dhcp instead of the static lines if your ZTP DHCP server has a reservation for this MAC. The book's lesson from about 90 failed tries: if you press Ctrl-C at the Aboot prompt, Aboot stops before it applies the NET settings, so ma1 has no address; let it boot on its own (p. 66). For TFTP, install tftpd-hpa on the Ubuntu server, put the image in /srv/tftp, and use SWI=tftp://172.100.100.5/vEOS-lab.swi. On vEOS the management device in Aboot may not be called ma1; check with ifconfig -a in task 1. Network boot is handy for labs and for a switch with failing flash, but every reload now depends on the server, so do not leave it that way.

Solution · manual fetch with wget 
Aboot# cd /mnt/flash
Aboot# ifconfig ma1 172.100.100.52 netmask 255.255.255.0
Aboot# ifconfig ma1
Aboot# wget http://172.100.100.5/images/vEOS-lab.swi -O test.swi
Aboot# swiinfo test.swi
Aboot# rm test.swi
Aboot# boot /mnt/flash/vEOS-lab.swi

Always give the netmask: without it, ifconfig uses the old classful mask (a /16 for 172.100.x.x, a /8 for 10.x.x.x), which quietly breaks routing to anything off-link (p. 66). udhcpc -i ma1 gets an address by DHCP instead. Check free space with df -h /mnt/flash before downloading an image. After the download, set SWI= in boot-config to the new file with vi, or boot it once with boot and fix boot-config from EOS with boot system flash:FILE (p. 66).

Solution · fullrecover (lab only, last resort) 
Aboot# fullrecover
! prompts: type "yes" and press Enter to erase /mnt/flash, or just Enter to cancel

fullrecover erases everything on /mnt/flash and writes back the factory recovery data: all .swi files, boot-config, startup-config, zerotouch-config, schedule logs and some console logs, and everything under /mnt/flash/persist (p. 65). The switch comes back on the factory EOS release with no config, which on hardware can be several releases older, and the boot can include FPGA updates with several reboots taking 10 minutes or more on some models (p. 65). It is the way to rescue a switch with lost Aboot and EOS passwords without sending it back (p. 65), and the book's author used it once on a switch that could no longer cancel or disable ZTP or take any config (p. 67). On vEOS there may be no recovery data to restore, so the node may simply stop at Aboot with an empty flash; wipe it in EVE-NG to start over. Never run it on a switch you cannot rebuild, and save configs off-box first.

Exam tip. The procedure questions are short: Ctrl-C at the Aboot prompt on the console, cd /mnt/flash, then either fix boot-config (SWI=) or move or edit startup-config. Know that boot system writes SWI=, boot secret writes the Aboot password, show boot reads it all, and fullrecover wipes flash.
Real world. Every one of these needs a console. Make sure every rack has a console server with working cabling and that its access is documented before you need it; Aboot work in a data center is otherwise a 2 a.m. drive. Before an upgrade, keep the old image on flash and check show boot points at the new one, and after a flash clean-up, run show boot again. If you change CONSOLESPEED, update the console server port too, or the next person sees only garbage.

Break / fix

Inject (on ztp1): a colleague frees flash space by renaming the image, then reloads: bash sudo mv /mnt/flash/vEOS-lab.swi /mnt/flash/old-image.bak, reload now. (Use your image name.)

Symptom: the node never reaches EOS; the console stops at an Aboot# prompt after an error about the image.

Root cause and fix

boot-config still says SWI=flash:vEOS-lab.swi, which no longer exists, so Aboot halts (p. 63). Fix it from the prompt:

Aboot# cd /mnt/flash
Aboot# more boot-config
Aboot# ls -l
Aboot# swiinfo old-image.bak
Aboot# mv old-image.bak vEOS-lab.swi
Aboot# reboot
! or: boot /mnt/flash/old-image.bak, then fix it in EOS with "boot system flash:FILE"
ztp1# show boot

Lesson: run show boot before deleting or renaming any .swi, and never remove the running image or the previous one (see the flash clean-up in objective 1.3).

Containers and VMs: EOS as a guest, guests on EOS (optional)

This section follows AW Ch 31 · Containers (pp. 175-180). It is real-world material and not an exam objective, but you already depend on it: every switch in this lab is a container.

Key ideas from the chapter. A container shares the host's kernel instead of emulating hardware like a VM, so it is small and starts almost instantly (p. 175). EOS and containers meet in two ways. EOS in a container (cEOS) lets EOS run on hardware Arista did not build, and lets large operators manage switch software with the same orchestration tools they use for every other container (p. 175). Containers in EOS: EOS includes Docker, managed from the CLI by container-manager so it fits normal switch workflows and CloudVision (pp. 177-178). An image is the package; a container is a running copy of it, and each docker run makes a new container with a random name (p. 179). A container stops as soon as its main command ends, so a container that only runs /bin/bash with no terminal exits at once (p. 178).

Where containers fit: use cases

Use caseHow it worksWatch for
Lab and trainingcEOS in containerlab, as in this guide. A full fabric runs on a laptop in minutes.No ASIC: no LANZ, no TAP aggregation, no hardware counters, MTU limits on veth links.
Pre-change testing ("digital twin")A CI pipeline builds the AVD configs, deploys them to a cEOS copy of the fabric, runs ANTA, and only then lets the change reach CloudVision. Arista's hosted ACT labs do the same with vEOS and cEOS.Keep the twin's topology and EOS release in step with production, or the test proves nothing.
White-box and large operatorscEOS on third-party merchant-silicon switches, deployed and upgraded by the same orchestrator that runs the rest of their containers (p. 175).Needs a supported hardware platform and its cEOS build, not cEOS-lab.
Cloud and virtual routingThe same EOS code runs as a VM or container in public clouds (CloudEOS) and in the lab (vEOS, cEOS), so one config style and one automation path cover all of them.Licensing and throughput depend on the cloud instance, not on a switch ASIC.
Small apps on the switchcontainer-manager runs a container on the switch: a monitoring or flow collector at a site with no server, an iperf3 endpoint for testing, a custom health checker, a small DNS or syslog relay.Switch CPU, RAM and flash are small and shared with EOS. You own patching the image. For tight integration with EOS, an EOS SDK agent or extension is usually better.
Automation toolingThe tools around the network ship as containers: ANTA, AVD build runners, gNMI collectors, Grafana and Prometheus stacks for streaming telemetry.These run on servers, not on the switches.

  1. On your lab host (not inside the lab), create two cEOS containers with plain docker create using the options from What containerlab does for you. Connect both to two Docker networks and start them. Give Ethernet1 on each an IP address in the same subnet, ping across, then check show lldp neighbors. Compare with any link in the containerlab fabric.

  2. On leaf1, try show container-manager ? and write down what cEOS supports.

  3. Read the hardware solution below and explain three things: why the first container showed as exited, what happens to containers and images when the switch reloads, and what a container backup costs.

Solution · two cEOS containers by hand 
$ for n in 1 2; do
    docker create --name=ceos$n --privileged \
      -e CEOS=1 -e container=docker -e EOS_PLATFORM=ceoslab \
      -e SKIP_ZEROTOUCH_BARRIER_IN_SYSDBINIT=1 -e ETBA=1 -e INTFTYPE=eth \
      -it ceos:4.34.2F /sbin/init
  done
$ docker network create net-1 && docker network create net-2
$ for n in 1 2; do docker network connect net-1 ceos$n; docker network connect net-2 ceos$n; done
$ docker start ceos1 ceos2
! wait a minute or two for EOS to boot
$ docker exec -it ceos1 Cli
localhost>enable
localhost#configure
localhost(config)#hostname ceos1
ceos1(config)#interface Ethernet1
ceos1(config-if-Et1)#no switchport
ceos1(config-if-Et1)#ip address 10.0.0.1/24
! same on ceos2 with 10.0.0.2/24, then:
ceos2#ping 10.0.0.1
ceos2#show lldp neighbors

$ docker rm -f ceos1 ceos2 && docker network rm net-1 net-2

The ping works but the LLDP table stays empty, because the Linux bridge behind each Docker network does not forward LLDP frames (p. 177). Which Docker network became Ethernet1 is also up to Docker, so check which interface your ping really used. If the backslash-continued command fails with "command not found" for -e, you have a space after a backslash (p. 176). In the containerlab fabric, show lldp neighbors on leaf1 lists both spines and leaf2, because each link is a veth pair.

Solution · container-manager (hardware and vEOS; reference only) 
switch# container-manager pull ubuntu
switch(config)# container-manager
switch(config-container-mgr)# container Ubuntu
switch(config-container-mgr-container-Ubuntu)# image ubuntu
switch(config-container-mgr-container-Ubuntu)# command /bin/sleep 300
switch(config-container-mgr-container-Ubuntu)# on-boot
switch(config-container-mgr-container-Ubuntu)# end
switch# container-manager start Ubuntu
switch# show container-manager info
switch# show container-manager containers brief
switch# bash sudo docker run -it ubuntu
switch# container-manager backup container Ubuntu
switch# show container-manager backup
switch# container-manager backup remove Ubuntu

cEOS does not run Docker inside itself, so expect most of these commands to be missing or to fail in this lab. Check show container-manager ? on your platform; availability and syntax depend on the hardware and EOS release. The first container exited because its command, /bin/bash with no terminal, finished straight away; a long-running command such as sleep or a real service keeps it up (pp. 178-179). Everything outside flash: and drive: is lost on reload, containers included. With on-boot, EOS rebuilds the container at boot by pulling the image again, which needs internet access (p. 179). container-manager backup saves the image to /mnt/flash/.containermgr/ so boot uses the local copy, but a single small Ubuntu image is about 90 MB of flash (pp. 179-180). sudo docker run from bash works too, but each run creates another unmanaged container; managing containers through container-manager keeps them in the config where CloudVision can see them (p. 178).

vEOS, and VMs on the switch

Key ideas from AW Ch 32 · vEOS. vEOS is EOS compiled as a VM, not a separate product, which is why a config tested on vEOS behaves the same on a switch. Only ASIC-dependent features are missing (p. 181). The book describes two versions: vEOS-lab, free for labs, and vEOS-router, a licensed build for AWS, Azure and GCP with faster forwarding plus VPN and IPsec (p. 181). Arista now sells the cloud version as CloudEOS. EOS can also run VMs itself with the virtual-machine commands (p. 184). The book does this for fun and is clear about the risk: a VM takes RAM and flash from the switch, and running out of memory is one of the worst things that can happen to a switch (pp. 184-187).
Reference · a VM on an EOS switch (hardware only) 
switch(config)# vlan 101
switch(config)# interface Vlan101
switch(config)# virtual-machine LABVM
switch(config-vm-LABVM)# disk-image flash:vEOS-lab-copy1.vmdk
switch(config-vm-LABVM)# memory-size 2047
switch(config-vm-LABVM)# virtual-nic 1 Management1
switch(config-vm-LABVM)# virtual-nic 2 Vlan101
switch(config-vm-LABVM)# enable
switch# show virtual-machine
switch# show virtual-machine detail
switch# virtual-machine LABVM console
! stop it and free the RAM
switch(config)# virtual-machine LABVM
switch(config-vm-LABVM)# no enable

What the chapter found, in short (pp. 184-187):

  • The default VM memory is tiny (128 MB) and the book's release capped it at 2047 MB. Two such VMs used 4 GB, which is all the RAM many switches have.
  • Each VM needs its own copy of the disk image, about 700 MB of flash each. Use a switch with an SSD if you do this at all.
  • A virtual-nic can attach only to Management1 or to an SVI, and the SVI must exist first.
  • Inside the vEOS guest, the IP address also had to be set on the matching vmnicet bridge interface in bash before traffic flowed.
  • The guests could reach each other and other devices, but not the host switch's own addresses.
  • An enabled VM in a saved config starts again at boot. Ctrl-C drops you out of the VM console, so leave configuration mode another way. A vEOS guest cannot run VMs of its own.

cEOS and vEOS do not support virtual-machine, so this is reading only. In practice, run VMs on servers and keep switch resources for the switch.

Real world. The most valuable container use case for most teams is the digital twin: test every change on a cEOS copy of the fabric before it reaches production. This lab already has every piece: the containerlab topology, the AVD build and the ANTA catalogs. Running containers on production switches is rarer. Do it only when there is truly no server nearby, cap the resources, and treat the image like any other software you must patch.

Checkpoint

  • I can stage a change in a session, show its diff from inside and outside the session, and commit it with a timer that I then confirm.
  • I can save a named checkpoint and return to it with configure replace checkpoint:NAME, and I know that replace removes anything not in the file.
  • I can use ranges with $, {start,end,step,pad} expansion, show active, | section, | nz, Linux filters and aliases, and I know why two stacked | include filters mislead.
  • I know that only copy merges, that checkpoint restore and session commit are replaces, and that a session commit removes direct edits made after the session started. I can recover a wiped switch from the console with configure replace flash:startup-config.
  • I can explain how agents use SysDB, show that killing the Stp agent causes no reconvergence while turning STP off and on does, and say why configure replace beats a SysDB restart for loading a new config.
  • I can use Bash on the switch: Cli -c into Linux tools, bash one-liners, captures inside a VRF namespace, and I know only flash, drive, USB and /persist survive a reload.
  • On vEOS I can copy an image over HTTP, verify it, set boot system, check show boot, reload into it, and recover from a deleted boot-config.
  • I can explain standard upgrade vs SSU vs MLAG ISSU vs chassis SSO and when each applies.
  • I can drain a leaf with maintenance mode, verify the drain from a spine, and bring it back.
  • I can install an extension persistently and clean it up again.
  • I can find what is filling flash and check CPU and memory per process.
  • I can describe password recovery and image recovery from Aboot, and on vEOS I have fixed a bad SWI=, set and removed boot secret, recovered a password, and booted an image over HTTP with the NET settings.
  • I can fetch an extension from a server, install it, prove it is lost on reload without boot-extensions, persist it, and remove it without leaving a stale entry.
  • No session is pending, no unit is in maintenance, and show running-config diffs is empty on every switch I touched.

Domain 2 · Operations exam

Lab D2 · CloudVision Operations

You onboard the fabric to CloudVision, organize it, and make changes the CloudVision way: configlets and change control with snapshots and rollback, reconcile, image bundles, then the same work in Studios with workspaces and tags. The exam runs in an environment with CloudVision Portal, and a large share of the Operations tasks happen in the CV UI.

Objectives
2.1, 2.2, 2.3
Time
3.5 h (Path A) or 2 h (Path B)
Start state
Fabric in its final AVD state (see Load the AVD end state in Lab D1). Path A also needs a CloudVision instance that can reach your switches, or the ATD / Academy lab with its own topology.
Devices
All six fabric switches; CloudVision (Path A).

Read first: AW Ch 15 · CloudVision · AW Ch 13 · Zero-Touch Provisioning · ATD: CVP Configlet, Change Control, and Rollback · ATD: CVP Advanced Change Control · ATD: Static Configuration Studio Migration · ATD: CV Studios Day 2 Operations · AVD docs

Watch out. Path B teaches the ideas, not the clicks. The Operations exam expects you to move around the CloudVision UI quickly. If you can get any CloudVision time (Arista Academy DCOPS labs, Arista Test Drive, a CVaaS trial or your employer's lab), do Path A there. The ATD guides linked above walk the same flows on ATD's topology; the tasks below are written so they work on any CloudVision lab.
Real world. The CloudVision UI changes between releases (menu names move, Network Provisioning is being replaced by Studios). The concepts do not: a designed config per device, a diff against running, a change control with approvals and rollback. Learn the concept, then find the button.

Using the "EVPN VXLAN 101" CloudVision lab

If you have a time-boxed CloudVision lab such as WWT's Arista EVPN VXLAN 101 lab, use it as Path A for this domain. Going by WWT's public description, it is about 8 hours of work in 10 labs on vEOS switches with CloudVision Portal, Cloudbuilder and IPAM. You build each part of the fabric as configlets and push them through CloudVision: underlay, MLAG, EVPN overlay, L2 VXLAN, external L3, asymmetric and then symmetric IRB, VRFs, a troubleshooting lab, and a CloudVision deep dive (upgrades, backups, maintenance, telemetry). It does not mention Studios. Your copy may differ, so check the lab's own guide.

Don't just follow its steps. At every push, also do the exam task in the right-hand column. That turns 8 hours of building into Operations practice.

EVPN VXLAN 101 labOur objectivesExtra exam practice while you are there

Watch out. This lab covers configlets (Network Provisioning), not Studios. Objective 2.3 (workspaces, tags, the static configuration studio, Software Management) still needs ATD, the Academy lab or Path B. Cloudbuilder and IPAM are add-ons that generate configlets and addresses. Learn what they produce, not their menus.
Exam tip. Access is time-boxed, so plan it. One way to fit 5 days: day 1 Labs 1-2, day 2 Labs 3-5, day 3 Labs 6-8, day 4 Lab 9 against the clock, day 5 Lab 10 and a second pass through change control and rollback. Keep a page of notes with every menu path you used; the exam allows open book, and finding a button fast saves real minutes.

2.1 CloudVision overview, setup and onboarding

Your manager asks whether to buy on-prem CVP or CVaaS, how big a cluster to run, and how to back it up. Then you onboard the six fabric switches.

TopicCVP (on-prem)CVaaS (cloud)
Form factorVM (ESXi, KVM) or appliance (DCA). Single node for labs, three-node cluster (primary, secondary, tertiary) for production redundancy.Arista-hosted, regional tenants, no servers to run.
OnboardingTerminAttr to <cvp-ip>:9910 with a token or key; or register by IP in the device inventory; or ZTP with the CVP bootstrap script.TerminAttr to the regional apiserver.*.arista.io:443 with an onboarding token (token-secure).
UpgradesYou upgrade the cluster (upgrade bundle, cvpi tools), following the supported path.Arista upgrades it.
Backup and restoreScheduled automatic backups plus manual backup with the cvpi CLI; restore onto a cluster of the same version. Copy backups off the cluster.Handled by Arista.
LicensingSubscription per managed device; the license tier sets features (for example telemetry vs full provisioning and Studios). Devices beyond the license count show as unlicensed.

  1. Path A. Generate an onboarding token in CloudVision (Devices, then device registration or onboarding; the exact page name varies by release). Save it on each switch as /tmp/token (or flash:token so it survives reboot).

  2. Path A. Configure the TerminAttr daemon on all six switches so they stream to CloudVision over the default VRF (this fabric's management is in the default VRF). Use a config session with a 5-minute commit timer.

  3. Path A. Confirm the daemon is running on the switch and that each device appears in the CloudVision inventory as streaming. Then, if your CV uses Network Provisioning, move the devices out of the Undefined container (next objective).

  4. Path B. Prove each switch is "onboardable" by automation: run show version over eAPI with curl, and read the hostname over gNMI with gnmic (port 6030).

  5. Answer in your notes: what breaks onboarding if the switch clock is wrong, and why a three-node cluster needs all nodes reachable from each other.

Verify

Outputs are representative and trimmed; your timers, MACs and counters will differ.

leaf1# show daemon TerminAttr
Agent: TerminAttr (running with PID 5123)
Uptime: 0:03:12 (Start time: Sun Oct 25 10:40:01 2026)
Configuration:
Option       Value
------------ ----------------------------------------------------------
exec         /usr/bin/TerminAttr -cvaddr=192.0.2.10:9910 -cvauth=token,/tmp/token -cvvrf=default -taillogs -smashexcludes=ale,flexCounter,hardware,kni,pulse,strata -ingestexclude=/Sysdb/cell/1/agent,/Sysdb/cell/2/agent

Status:
Data         Value
------------ ----------------------------------------------------------
...          (connection status lines vary by TerminAttr version)
host$ curl -sk -u admin:admin https://172.100.100.21/command-api \
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"runCmds","params":{"version":1,"cmds":["show version"],"format":"json"},"id":1}'
{"jsonrpc": "2.0", "id": 1, "result": [{"modelName": "cEOSLab", "version": "4.34.2F-...", "systemMacAddress": "00:1c:73:..", "serialNumber": "...", ...}]}
host$ gnmic -a 172.100.100.21:6030 -u admin -p admin --insecure get --path /system/state/hostname
[
  {
    "source": "172.100.100.21:6030",
    "updates": [ { "Path": "system/state/hostname", "values": { "system/state/hostname": "leaf1" } } ]
  }
]

Path A: all six switches show in the CV inventory with streaming status active and the correct EOS version. Path B: each switch answers eAPI and gNMI.

Solution · TerminAttr (on-prem CVP and CVaaS) 
! put the token on the switch (paste it, or copy it from a server)
leaf1# bash echo '<paste token>' > /tmp/token

leaf1# configure session CV-ONBOARD
leaf1(config-s-CV-ONB)# daemon TerminAttr
leaf1(config-s-CV-ONB-daemon-TerminAttr)# exec /usr/bin/TerminAttr -cvaddr=192.0.2.10:9910 -cvauth=token,/tmp/token -cvvrf=default -taillogs -smashexcludes=ale,flexCounter,hardware,kni,pulse,strata -ingestexclude=/Sysdb/cell/1/agent,/Sysdb/cell/2/agent
leaf1(config-s-CV-ONB-daemon-TerminAttr)# no shutdown
leaf1(config-s-CV-ONB-daemon-TerminAttr)# commit timer 00:05:00
leaf1# configure session CV-ONBOARD commit
leaf1# show daemon TerminAttr
leaf1# bash tail -20 /var/log/agents/TerminAttr-*

! CVaaS variant of the exec line (use the address shown on your tenant's onboarding page):
!   exec /usr/bin/TerminAttr -cvaddr=apiserver.arista.io:443 -cvauth=token-secure,/tmp/cv-onboarding-token -cvvrf=default -taillogs ...
! A cluster: list every node, -cvaddr=10.0.0.1:9910,10.0.0.2:9910,10.0.0.3:9910
! Management in VRF MGMT (common, and in ATD): -cvvrf=MGMT

Replace 192.0.2.10 with your CloudVision address. CloudVision's onboarding page shows a ready-made daemon TerminAttr block for your release; prefer it over typing flags from memory.

Solution · Path B onboarding check for all switches 
host$ for ip in 11 12 21 22 23 24; do
  curl -sk -u admin:admin https://172.100.100.$ip/command-api \
   -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"runCmds","params":{"version":1,"cmds":["show hostname"],"format":"json"},"id":1}' \
   | python3 -c 'import sys,json; print(json.load(sys.stdin)["result"][0]["hostname"])'
done

Clock: TerminAttr uses TLS (and token-secure validates certificates), so a switch clock far off from CloudVision makes the certificate "not yet valid" and the stream never comes up. Configure NTP (the AVD config already has ntp server 0.pool.ntp.org; in a closed lab point it at a reachable server). Cluster: CVP nodes replicate data (HBase, Kafka, ZooKeeper style services) and need each other for quorum; a node that cannot reach the others degrades the cluster.

Exam tip. Onboarding problems in a lab are almost always one of: wrong -cvvrf (management VRF name), wrong -cvaddr/port, missing or expired token file, no route from that VRF to CloudVision, or clock skew. Check show daemon TerminAttr and the TerminAttr log before touching CloudVision.
Real world. Push TerminAttr with the rest of your base config (a CV configlet, an AVD daemon_terminattr setting or ZTP), never by hand. Keep CVP backups off the cluster and test a restore once a year; a backup you never restored is a hope, not a plan.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf2): configure TerminAttr with -cvvrf=MGMT as many copy-pasted examples have.

Symptom: leaf2 never appears in CloudVision (or shows as not streaming). show daemon TerminAttr shows the process running, but the log repeats connection errors.

Root cause and fix

There is no VRF called MGMT on this fabric: Management0 is in the default VRF (show vrf, show ip interface brief). TerminAttr opens its connection in a VRF that does not exist. Change the exec line to -cvvrf=default, then shutdown and no shutdown the daemon so it restarts with the new arguments. Verify in the log and in the CV inventory.

2.2 CloudVision provisioning: containers, configlets, change control, reconcile, image bundles

The fabric is streaming. Now operations wants CloudVision to be the source of truth: devices organized, the running configs captured, a common base configlet for all switches, a templated SNMP location, safe change control with snapshots and rollback, and an image bundle ready for the next upgrade.

Path A: CloudVision Network Provisioning

  1. Build a container hierarchy: L5DC with children Spines and Leaves, and under Leaves the children POD_A and POD_B. Move each device from Undefined into its container. When CloudVision asks how to handle the existing config, keep it (the running config is captured into a device configlet; this is the import or reconcile step). Execute the resulting tasks through a change control.

  2. Create a configlet BASE-ALL with a login banner, the alias nz show interfaces counters | nz, and logging buffered 10000. Validate it against one device, then apply it to the L5DC container. Review the designed vs running diff for one device before saving.

  3. Create a configlet builder SNMP-LOCATION with one form field "location" that generates snmp-server location <value> and snmp-server contact noc@example.net. Run it against POD_A with location DC1-ROW1 and POD_B with DC1-ROW2.

  4. Turn the pending tasks into one change control. Add pre and post snapshots (or health check actions) that collect show ip bgp summary, show mlag and show bgp evpn summary. Run it in series with the MLAG peers in separate stages. Review, approve, execute, then compare the pre and post snapshots.

  5. Roll back the change control and confirm the banner and alias are gone from the devices and the configlets are no longer applied.

  6. Simulate drift: on leaf3 CLI add ip name-server vrf default 9.9.9.9. In CloudVision find leaf3 out of compliance and resolve it twice: once by reconcile (keep the CLI change as a reconcile configlet), once by pushing the designed config again (remove it).

  7. Create an image bundle containing an EOS image (and optionally the TerminAttr SWIX), attach it to POD_B, and look at the tasks it generates. Do not execute them in the lab unless you are on hardware or vEOS with the matching image.

Solution · Path A UI walk-through (generic)
  1. Provisioning, then Network Provisioning. Right-click the top container, add container L5DC, then its children. Right-click a device in Undefined, choose Move, pick the target container. Choose to keep (import) the running configuration when prompted. Click Save. Pending tasks show a "T" on each device.
  2. Provisioning, then Configlets, then + and Configlets. Paste the lines, name it, use the validate option against a device, Save. In Network Provisioning right-click L5DC, Manage, Configlet, select BASE-ALL, Update, then Save on the topology page.
  3. Provisioning, Configlets, + and Configlet Builder. Add a form text field with id location, add the script below, Save. Apply it to a container via Manage, Configlet: CV asks for the form value and generates a configlet per device.
  4. Provisioning, Tasks. Select the assignable tasks, Create Change Control. Set series or parallel and stages; add snapshot or health check actions before and after (older releases: snapshot templates under the Snapshots page; newer releases: change control actions). Review and Approve shows designed vs running per device. Approve, then Execute Change Control.
  5. Open the executed change control and click Rollback, select the devices, Create Rollback Change Control, Review and Approve, Execute.
  6. Devices or Network Provisioning shows leaf3 out of compliance (config mismatch). Right-click leaf3, Manage, Configlet, then Reconcile: CV builds a reconcile configlet with the extra line; Save and run the task (no device change, CV now designs it). For the other outcome, delete the reconcile configlet and push the task: the designed config (without the line) is applied and the line is removed from leaf3.
  7. Provisioning, Image Repository, add an image bundle, upload the image and SWIX. In Network Provisioning right-click POD_B, Manage, Image Bundle, select it, Save. Tasks are created for leaf3 and leaf4.
# Configlet builder script (Python). Library names follow the CVP builder docs;
# check the builder editor's built-in help on your release.
from cvplibrary import Form

location = Form.getFieldById('location').getValue()
print('snmp-server location %s' % location)
print('snmp-server contact noc@example.net')

Follow ATD: CVP Configlet, Change Control, and Rollback for screenshots of the same flow.

Path B: the same intents with EOS and AVD

  1. "Designed vs running": from the host, fetch each switch's running config and diff it against the AVD intended config. Explain every difference (you should see the eAPI block you appended in Lab D1 and little else).

  2. "Change control with snapshots": on leaf3 and leaf4, save checkpoint pre-chg2002 and capture show ip bgp summary and show mlag to flash. Apply the BASE-ALL lines (banner, alias, logging buffered) in a session CHG2002, review the diff, commit with a timer, confirm. Capture the same outputs to flash again and compare.

  3. "Rollback": return leaf3 and leaf4 to pre-chg2002 with configure replace.

  4. "Reconcile": add the out-of-band name-server line on leaf3, detect it with the running vs intended diff, then remove it by replacing with the intended config inside a session (the way CV pushes the designed config).

  5. Read the AVD cv_deploy concept (AVD docs, CloudVision deploy role): AVD sends each device's intended config to CloudVision as a static configlet in a Studios workspace and can create and run the change control. That is how most production AVD shops combine both tools.

Solution · Path B 
host$ for n in leaf3:23 leaf4:24; do h=${n%%:*}; ip=${n##*:}
  ssh admin@172.100.100.$ip "show running-config" > /tmp/l5dc/$h.running
  diff -u labs/avd/intended/configs/$h.cfg /tmp/l5dc/$h.running
done

leaf3# configure checkpoint save pre-chg2002
leaf3# show ip bgp summary | redirect flash:pre-chg2002-bgp.txt
leaf3# show mlag | redirect flash:pre-chg2002-mlag.txt
leaf3# configure session CHG2002
leaf3(config-s-CHG2002)# banner login
Enter TEXT message.  Type 'EOF' on its own line to end.
Authorized access only. L5DC lab.
EOF
leaf3(config-s-CHG2002)# alias nz show interfaces counters | nz
leaf3(config-s-CHG2002)# logging buffered 10000
leaf3(config-s-CHG2002)# show session-config diffs
leaf3(config-s-CHG2002)# commit timer 00:05:00
leaf3# configure session CHG2002 commit
leaf3# show ip bgp summary | redirect flash:post-chg2002-bgp.txt
leaf3# bash diff /mnt/flash/pre-chg2002-bgp.txt /mnt/flash/post-chg2002-bgp.txt

leaf3# configure replace checkpoint:pre-chg2002

leaf3(config)# ip name-server vrf default 9.9.9.9
! detect with the host diff above, then:
leaf3# configure session RECONCILE
leaf3(config-s-RECONC)# rollback clean-config
leaf3(config-s-RECONC)# copy flash:avd.cfg session-config
leaf3(config-s-RECONC)# show session-config diffs
leaf3(config-s-RECONC)# commit

If | redirect is not accepted on your release, use | append flash:file or run the command through bash FastCli -p 15 -c "show ip bgp summary" > /mnt/flash/file.

Verify

leaf3# show running-config | include ^banner|^alias|logging buffered
alias nz show interfaces counters | nz
logging buffered 10000
banner login
leaf3# show running-config | include name-server
ip name-server vrf default 1.1.1.1

After the change the three lines exist on every target device. After rollback they are gone. After reconcile-by-push only the AVD name-server 1.1.1.1 remains. In Path A, every device is in compliance and no tasks are left pending.

TermMeaning
ContainerA folder in Network Provisioning. Configlets and image bundles applied to a container are inherited by everything below it.
ConfigletA block of EOS config. The designed config of a device is all inherited configlets plus its own, merged in order.
Configlet builderPython that generates a configlet, optionally from a form or from device facts.
Designed vs runningWhat CV would push vs what the device has. A mismatch marks the device out of compliance.
TaskA pending push for one device, created when its designed config or image changes.
Change controlA set of tasks with ordering (series, parallel, stages), actions (snapshots, health checks, BGP maintenance), approval and execution. Can be rolled back.
ReconcileMake CV's designed config match the device by generating a reconcile configlet from the device's extra lines.
Image bundleAn EOS image plus optional extensions, applied to containers or devices.
ZTP / ZTRZero Touch Provisioning onboards a new device from factory state. Zero Touch Replacement swaps a failed device for a new one that inherits the old device's configlets and image.
Exam tip. Configlet order and inheritance cause most "my change did not appear" problems: a device-level configlet later in the order can override a container configlet line. When CV shows a diff you do not expect, open the device's designed config view and find which configlet contributes the line. And always finish by executing the change control: a saved configlet with pending tasks has not changed any device.
Real world. Reconcile is a decision, not a cleanup button. Reconciling accepts whatever someone typed on the CLI as the new design. In a disciplined shop you push the designed config (removing the drift) unless the change was an approved emergency fix, which then gets added to the proper configlet or AVD input, not left in a reconcile configlet forever.

Break / fix

Inject (Path A): in BASE-ALL add the line interface Ethernet1 followed by mtu 9214, apply to L5DC and run the change control. (Path B: apply the same in a session on spine1 and leaf1 only.)

Symptom: the change control succeeds and BGP stays up (TCP adjusts its segment size), so nothing looks wrong at first. But show interfaces Ethernet1 | include MTU now differs from the far end on several links (spine1 Et1 faces leaf1 Et1, but leaf2 Et1 faces spine1 Et2, which still has 1500). ANTA or AVD validation flags the MTU mismatch, an OSPF underlay would refuse to form adjacencies, and large frames are dropped in one direction.

Root cause and fix

A container-wide configlet applied interface config to every device, but "Ethernet1" means a different link on spines (to leaf1) than on leaves (uplink to spine1). Container configlets should hold device-independent config only; interface config belongs in device configlets, builders or Studios. Fix: roll back the change control (or configure replace checkpoint:pre-...), remove the interface lines from BASE-ALL, and if jumbo MTU is really wanted, set it on both ends of every P2P link through the design (AVD p2p_uplinks_mtu).

CloudVision drills for the EVPN VXLAN 101 lab

This section follows AW Ch 15 · CloudVision (pp. 68-74). The book's screenshots are from an older CloudVision Portal, so menus have moved, but the model it explains is the one the EVPN VXLAN 101 lab uses: Network Provisioning with containers, configlets, tasks, change control, snapshots, image bundles and rollback. Do these drills in that lab (how it maps to our objectives); each one fits between the lab's own steps and takes 10 to 20 minutes.

Key ideas from the chapter. CloudVision is a family: CloudVision eXchange (CVX, a vEOS used as a controller, for example for VXLAN flood lists) and CloudVision Portal (CVP), which is what people usually mean (p. 68). CVP is a large VM or cluster, not something for a laptop, and it works best with AAA set up, because the user who logs in to the portal is the user who changes the switches (p. 68). Switches need eAPI enabled to be added by hand; ZTP-onboarded switches get it automatically (p. 68). CVP does not SSH in and type commands: it pushes changes through eAPI into a configuration session, so every change is a session replace (p. 69, AW Ch 9). Telemetry comes from TerminAttr streaming SysDB state in real time, not from SNMP polling (p. 69). Network Provisioning is a configuration hierarchy, not a network map: devices sit in containers, containers inside containers, and a configlet attached to a container applies to every device below it (pp. 70-71). New ZTP devices land in the Undefined container and get only what the root container carries (pp. 70-71). Applying a configlet does not change anything yet: it creates a task (a yellow T), and tasks are run individually or grouped in a change control, which takes pre and post snapshots (pp. 71-72). A snapshot is the output of chosen commands at one point in time, for a device or a container; you can define custom ones, for example show ip bgp (p. 72). An "image" in Image Management is really an image bundle: an EOS .swi plus extensions such as TerminAttr (p. 72). Rollback can restore configuration, image or both, for one device (device rollback) or a whole container (network rollback), picked from a timeline (pp. 72-73). Network Provisioning is optional: you can use CVP only for telemetry and manage configs with Ansible or by hand (p. 71).
Book conceptWhere to look in a current release
Network Provisioning, containers, configlets, configlet builder (pp. 70-71)Provisioning, then Network Provisioning and Configlets. Newer releases add Studios and the Static Configuration Studio (objective 2.3).
Tasks and change control (pp. 71-72)Provisioning, then Tasks and Change Control. Newer releases group them into a change control with stages, approvals and actions.
Snapshots (p. 72)Snapshot templates, or the pre and post check actions inside a change control.
Image Management (p. 72)Image bundles under Provisioning, or the Software Management studio.
Device and network rollback (pp. 72-73)Right-click a device or container, then Manage and Rollback, or roll back an executed change control.
Events, Topology, Cloud Tracer (pp. 73-74)Events, Network (topology), and Cloud Tracer under Devices or Dashboards, depending on release (objective 3.2).

  1. Hierarchy. Before the lab's first push, sketch the container tree it uses and list which configlets sit at which level. For one leaf, write down every configlet it inherits and from where. Then open the designed config for that leaf and match each block to a configlet.

  2. Configlet and task. Create a small configlet (a login banner and the alias senz show interfaces counters errors | nz) and attach it to one container. Count the pending tasks it creates, open one, and read the designed vs running diff. Do not run them yet.

  3. Custom snapshot. Create a snapshot template that collects show ip bgp summary, show bgp evpn summary and show mlag. Use it as the pre and post snapshot of a change control that runs the tasks from task 2, then compare the two snapshots.

  4. Rollback, both kinds. Roll back one device to before the change (device rollback) and check the banner is gone from it only. Then roll back the whole container (network rollback) and check the rest. Look at what the rollback page offers: config, image, or both.

  5. Out-of-band change. On one leaf's CLI, add a description to an unused interface. Find the device as out of compliance, read the diff, and then run any change control that touches that device without reconciling. What happens to your description, and why?

  6. Image bundle. Open an image bundle in the lab and list what is inside it. Note which containers and devices it is applied to.

  7. Telemetry. While a change control runs, watch Events and the device's interface and BGP views. Find the event for the BGP or MLAG change, and use the timeline to look at the minutes before it.

Solution · notes for each drill
  1. Devices inherit every configlet from each container above them, plus any configlet attached to the device itself (pp. 70-71). The designed config is that stack in order. If two configlets set the same line, the one applied later in the order usually wins in the designed config, which is a common source of surprise; always read the designed config rather than guessing.
  2. One task per device under the container, each shown with a T until it runs (p. 71). The diff shows only the banner and alias, because CVP compares the full designed config with the running config.
  3. Snapshot templates take any show command, and a change control can take a snapshot of each device before and after (p. 72). Comparing them is the CloudVision version of "run the show command, make the change, run it again" (p. 72).
  4. Device rollback affects one node; network rollback, offered on a container, rolls back every device below it to the same point in time (pp. 72-73). Both create tasks and run through a change control, as a session replace on each switch.
  5. The next push replaces the whole running config with the designed one, so the description disappears (p. 69, AW Ch 9). That is the session snapshot trap from the configuration management traps at the scale of a whole network. Either reconcile it into a configlet first, or put it in a configlet yourself.
  6. Expect an EOS .swi and at least a TerminAttr .swix (p. 72). Pushing the bundle installs both, and the extension is made persistent for you (compare the extension system).
  7. Events are graded Info, Warning, Error and Critical, and each event type opens the data that matters for it, for example interface counters for output errors or per-core CPU for a high CPU event (p. 73). This is streamed state, so the graphs update as you watch (p. 73).
Exam tip. Know the life cycle in order: attach a configlet (or image bundle), which creates tasks; tasks run through a change control with snapshots; anything can be rolled back per device or per container. And know that a change from CloudVision is a configuration session replace through eAPI, which is why the designed config always wins over CLI changes.
Real world. Put configlets at the highest level that is true for every device below it (AAA, NTP, logging, aliases at the top; role-specific blocks at spine and leaf containers; only the truly unique lines on the device). Keep the tree shallow enough that a new engineer can tell where a line comes from. Even if your configs come from AVD or Ansible, keep TerminAttr streaming: the telemetry, events and compliance views are useful on their own (p. 71).

Break / fix

Inject (in the CloudVision lab): attach a configlet containing no lldp run to the root container, but do not run the tasks.

Symptom: every device in the lab shows a pending task, including devices you never meant to touch, and the topology view would lose its links if the tasks ran.

Root cause and fix

A configlet on the root container applies to every device below it, including any new device that ZTP drops into Undefined (pp. 70-71). Remove the configlet from the root container and cancel the pending tasks before anyone runs a change control. Always check the task count after you attach a configlet: it should match the number of devices you meant to change.

Zero Touch Provisioning: build it on EVE-NG

This section follows AW Ch 13 · Zero-Touch Provisioning (pp. 58-61). ZTP is how CloudVision onboards every new switch and replaces failed ones (ZTR), so the D2 questions about onboarding make much more sense once you have built the pieces yourself. You need a vEOS node in EVE-NG and your Ubuntu automation server; cEOS in containerlab always gets a startup-config, so it never enters ZTP.

Key ideas from the chapter. A fixed-configuration switch with no startup-config, or a zero-byte one, boots into ZTP (p. 58). It puts every Ethernet and management port into routed mode (no switchport) and sends DHCP on every connected port, retrying forever (p. 58). Anything you configure meanwhile is thrown away on the next retry, and current EOS refuses to save it ("Cannot copy to startup-config when ZeroTouch is enabled", p. 58). You need three things: a DHCP server, a web server for the file, and a connected port (p. 58). The DHCP answer gives an address and a bootfile URL (option 67); the switch downloads that file (p. 59). If the file's first line is a shebang (#! and an interpreter path), it runs as a script; otherwise it is saved as startup-config. Either way, the switch reloads afterwards (pp. 60-61). zerotouch cancel skips ZTP for one boot only: it writes a one-time flag to boot-config, reloads at once without asking, and the ZTP banner stays in the config (pp. 59-60). zerotouch disable stops it for good by writing DISABLE=True to flash:zerotouch-config, and also reloads at once (p. 59). Once disabled, zerotouch enable is refused at the CLI; delete flash:zerotouch-config and boot with an empty startup-config to bring ZTP back (p. 60).
Command or fileEffectSurvives the next reload
zerotouch cancelSkip ZTP and reload now (p. 59)No. Erase startup-config and reload, and ZTP is back (p. 59).
zerotouch disableTurn ZTP off and reload now (p. 59)Yes, through flash:zerotouch-config (p. 59).
zerotouch script-exec-timeoutHow long a downloaded script may run (p. 59)Yes
delete flash:zerotouch-configRemoves the disable flag (p. 60)ZTP runs at the next boot with no startup-config.
Bootfile without #!Saved as startup-config, then reload (p. 60)It is the new startup-config.
Bootfile with #!/usr/bin/Cli, #!/bin/bash or #!/usr/bin/env python3Run as a script, then reload (pp. 60-61)Only what the script wrote to flash or boot-config. Running-config changes are lost in the reload (p. 61).

Build the ZTP lab

Add one vEOS node called ztp1 to your EVE-NG lab and connect only its first port (Management1) to the management network the automation server is on (172.100.100.5, see Running this lab on EVE-NG). Leave its other ports unconnected so DHCP goes out of one port only. Before you start, check whether anything else on that segment already serves DHCP; if it does, use a separate EVE-NG network that only the Ubuntu server and ztp1 share.

  1. Start ztp1 and watch its console. Write down the ZTP log lines, the ports it tries, and the MAC address of Management1 (show interfaces Management1). Then type hostname TEST, try write memory, and see what happens at the next retry.

  2. On the Ubuntu server, install a DHCP server and nginx. Give ztp1 a reservation for 172.100.100.51 with a bootfile URL, and serve only known clients.

  3. Serve a plain startup-config as the bootfile (no shebang): hostname ztp1, the admin user, a static Management1 address and default route, and eAPI. Let the node ZTP, and confirm it came up with that config and that you can SSH to it.

  4. Wipe ztp1 (write erase, reload now). Change the bootfile to a Cli script that copies the startup-config from the web server and sets the boot image. Watch the script run, and explain why it needs no write memory.

  5. Stretch: replace the script with a Bash script that picks the config file by the switch's system MAC address, so one bootfile URL serves every new switch.

  6. Compare cancel and disable: on ztp1 with an empty startup-config, run zerotouch cancel, then erase and reload again. Then run zerotouch disable, erase and reload. Bring ZTP back.

Solution · what a switch in ZTP looks like 
localhost ZeroTouch: %ZTP-5-INIT: No startup-config found, starting Zero Touch Provisioning
localhost ZeroTouch: %ZTP-5-DHCP_QUERY: Sending DHCP request on [ Management1 ]
localhost ZeroTouch: %ZTP-5-DHCP_QUERY_FAIL: Failed to get a valid DHCP response
localhost ZeroTouch: %ZTP-5-RETRY: Retrying Zero Touch Provisioning from the beginning (attempt 2)

Representative output. The switch lists only connected ports (p. 58). hostname TEST works until the next retry puts everything back to defaults, and write memory is refused while ZTP is on (p. 58). That is the classic first-day surprise the chapter describes; the console banner explains it, but nobody reads it (p. 58).

Solution · DHCP and web server on the Ubuntu automation server 
$ sudo apt install isc-dhcp-server nginx
$ sudo mkdir -p /var/www/html/ztp
$ ip -br addr     # find the NIC that has 172.100.100.5
$ sudo sed -i 's/^INTERFACESv4=.*/INTERFACESv4="ens19"/' /etc/default/isc-dhcp-server
# /etc/dhcp/dhcpd.conf
authoritative;
deny unknown-clients;
subnet 172.100.100.0 netmask 255.255.255.0 {
  option routers 172.100.100.1;
  option domain-name-servers 172.100.100.1;
  host ztp1 {
    hardware ethernet 50:00:00:aa:bb:01;      # Management1 MAC of ztp1
    fixed-address 172.100.100.51;
    option bootfile-name "http://172.100.100.5/ztp/ztp1";   # option 67
  }
}
$ sudo dhcpd -t -cf /etc/dhcp/dhcpd.conf      # syntax check
$ sudo systemctl restart isc-dhcp-server
$ journalctl -u isc-dhcp-server -f

Use your own NIC name and ztp1's real MAC. deny unknown-clients means the server answers only reserved MACs, so it cannot hand addresses to anything else on the segment. The book uses a dynamic pool with option bootfile-name, which is fine on an isolated network (p. 60). ISC dhcpd is end-of-life but still packaged; on newer servers Kea is its replacement. The same reservation in Kea's kea-dhcp4.conf is a reservations entry with "hw-address", "ip-address" and "boot-file-name": "http://172.100.100.5/ztp/ztp1".

Solution · bootfile 1: a plain startup-config 
# /var/www/html/ztp/ztp1   (no shebang, so EOS saves it as startup-config)
hostname ztp1
username admin privilege 15 role network-admin secret LabPass123
!
interface Management1
   ip address 172.100.100.51/24
!
ip route 0.0.0.0/0 172.100.100.1
!
management api http-commands
   no shutdown
!
end
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-DHCP_SUCCESS: DHCP response received on Management1 [ Ip Address: 172.100.100.51/24; Boot File: http://172.100.100.5/ztp/ztp1 ]
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-CONFIG_DOWNLOAD: Attempting to download the startup-config from http://172.100.100.5/ztp/ztp1
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-CONFIG_DOWNLOAD_SUCCESS: Successfully downloaded config from http://172.100.100.5/ztp/ztp1
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-RELOAD: Rebooting the system
$ tail /var/log/nginx/access.log
$ ssh admin@172.100.100.51
ztp1# show running-config | include hostname|Management1
ztp1# show zerotouch

Representative log lines. The switch saved the file as its startup-config and reloaded with it (p. 60). The nginx access log shows the download with status 200, which is the first place to look when a switch says the download failed. Keep the secret out of files you commit; on a real network use a hashed secret sha512 value.

Solution · bootfile 2: a Cli script 
# /var/www/html/ztp/ztp1-startup.cfg   (the config from bootfile 1, renamed)
# /var/www/html/ztp/ztp1   (now a script)
#!/usr/bin/Cli -p2
enable
copy http://172.100.100.5/ztp/ztp1-startup.cfg flash:startup-config
! optional: stage an image and point boot-config at it
! copy http://172.100.100.5/ztp/vEOS64-lab-4.34.2F.swi flash:
! configure
! boot system flash:vEOS64-lab-4.34.2F.swi
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-CONFIG_DOWNLOAD_SUCCESS: Successfully downloaded config script from http://172.100.100.5/ztp/ztp1
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-EXEC_SCRIPT: Executing the downloaded config script
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-EXEC_SCRIPT_SUCCESS: Successfully executed the downloaded config script
localhost ConfigAgent: %ZTP-6-RELOAD: Rebooting the system

Representative output. The script copies the real config straight into flash:startup-config and the boot image into boot-config; both are files on flash, and ZTP reloads at the end, so the switch boots from them (pp. 60-61). A write memory at the end would save the running-config, which is still the empty ZTP config, over the file you just copied. This is the answer to the book's "no wr mem?" puzzle (p. 61). The same idea explains why setting the hostname in the script's running-config does nothing: put the hostname in the copied file. Upgrading in the same pass is how 50 switches arrive on the right config and the right EOS release with nobody on site (p. 61). On vEOS, image staging works but is slow; skip it unless you want to test it.

Solution · stretch: one script for every switch 
#!/bin/bash
# ZTP bootfile: fetch the startup-config named after this switch's system MAC.
SERVER=http://172.100.100.5/ztp
MAC=$(FastCli -p 15 -c "show version" | awk '/System MAC address/ {print $4}' | tr -d '.:')
FastCli -p 15 -c "copy $SERVER/configs/$MAC.cfg flash:startup-config" || exit 1

Save the per-switch files as /var/www/html/ztp/configs/<mac>.cfg, for example 5000aabb0000.cfg (check the format your node prints). Hardware switches usually key on serial number instead (show version | json, serialNumber); vEOS often has no serial. The book mentions customers who archive every switch's config daily and point ZTP at the archive, so a replacement switch rebuilds itself from the failed one's last config, and its author's training-lab script picks the config from which management-switch port the new switch sees in show lldp neighbors (p. 61). Arista's open-source ztpserver packages the same ideas without CloudVision (p. 61).

Solution · cancel vs disable, and bringing ZTP back 
localhost# zerotouch cancel
! reloads at once, no confirmation; boots without ZTP this time only
localhost# bash cat /mnt/flash/boot-config
localhost# write erase
localhost# reload now
! ZTP starts again

localhost# zerotouch disable
! reloads at once; ZTP is now off for good
localhost# more flash:zerotouch-config
localhost# configure
localhost(config)# zerotouch enable
! refused: ZeroTouch can not be enabled interactively
localhost# delete flash:zerotouch-config
localhost# write erase
localhost# reload now
! ZTP starts again

After a cancel, boot-config may briefly hold a one-time flag such as disable=once or disable=nextReload, depending on the release; it is removed at boot, which is why ZTP returns next time (p. 60). zerotouch-config contains DISABLE=True; the book suggests deleting it rather than editing it (p. 60). The book's discovery story is a good habit for any EOS mystery: the feature had to be controlled by a file somewhere, and dir flash: found it (p. 60).

ZTP with CloudVision, and in your EVPN VXLAN 101 lab

CloudVision is a ZTP server: you point option 67 at CloudVision's bootstrap URL (for example http://<cvp>/ztp/bootstrap), and its script installs TerminAttr so the switch registers and starts streaming. CloudVision can run its own DHCP server, or you keep yours and only change the bootfile URL (p. 61). The new switch then appears waiting to be provisioned: in the Undefined container under Network Provisioning, or in the Inventory and Topology Studio on newer releases. From there you move it into a container, attach configlets and an image bundle, and CloudVision pushes the config with the session and rollback clean-config method from the configuration management traps. ZTR is the same flow for a failed switch: the replacement ZTPs, and you tell CloudVision which old device it replaces, so it inherits the old device's configlets and image.

In the EVPN VXLAN 101 lab, look for these pieces: run show zerotouch and show running-config section TerminAttr on a device and note the -cvaddr it streams to; check whether any device sits in the Undefined container or in a "pending onboarding" state; and if the lab lets you, run write erase and reload on one device and watch it come back through ZTP into CloudVision. Write down the order of steps, because it is a likely D2 question.

Solution · a hand-written TerminAttr bootstrap (what CloudVision's script does for you) 
#!/usr/bin/Cli -p2
enable
configure
hostname leaf5
interface Management1
   ip address 172.100.100.25/24
ip route 0.0.0.0/0 172.100.100.1
username admin privilege 15 role network-admin secret LabPass123
management api http-commands
   no shutdown
daemon TerminAttr
   exec /usr/bin/TerminAttr -cvaddr=192.0.2.10:9910 -cvauth=token,/tmp/token -cvvrf=default -taillogs
   no shutdown
end
copy running-config startup-config

This is the one case where a script ends with a save: it builds the config in running-config (including TerminAttr and the onboarding token), so it must save it before ZTP reloads. Compare with bootfile 2, which copies a finished file to startup-config and must not save. Check whether your release still refuses saves during ZTP when the save comes from the ZTP script itself; CloudVision's own bootstrap handles this for you.

Exam tip. Know the chain in order: no (or empty) startup-config, every connected port goes routed, DHCP on all of them, option 67 bootfile URL, download, script or startup-config chosen by the shebang, reload. Know cancel (one boot) vs disable (flash:zerotouch-config, permanent), and that both reload without asking. For CloudVision: bootfile URL to CloudVision, TerminAttr, the device appears for provisioning, then containers or Studios push the config.
Real world. ZTP is only as good as its inputs. Keep the per-switch configs generated (AVD from the same inventory you use for the fabric), keep the DHCP reservations next to them in Git, and use deny unknown-clients or a dedicated provisioning VLAN so a switch never picks up the wrong file. Plan the management network first: ZTP needs DHCP and HTTP reachability on day 0, before any routing is configured.
