If a lab box keeps falling into ZTP, cancel it, configure it and save; don't disable ZTP on production gear you will later want to rebuild remotely.

Break / fix · three ZTP failures

Inject one at a time on ztp1, then write erase and reload now:

  1. Leave flash:zerotouch-config in place from the disable task.
  2. On the server, remove the first line (#!/usr/bin/Cli -p2) from the bootfile 2 script.
  3. On the server, change one character of ztp1's MAC in dhcpd.conf and restart the service.

Symptoms: (1) the switch boots straight to a login prompt with the hostname localhost and no ZTP messages. (2) The download succeeds and the switch reloads, but it comes up as localhost with no management address, and show startup-config contains the script's lines. (3) The console shows ZTP-5-DHCP_QUERY_FAIL and RETRY over and over.

Root cause and fix

(1) ZTP is disabled by DISABLE=True in flash:zerotouch-config; an empty startup-config is not enough on its own (pp. 59-60). Delete the file and reload. (2) Without the shebang, EOS saved the script as a startup-config (p. 60); lines such as enable and copy http://... are not configuration, so they were rejected and nothing useful was applied. Put the shebang back as the very first line. The same failure happens when the file was edited on Windows and has CRLF line endings (the interpreter path then ends in an invisible carriage return) or starts with a blank line; check with head -1 FILE | od -c and fix with dos2unix. (3) The reservation no longer matches, and deny unknown-clients tells the server to ignore the switch. Confirm on the server with journalctl -u isc-dhcp-server (a "no free leases" or "unknown client" line) and sudo tcpdump -ni ens19 port 67 or port 68: you see the DISCOVER and no OFFER. Other causes of the same symptom: the server is listening on the wrong NIC (INTERFACESv4), the EVE-NG link is not connected, or another DHCP server answers first. A 404 in /var/log/nginx/access.log with ZTP-6-CONFIG_DOWNLOAD_FAIL on the switch means DHCP worked and the file path is wrong.

2.3 CloudVision Studios: workspaces, tags, static configuration, software management, Day 2

Your team moves from Network Provisioning to Studios. Every change now goes through a workspace: you edit studios and tags, review the build, submit, and a change control executes it.

Path A: CloudVision Studios

  1. Create a workspace onboard-studios. In the Inventory and Topology Studio accept the six fabric switches into Studios. Review and submit the workspace. (This step registers devices and does not create a change control.)

  2. Create a workspace tags-and-scs. Tag devices: Role:Spine or Role:Leaf, Pod:POD_A or Pod:POD_B, and on leaf3 and leaf4 also Border:yes. Tag interfaces Ethernet5 on all leaves with Server:host.

  3. In the same workspace, open the Static Configuration Studio. Build a hierarchy: All Devices (all six), with children matched by tag Role:Spine and Role:Leaf, and under Leaf a child matched by Border:yes. Add a configlet BASE-ALL (banner, alias, logging buffered 10000) at the top and a configlet BORDER-NOTE (snmp-server location DC1-BORDER) on the Border child.

  4. Review the workspace. Read the proposed config diff for spine1 and leaf3 and confirm only the expected lines change. Submit. Open the generated change control, review and approve, execute. Verify on the CLI.

  5. Management and authentication: in a new workspace configure NTP, DNS, and a local user noc (role network-operator) using the studio your release provides for these (for example a Date and Time studio and an Authentication or AAA studio), or with an SCS configlet if no such studio exists. Submit and execute.

  6. Software management: in a workspace open the Software Management Studio, assign an EOS image to devices matched by Pod:POD_B, review (CloudVision orders MLAG peers so they are not upgraded together) and submit. Look at the change control it builds but do not execute it on cEOS.

  7. Day 2: follow the ATD Day 2 guide pattern on your own CV lab: change a value in a studio (for example a VLAN name or an added VLAN 40 WEB in the EVPN services studio if your fabric is Studios-built), review, submit, execute, then roll back the change control.

Solution · Path A notes
  • Navigation: Provisioning, then Studios, then Create Workspace. Inside a workspace open each studio, make edits, then Review Workspace. The build must pass before Submit Workspace. Submit creates a change control (View Change Control), which you Review and Approve, then Execute.
  • Tags are workspace changes too: they only take effect when the workspace is submitted. Use the Tags page (device and interface tags) or the tag field inside a studio. SCS containers can match devices by tag query, so new devices with the right tags land in the right place automatically.
  • When migrating from Network Provisioning, remove the old configlet assignments after SCS takes over, otherwise the device has two sources for the same lines. The ATD SCS migration guide walks this.
  • Mirroring and other operational settings appear as studios or as SCS configlets depending on release. If the exam asks for something you cannot find as a studio, an SCS configlet on the right container is the fallback that always works.
  • Rollback: open the executed change control and use Rollback, or start a workspace that reverts the studio inputs. Studio inputs and the device config must agree, so prefer reverting inputs for Studio-built config.

Path B: Studios concepts with AVD

  1. "Workspace": copy labs/avd to a scratch folder (do not edit the repo). In group_vars/L5DC_NETWORK_SERVICES.yml add SVI 40 WEB with ip_address_virtual: 10.40.40.1/24 under VRF TENANT-A.

  2. "Review": run python3 build.py in the copy and diff the new intended configs against the old ones. Confirm VLAN 40, Vlan40 SVI, vxlan vlan 40 vni 10040 and the EVPN vlan 40 block appear on all four leaves and nothing changes on the spines.

  3. "Tags": open inventory.yml and group_vars and identify what plays the role of tags (inventory groups such as POD_A, POD_B and the leaf and spine groups; connected_endpoints for interface roles).

  4. "Submit and change control": apply the new leaf1 and leaf2 configs with the session-based replace from 2.2 (with commit timer), verify, and then roll back to the original intended config.

Solution · Path B 
# in a scratch copy of labs/avd
        svis:
          - id: 40
            name: WEB
            enabled: true
            ip_address_virtual: 10.40.40.1/24

host$ python3 build.py
host$ diff -ru /path/to/repo/labs/avd/intended/configs intended/configs
+vlan 40
+   name WEB
+interface Vlan40
+   description WEB
+   no shutdown
+   vrf TENANT-A
+   ip address virtual 10.40.40.1/24
+   vxlan vlan 40 vni 10040
+   vlan 40
+      rd 10.255.0.3:10040
+      route-target both 10040:10040
+      redistribute learned

The server trunk (switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30) does not change because L5DC_CONNECTED_ENDPOINTS.yml lists the VLANs explicitly. That is the AVD version of "tags drive config": until you change the endpoint definition, host1 does not get VLAN 40. Push to leaf1/leaf2 with the same rollback clean-config session pattern after appending the eAPI block, as in Lab D1.

Verify

leaf3# show running-config | include location|^alias
leaf1# show vxlan vni 10040
alias nz show interfaces counters | nz
snmp-server location DC1-BORDER

VNI to VLAN Mapping for Vxlan1
VNI         VLAN       Source       Interface       802.1Q Tag
----------- ---------- ------------ --------------- ----------
10040       40         static       Vxlan1          40

Path A: the workspace shows as submitted, its change control as completed, and every device is compliant. Only border leaves have the border location. Path B: VLAN 40 exists on the leaves while the change is applied and is gone after rollback.

Exam tip. A workspace that is built but not submitted, or a change control that is approved but not executed, means the devices never changed. After every Studios task, check three things: workspace submitted, change control executed successfully, and the device config on the CLI or in the device's configuration view.
Real world. The L3LS-EVPN studio uses the AVD data model under the hood, and many teams run AVD from Git and send the result to CloudVision with cv_deploy, so Studios workspaces and AVD pull requests are two faces of the same idea: a reviewed, versioned change set with a rollback.

Break / fix

Inject (Path A): tag leaf4 Role:Spine by mistake in a workspace and submit. (Path B: in the scratch AVD copy, move leaf4 under the spine group in inventory.yml and build.)

Symptom: Path A: the workspace review shows leaf4 picking up spine configlets in SCS, and the change control would push spine config to a leaf. Path B: build.py fails validation or produces a leaf4 config that looks like a spine with no MLAG or VXLAN.

Root cause and fix

Tags (or inventory groups) decide which config a device receives. Fix the tag (Role:Leaf) in a new workspace before submitting, or do not approve the change control. In Path B restore leaf4 to its pod group. Lesson: always read the proposed diff of every device in the review, not only the one you changed.

Checkpoint

  • I can explain CVP vs CVaaS, single node vs three-node cluster, backups and licensing in two sentences each.
  • I can write a working daemon TerminAttr block for on-prem and CVaaS and troubleshoot one that does not connect.
  • I can build containers, apply configlets and a configlet builder, run a change control with snapshots, and roll it back.
  • I can tell reconcile from pushing the designed config and choose correctly.
  • I know what an image bundle does and how ZTP and ZTR onboard and replace devices.
  • In the EVPN VXLAN 101 lab I have traced a leaf's configlets through the container tree, run a change control with custom snapshots, done a device and a network rollback, and watched an out-of-band change get overwritten.
  • I can build ZTP on EVE-NG with DHCP option 67 and nginx, serve a startup-config and a script bootfile, explain cancel vs disable, and fix a missing shebang, a stale zerotouch-config and a DHCP server that does not answer.
  • I can create a workspace, tag devices, use the Static Configuration Studio, review, submit and execute.
  • Path B: I can diff designed vs running and remediate drift with a session-based replace.

Domain 3 · Operations exam

Lab D3 · Monitoring and Troubleshooting

You make the fabric watch itself (scheduler, event monitor, event handlers), export what it sees (SNMP, sFlow, streaming telemetry to CloudVision), capture traffic (mirroring, tcpdump), and then fix a set of injected faults with a repeatable method. Troubleshooting under time pressure is where most exam points are won or lost.

Objectives
3.1, 3.2, 3.3, 3.4
Time
4 h
Start state
Fabric in its final AVD state (see Load the AVD end state in Lab D1), host1 and host2 pinging each other in VRFs H10 and H20. For 3.2 Path A, TerminAttr streaming to CloudVision (Lab D2, 2.1).
Devices
leaf1 to leaf4, spine1, spine2, host1, host2; the containerlab host for SNMP and sFlow collectors.

Read first: AW Ch 24 · Scheduler · AW Ch 27 · Event Manager · AW Ch 28 · Event Monitor · AW Ch 22 · Email · AW Ch 23 · LANZ · AW Ch 25 · tcpdump and Advanced Mirroring · AW Ch 26 · Tap Aggregation · AW Ch 29 · Troubleshooting · ATD: CVP Telemetry and Dashboards · ATD: CloudVision Custom Events · ANTA docs

3.1 Advanced Event Management: scheduler, event monitor, event handler

The NOC wants evidence, not guesses: periodic BGP snapshots on POD_A, a record of every route and MAC change, and automatic data collection the moment an uplink fails or a BGP session drops.

  1. On leaf1, schedule show ip bgp summary every 5 minutes, keeping 12 files. Find the output files and read one. Review the built-in tech-support schedule and change it to keep 24 files.

  2. Confirm the event monitor is running on leaf1. Flap host1's link to leaf1 (shut and no shut Ethernet5 on leaf1) and use the event monitor to show the resulting MAC, ARP and route events. Query the route history with SQL for the POD_B VTEP 10.255.1.5.

  3. Create event handler UPLINK-WATCH on leaf1: when Ethernet1 changes operational state, wait 5 seconds, write a syslog message naming the interface and new state, and save show ip bgp summary to a timestamped file on flash.

  4. Create event handler BGP-DOWN on leaf1 that triggers on the syslog message for a BGP neighbor leaving Established and saves show ip bgp summary and show logging last 5 minutes to flash.

  5. Create an on-boot handler BOOT-NOTE that logs "leaf1 booted, config check pending" and an on-counters handler INERR that fires when Ethernet1 input errors grow by more than 10 in a poll interval of 60 seconds.

  6. Test: shut Ethernet1 on spine1 (the spine side of leaf1's uplink) for 30 seconds, then bring it back. Show that both handlers fired and that their files exist.

Verify

Outputs are representative and trimmed; your timers, MACs and counters will differ.

leaf1# show schedule summary
Maximum concurrent jobs  1
Prepend host name to logfile: Yes
Name           At Time    Last     Interval   Timeout   Max     Logfile Location              Status
                          Time     (mins)     (mins)    Log
                                                        Files
-------------- ---------- -------- ---------- --------- ------- ----------------------------- -------
bgp-snap       now        11:05    5          30        12      flash:schedule/bgp-snap/      Success
tech-support   now        10:13    60         30        24      flash:schedule/tech-support/  Success
leaf1# show event-handler UPLINK-WATCH
Event-handler UPLINK-WATCH (BUILT-IN = False)
Trigger: on-intf Ethernet1 on operstatus delay 5 seconds
Threshold Time Window: 0 Seconds, Event Count: 1 times
Action: logger -p local4.warning -t UPLINK "$INTF is now $OPERSTATE"; FastCli -p 15 -c "show ip bgp summary" > /mnt/flash/uplink-$(date +%s).txt
Action expected to finish in less than 20 seconds
Last Trigger Detection Time: 1 minute 12 seconds ago
Total Trigger Detections: 2
Last Trigger Activation Time: 1 minute 7 seconds ago
Total Trigger Activations: 2
Last Action Time: 1 minute 7 seconds ago
Total Actions: 2
leaf1# show event-monitor mac
% Displaying entries from the event monitor database
2026-10-25 11:12:40.512|10|00:1c:73:31:00:01|Port-Channel5|learnedDynamicMac|removed|...
2026-10-25 11:12:58.104|10|00:1c:73:31:00:01|Port-Channel5|learnedDynamicMac|added|...
leaf1# show logging last 5 minutes | include UPLINK|BGP
Oct 25 11:20:03 leaf1 Bgp: %BGP-3-NOTIFICATION: sent to neighbor 10.255.255.0 (VRF default AS 65100) 6/6 (Cease/other configuration change) 0 bytes
Oct 25 11:20:03 leaf1 Bgp: %BGP-5-ADJCHANGE: peer 10.255.255.0 (VRF default AS 65100) old state Established event Stop new state Idle
Oct 25 11:20:08 leaf1 UPLINK: Ethernet1 is now linkdown

Both handlers show at least one activation, dir flash: lists uplink-*.txt and bgpdown-*.txt files, and show event-monitor route shows routes via 10.255.255.0 removed and re-added. The exact message wording and the state names in $OPERSTATE vary by release; build your regex from what show logging really prints.

Solution · scheduler and event monitor (leaf1) 
leaf1(config)# schedule bgp-snap interval 5 max-log-files 12 command show ip bgp summary
leaf1(config)# schedule tech-support interval 60 timeout 30 max-log-files 24 command show tech-support
leaf1# show schedule summary
leaf1# show schedule bgp-snap
leaf1# dir flash:schedule/bgp-snap/
leaf1# bash zcat /mnt/flash/schedule/bgp-snap/*.gz | head -30

leaf1# show running-config all | include event-monitor
! off by default since EOS 4.21; enable it if needed
leaf1(config)# event-monitor
leaf1# show event-monitor mac
leaf1# show event-monitor arp
leaf1# show event-monitor route
leaf1# show event-monitor route match-ip 10.255.1.5/32
leaf1# bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db "select * from route where prefix like '10.255.1.5%' order by time desc limit 10;"
! open the database interactively: .tables lists tables, .quit leaves
leaf1# bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db

Check the filters with show event-monitor route ?; field and table names can differ slightly by release, so look at .tables and .schema route before writing SQL. The older show event-monitor sqlite and event-monitor interact commands stopped working after EOS 4.16, which is why the solution uses bash sqlite3 (AW Ch 28, p. 150). To keep history across reboots configure a backup path; the Event Monitor drill covers it.

Solution · event handlers (leaf1) 
leaf1(config)# event-handler UPLINK-WATCH
leaf1(config-handler-UPLINK-WATCH)# trigger on-intf Ethernet1 operstatus
leaf1(config-handler-UPLINK-WATCH)# delay 5
leaf1(config-handler-UPLINK-WATCH)# action bash logger -p local4.warning -t UPLINK "$INTF is now $OPERSTATE"; FastCli -p 15 -c "show ip bgp summary" > /mnt/flash/uplink-$(date +%s).txt
leaf1(config-handler-UPLINK-WATCH)# timeout 20
leaf1(config-handler-UPLINK-WATCH)# exit

leaf1(config)# event-handler BGP-DOWN
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN)# trigger on-logging
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN-trigger-on-logging)# regex BGP-5-ADJCHANGE.*old state Established
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN-trigger-on-logging)# exit
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN)# action bash FastCli -p 15 -c "show ip bgp summary" > /mnt/flash/bgpdown-$(date +%s).txt; FastCli -p 15 -c "show logging last 5 minutes" >> /mnt/flash/bgpdown-$(date +%s).txt
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN)# exit

leaf1(config)# event-handler BOOT-NOTE
leaf1(config-handler-BOOT-NOTE)# trigger on-boot
leaf1(config-handler-BOOT-NOTE)# action bash logger -t BOOT "leaf1 booted, config check pending"
leaf1(config-handler-BOOT-NOTE)# exit

leaf1(config)# event-handler INERR
leaf1(config-handler-INERR)# trigger on-counters
leaf1(config-handler-INERR-trigger-on-counters)# poll interval 60
leaf1(config-handler-INERR-trigger-on-counters)# condition ( Ethernet1.statistics.inErrors - prev(Ethernet1.statistics.inErrors) ) > 10
leaf1(config-handler-INERR-trigger-on-counters)# exit
leaf1(config-handler-INERR)# action bash logger -p local4.err -t INERR "Ethernet1 input errors rising"
leaf1(config-handler-INERR)# exit

spine1(config)# interface Ethernet1
spine1(config-if-Et1)# shutdown
! wait 30 s
spine1(config-if-Et1)# no shutdown

leaf1# show event-handler
leaf1# dir flash: | include uplink|bgpdown
leaf1# more flash:uplink-1792926008.txt

The on-counters condition language and the on-logging submode prompts vary; use condition ? and the EOS manual Event Handler examples to confirm. Other triggers worth knowing: on-startup-config (fires when startup-config changes, good for "back up the config on every write"), on-maintenance (runs actions as a unit enters or leaves maintenance), and vm-tracer vm. Mail actions use the email client from AW Ch 22 (email config and email -s "subject" to@x in bash).

Exam tip. Event handler actions run in bash, not in the EOS CLI. A bare show ip bgp summary in an action fails silently; wrap CLI commands in FastCli -p 15 -c "...". Check show event-handler NAME for activation counts after you test, because a handler that never fires still looks fine in the running config.
Real world. The most useful handlers collect evidence the moment something happens (BGP drop, link flap, high CPU) so you do not have to reproduce an intermittent fault. Keep actions short, rate-limit with threshold and delay, and clean up their files (or write to flash:schedule/-style rotating names) so a flapping link cannot fill flash.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf2): event-handler UPLINK2 with trigger on-intf Ethernet1 operstatus and action bash show ip bgp summary > /mnt/flash/up2.txt. Then flap spine1 Ethernet2 (leaf2's uplink).

Symptom: show event-handler UPLINK2 shows the trigger detected and the action run, but flash:up2.txt is empty.

Root cause and fix

The action ran show in bash, where it is not a command; the error went to stderr and the redirect created an empty file. Change the action to action bash FastCli -p 15 -c "show ip bgp summary" > /mnt/flash/up2.txt, flap the link again and read the file. You can test any action line by running it with bash from exec mode first.

Event Manager deep dive (optional)

This drill follows AW Ch 27 · Event Manager (pp. 143-148) and builds on objective 3.1 above. Event handlers are named in objective 3.1, so expect them on the Operations exam, even if only as one task. Everything here runs on cEOS.

Key ideas from the chapter. A handler is a trigger plus an action, with an optional delay between them (p. 143). The feature is called Event Manager, but the commands say event-handler. Triggers include on-boot, on-counters, on-intf (with ip, ip6 and operstatus), on-logging, on-maintenance, on-startup-config and vm-tracer. When an on-intf action runs, EOS passes it variables such as $INTF and $OPERSTATE, plus the interface's IP addresses (p. 145). A handler becomes active only when you leave its config mode, and leaving the mode can fire it straight away (pp. 143, 146). EOS accepts any action text without checking it, so a broken action only shows up when it runs and fails (p. 146). on-startup-config fires on write memory or copy running-config startup-config, but not when someone edits the file from bash (pp. 145, 147).

  1. On leaf1, count how many counters the on-counters trigger can use, then list only the CPU idle counters. Hardware switches list thousands of counters, and the list depends on the ASIC and EOS release (p. 143). cEOS will show far fewer.

  2. Create handler ET5-STATE for leaf1 Ethernet5 (host1). On any operational state change it should log $INTF is now $OPERSTATE to syslog. Add a multi-line comment saying what it is for and who added it. Flap the port and find the log line.

  3. Create handler CFG-BACKUP with an on-startup-config trigger. It should run a script on flash that copies startup-config to /mnt/flash/cfg-backup/ with a timestamp in the name and keeps only the newest 10 copies.

  4. Run write memory five times as fast as you can and count the backup files. Set delay 0 on the handler, repeat, and count again. Explain the difference.

  5. Create handler BROKEN on on-intf Ethernet5 operstatus with the action email -s "$INTF is $OPERSTATE" (no recipient, no mail server). Flap the port, find the failure in the log, and use show event-handler BROKEN to work out what went wrong. Then fix the handler so it logs instead.

  6. Build a CPU alarm CPU-HIGH with on-counters: poll every 5 seconds and fire when a CPU idle counter drops below 20, but only if that happens 2 times within 20 seconds. Log a warning when it fires. Put some load on the container and see whether it fires.

  7. Show all handlers, including any built-in ones, and read the trigger and action counters for each.

Solution · counters, interface handler, comment 
leaf1# show event-handler trigger counters | wc -l
leaf1# show event-handler trigger counters | grep -i idle

leaf1(config)# event-handler ET5-STATE
leaf1(config-handler-ET5-STATE)# comment
Enter TEXT message. Type 'EOF' on its own line to end.
Log host1 link changes on leaf1 Et5
Added for lab D3 by student
EOF
leaf1(config-handler-ET5-STATE)# trigger on-intf Ethernet5 operstatus
leaf1(config-handler-ET5-STATE)# action bash logger -t ET5 "$INTF is now $OPERSTATE"
leaf1(config-handler-ET5-STATE)# delay 2
leaf1(config-handler-ET5-STATE)# exit

leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet5
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# shutdown
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# no shutdown
leaf1# show logging last 2 minutes | include ET5|EVENT

Leaving the handler mode can produce one activation before you flap anything. That is the "fires on exit" behaviour from the chapter, not a mistake in your handler.

Solution · config backup on every write 
leaf1# bash
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ cat > /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh <<'END'
#!/bin/bash
# Save a timestamped copy of startup-config and keep the newest 10
DIR=/mnt/flash/cfg-backup
mkdir -p "$DIR"
cp /mnt/flash/startup-config "$DIR/startup-config_$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S)"
ls -1t "$DIR"/startup-config_* | tail -n +11 | xargs -r rm -f
END
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ chmod +x /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh && ls /mnt/flash/cfg-backup/
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ exit

leaf1(config)# event-handler CFG-BACKUP
leaf1(config-handler-CFG-BACKUP)# trigger on-startup-config
leaf1(config-handler-CFG-BACKUP)# action bash /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh
leaf1(config-handler-CFG-BACKUP)# exit

leaf1# write memory
! repeat quickly 5 times, then:
leaf1# bash ls -l /mnt/flash/cfg-backup/

leaf1(config)# event-handler CFG-BACKUP
leaf1(config-handler-CFG-BACKUP)# delay 0
leaf1(config-handler-CFG-BACKUP)# exit
! 5 fast writes again, then count the files

With the default delay, writes that land close together are handled as one trigger, so you get fewer files than writes. With delay 0 each write gets its own run (pp. 146-147). Files named to the second can still overwrite each other on very fast writes. The tail -n +11 line is the rotation that stops a busy operator from filling flash, which the book warns about (p. 147). The book also points out that AAA command accounting is the better tool for tracking who changed what. This handler is a cheap extra that gives you a usable config after every save.

Solution · finding a broken action 
leaf1# show logging last 5 minutes | include EVENT
! look for SYS-6-EVENT_TRIGGERED followed by SYS-5-EVENT_ACTION_FAILED ... exit code N
leaf1# show event-handler BROKEN
! Trigger Detections and Activations go up; the Action line shows exactly what ran
leaf1# bash email -s "test"
! run the action by hand to see the real error

leaf1(config)# event-handler BROKEN
leaf1(config-handler-BROKEN)# action bash logger -t BROKEN "$INTF is $OPERSTATE"
leaf1(config-handler-BROKEN)# exit

The book's version of this mistake is an email action with no recipient address (p. 146). The method is what matters: the log tells you the action failed and its exit code, show event-handler shows the exact command, and running that command by hand from bash shows the real error. Exit code 126 usually means a script is not executable, and 127 means the command was not found.

Solution · CPU alarm with on-counters 
leaf1(config)# event-handler CPU-HIGH
leaf1(config-handler-CPU-HIGH)# trigger on-counters
leaf1(config-handler-CPU-HIGH-counters)# poll interval 5
leaf1(config-handler-CPU-HIGH-counters)# condition tcollector.proc.stat.cpu.type=idle < 20
leaf1(config-handler-CPU-HIGH-counters)# action bash logger -p local4.warning -t CPU "CPU idle below 20 percent"
leaf1(config-handler-CPU-HIGH-counters)# threshold 20 count 2
leaf1(config-handler-CPU-HIGH-counters)# exit

! load: two busy loops for 30 s
leaf1# bash timeout 30 sh -c 'yes > /dev/null & yes > /dev/null & wait'
leaf1# show event-handler CPU-HIGH

Use a counter name from your own grep idle output; the name above shows the pattern, not a promise. The chapter also shows a second style, where the condition is the output of a bash command (condition bashCmd."..." > 80) that works out CPU use from vmstat (p. 146). A container shares the host's CPUs, so two busy loops may not push idle below 20. If it never fires, raise the threshold to test the plumbing. In production, CPU alarms belong in CloudVision, as the book says.

Solution · status of every handler 
leaf1# show event-handler
leaf1# show event-handler CFG-BACKUP

Hardware switches often have a built-in handler that watches drop counters (the book shows DropCountersHandler marked BUILT-IN, pp. 147-148). Read Total Trigger Detections, Total Trigger Activations and Total Actions: detections without activations mean the threshold or delay held it back, and activations followed by EVENT_ACTION_FAILED logs mean the action is broken.

Real world · coming from Cisco EEM. The ideas map closely. An EEM applet is an event-handler. event syslog pattern is trigger on-logging with a regex. Interface and tracking events are trigger on-intf. EEM timer and cron events are best done with the CLI schedule command (objective 3.1). action cli command becomes FastCli -p 15 -c "..." inside a bash action, and action syslog msg becomes logger. EEM Tcl policies become bash or Python scripts on flash. The big difference is that EOS actions are Linux commands, so you get the full shell (files, curl, Python) instead of a fixed action list.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf2): create /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh as in the solution but skip the chmod +x, then add handler CFG-BACKUP with trigger on-startup-config and action bash /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh. Run write memory.

Symptom: no backup files appear, and the log shows the handler triggered and then failed.

Root cause and fix

The script is not executable, so bash refuses to run it (exit code 126 in the failure message). Fix it with bash chmod +x /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh, or change the action to action bash bash /mnt/flash/cfg-backup.sh. Run write memory again and check /mnt/flash/cfg-backup/.

Event Monitor: prove what changed and when

This drill follows AW Ch 28 · Event Monitor (pp. 150-156). Event monitor is part of objective 3.1, and it is the fastest way to answer "what changed?" during troubleshooting. Everything here runs on cEOS.

Key ideas from the chapter. Event monitor writes changes to the ARP, MAC, route, LACP, IGMP snooping and mroute tables into a SQLite database at /var/log/eventMon.db (p. 150). Since EOS 4.21 it is off by default. The error you get when it is off suggests event-monitor sync, but that does not turn it on; the config command event-monitor does (p. 150). The old show event-monitor sqlite and interact commands stopped working after EOS 4.16, so for SQL use bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db, and for everyday work use the CLI filters: match-interface, match-mac, match-ip, match-time and group-by (pp. 150-151). The CLI accepts short interface names like e5; raw SQL needs the exact stored name, such as Ethernet5 (p. 151). /var/log is wiped on reboot, so set a backup path on flash to keep history (p. 155).

  1. On leaf1, check whether event monitor is running. If it is not, enable it. List the table options with show event-monitor ?.

  2. Find host1's MAC address for VLAN 10 (on host1, show interfaces Vlan10), ping the gateway from host1's VRF H10, and show that MAC's history on leaf1 with match-mac.

  3. Simulate a "host moved" ticket: shut leaf1 Ethernet5 for 20 seconds, then bring it back, and keep host1 pinging the whole time. Use match-mac again to show when the MAC moved off Port-Channel5, where it went, and when it came back.

  4. Read the LACP events from that flap, first plain, then with verbose. Name the state host1's link went through.

  5. Flap leaf3's Loopback0 (10.255.0.5) and use match-ip on leaf1 to show the route being removed and re-added. Then add and remove a static route ip route 20.20.20.0/24 Null0 on leaf1 and find both events.

  6. Show the ARP table history grouped by IP address, and only the ARP events for Ethernet1 using the short name e1. Then try the same filter in raw SQL with intf='e1' and explain why it returns nothing.

  7. Make the history survive a reboot: store it on flash as em.log and keep 10 files. Find the files with dir flash:.

Solution · enable, MAC history, host move 
leaf1# show event-monitor arp
! "% event-monitor not running" means it is off; event-monitor sync will not fix that
leaf1(config)# event-monitor
leaf1# show event-monitor ?

host1# show interfaces Vlan10 | include address
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.1 repeat 1000 interval 1

leaf1# show event-monitor mac match-mac 00:1c:73:31:00:01
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet5
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# shutdown
! wait 20 s
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# no shutdown
leaf1# show event-monitor mac match-mac 00:1c:73:31:00:01
2026-10-25 13:02:10.418|10|00:1c:73:31:00:01|Port-Channel5|learnedDynamicMac|added|41
2026-10-25 13:05:31.007|10|00:1c:73:31:00:01|Port-Channel5||removed|57
2026-10-25 13:05:31.009|10|00:1c:73:31:00:01|Port-Channel10|learnedDynamicMac|added|58
2026-10-25 13:05:53.664|10|00:1c:73:31:00:01|Port-Channel10||removed|61
2026-10-25 13:05:53.666|10|00:1c:73:31:00:01|Port-Channel5|learnedDynamicMac|added|62

Representative output; use host1's real MAC. The columns are time, FID (an internal VLAN ID, usually the VLAN), MAC, interface, type, change and a running counter (p. 151). While leaf1's link was down, host1's traffic arrived through leaf2, so leaf1 pointed the MAC at the MLAG peer link Port-Channel10. That is the same evidence the book uses to show a user moved a server to a different port (p. 152): a timestamped line showing the MAC appear on a new interface. On a hardware switch the type also tells you a static entry (configuredStaticMac) from a learned one.

Solution · LACP 
leaf1# show event-monitor lacp
leaf1# show event-monitor lacp verbose
leaf1# show event-monitor lacp match-interface e5

The plain output is one long pipe-separated row per change and is hard to read. verbose prints columns for port, port-channel, state and a reason, so you can follow Ethernet5 through states such as PortDisabled, LacpDisabled or Expired and back to selected (p. 154). The state names come from the LACP receive and mux state machines; you do not need the IEEE spec, only the order of events and the time they happened.

Solution · routes and ARP 
leaf3(config)# interface Loopback0
leaf3(config-if-Lo0)# shutdown
leaf3(config-if-Lo0)# no shutdown
leaf1# show event-monitor route match-ip 10.255.0.5/32

leaf1(config)# ip route 20.20.20.0/24 Null0
leaf1(config)# no ip route 20.20.20.0/24 Null0
leaf1# show event-monitor route match-ip 20.20.20.0/24

leaf1# show event-monitor arp group-by ip
leaf1# show event-monitor arp match-interface e1
leaf1# bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db "select * from arp where intf='e1';"
leaf1# bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db "select * from arp where intf='Ethernet1';"
leaf1# bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db ".schema route"

A route "added" row carries a protocol name, and the "removed" row has the extra fields empty (p. 152). The book notes that the metric and preference fields are not reliable, so trust the time, prefix and change columns. The first raw SQL query returns nothing because the database stores the full name Ethernet1; only the CLI expands e1 (p. 151). group-by ip keeps the latest row per address, like SQL GROUP BY. The route table has no VRF column, so tenant (VRF TENANT-A) changes may not show up here; check on your release.

Solution · keep history across reboots 
leaf1(config)# event-monitor backup path em.log
leaf1(config)# event-monitor backup max-size 10
leaf1# dir flash: | include em.log
! one rotating file per table, for example macem.log.0, routeem.log.0, arpem.log.0

! other settings
leaf1(config)# event-monitor buffer max-size ?
leaf1(config)# no event-monitor all
leaf1(config)# event-monitor route
leaf1(config)# event-monitor all

A plain file name lands on flash, which survives a reboot. A path like /home/admin/em.log is still read as a flash path and ends up as flash:home/admin/em.log; to really write to Linux paths you need the file: prefix, and /home does not survive a reboot anyway (p. 155). Each backup file holds a fixed number of events, then rotates with a new numeric suffix, and the oldest is deleted when the max-size count is reached (default 200 files, p. 155). no event-monitor all turns everything off, event-monitor route turns on one table, and event-monitor all turns them all back on.

Exam tip. If a task says "find when" or "prove what changed", reach for show event-monitor before the logs. If it prints "not running", enabling it now only records new events, so make the change happen again (flap the link, re-add the route) after you enable it.
Real world. Leave event monitor on and give it a flash backup path in your base config. The book's point is that it pays off on the day someone insists "nothing changed": a MAC that moved to a different port, or a route that flapped at 2 a.m., is right there with a timestamp (pp. 152, 156). CloudVision keeps the same kind of history fabric-wide, but event monitor works when CloudVision is not there.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf2): no event-monitor all.

Symptom: during an outage review, show event-monitor mac on leaf2 reports that event monitor is not running, and event-monitor sync does not help.

Root cause and fix

All event monitor tables were turned off. Fix it with event-monitor all (or event-monitor), add a backup path so the history is kept, and confirm with show event-monitor mac after flapping leaf2 Ethernet5. Events from the period when it was off are gone for good; note that in the incident report.

3.2 Monitoring with CloudVision: dashboards, events, compliance, endpoint search, topology

With the fabric streaming to CloudVision, the NOC wants a POD_A dashboard, an alert when any uplink drops, a daily compliance check, and the ability to find any server in seconds.

Path A: CloudVision

  1. Confirm all six switches stream (Lab D2, 2.1). In the device view for leaf1, look at the telemetry pages for interfaces, BGP, MLAG and the configuration timeline; find when you made the D1 changes.

  2. Build a dashboard POD_A Health with: interface traffic for leaf1 and leaf2 Ethernet1, Ethernet2 and Port-Channel5; BGP neighbor states on leaf1 and leaf2; CPU and memory for both. Save it and share it.

  3. Shut leaf1 Ethernet1 for two minutes. Find the resulting events (link down, BGP peer down) in the Events view, acknowledge them, and look at the event group (events correlated in time). Bring the link back up.

  4. Create a custom event rule (or adjust an event threshold) that raises a warning when any interface on a device tagged Role:Leaf exceeds 1 percent input errors or when interface utilization exceeds 80 percent. Follow the ATD Custom Events guide for the UI path.

  5. Compliance: check configuration compliance (designed vs running), image compliance (devices on the intended image) and bug exposure for the fabric. Make leaf4 non-compliant with a CLI change, find it in the compliance view, then remediate.

  6. Endpoint search: find host1's IP 10.10.10.101 and its MAC. CloudVision should show the switch and interface (leaf1 and leaf2 Port-Channel5) and the location history.

  7. Topology: open the topology view, confirm the LLDP-derived physical links match the cabling (each leaf to both spines, peer links, hosts), and use the overlay or VXLAN layer if your release shows it.

Solution · Path A notes
  • Dashboards: Dashboards, then New Dashboard. Add widgets (time series, table, top-N) and pick metrics by device and interface. Group the widgets for POD_A and save.
  • Events: the Events page lists active and past events with severity; filter by device or type, open one to see the timeline and related metrics. Acknowledge it so it does not page twice. Event rules and thresholds live under the Events settings (often called event generation or custom events).
  • Compliance: the device inventory and the compliance or Device Health views show configuration compliance, image compliance and bug exposure. A config mismatch here is the same designed vs running diff you saw in Lab D2.
  • Config sanity: CloudVision validates config during configlet and workspace review (syntax checks against the device), and its MLAG and topology checks surface the same problems as show mlag config-sanity.
  • Endpoint search: the search bar on the main page accepts IP, MAC or host name, and returns the attached switch, interface, VLAN and history (from MAC, ARP and LLDP state streamed by TerminAttr).
  • Topology: built from LLDP. Unknown neighbors usually mean LLDP is off on one side or a device is not streaming.

Follow ATD: CVP Telemetry and Introduction to Dashboards and ATD: CloudVision Custom Events for screenshots.

Path B: the same questions answered from EOS, gNMI and ANTA

  1. "Dashboard": stream leaf1 Ethernet1 counters with gNMI from the host for one minute.

  2. "Events": after shutting leaf1 Ethernet1 again, reconstruct the incident from show logging and the event monitor on leaf1 and spine1.

  3. "Compliance": run ANTA with the AVD-generated catalog for leaf1 (labs/avd/intended/test_catalogs/leaf1-catalog.yml) and read the failures, if any. Then diff designed vs running as in Lab D2.

  4. "Endpoint search": find host1's 10.10.10.101 on the fabric using only CLI on any leaf: ARP in the VRF, MAC table, EVPN type-2 route, and the VTEP behind it.

  5. "Topology": draw the physical topology from show lldp neighbors on the spines and leaves and compare with the topology file.

Solution · Path B 
host$ gnmic -a 172.100.100.21:6030 -u admin -p admin --insecure subscribe \
    --path "/interfaces/interface[name=Ethernet1]/state/counters" --mode stream --stream-mode sample --sample-interval 10s

leaf1# show logging last 15 minutes | include Ethernet1|BGP
spine1# show event-monitor route match-ip 10.255.0.3/32

host$ anta nrfu --help
host$ anta nrfu -u admin -p admin --insecure -i anta-inventory.yml -c labs/avd/intended/test_catalogs/leaf1-catalog.yml table
#   anta-inventory.yml: anta_inventory: hosts: [{host: 172.100.100.21, name: leaf1}]
#   (option names change between ANTA releases; check the help output)

leaf3# show ip arp vrf TENANT-A 10.10.10.101
leaf3# show mac address-table vlan 10
leaf3# show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip 10.10.10.101
leaf3# show vxlan address-table vlan 10
leaf1# show mac address-table address 00:1c:73:31:00:01
leaf1# show lldp neighbors
leaf3# show vxlan address-table vlan 10
          Vxlan Mac Address Table
----------------------------------------------------------------------
VLAN  Mac Address     Type      Prt  VTEP             Moves   Last Move
----  -----------     ----      ---  ----             -----   ---------
  10  001c.7331.0001  EVPN      Vx1  10.255.1.3       1       0:42:10 ago
Total Remote Mac Addresses for this criterion: 1

Verify

leaf1# show lldp neighbors
Port          Neighbor Device ID   Neighbor Port ID    TTL
------------- -------------------- ------------------- ----
Et1           spine1               Ethernet1           120
Et2           spine2               Ethernet1           120
Et3           leaf2                Ethernet3           120
Et4           leaf2                Ethernet4           120
Et5           host1                Ethernet1           120
Ma0           ...                  ...                 120

Path A: the dashboard shows live data, the uplink event appears and clears, compliance is green after remediation, and endpoint search finds host1 on leaf1/leaf2 Po5. Path B: you can place host1 on VTEP 10.255.1.3 (leaf1/leaf2) from leaf3 using only CLI.

Exam tip. CloudVision can only show what it receives. If a device is missing from dashboards, topology or endpoint search, check streaming first (inventory status, then show daemon TerminAttr on the switch) rather than the widget.
Real world. Telemetry beats polling: TerminAttr streams state changes as they happen, so CloudVision can show the exact second a BGP session dropped and what changed in config just before it. That timeline view, aligned with the change control history, is what makes root cause analysis fast.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf4): daemon TerminAttr then shutdown. Also on leaf4: no lldp run.

Symptom: Path A: leaf4 shows as not streaming; dashboards for leaf4 flat-line; topology loses leaf4's links; endpoint search cannot locate host2 on leaf4. Path B: show lldp neighbors on leaf3 and the spines shows no leaf4 entries.

Root cause and fix

No streaming agent means no telemetry, and no LLDP means no topology links. Restore both: daemon TerminAttr, no shutdown; lldp run. Verify with show daemon TerminAttr, show lldp neighbors and the CV inventory status. The AVD config does not contain lldp run because LLDP is on by default; the explicit no lldp run is the change to remove.

3.3 EOS monitoring tools: SNMP, sFlow, watch, LANZ, mirroring, TAP aggregation

The legacy NMS needs SNMP, the flow collector needs sFlow, and the security team wants a copy of host1's traffic. Run collectors on the containerlab host (172.100.100.1 on the management bridge) or in a small Linux container on the l5dc-mgmt network.

  1. Configure SNMP on leaf1: v2c read-only community l5dc-ro limited to the management subnet with a standard ACL, location DC1-ROW1, contact noc@example.net. Add SNMPv3: view ALL (iso), group NETOPS with authPriv, user nms with SHA authentication and AES privacy. Send v3 traps to 172.100.100.1. Test both with snmpwalk from the host.

  2. Configure sFlow on leaf1 and leaf2: sample rate 16384, polling interval 30 s, collector 172.100.100.1 port 6343, source Management0, enabled. Check status and run sflowtool or tcpdump udp port 6343 on the host.

  3. Watch the uplink counters on leaf1 update every 2 seconds with changes highlighted while host1 pings host2 with repeat 10000.

  4. Mirror host1's traffic on leaf1 (Ethernet5, both directions) to the CPU and capture it with tcpdump. Then remove the session.

  5. Write the config (notes only, cEOS has no hardware for it) for LANZ on leaf1's uplinks and for a TAP aggregation switch with two tap ports and one tool port.

Verify

leaf1# show snmp user
User name      : nms
Security model : v3
Engine ID      : f5717f001c7300210000
Authentication : SHA
Privacy        : AES-128
Group          : NETOPS
host$ snmpwalk -v3 -l authPriv -u nms -a SHA -A AuthPass123 -x AES -X PrivPass123 172.100.100.21 SNMPv2-MIB::sysName
host$ snmpget -v2c -c l5dc-ro 172.100.100.21 SNMPv2-MIB::sysLocation.0
SNMPv2-MIB::sysName.0 = STRING: leaf1
SNMPv2-MIB::sysLocation.0 = STRING: DC1-ROW1
leaf1# show sflow
sFlow Configuration
-------------------
Destination(s):
  172.100.100.1:6343 (VRF: default)
Source(s):
  172.100.100.21 (Management0)
Sample Rate: 16384
Polling Interval (sec): 30

Status
------
Running: Yes
Polling On: Yes
Sampling On: Yes
Number of Samples Sent: 112
leaf1# show monitor session
Session SPAN-HOST1
------------------------
Programmed in HW: Yes
Source Ports:
  Both:      Et5
Destination Ports:
    Cpu :  active (mirror0)

Both SNMP versions answer from the host; the v2c community fails from any address outside 172.100.100.0/24. sFlow shows Running and the collector receives datagrams (on cEOS sampling of forwarded traffic may be limited because there is no hardware sampler; counter polling still flows). The monitor session is active and tcpdump shows host1's ICMP.

Solution · SNMP and sFlow (leaf1) 
leaf1(config)# ip access-list standard SNMP-MGMT
leaf1(config-std-acl-SNMP-MGMT)# permit 172.100.100.0/24
leaf1(config-std-acl-SNMP-MGMT)# exit
leaf1(config)# snmp-server community l5dc-ro ro SNMP-MGMT
leaf1(config)# snmp-server location DC1-ROW1
leaf1(config)# snmp-server contact noc@example.net
leaf1(config)# snmp-server view ALL iso included
leaf1(config)# snmp-server group NETOPS v3 priv read ALL notify ALL
leaf1(config)# snmp-server user nms NETOPS v3 auth sha AuthPass123 priv aes PrivPass123
leaf1(config)# snmp-server host 172.100.100.1 version 3 priv nms
leaf1(config)# snmp-server enable traps
leaf1# show snmp
leaf1# show snmp community
leaf1# show snmp group
leaf1# show snmp user

leaf1(config)# sflow sample 16384
leaf1(config)# sflow polling-interval 30
leaf1(config)# sflow destination 172.100.100.1 6343
leaf1(config)# sflow source-interface Management0
leaf1(config)# sflow run
leaf1# show sflow
leaf1# show sflow interfaces
host$ sudo tcpdump -ni any udp port 6343 -c 5

Newer releases also offer auth sha256/sha512 and priv aes256; check with ? and match the options on the NMS. If management were in a VRF you would add snmp-server vrf MGMT and sflow vrf MGMT ... forms.

Solution · watch, mirroring, tcpdump (leaf1) 
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 repeat 10000
leaf1# show interfaces counters rates
leaf1# show interfaces Ethernet1-2 counters | nz
leaf1# bash watch -n 2 -d 'FastCli -p 15 -c "show interfaces Ethernet1-2 counters"'
! EOS also has a native watch command on many releases; try: watch ?

leaf1(config)# monitor session SPAN-HOST1 source Ethernet5 both
leaf1(config)# monitor session SPAN-HOST1 destination Cpu
leaf1# show monitor session
leaf1# bash sudo tcpdump -ni mirror0 icmp -c 10
! remove
leaf1(config)# no monitor session SPAN-HOST1

Mirroring to the CPU is a hardware feature on real switches; on cEOS the session may show as not programmed or capture nothing. If so, capture on the kernel interface instead (next objective) and treat the mirror config as the deliverable. Other forms: destination another Ethernet port (local SPAN), or a GRE tunnel to a remote analyzer (monitor session NAME destination tunnel mode gre source ... destination ..., check ?), plus ACL filters on the source to cut volume.

Solution · LANZ and TAP aggregation (concept config) 
! LANZ (Latency Analyzer): queue-length monitoring on supported ASICs
leaf1(config)# queue-monitor length
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet1-2
leaf1(config-if-Et1-2)# queue-monitor length thresholds 512 256
leaf1(config)# queue-monitor streaming
! "queue-monitor streaming" feeds LANZ data to CloudVision/clients; thresholds are in segments or bytes by platform
leaf1# show queue-monitor length
leaf1# show queue-monitor length status

! TAP aggregation: a dedicated switch in exclusive tap-agg mode
tapagg(config)# tap aggregation
tapagg(config-tap-agg)# mode exclusive
tapagg(config)# interface Ethernet1-2
tapagg(config-if-Et1-2)# switchport mode tap
tapagg(config-if-Et1-2)# switchport tap default group MON
tapagg(config)# interface Ethernet10
tapagg(config-if-Et10)# switchport mode tool
tapagg(config-if-Et10)# switchport tool group set MON
tapagg# show tap aggregation groups

Neither feature works in cEOS (no buffers to monitor, no tap-agg hardware). Know the concepts: LANZ reports queue congestion events and microbursts that average counters hide; TAP aggregation turns a switch into a packet broker that takes in tap and SPAN feeds (tap ports) and forwards them, filtered and possibly truncated or timestamped, to tool ports, with no normal switching between them.

Exam tip. SNMPv3 has three parts that must agree: the view, the group's security level (noauth, auth, priv), and the user's auth/priv settings. When a v3 walk fails, compare show snmp group and show snmp user with the security level the client asks for (-l authPriv).
Real world. sFlow sampling at 1:16384 is enough to see top talkers on 10G and above without loading the CPU. For the full stream use mirroring or a TAP aggregation layer; for congestion use LANZ; for state and counters use streaming telemetry. SNMP survives mainly for legacy NMS and inventory.
Real world (optional, not on the lab exam): Tap Aggregation. Background from AW Ch 26 · Tap Aggregation (pp. 140-142). A tap aggregation switch is a packet broker: feeds from optical taps and SPAN ports come in on tap ports and leave on tool ports toward analyzers and recorders. Key points to remember:
  • Exclusive mode turns the whole switch into a tap aggregator and stops normal switching, so it belongs on a dedicated box, never on a production leaf. Mixed mode (on platforms that support it) lets some ports do tap aggregation while the rest keep switching.
  • Tap and tool ports do not take part in normal L2 control protocols: STP and LLDP are not run on them, because they only receive or only send copies of traffic.
  • Traffic is steered with aggregation groups. A tap port feeds one default group; a tool port can belong to many groups. Build the tool port's list with switchport tool group set, then change it with switchport tool group add or remove (set replaces the whole list, a classic mistake).
  • Arista also offers a TapAgg web GUI and CloudVision views to manage the groups, which is how most operations teams drive it day to day.

cEOS cannot run tap aggregation, so the hands-on part for this objective is the mirror-to-CPU drill above (monitor session ... destination Cpu, then bash sudo tcpdump -ni mirror0). That is the skill you will actually use to prove what is on the wire.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf1): no snmp-server group NETOPS v3 priv read ALL notify ALL, then snmp-server group NETOPS v3 auth read ALL.

Symptom: the authPriv snmpwalk from the host now times out or returns an authorization error, while v2c still works.

Root cause and fix

The group now requires only auth (no privacy), and the request uses authPriv with a user bound to that group; the security levels do not match the group's access entry, so the agent rejects or drops the request. Restore snmp-server group NETOPS v3 priv read ALL notify ALL (remove the auth variant), re-test with snmpwalk -l authPriv. Verify with show snmp group.

tcpdump and advanced mirroring (vEOS lab)

This section follows AW Ch 25 · tcpdump and Advanced Mirroring (pp. 133-139). Objective 3.3 configures a mirror to the CPU on cEOS; here you build the book's three-switch test on vEOS in EVE-NG and work through capture, mirroring, ACL filters and truncation.

Key ideas from the chapter. tcpdump on a switch sees only packets to or from the CPU; traffic the ASIC forwards never reaches it (p. 133). In Bash, tcpdump wants Linux interface names: et2 for Ethernet2, ma1 for Management1, all lowercase, no abbreviations, and slashes become underscores (et1_1_1); ifconfig -s lists them (p. 133). Write captures with -w to get a pcap file for Wireshark and read them with -r, and keep them on /mnt/flash or, better, an SSD (pp. 133-134). The EOS tcpdump command takes EOS names and keywords (interface, filter, file, packet-count, size, monitor, verbose), and its file flash:NAME option works as expected on current releases (p. 134). Normal mirror sessions send copies to another Ethernet port, a port-channel or a GRE tunnel, all handled by the ASIC, so the switch itself still cannot see them (p. 135). Advanced mirroring on supported platforms adds destination cpu: the copies appear on a Linux interface mirror0 (then mirror1 and so on, per session), which tcpdump can read (pp. 135-136). Copies sent to the CPU are limited by CoPP (the book quotes about 800 Mbps), so you cut them down with an ACL on the session (ip access-group) and with truncate (pp. 136-139).

Build the test lab

Three vEOS nodes in a line, routed, as in the book (p. 135). Use spare EVE-NG nodes, not the fabric.

NodeLinksAddresses
capAEthernet1 to capZ Ethernet1Et1 10.10.1.2/30, Loopback0 11.0.0.11/32, static route to 12.0.0.12 via 10.10.1.1
capZEthernet1 to capA, Ethernet2 to capBEt1 10.10.1.1/30, Et2 10.10.2.1/30, static routes to both loopbacks
capBEthernet1 to capZ Ethernet2Et1 10.10.2.2/30, Loopback0 12.0.0.12/32, static route to 11.0.0.11 via 10.10.2.1

  1. Build the lab, then traceroute 12.0.0.12 source 11.0.0.11 from capA to prove the path goes through capZ.

  2. On capZ, in Bash, list the Linux interface names, try tcpdump -i e2 and tcpdump -i Ethernet2, then capture 10 packets on the right name. Write a capture of the management port to /mnt/flash/z.pcap and read it back. Then use the EOS tcpdump command to capture on Ethernet2 with a filter for ICMP only, and to write a file to flash.

  3. Start a continuous ping from capA's loopback to capB's loopback (bash ping -I 11.0.0.11 12.0.0.12). Capture on capZ Ethernet2 and note whether you see the transit pings. Then ping capZ's own Ethernet2 address from capB and capture again.

  4. Create monitor session CAP on capZ: Ethernet1 and Ethernet2 received traffic, destination CPU. Find its Linux interface with show monitor session and capture five packets on it.

  5. Add an ACL to the session that matches only traffic to or from 12.0.0.12, and check show monitor session CAP. Generate other traffic at the same time (an iperf3 test or a big ping to capB's Ethernet1 address) and show that only the loopback traffic reaches mirror0.

  6. Turn on truncation for the session, send pings of 1000 bytes, and read the length fields in tcpdump.

  7. Clean up: remove the session, the ACL and every capture file.

Solution · tcpdump in Bash and in EOS 
capZ# bash
[admin@capZ ~]$ ifconfig -s
[admin@capZ ~]$ sudo tcpdump -i e2
! fails: no such device
[admin@capZ ~]$ sudo tcpdump -i et2 -c 10 -n
[admin@capZ ~]$ sudo tcpdump -i ma1 -c 50 -w /mnt/flash/z.pcap
[admin@capZ ~]$ sudo tcpdump -r /mnt/flash/z.pcap -n | head
[admin@capZ ~]$ exit
capZ# tcpdump interface Ethernet2 filter icmp packet-count 10
capZ# tcpdump interface Management1 file flash:z2.pcap packet-count 50
capZ# dir flash: | include pcap

Linux names follow the CLI short names in lowercase (p. 133). -c limits the count, -n skips name lookups, -X adds a hex and ASCII dump (p. 134). On vEOS without sudo you may get a permission error; the EOS tcpdump command needs no sudo. A file written with -w or file is pcap, so read it with tcpdump -r or copy it off the box to Wireshark (scp from the Ubuntu server) (p. 134).

Solution · transit traffic vs traffic to the switch 
capA# bash ping -I 11.0.0.11 12.0.0.12
capZ# tcpdump interface Ethernet2 filter icmp packet-count 10
capB# ping 10.10.2.1 repeat 5
capZ# tcpdump interface Ethernet2 filter icmp packet-count 10

On a hardware switch the first capture shows only LLDP and BGP or other control traffic, never the transit pings, while the second shows the pings to capZ's own address (pp. 135-136). On vEOS the forwarding is done in software, so you may see transit packets too; that is a vEOS artifact, and the lesson for hardware is the book's. This is also why a "tcpdump shows nothing" result on a switch proves very little about the data plane.

Solution · mirror to CPU, ACL filter, truncation 
capZ(config)# monitor session CAP source Ethernet1 rx
capZ(config)# monitor session CAP source Ethernet2 rx
capZ(config)# monitor session CAP destination cpu
capZ# show monitor session CAP
! Destination Ports:  Cpu : active (mirror0)
capZ# bash sudo tcpdump -i mirror0 -c 5 -n

capZ(config)# ip access-list Filter12
capZ(config-acl-Filter12)# 10 permit ip host 12.0.0.12 any
capZ(config-acl-Filter12)# 20 permit ip any host 12.0.0.12
capZ(config-acl-Filter12)# exit
capZ(config)# monitor session CAP ip access-group Filter12
capZ# show monitor session CAP

capZ(config)# monitor session CAP truncate
capA# bash ping -I 11.0.0.11 -c 3 -s 1000 12.0.0.12
capZ# bash sudo tcpdump -i mirror0 -n

capZ(config)# no monitor session CAP
capZ(config)# no ip access-list Filter12
capZ# delete flash:z2.pcap
capZ# bash sudo rm /mnt/flash/z.pcap

Representative steps; check each with ?, because CPU destinations, ACL filters and truncation depend on the platform and release (pp. 135-139), and vEOS may not support all of them. The destination name (mirror0) is assigned per session in creation order (p. 136). With the ACL, the session only copies matching packets, so a large iperf transfer between the Ethernet addresses does not reach the CPU at all (pp. 136-137). The book uses RX sources because its platform could not apply mirror ACLs on TX (p. 137). With truncation, tcpdump shows each packet as truncated with hundreds of bytes missing, but the headers are intact (pp. 138-139); some platforms let you pick the size (truncate size ?). To run iperf between switches as the book does, the server side needs its control-plane ACL to permit the iperf port (p. 137); on current EOS use system control-plane and an ip access-group there, and remember iperf3 uses TCP 5201 by default, not 5001.

Exam tip. Know three facts: on hardware, a switch capture sees only CPU traffic; destination cpu in a monitor session creates mirrorN for tcpdump; and you control the volume with an ACL on the session and truncation, because CoPP limits what reaches the CPU.
Real world. Mirroring to the CPU is an emergency tool for when no sniffer or TAP is available, for example at 4 a.m. on a remote site (p. 139). Filter at the source with an ACL, truncate, limit the packet count, and stop the session as soon as you have what you need; a forgotten session to the CPU wastes CoPP capacity that control-plane protocols need. Captures fill flash fast, so delete them or write them to an SSD (drive:) and copy them off-box (p. 134). For permanent visibility, use TAP aggregation or a packet broker instead (TAP aggregation).

Break / fix

Inject (on capZ): a colleague left tcpdump interface Ethernet2 file flash:big.pcap running with no packet count in a background session while capA ran a long iperf test.

Symptom: write memory on capZ fails or warns about space, and dir flash: shows very little free space.

Root cause and fix

The capture grew without limit and filled flash (p. 134). Stop it (Ctrl-C in its session, or bash sudo pkill tcpdump), delete the file, and check dir flash: again. Next time use packet-count, a filter, a size limit (-C and -W in Bash to rotate files), or write to drive:. The flash health check in objective 1.3 finds hidden files like this.

Microbursts, counter math and LANZ

This drill follows AW Ch 23 · LANZ. The chapter's argument (p. 123) is that a 5-minute SNMP graph is just two counter readings subtracted and divided, so it averages away bursts that last a few milliseconds and still fill a buffer. For why bursts fill buffers in the first place (fabric collisions, incast, head-of-line blocking), see Design math (AW Ch 3). You will do that math by hand on cEOS, then practise the LANZ configuration you would use on hardware to see the bursts.

Watch out. LANZ reads queue depth from the switching ASIC, so it only works on platforms whose silicon supports it. It is not available on vEOS or cEOS (AW Ch 23, p. 123). In this lab the LANZ commands are for syntax practice; expect them to be rejected or to report nothing.

  1. Start steady traffic across leaf1's uplink. From host1, run a long ping to host2 with large packets.

    host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 size 1400 repeat 100000 interval 0.01

    If your EOS release rejects interval, drop it and run two pings in parallel sessions.

  2. Do the SNMP poller's job yourself, as the book does on p. 123. Read the outgoing octet counter on leaf1 Ethernet1, wait exactly 60 seconds, read it again, then turn the difference into a bit rate with bash expr.

    leaf1# show interfaces Ethernet1 counters outgoing
   (note OutOctets, wait 60 s)
leaf1# show interfaces Ethernet1 counters outgoing
leaf1# bash expr <second> - <first>
leaf1# bash expr <delta> \* 8 / 60

    The asterisk needs a backslash because expr runs in a Linux shell. Compare your answer with show interfaces Ethernet1 | include rate.

  3. Shorten the averaging window and watch the rate react faster. load-interval changes only the reported rate, never the forwarding (p. 125).

    leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet1
leaf1(config-if-Et1)# load-interval 5
leaf1# show interfaces counters rates

  4. Write the LANZ configuration for leaf1's uplinks as you would on a supported switch: enable it globally, set a high and a low threshold per interface, then switch from polling mode to notifying mode. Look up the allowed threshold range with ? first; it is in bytes on newer ASICs and in buffer segments on older ones (p. 125).

  5. Write the show commands you would use to read the results and to export them for a graph.

Solution · LANZ configuration and reading (supported hardware only) 
leaf1(config)# queue-monitor length
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet1-2
leaf1(config-if-Et1-2)# queue-monitor length thresholds <high> <low>
leaf1(config-if-Et1-2)# exit
leaf1(config)# queue-monitor length notifying

leaf1# show queue-monitor length status
leaf1# show queue-monitor length Ethernet1
leaf1# show queue-monitor length csv > flash:lanz-et1.csv

Thresholds work like the high and low marks on a tank (p. 124). Crossing the high threshold starts a congestion event, updates follow while the queue stays above the low threshold, and dropping below the low threshold ends it. In notifying mode you get Start, Update and End records with queue depth and duration. In polling mode, the default on some platforms, you get one Polling record per interval instead, which is less detailed (p. 126). The CSV export keeps the most recent over-threshold events, oldest first, so redirect it to a file instead of the screen. If you skip the low value, some platforms accept a single high threshold, so check what you typed.

Verify

leaf1# show interfaces Ethernet1 counters outgoing
Port          OutOctets    OutUcastPkts    OutMcastPkts    OutBcastPkts
Et1           418823311          301562            2210               0

Your hand-calculated rate is within a few percent of the rate EOS reports for the same window, and you can explain why neither number would show a 2 ms burst.

Real world. On hardware, the book builds congestion on purpose (pp. 124-125): a static route for one address pointing at each other on two directly connected switches makes a routing loop, shape rate on one side makes the link behave like a slower port, and iperf from bash pushes UDP into the loop until the TTL runs out. The shaped port buffers and LANZ records the event. Use that pattern only in an isolated lab, never on a production link. In production, stream LANZ to CloudVision rather than reading CSV files.
Exam tip. Expect to explain why averages hide microbursts and which tool shows them: LANZ for queue depth, streaming telemetry for fine-grained counters, sFlow for who is sending. Know that queue-monitor length is the global enable and the per-interface command takes the thresholds.

Break / fix

Scenario: on a hardware switch, LANZ is configured on Ethernet1 with thresholds, but show queue-monitor length Ethernet1 stays empty during a known congestion test.

Root cause and fix

Check show queue-monitor length status first. The usual causes are: the global queue-monitor length line is missing, so the per-interface thresholds do nothing; the high threshold is set above what the queue ever reaches; or the congested queue is on a different port than the one being monitored (traffic leaves on the shaped egress port, so that is where the buffer fills). Enable it globally, lower the threshold to the platform minimum shown by ?, and monitor the egress port.

Troubleshooting method

Use the same sequence every time. Under exam pressure it stops you from changing three things at once and losing track.

StepQuestionTypical commands
1. DefineWhat exactly fails, from where to where, since when? What changed?ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102, show logging last 30 minutes, CV change control history, show configuration sessions detail, show running-config diffs
2. ScopeOne host, one VLAN, one pod, everything? Which layer is common to all failures?Test other VLANs and VRFs, the other pod, the diag loopbacks (ping vrf TENANT-A 10.255.10.5 source 10.255.10.3)
3. Physical and healthLinks up, errors, optics, device healthy?show interfaces status, show interfaces counters errors, show interfaces transceiver, show processes top once, show logging
4. Layer 2 and MLAGPort-channels bundled, MLAG active and consistent, VLANs allowed?show port-channel summary, show mlag, show mlag config-sanity, show vlan, show spanning-tree blockedports
5. UnderlayBGP up, loopbacks and VTEP IPs reachable with ECMP?show ip bgp summary, show ip route 10.255.1.5, ping 10.255.1.5 source Loopback1
6. OverlayEVPN sessions, VNI mappings, flood lists, type-2/3/5 routes?show bgp evpn summary, show vxlan config-sanity, show vxlan vtep, show vxlan flood vtep, show bgp evpn route-type imet
7. ServicesGateway, ARP, VRF routes, external routes?show ip route vrf TENANT-A, show ip arp vrf TENANT-A, show ip virtual-router
8. Fix and verifyOne change at a time, in a session, then re-test the original symptom and run a health check.configure session, the original ping, ANTA or the 3.4 health check list
Exam tip. Write down every change you make (device, command, why). If a fix does not help, undo it before trying the next one. Graders check the end state; a leftover "test" change can cost you points on another task.

3.4 Troubleshooting: health, physical errors, captures, health checks

First build your health-check toolkit on a working fabric so you know what "good" looks like. Then fix three tickets that a partner (or the inject script) breaks without telling you which device.

  1. System and software health on leaf3: version, uptime, reload cause, CPU and memory top processes, agent uptimes and crashes, log errors in the last hour.

  2. Physical: interface status and errdisabled ports, error and discard counters on all leaves, and optics (note what cEOS returns for transceivers). Clear counters on leaf3 and confirm they stay clean while traffic runs.

  3. Captures: on leaf1 capture BGP keepalives on Ethernet1 with the EOS tcpdump command and with bash tcpdump; capture VXLAN (UDP 4789) on the uplinks while host1 pings host2 in VRF H10 and identify the outer source and destination VTEP IPs and the VNI.

  4. Throughput and MTU: check whether iperf3 is available on your cEOS (bash which iperf3); if it is, run a 10-second test between host1 and host2 VRF H10 addresses, otherwise use a large ping test. Ping from host1 to host2 with size 1400 and then size 1480 with the DF bit set, and explain the result using the underlay MTU (1500) and the 50-byte VXLAN overhead.

  5. CLI health check: write a short list of commands that proves the fabric is healthy and run it on leaf1. Then run ANTA with the AVD catalog for all six switches.

Verify

leaf3# show interfaces counters errors
Port         FCS      Align    Symbol    Rx      Runts   Giants     Tx
Et1            0          0         0     0          0        0      0
Et2            0          0         0     0          0        0      0
Et3            0          0         0     0          0        0      0
Et4            0          0         0     0          0        0      0
Et5            0          0         0     0          0        0      0
Et6            0          0         0     0          0        0      0
leaf1# tcpdump interface Ethernet1 filter udp port 4789 max-packets 4 verbose
11:41:02.118 IP 10.255.1.3.53211 > 10.255.1.5.4789: VXLAN, flags [I] (0x08), vni 10010
    IP 10.10.10.101 > 10.10.10.102: ICMP echo request, id 41, seq 12, length 80
11:41:02.121 IP 10.255.1.5.61044 > 10.255.1.3.4789: VXLAN, flags [I] (0x08), vni 10010
    IP 10.10.10.102 > 10.10.10.101: ICMP echo reply, id 41, seq 12, length 80
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 size 1480 df-bit repeat 2
PING 10.10.10.102 (10.10.10.102) 1480(1508) bytes of data.

--- 10.10.10.102 ping statistics ---
2 packets transmitted, 0 received, 100% packet loss

You can name every part of the VXLAN capture (outer VTEP IPs 10.255.1.3 and 10.255.1.5, UDP 4789, VNI 10010 for VLAN 10; routed traffic between VLAN 10 and 20 uses L3 VNI 50001). The 1400-byte ping works; the 1480-byte DF ping fails because 1508 bytes of IP plus 50 bytes of VXLAN encapsulation exceeds the 1500-byte underlay MTU. (In cEOS the kernel can behave differently from hardware, so the exact failure point may move; the reasoning is what matters.)

Solution · health, physical, captures 
leaf3# show version
leaf3# show uptime
leaf3# show reload cause
leaf3# show processes top once
leaf3# show agent uptime
leaf3# show system coredump
leaf3# show logging last 1 hours | include ERR|WARN|CRASH|%

leaf3# show interfaces status
leaf3# show interfaces status errdisabled
leaf3# show interfaces counters errors
leaf3# show interfaces counters discards
leaf3# show interfaces Ethernet1 | include error|CRC|rate
leaf3# show interfaces transceiver
! cEOS: no optics, so this is empty or shows not-present; on hardware read Tx/Rx power vs thresholds
leaf3# clear counters
leaf3# show interfaces counters errors

leaf1# tcpdump interface Ethernet1 filter tcp port 179 max-packets 5
leaf1# bash sudo tcpdump -ni et1 -c 5 tcp port 179
! in cEOS the kernel device may be named eth1 instead of et1: bash ip link | grep -E 'et|eth'
leaf1# bash sudo tcpdump -ni et1 -c 6 udp port 4789 -vv
leaf1# bash sudo tcpdump -ni et1 udp port 4789 -w /mnt/flash/vxlan.pcap -c 50
! copy the pcap off the box and open it in Wireshark

host1# bash which iperf3
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 size 1400 df-bit repeat 5
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 size 1480 df-bit repeat 5

Use tcpdump ? to see the EOS options on your release (interface, filter, max-packets, size, file). The EOS command can only see traffic that reaches the CPU on hardware switches (control plane and mirrored traffic); on cEOS everything is software forwarded, so you can also see transit VXLAN. iperf3 is not part of every EOS image; on hardware you would run it between servers, not switches.

Solution · CLI health check and ANTA 
leaf1# show mlag | include state|negotiation|peer-link
leaf1# show mlag config-sanity
leaf1# show port-channel summary
leaf1# show ip bgp summary
leaf1# show bgp evpn summary
leaf1# show vxlan config-sanity
leaf1# show vxlan vtep
leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A
leaf1# ping vrf TENANT-A 10.255.10.5 source 10.255.10.3 repeat 2
leaf1# show interfaces counters errors | nz

host$ anta nrfu -u admin -p admin --insecure -i anta-inventory.yml \
        -c labs/avd/intended/test_catalogs/leaf1-catalog.yml table
# repeat per device catalog, or merge catalogs; see "anta nrfu --help" for your version

Break / fix · three tickets

Have a partner apply one ticket at a time, or apply it yourself and wait ten minutes before you look. Save a checkpoint on all switches first. Use the method table, and record root cause, fix and verification for each.

Ticket 1 inject (leaf2): interface Loopback1, ip address 10.255.1.4/32.
Symptom: "Intermittent loss between host1 and host2, about half the flows."

Ticket 1 root cause and fix

leaf1 and leaf2 must share VTEP IP 10.255.1.3. leaf2 now sources VXLAN from 10.255.1.4, so remote VTEPs see two different VTEPs for the same MLAG hosts and replies via leaf2 go to the wrong place or are dropped. Find it with show vxlan config-sanity (MLAG VTEP IP mismatch), show mlag config-sanity, and on leaf3 show vxlan vtep showing an unexpected 10.255.1.4. Fix: interface Loopback1, ip address 10.255.1.3/32 on leaf2. Verify config-sanity is clean and a long ping from host1 has no loss.

Ticket 2 inject (spine2): router bgp 65100, neighbor 10.255.255.11 remote-as 65103.
Symptom: "CloudVision shows a BGP peer down event on leaf3; traffic still flows."

Ticket 2 root cause and fix

spine2 (10.255.255.10) peers with leaf3 at 10.255.255.11, and that neighbor now expects AS 65103 while leaf3 is AS 65102. show ip bgp summary on spine2 shows the leaf3 peer in Active or Idle; show logging | include BGP shows a bad peer AS notification. Fix: restore neighbor 10.255.255.11 remote-as 65102 on spine2. Traffic flowed through spine1 thanks to ECMP, which is why users did not notice. Verify both spines show leaf3 Established and show ip route 10.255.1.5 on leaf1 has two paths.

Ticket 3 inject (leaf4): interface Port-Channel5, switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30.
Symptom: "host2's DB server (VLAN 20) is slow and sometimes unreachable; APP is fine."

Ticket 3 root cause and fix

VLAN 20 is no longer allowed on leaf4's side of the MLAG port-channel, so host2's VLAN 20 frames hashed to the leaf4 link are dropped. show mlag config-sanity on leaf3/leaf4 flags the trunk allowed VLAN mismatch for Po5; show interfaces Port-Channel5 trunk confirms. Fix: switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30 on leaf4 Po5. Verify config-sanity, then ping vrf H20 10.20.20.101 from host2 with repeat 200.

Exam tip. Three commands find a large share of MLAG and VXLAN faults in seconds: show mlag config-sanity, show vxlan config-sanity and show ip bgp summary (plus show bgp evpn summary). Run them on both peers before you dig deeper.
Real world. Intermittent problems on MLAG pairs usually mean the two peers disagree on something (VTEP IP, allowed VLANs, VARP MAC, MTU), because hashing sends some flows to each peer. When "half the traffic" fails, compare the peers line by line: show running-config section on both, or the CloudVision config compare view.

Logs, CPU and agent tracing

This drill follows AW Ch 29 · Troubleshooting (pp. 156-161) and adds the tools objective 3.4 does not cover yet: following logs live, reading top properly, and tracing an EOS agent, which is how EOS does what other vendors call debugging. It runs on cEOS.

Key ideas from the chapter. Syslog lives in /var/log/messages (you need sudo to read it), and show logging follow is the CLI version of tail -f. Everything outside flash: is in memory and is lost on reboot; switches with an SSD archive logs to drive: (/mnt/drive) (p. 156). In top, the load average and the %Cpu(s) line matter most, and they are a snapshot, not history. High CPU hurts the control plane (BGP, STP), not forwarding, because the ASIC forwards packets (pp. 156, 158). EOS has only a small debug command; instead you trace agents, not protocols. Trace output goes to a file, never to the console, so it cannot lock up your session (p. 158). Facility names are case-sensitive and must match show trace exactly (p. 159). There is no "undebug all": each trace stays on, and keeps filling its file, until you turn it off agent by agent (p. 160).

  1. On leaf1, follow the log live while someone shuts and re-enables spine1 Ethernet1. Then read the same events from /var/log/messages in bash, and list what else is in /var/log.

  2. Take a show processes top once snapshot on leaf1. Start a CPU load for 30 seconds and take another. For both, write down the 1-minute load average and the us, sy and id values, and name the busiest process.

  3. List the agents on leaf1 and find the one that runs BGP. Explain why it is Bgp and not Rib in this fabric.

  4. Trace the BGP agent to flash:bgp-trace.txt at all levels for one facility group. Clear the session to spine1 (10.255.255.0) and watch it come back, first with trace monitor, then with tail -f filtered on the neighbor address.

  5. Turn the trace off completely: stop tracing, remove the file name from the config, delete the file, and prove nothing is left in the running config or on flash.

Solution · logs 
leaf1# show logging follow
! shut / no shut spine1 Ethernet1 now, then Ctrl-C
leaf1# bash sudo tail -n 20 /var/log/messages
leaf1# bash sudo grep -E "ADJCHANGE|LINEPROTO" /var/log/messages | tail
leaf1# bash ls /var/log
leaf1# dir drive:
! hardware with an SSD only; cEOS has no drive:

Without sudo, tail /var/log/messages fails with a permission error (p. 156). /var/log also holds eventMon.db (the event monitor database from the previous drill) and per-agent logs under /var/log/agents, which are worth a look when an agent keeps restarting.

Solution · reading top 
leaf1# show processes top once
leaf1# bash timeout 30 sh -c 'yes > /dev/null & yes > /dev/null & wait' &
leaf1# show processes top once
top - 13:20:11 up  2:41,  1 user,  load average: 1.62, 0.71, 0.33
%Cpu(s): 24.1 us,  1.2 sy,  0.0 ni, 74.3 id,  0.0 wa,  0.0 hi,  0.4 si,  0.0 st
  PID USER  PR NI   VIRT  RES  SHR S %CPU %MEM   TIME+ COMMAND
 8811 admin 20  0   2400  740  672 R 99.7  0.0 0:12.40 yes
 8812 admin 20  0   2400  740  672 R 99.7  0.0 0:12.38 yes

Representative output. The three load averages are 1, 5 and 15 minutes; what is normal depends on the platform, so learn your baseline (p. 156). In the %Cpu(s) line: us is user processes (mostly EOS agents), sy the kernel, ni niced processes, id idle, wa waiting for I/O, hi and si hardware and software interrupts, and st time taken by a hypervisor (pp. 156, 158). The list is sorted by %CPU. On a healthy switch idle is high; if a protocol agent stays near the top for minutes, that is a TAC case. Because cEOS shares the host's CPUs, the percentages here reflect your laptop as much as the switch.

Solution · tracing the BGP agent 
leaf1# show agent names | grep -i -E "bgp|rib"
leaf1# show running-config | include service routing
