! "service routing protocols model multi-agent": BGP runs in its own Bgp agent
leaf1(config)# debug ip ?
! only a few topics; tracing is the real tool

leaf1# show trace Bgp | head -40
! the facility list: copy the exact names, case and colons included
leaf1(config)# trace Bgp filename flash:bgp-trace.txt
leaf1(config)# trace Bgp enable <Facility>::* all
! replace <Facility> with a name from show trace Bgp; check levels with "enable <Facility>::* ?"
leaf1# show trace Bgp | include 0123456789
leaf1# clear ip bgp 10.255.255.0
leaf1(config)# trace monitor Bgp
! read the session coming up, then Ctrl-C (the CLI is busy until you do)
leaf1# bash tail -f /mnt/flash/bgp-trace.txt | grep 10.255.255.0
leaf1# dir flash:bgp-trace.txt

The book traces the Rib agent because its switches used the older routing model; with the multi-agent model (which AVD configures and EVPN needs) BGP has its own Bgp agent (pp. 158-159). Facility names differ between releases, which is why you copy them from show trace Bgp rather than from a guide; lower-case or all-caps versions are silently wrong (p. 159). After you enable a facility, show trace lists the active levels (0123456789cf for all). trace monitor cannot be piped to grep, which is why bash tail -f ... | grep is the more useful method (p. 160). Send traces to flash: (or drive:), never to a RAM path such as your home directory, where a large file can hurt the whole system (p. 160).

Solution · turning it off 
leaf1(config)# no trace Bgp enable * all
leaf1(config)# no trace Bgp filename flash:bgp-trace.txt
leaf1# delete flash:bgp-trace.txt
leaf1# show running-config | include ^trace
leaf1# dir flash: | include trace

Both checks should print nothing. Copy the file off first (copy flash:bgp-trace.txt scp:...) if TAC wants it (p. 160). A single trace left running for an hour can grow by tens of megabytes, and the book shows a switch with zero bytes free from forgotten traces (p. 160).

Exam tip. The exam grades the final device state. A trace or debug you forgot to remove is an unexpected difference in the running config, and on a long exam it can also fill flash. Clean up every diagnostic you add before you move on.
Real world. Most engineers only trace when TAC asks them to (p. 161), and TAC will tell you the agent, facility and level. Two other things from the chapter are worth knowing. If an SSH login says "Cannot connect to CLI. Entering standalone shell", the CLI could not reach Sysdb, usually because the switch is very busy (for example a configure replace removing a huge config). The standalone CLI sees no interfaces or agents. Don't panic: wait a minute or two and log in again (p. 161). And open a TAC case early with a show tech-support attached; Arista TAC is responsive and has the tools to read it.

Break / fix

Inject (leaf2): trace Bgp filename flash:old-trace.txt and trace Bgp enable * all, then clear a BGP session a few times.

Symptom: dir flash: shows free space shrinking, and a config compare in CloudVision (or a diff against the AVD intended config) shows unexpected lines on leaf2.

Root cause and fix

A trace was left running. Find it with show running-config | include ^trace, then no trace Bgp enable * all, no trace Bgp filename flash:old-trace.txt and delete flash:old-trace.txt. Check other agents too; each trace has to be removed on its own.

Checkpoint

  • On vEOS I can capture with tcpdump in Bash and in EOS, explain why a switch capture misses transit traffic, mirror to the CPU, and filter and truncate the session.
  • I can schedule a command, find its output files, and adjust the tech-support schedule.
  • I can query the event monitor for MAC, ARP and route history, including with SQL.
  • I can write on-intf, on-logging, on-boot and on-counters event handlers whose actions use FastCli, and prove they fired.
  • I can build a CloudVision dashboard, find and acknowledge events, check compliance and locate an endpoint (or do the CLI equivalents).
  • I can configure SNMP v2c with an ACL and SNMPv3 authPriv, and sFlow, and test both from a collector.
  • I can mirror a port to the CPU and capture with tcpdump, and explain LANZ and TAP aggregation.
  • I can find a failed event handler action from the log and show event-handler, and back up the config on every write.
  • I can enable event monitor, prove a MAC move or route flap with match-mac and match-ip, and keep its history on flash.
  • I can follow the log live, read the %Cpu(s) line in top, trace the Bgp agent to a file, and remove every trace afterwards.
  • I can read a VXLAN capture and explain an MTU failure with the 50-byte overhead.
  • I follow the troubleshooting method and fixed all three tickets with one change each.
  • All injected faults and test configs (event handlers on leaf2, mirror sessions, SNMP test changes) are removed or intended, and show running-config diffs shows only what I mean to keep.

Domain 4 · Engineering exam

Lab D4 · Layer 2 and Layer 3 designs

You build a complete Layer 2 leaf-spine (L2LS) from the day-0 configs: VLANs, MSTP with guards, three MLAG domains, MLAG-to-MLAG port-channels and VARP gateways on the spines. You break it the way real networks break, then tear it down and lay the routed foundation for the Layer 3 leaf-spine (L3LS) that every later lab uses.

Objectives
4.1, 4.2, 4.3
Time
3.5 to 4 h
Start state
Day-0 startup configs (management, admin/admin, eAPI, LLDP, spanning-tree mode mstp). Hosts and core1 are pre-built.
Devices
spine1, spine2, leaf1 to leaf4, host1, host2

Read first: AW Ch 2 · Network Designs · AW Ch 11 · LLDP · AW Ch 17 · MSTP · AW Ch 18 · MLAG · AW Ch 19 · First-Hop Redundancy · ATD lab guides (Data Center: Layer 2 Leaf-Spine) · EOS User Manual

4.1 Before you start: save day 0 and prove the cabling

Everything in this lab is built by hand on top of the day-0 configs. At the end of 4.1 you will wipe the L2LS and start the L3LS from a clean slate, so first keep a copy of the day-0 config on each switch. Then use LLDP to confirm that the cabling matches the topology before you configure a single port. In the exam, a two-minute LLDP check prevents an hour of chasing a port-channel built on the wrong interfaces.

  1. On every fabric switch (spine1, spine2, leaf1 to leaf4), save the current running config to flash as day0.cfg. You will use it with configure replace in 4.2.

    spine1# copy running-config flash:day0.cfg
Copy completed successfully.
spine1# dir flash:day0.cfg

  2. Confirm the cabling with LLDP on spine1 and leaf1. Compare it with the cabling table: leafN Eth1 goes to spine1 EthN, leafN Eth2 goes to spine2 EthN, leaf peer links are Eth3-4, the spine pair uses Eth5-6, hosts are on leaf Eth5.

    spine1# show lldp neighbors

Verify

Outputs are representative and trimmed; your timers, MACs and counters will differ.

spine1# show lldp neighbors
Last table change time   : 0:04:12 ago
Number of table inserts  : 6
Number of table deletes  : 0
Number of table drops    : 0
Number of table age-outs : 0

Port          Neighbor Device ID       Neighbor Port ID    TTL
------------- ------------------------ ------------------- ---
Et1           leaf1                    Ethernet1           120
Et2           leaf2                    Ethernet1           120
Et3           leaf3                    Ethernet1           120
Et4           leaf4                    Ethernet1           120
Et5           spine2                   Ethernet5           120
Et6           spine2                   Ethernet6           120
Ma0           ...                      ...                 120

spine1 sees leaf1 to leaf4 on Eth1 to Eth4 (each on the leaf's Ethernet1) and spine2 on Eth5 and Eth6. leaf1 sees spine1 on Eth1, spine2 on Eth2, leaf2 on Eth3 and Eth4, and host1 on Eth5.

LLDP in more depth

This follows AW Ch 11 · LLDP (p. 54). LLDP (IEEE 802.1AB) is on by default on every EOS port, management included, sending every 30 seconds with a 120-second hold time; a new neighbor is logged as LLDP-5-NEIGHBOR_NEW with no configuration at all (p. 54). The Neighbor Device ID is the hostname plus the configured domain name, so a Cisco switch with no domain shows up as hostname.cisco.com (p. 54). Cisco switches listen to CDP and often do not run LLDP by default: an Arista switch sees the Cisco neighbor, but the Cisco switch does not see the Arista one until someone enters lldp run there, and even then it appears in show lldp neighbors, not in show cdp neighbors (p. 54). Arista's detail view is sorted by local interface and lists every port, including those with no neighbor (p. 54).

  1. On leaf1, read show lldp (timers, enabled TLVs, per-port Tx and Rx), then show lldp neighbors Ethernet1 and show lldp neighbors Ethernet1 detail. From the detail, write down spine1's management address, system description (EOS version) and the port VLAN ID.

  2. Turn off LLDP transmit on leaf1 Ethernet3, wait two minutes, and check leaf2's table. Then turn it back on.

  3. Interop (if you have a Cisco IOS or NX-OS image in EVE-NG): connect it to a spare vEOS port. Check both sides before and after lldp run on the Cisco device, and compare show cdp neighbors with show lldp neighbors there.

Solution · LLDP detail and per-port control 
leaf1# show lldp
leaf1# show lldp neighbors Ethernet1
leaf1# show lldp neighbors Ethernet1 detail
leaf1# show lldp neighbors detail | include Interface|System Name|Management Address

leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet3
leaf1(config-if-Et3)# no lldp transmit
leaf2# show lldp neighbors
! after the 120 s hold time, leaf1 Et3 disappears from leaf2's table
leaf2# show lldp neighbors | include table
leaf1(config-if-Et3)# lldp transmit

! Cisco side, for the interop task
cisco(config)# lldp run
cisco# show lldp neighbors
cisco# show cdp neighbors

The table header counts inserts, deletes and age-outs, so a rising age-out count after a change shows a neighbor that stopped sending (p. 54). no lldp receive is the other half; no lldp run turns it off for the whole switch. The detail output also carries the Cisco IOS version string and the 802.3 MAC/PHY autonegotiation fields, which is a quick way to identify an unknown device on a port (p. 54). On the Cisco device, the Arista switch is visible only through LLDP, and you may discover other LLDP speakers (phones, small switches) at the same time (p. 54).

Real world. LLDP is what CloudVision's Topology view, the ZTP scripts that pick a config by upstream port (ZTP lab), and ANTA's cabling tests are built on, so leave it on everywhere inside the data center. Turn off transmit on ports facing untrusted networks, such as an ISP or a customer, where it would reveal your model and EOS version. In mixed Cisco and Arista networks, enable lldp run globally on the Cisco side so both teams see the same neighbor tables.
Exam tip. The exam topology page shows the intended cabling, but treat it as a claim to verify. show lldp neighbors on each switch is the fastest way to map ports, and it also proves the link is up at Layer 1 and Layer 2.

Design math: topologies, oversubscription and buffers

This section follows AW Ch 2 · Network Designs (pp. 16-17) and AW Ch 3 · Buffers (pp. 18-21). It is pen-and-paper work, about 30 minutes, that explains why this lab's fabric looks the way it does and why the "why is this link dropping" questions in D3 and D5 have the answers they do.

Key ideas from the chapters. The old three-tier core, distribution and access design was built for north-south traffic; data centers now carry mostly east-west traffic between servers, which changed the designs (p. 16). Bow tie MLAG is two MLAG pairs joined by one port-channel, a Layer 2 design (p. 16). Leaf-spine is a Clos fabric: every leaf connects to every spine, so any leaf is one spine hop from any other, the spine layer can lose switches and still forward, and with Layer 3 and ECMP all uplinks carry traffic at once (p. 16). How wide it can be depends on the ECMP limit of the hardware (the book cites 128-way on a 7500R, p. 16). A spline is a collapsed leaf-spine where servers plug straight into the spine pair, an end-of-row design for smaller networks (p. 17). A VXLAN overlay rides Layer 2 segments over the routed leaf-spine (p. 17), and a universal spine joins different pods and cloud connections under one spine layer (p. 17). On buffers: a switch fabric connects every input to every output; when two inputs send to one output at the same time, one must wait, and without a buffer the packet is dropped (pp. 18-19). Buffers trade drops for latency (p. 19). A microburst fills a buffer for milliseconds but disappears in one- and five-minute averages (pp. 19-20). Blocking means the fabric cannot carry every port at line rate (oversubscription), and a "480 Gbps" fabric figure may count both directions (p. 20). Head-of-line blocking is a packet at the front of an input queue, waiting for a busy output, holding up packets behind it bound for idle outputs; virtual output queuing (VOQ) fixes it by keeping a separate queue per output at each input (pp. 20-21). Deep buffers help in the data center, where speeds are equal or step down only a little; very large buffers in front of a slow access link (buffer bloat) hurt instead (p. 21).
Design (AW Ch 2)Where you build it in this guide
Bow tie MLAG (p. 16)Lab D4 L2LS: the spine MLAG pair connected to each leaf MLAG pair by one port-channel (4.1).
Layer 3 leaf-spine with ECMP (p. 16)Lab D5 L3LS underlay with eBGP (5.1).
Spline (p. 17)Not built; it is the L2LS with the leaves removed and hosts on the spines.
VXLAN overlay (p. 17)Lab D6 EVPN VXLAN (6.2).
Universal spine (p. 17)Not built; core1 and the border leaves are a small version of the external connection.

  1. Oversubscription. A leaf has 48 ports of 25 Gbps toward servers and 6 ports of 100 Gbps toward the spines. What is its oversubscription ratio? How many spines can it use, and how many uplinks would make it non-blocking?

  2. Spec-sheet reading. A 48-port 10 Gbps switch claims a "480 Gbps" fabric. Is it non-blocking? What if the figure were "960 Gbps"?

  3. ECMP in this lab. On leaf1 (after Lab D5), show how many paths it has to leaf3's loopback and which interfaces they use. If a third spine were added, what would change in the routing table and in the capacity per leaf?

  4. Incast. Ten servers each send 5 Gbps at the same moment to one storage array on a 10 Gbps port. How much traffic must be buffered or dropped each millisecond? How long until a 12 MB buffer for that port is full?

  5. Averages. A 10 Gbps link carries a steady 1 Gbps, plus a 100 ms burst at line rate once a minute. What do a one-minute and a five-minute average show? What does the port's buffer see during each burst?

  6. Buffer drain. How long does a full 24 MB buffer take to drain onto a 10 Gbps link, and onto a 50 Mbps link? Which one is a problem, and why?

  7. HOL and VOQ. Input port 1 has a packet for output 3 (busy) at the front of its queue and packets for output 2 (idle) behind it. What happens with a single input FIFO, and what happens with VOQ?

Solution · the numbers
  1. Down 48 × 25 = 1,200 Gbps, up 6 × 100 = 600 Gbps, so 2:1. Six uplinks means up to six spines (one link each). Non-blocking needs 12 × 100 Gbps of uplink.
  2. 48 × 10 Gbps is 480 Gbps in each direction, 960 Gbps counting both. If "480 Gbps" counts both directions, only 24 ports can run at line rate in and out at once, so it is 2:1 blocking; "960 Gbps" (full duplex) would be non-blocking (p. 20). Read the spec as packets per second and per direction, and ask.
  3. show ip route 10.255.0.5 (leaf3's Loopback0 in this lab, check yours) lists two next hops, one per spine, through Ethernet1 and Ethernet2. A third spine adds a third ECMP next hop and one more uplink, so capacity per leaf grows by half and losing one spine costs a third of the capacity instead of half (p. 16).
  4. Offered 50 Gbps, delivered 10 Gbps, so 40 Gbps is in excess: 40 Mb, which is 5 MB, every millisecond. 12 MB is full in about 2.4 ms, after which packets drop (p. 20). This is the storage case the chapter warns about (pp. 19-20).
  5. Each burst adds 100 ms × 9 Gbps of extra data, about 0.9 Gb a minute, or about 15 Mbps on average. Both averages show about 1.015 Gbps, a calm link (pp. 19-20). During each burst the link runs at 100 percent for 100 ms, and if other ports send to the same output at the same time the buffer fills. Only LANZ, fine-grained streaming telemetry or drop counters show it (Lab D3 LANZ drill).
  6. 24 MB = 192 Mb. At 10 Gbps that is 19.2 ms; at 50 Mbps it is 3.84 s (p. 21). Seconds of queueing confuse TCP and make interactive traffic unusable, which is why deep buffers suit the data center and not a slow access link (p. 21).
  7. With one FIFO, the packets for idle output 2 wait behind the blocked packet: head-of-line blocking (p. 20). With VOQ, each input has a queue per output, so the packets for output 2 leave at once and only the queue for output 3 waits (pp. 20-21).
Exam tip. Expect a short calculation or a "which design" question: oversubscription is downlink bandwidth over uplink bandwidth; leaf-spine gives equal-cost paths and one spine hop between any two leaves; averages hide microbursts, and LANZ shows them.
Real world. Size uplinks from the traffic pattern, not from a rule of thumb: 3:1 is common for general compute, storage and AI clusters often need 1:1. Plan the spine count for failure: with two spines, losing one halves capacity. Put storage and other incast-heavy traffic on deep-buffer platforms, and watch drop counters and LANZ rather than five-minute graphs (p. 21).

4.1 L2LS part 1: VLANs, trunks, LACP and three MLAG domains

The L2LS design in this lab is the classic Arista two-tier MLAG design. The two spines form one MLAG domain (SPINES) and act as the Layer 2 and Layer 3 core. Each leaf pair forms its own MLAG domain (POD_A, POD_B). Each leaf pair connects to the spine pair with a single logical port-channel that is an MLAG on both ends (MLAG-to-MLAG, the "bow-tie" design, AW Ch 18, p. 96). Each pair keeps its own domain ID; the pairs only ever see each other as one LACP partner. Because every link between tiers is part of one logical LAG, spanning tree sees no loop and nothing is blocked; STP stays on as a safety net.

DeviceMLAG domainPeer linkVlan4094 (local)peer-addressPort-channels toward the other tierHost port-channel
spine1SPINESPo10 = Eth5-610.0.0.1/3010.0.0.2Po11 = Eth1-2 (mlag 11, POD_A); Po12 = Eth3-4 (mlag 12, POD_B)-
spine2SPINESPo10 = Eth5-610.0.0.2/3010.0.0.1Po11 = Eth1-2 (mlag 11); Po12 = Eth3-4 (mlag 12)-
leaf1POD_APo10 = Eth3-410.255.252.0/3110.255.252.1Po1 = Eth1-2 (mlag 1)Po5 = Eth5 (mlag 5)
leaf2POD_APo10 = Eth3-410.255.252.1/3110.255.252.0Po1 = Eth1-2 (mlag 1)Po5 = Eth5 (mlag 5)
leaf3POD_BPo10 = Eth3-410.255.252.4/3110.255.252.5Po1 = Eth1-2 (mlag 1)Po5 = Eth5 (mlag 5)
leaf4POD_BPo10 = Eth3-410.255.252.5/3110.255.252.4Po1 = Eth1-2 (mlag 1)Po5 = Eth5 (mlag 5)

Why spine Eth1-2 form POD_A: spine1 Eth1 goes to leaf1 and spine1 Eth2 goes to leaf2. On the spine pair, Po11 is therefore four links (spine1 Eth1-2 plus spine2 Eth1-2) that land on leaf1 Eth1-2 and leaf2 Eth1-2, which is exactly the leaf pair's Po1. Both ends see one LACP partner system ID (the peer pair's MLAG system ID), so LACP bundles all four links.

  1. On all six fabric switches, create VLAN 10 named APP, VLAN 20 named DB and VLAN 30 named L2_STRETCH. Create VLAN 4094 named MLAG for MLAG peering, restricted to trunk group MLAG so that it never leaks onto uplinks, and exclude it from spanning tree.

  2. Build the SPINES MLAG domain. Bundle spine Eth5-6 into Port-Channel10 with LACP active, make it a trunk that carries trunk group MLAG, give Vlan4094 the addresses in the table with no autostate, and set reload-delay mlag 300 and reload-delay non-mlag 330. The domain-id is case-sensitive and must match on both peers (AW Ch 18, p. 90).

  3. Build the POD_A and POD_B MLAG domains the same way on leaf Eth3-4, using the values in the table.

  4. Build the MLAG-to-MLAG uplinks. On each leaf, Eth1-2 form Port-Channel1 (LACP active), a trunk allowing VLANs 10, 20, 30, as mlag 1 on both peers (AW Ch 18, p. 92). On the spines, Po11 (Eth1-2, mlag 11) faces POD_A and Po12 (Eth3-4, mlag 12) faces POD_B, with the same allowed VLANs.

  5. Configure the host-facing port: leaf Eth5 joins Port-Channel5 (LACP active), a trunk allowing VLANs 10, 20, 30, as mlag 5. host1 and host2 are already configured with Po1 in LACP active mode.

  6. Enable ip routing on the spines only (they become the gateways in a later part). The leaves stay pure Layer 2.

Solution · spine1 (spine2 follows the pattern; see table) 
spine1(config)# vlan 10
spine1(config-vlan-10)# name APP
spine1(config-vlan-10)# vlan 20
spine1(config-vlan-20)# name DB
spine1(config-vlan-20)# vlan 30
spine1(config-vlan-30)# name L2_STRETCH
spine1(config-vlan-30)# vlan 4094
spine1(config-vlan-4094)# name MLAG
spine1(config-vlan-4094)# trunk group MLAG
spine1(config-vlan-4094)# exit
spine1(config)# no spanning-tree vlan-id 4094
spine1(config)# interface Port-Channel10
spine1(config-if-Po10)# description MLAG_spine2_Port-Channel10
spine1(config-if-Po10)# switchport mode trunk
spine1(config-if-Po10)# switchport trunk group MLAG
spine1(config-if-Po10)# interface Ethernet5-6
spine1(config-if-Et5-6)# description MLAG_spine2
spine1(config-if-Et5-6)# channel-group 10 mode active
spine1(config-if-Et5-6)# interface Vlan4094
spine1(config-if-Vl4094)# description MLAG
spine1(config-if-Vl4094)# no autostate
spine1(config-if-Vl4094)# ip address 10.0.0.1/30
spine1(config-if-Vl4094)# mlag configuration
spine1(config-mlag)# domain-id SPINES
spine1(config-mlag)# local-interface Vlan4094
spine1(config-mlag)# peer-address 10.0.0.2
spine1(config-mlag)# peer-link Port-Channel10
spine1(config-mlag)# reload-delay mlag 300
spine1(config-mlag)# reload-delay non-mlag 330
spine1(config-mlag)# exit
spine1(config)# interface Port-Channel11
spine1(config-if-Po11)# description POD_A_leaf1_leaf2
spine1(config-if-Po11)# switchport mode trunk
spine1(config-if-Po11)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30
spine1(config-if-Po11)# mlag 11
spine1(config-if-Po11)# interface Ethernet1-2
spine1(config-if-Et1-2)# channel-group 11 mode active
spine1(config-if-Et1-2)# interface Port-Channel12
spine1(config-if-Po12)# description POD_B_leaf3_leaf4
spine1(config-if-Po12)# switchport mode trunk
spine1(config-if-Po12)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30
spine1(config-if-Po12)# mlag 12
spine1(config-if-Po12)# interface Ethernet3-4
spine1(config-if-Et3-4)# channel-group 12 mode active
spine1(config-if-Et3-4)# exit
spine1(config)# ip routing

spine2 is identical except ip address 10.0.0.2/30 on Vlan4094 and peer-address 10.0.0.1.

leaf1(config-if-Et5)# description SERVER_host1_Ethernet1
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# channel-group 5 mode active

leaf2 differs only in ip address 10.255.252.1/31, peer-address 10.255.252.0 and the descriptions. leaf3 and leaf4 use domain-id POD_B, 10.255.252.4/31 and 10.255.252.5/31, and host2 in the descriptions. A single-homed access port would be switchport mode access plus switchport access vlan 10; our hosts are VLAN-tagged trunks, which is typical for hypervisors.

Verify

leaf1# show mlag

leaf1# show mlag interfaces
spine1# show mlag interfaces
                                                                 local/remote
   mlag       desc                state       local       remote          status
---------- ------------------- ----------- ----------- ------------ ------------
      1       UPLINK_SPINES     active-full        Po1          Po1           up/up
      5       SERVER_host1      active-full        Po5          Po5           up/up

   mlag       desc                state       local       remote          status
---------- ------------------- ----------- ----------- ------------ ------------
     11       POD_A_leaf1_leaf2 active-full       Po11         Po11           up/up
     12       POD_B_leaf3_leaf4 active-full       Po12         Po12           up/up
leaf1# show lacp neighbor
leaf1# show interfaces trunk

All three domains show state Active, negotiation status Connected, peer-config consistent. Every MLAG port is active-full (up on both peers). In show lacp neighbor, leaf1 Eth1 and Eth2 see the same partner system ID (the SPINES MLAG system ID) even though they connect to two different switches. show interfaces trunk shows VLANs 10, 20, 30 on Po1 and Po5 and VLAN 4094 only on Po10. The downstream device only speaks LACP: without MLAG it would see two different partner system IDs and leave one link out of the bundle; with MLAG it sees one (AW Ch 18, p. 91).

leaf1# show mlag detail | include State|System|Primary
leaf2# show mlag config-sanity all

Note which peer of each pair is primary. The peer with the lower system MAC wins the election, and the MLAG system ID is built from the primary's MAC with the locally administered bit set (hence 02:1c:73...). The system ID does not change when the primary changes, only when MLAG is reconfigured on both peers, so LACP partners never see a new system ID during a failover (AW Ch 18, p. 93). config-sanity all also lists the settings that match, which is useful when you want proof rather than silence.

Exam tip. active-partial means the MLAG is up on only one peer: check the member links and the MLAG ID on the other switch. The MLAG number must match on both peers, but it does not have to match the port-channel number (spine Po11 is mlag 11 here, but it could be any ID 1 to 2000 as long as both spines agree).
Real world. Keep the MLAG ID equal to the port-channel number wherever you can. It makes show mlag interfaces readable at 2 a.m. and is what AVD generates. The peer-link VLAN is usually 4094 with a trunk group so that the SVI can never be extended to another switch by an accidental switchport trunk allowed vlan all.
Watch out. Forgetting no autostate on Vlan4094 is a classic: if the peer link flaps, the SVI can go down with it and MLAG takes longer to recover. Forgetting switchport trunk group MLAG on one side of the peer link stops VLAN 4094 from crossing, and MLAG never forms (you will break this deliberately below).

4.1 L2LS part 2: MSTP and STP enhancements

With MLAG everywhere, STP blocks nothing in the steady state. It still matters: it protects against cabling mistakes and rogue switches, and the exam expects you to place the root deliberately and apply the right guard on the right port. An MLAG pair runs STP as one logical bridge using the MLAG system ID, so both peers must carry the same STP settings. Only the primary's STP configuration is in effect: STP changes on the secondary are accepted silently and only take effect when it becomes primary, and config-sanity does not flag an STP priority difference (AW Ch 18, p. 97-98). You will exploit that trap in the MLAG troubleshooting part.

  1. On all six switches, configure the MST region: name L5DC, revision 1, instance 1 maps VLANs 10, 20 and 30. Everything else stays in the CIST (instance 0).

  2. Make the SPINES MLAG pair the root for instance 0 and instance 1 with priority 4096 on both spines. Give the leaves priority 16384 for both instances so that they are the next choice.

  3. On the spine downlinks Po11 and Po12, enable root guard so that no leaf pod can ever become root.

  4. On the leaf host port-channel Po5, make the port an edge port (portfast) and enable BPDU guard.

  5. Watch what happens to Po5. host1 and host2 are EOS switches emulating servers and they run MSTP, so they send BPDUs. Explain the result, then make the hosts behave like servers by filtering BPDUs on their side (spanning-tree bpdufilter enable on host Port-Channel1; this is the only host change in the guide). Recover the error-disabled port.

Solution · spine1 (spine2 identical) 
spine1(config)# spanning-tree mst configuration
spine1(config-mst)# name L5DC
spine1(config-mst)# revision 1
spine1(config-mst)# instance 1 vlan 10,20,30
spine1(config-mst)# exit
spine1(config)# spanning-tree mst 0 priority 4096
spine1(config)# spanning-tree mst 1 priority 4096
spine1(config)# interface Port-Channel11
spine1(config-if-Po11)# spanning-tree guard root
spine1(config-if-Po11)# interface Port-Channel12
spine1(config-if-Po12)# spanning-tree guard root
Solution · leaf1 (leaf2 to leaf4 identical), host fix and recovery 
leaf1(config)# spanning-tree mst configuration
leaf1(config-mst)# name L5DC
leaf1(config-mst)# revision 1
leaf1(config-mst)# instance 1 vlan 10,20,30
leaf1(config-mst)# exit
leaf1(config)# spanning-tree mst 0 priority 16384
leaf1(config)# spanning-tree mst 1 priority 16384
leaf1(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf1(config-if-Po5)# spanning-tree portfast
leaf1(config-if-Po5)# spanning-tree bpduguard enable

! host1 sends a BPDU, BPDU guard error-disables the leaf port
leaf1# show interfaces status errdisabled

! make host1 (and host2) stop sending BPDUs
host1(config)# interface Port-Channel1
host1(config-if-Po1)# spanning-tree bpdufilter enable

! recover the leaf port (on leaf1 and leaf2)
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet5
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# shutdown
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# no shutdown

! optional: automatic recovery instead of shut/no shut
leaf1(config)# errdisable recovery cause bpduguard
leaf1(config)# errdisable recovery interval 60

BPDU guard does exactly what it should: an edge port that receives a BPDU is error-disabled, because a BPDU means a switch was plugged into a server port. Real servers do not send BPDUs, so the host-side filter makes host1 behave like one. Note that portfast on a port-channel applies to the logical interface; EOS does not need a separate "trunk" keyword for portfast on a trunk port.

Verify

leaf1# show spanning-tree mst 1
MST1
  Spanning tree enabled protocol mstp
  Root ID    Priority    4097
             Address     021c.73d4.e5f6
             Cost        1999 (Ext) 0 (Int)
             Port        1 (Port-Channel1)
  Bridge ID  Priority    16385  (priority 16384 sys-id-ext 1)
             Address     021c.73a1.b2c3

Interface        Role       State      Cost      Prio.Nbr Type
---------------- ---------- ---------- --------- -------- --------------------
Po1              root       forwarding 1999      128.1    P2p
Po5              designated forwarding 1999      128.5    Edge, P2p
spine1# show spanning-tree mst configuration
spine1# show spanning-tree mst 1
spine2# show spanning-tree mst 1

The root bridge address on every switch is the SPINES MLAG system ID (it starts with 02:1c:73 because MLAG builds it from a locally administered MAC), with priority 4097 for MST1 (4096 plus the instance number). Both spines report themselves as root. Leaf Po1 is the root port and forwarding; there are no blocked ports anywhere. Po5 shows Edge. The region name, revision and VLAN map are identical on all six switches.

Exam tip. Know which guard goes where: BPDU guard on edge (server) ports, root guard on downlinks of the switches that must stay root, loop guard on non-designated ports where unidirectional links are a risk. A task such as "ensure no access switch can ever become root" is asking for root guard on the root's downlinks, not a priority change on the access switch.
Real world. Many teams enable BPDU guard globally on every edge port with spanning-tree edge-port bpduguard default and only configure portfast per port. Hypervisor hosts with virtual switches normally do not send BPDUs, but a misconfigured software bridge can, and BPDU guard is what saves the fabric.

4.1 L2LS part 3: SVIs and first-hop redundancy (VARP and VRRP)

In this L2LS the spines are the default gateways. You run the two Arista first-hop options side by side: VLAN 10 uses VARP and VLAN 20 uses VRRP. Then you compare how each one forwards traffic over MLAG. Page references are to AW Ch 19 · First-Hop Redundancy, which was written for EOS 4.21. The commands here are the current forms; check them with ? on your release.

VRRP basics (AW Ch 19, p. 99 and 101): the routers in a group form one virtual router with a virtual IP (VIP) and the virtual MAC 0000.5e00.01xx (xx is the group number in hex). One router is master and owns the VIP. Only the master sends advertisements, once per second by default, and the backups take over when advertisements stop for the master-down interval. Priority defaults to 100 and preemption is on by default. At equal priority there is no takeover: a router that joins a group which already has a master becomes backup. Every SVI needs its own real IP as well as the VIP, and ip routing must be on, otherwise show vrrp shows nothing.

VARP basics (AW Ch 19, p. 103-104): every switch in the pair owns the same VIP and answers ARP for it with one shared virtual MAC. Each switch routes whatever it receives, so VARP is active-active, and there is no protocol, election or timer. VARP works only on SVIs, not routed ports. It needs the global ip virtual-router mac-address plus ip virtual-router address on each SVI, and one VLAN can carry several VIPs. A VIP is never used as a source address. Pings and traceroutes from the switch, and routing protocol packets, use the SVI's real IP.

SVIspine1spine2Gateway (VIP)Method
Vlan1010.10.10.2/2410.10.10.3/2410.10.10.1VARP, virtual MAC 00:1c:73:00:dc:01
Vlan2010.20.20.2/2410.20.20.3/2410.20.20.1VRRP group 20 for now (spine1 priority 110, spine2 100), then converted to VARP at the end
Vlan30No SVI: VLAN 30 is Layer 2 only

  1. On both spines, set the global virtual router MAC to 00:1c:73:00:dc:01. Create Vlan10 with the real address from the table and VARP gateway 10.10.10.1.

  2. On both spines, create Vlan20 with its real address and VRRP group 20 for 10.20.20.1. Configure spine2 first and leave it at the default priority. Then configure spine1 with priority-level 110. Give the group a session description. Set a preemption delay of 30 seconds, and 60 seconds after a reload, so that a recovering spine does not take mastership back before it is ready. Look at show vrrp after each step. Note which spine was master before spine1's priority was raised, and why (AW Ch 19, p. 101).

  3. Try two things EOS should refuse or treat specially. Configure the VIP equal to spine1's own Vlan20 address 10.20.20.2 and read the error. Then add 10.20.20.254 as a secondary VIP in group 20, check it, and remove it (AW Ch 19, p. 102).

  4. Preemption test: shut spine1 Vlan20 and confirm that spine2 becomes master. Then no shutdown and confirm that spine1 takes mastership back after the 30-second preemption delay (AW Ch 19, p. 102).

  5. Object tracking: spine1 must give up mastership of group 20 when its POD_A-facing port-channel (Po11) goes down. Create track object POD_A on Po11 line protocol and decrement spine1's priority by 20 when it fails. Test it by shutting spine1 Eth1-2: spine1 drops to 90, and spine2 (100) preempts. Restore Eth1-2 (AW Ch 19, p. 103).

  6. From host1, verify the gateways, a same-subnet host across the fabric, a routed destination and the Layer 2 only VLAN. Compare the gateway MAC in VRF H10 with VRF H20.

    host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.1
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102
host1# ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102
host1# ping vrf H20 10.20.20.1
host1# ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102
host1# show arp vrf H10
host1# show arp vrf H20

  7. Traffic-path exercise. On both spines, clear the counters and record the peer-link (Po10) counters. From host1, send routed traffic through the VRRP gateway (ping vrf H20 10.10.10.102 repeat 2000), then through the VARP gateway (ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102 repeat 2000). Compare how much each test adds to the Po10 counters, and explain the difference (AW Ch 19, p. 103-104).

    spine1# clear counters
spine1# show interfaces Port-Channel10 counters
spine2# show interfaces Port-Channel10 counters

  8. Convert VLAN 20 to VARP so that both VLANs use the same design: remove VRRP group 20 and the track object, and add ip virtual-router address 10.20.20.1 on both spines.

Solution · spine1 (spine2 uses .3, no priority-level and no tracking) 
spine1(config)# ip routing
spine1(config)# ip virtual-router mac-address 00:1c:73:00:dc:01
spine1(config)# interface Vlan10
spine1(config-if-Vl10)# description APP
spine1(config-if-Vl10)# ip address 10.10.10.2/24
spine1(config-if-Vl10)# ip virtual-router address 10.10.10.1
spine1(config-if-Vl10)# exit
spine1(config)# track POD_A interface Port-Channel11 line-protocol
spine1(config)# interface Vlan20
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# description DB
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# ip address 10.20.20.2/24
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# vrrp 20 ipv4 10.20.20.1
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# vrrp 20 priority-level 110
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# vrrp 20 session description DB_GATEWAY
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# vrrp 20 preempt delay minimum 30 reload 60
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# vrrp 20 tracked-object POD_A decrement 20

! task 3 experiments
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# vrrp 20 ipv4 10.20.20.2
! rejected: the VIP cannot equal the interface's own address
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# vrrp 20 ipv4 10.20.20.254 secondary
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# no vrrp 20 ipv4 10.20.20.254 secondary

! final step: VLAN 20 back to VARP
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# no vrrp 20
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# ip virtual-router address 10.20.20.1
spine1(config-if-Vl20)# exit
spine1(config)# no track POD_A

Notes. The advertisement timer is vrrp 20 advertisement interval <seconds>. Keep the default of 1 second unless a scenario asks otherwise, and keep it equal on both routers. vrrp 20 disabled turns a group off without deleting it. no vrrp 20 preempt disables preemption. Instead of decrementing priority, a tracked object can shut the group (vrrp 20 tracked-object POD_A shutdown). The old forms vrrp 20 ip, vrrp 20 priority, vrrp 20 track and vrrp 20 shutdown are deprecated, but older releases and the book still show them.

Why spine2 was master first: it was configured first and became master alone. spine1 then joined at the same priority (100), and equal priority never takes over from an existing master. Raising spine1 to 110 with preemption on made it master.

Authentication (AW Ch 19, p. 102): VRRP can authenticate with plain text or IETF MD5. Plain text travels in clear in every advertisement, so it only guards against accidental misconfiguration, not against an attacker. Use MD5 if you need authentication, and check the exact keywords with vrrp 20 ? on your release.

Solution · traffic-path exercise

With VRRP, only the master (spine1) routes for VLAN 20. leaf1's Po1 hashes about half of host1's flows to spine2. spine2 is backup, so it switches those frames across the peer link Po10 to spine1, which routes them. Po10 counters rise by roughly half the test traffic. With VARP, whichever spine receives a frame routes it locally, and Po10 hardly moves. This is the main reason VARP is the default gateway design on Arista MLAG pairs: no hairpin across the peer link, no wasted peer-link bandwidth, and no single router doing all the work (AW Ch 19, p. 103-104). Your exact numbers depend on the LAG hash and on any traffic that crosses the peer link anyway.

Verify

spine1# show vrrp
Vlan20 - Group 20
  VRF is default
  VRRP Version 2
  State is Master
  Virtual IPv4 address is 10.20.20.1
  Virtual MAC address is 0000.5e00.0114
  Mac Address Advertisement interval is 30s
  VRRP Advertisement interval is 1s
  Preemption is enabled
  Preemption delay is 30s
  Preemption reload delay is 60s
  Priority is 110
  Authentication is disabled
  Master Router is 10.20.20.2 (local), priority is 110
  Master Advertisement interval is 1s
  Skew time is 0.570s
  Master Down interval is 3.570s
spine1# show vrrp brief
spine1# show track brief
Interface   Vrf       Id   Ver  Pri  Time  State    VR IP Addresses
----------- --------- ---- ---- ---- ----- -------- ---------------
Vl20        default   20   2    110  0     Master   10.20.20.1

Tracked object    Type                Status
POD_A             interface           up
spine1# show ip virtual-router
Interface       Vrf           Virtual IP Address       Protocol       State
--------------- ------------- ------------------------ -------------- ------
Vl10            default       10.10.10.1               U              active
host1# show arp vrf H10
host1# show arp vrf H20
Address         Age (sec)  Hardware Addr   Interface
10.10.10.1        0:00:41  001c.7300.dc01  Vlan10, Port-Channel1

Address         Age (sec)  Hardware Addr   Interface
10.20.20.1        0:00:12  0000.5e00.0114  Vlan20, Port-Channel1

VARP: both spines show 10.10.10.1 as active, and host1 resolves it to the configured virtual MAC 001c.7300.dc01. VRRP: spine1 is Master with priority 110 and spine2 is Backup. host1 resolves 10.20.20.1 to 0000.5e00.0114 (group 20 = 0x14). While spine1 Eth1-2 are shut, show vrrp brief on spine1 shows priority 90 and state Backup, and show track brief shows POD_A down. All host pings succeed. After the final step, show ip virtual-router lists Vl10 and Vl20 as active on both spines and show vrrp is empty.

Exam tip. If a task says "both gateways must forward traffic" or "active-active first hop", the answer is VARP (or ip address virtual). If it says "spine1 must be the gateway unless its uplink fails", the answer is VRRP with priority, preemption and a tracked object. Watch the spelling of show track brief: a typo in the keyword is easy to miss and returns an error, or output you didn't intend (AW Ch 19, p. 103). A traceroute through either gateway shows the real SVI address (.2 or .3) of the spine that routed the packet, never the VIP.
Real world. There are two Arista anycast gateway styles. ip virtual-router address (VARP) needs a unique real IP on every SVI plus the VIP, which costs addresses but lets the switch source pings and routing sessions from the subnet. ip address virtual 10.10.10.1/24 makes the SVI carry only the shared address, with no unique IP. It is the standard for EVPN leaves (the AVD configs in Lab D6 use it), where dozens of leaves share a gateway and per-switch addresses would waste the subnet. VRRP is still the right tool when the gateways are not an MLAG pair, in multi-vendor pairs, or when a firewall cluster expects standard behavior.

Break / fix · VARP that looks configured but isn't

Inject on spine2 (after VLAN 20 is back on VARP): no ip virtual-router mac-address.

Symptom: show running-config interfaces Vlan10 on spine2 still shows ip virtual-router address 10.10.10.1. Yet show ip virtual-router on spine2 no longer lists the VIPs as active (or reports the virtual router as not configured). Some host flows have intermittent trouble: hosts whose ARP for 10.10.10.1 lands on spine2 get no answer. The effect depends on which spine the MLAG hash and ARP timing pick.

Root cause and fix

VARP needs both halves: the per-SVI VIP and the global virtual MAC. Without the global MAC, spine2 has no MAC to answer ARP for the VIP with, so its VARP is inactive while the interface config looks correct (AW Ch 19, p. 104). spine1 still answers ARP and still routes, so the fault is partial and easy to miss. Fix with ip virtual-router mac-address 00:1c:73:00:dc:01 on spine2. The MAC must be identical on both peers. Always check show ip virtual-router state on both switches, not just the running config.

Break / fix · VRRP group that never changes master

Inject (while VLAN 20 is still VRRP): on spine1 no vrrp 20 preempt, then shut and restore spine1 Vlan20.

Symptom: after spine1 Vlan20 comes back, spine2 stays master even though spine1 has priority 110.

Root cause and fix

Without preemption, a higher-priority router does not take over from an existing master. It waits until the master fails. Restore vrrp 20 preempt delay minimum 30 reload 60 on spine1 and spine1 takes over after 30 seconds. Preemption is on by default, so a "master will not move back" fault almost always means someone disabled it, or the priorities are equal (AW Ch 19, p. 101).

4.1 Troubleshooting STP

Each break/fix below is injected on a working L2LS. Inject it, observe the symptom using only show commands, find the root cause before you open the answer, then fix it and confirm the fabric is clean again.

Break / fix 1 · a pod tries to become root

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4: spanning-tree mst 0 priority 0 and spanning-tree mst 1 priority 0.

Symptom: host2 can no longer reach its gateway or host1. On spine1 and spine2, show spanning-tree mst 1 shows Po12 in a root-inconsistent state (blocking) and the spine log has a root guard message.

Root cause and fix

POD_B advertised a superior BPDU (priority 0 beats 4096) on Po12. Root guard on the spine downlink refuses to accept a root through that port and blocks it, which isolates POD_B but protects the rest of the fabric. That is the intended behavior: root guard turns a fabric-wide reconvergence into a contained outage. Fix by restoring spanning-tree mst 0 priority 16384 and spanning-tree mst 1 priority 16384 on leaf3 and leaf4. Root guard recovers automatically once superior BPDUs stop.

Break / fix 2 · MST region mismatch

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4: under spanning-tree mst configuration, set revision 2.

Symptom: traffic still flows, but on spine1 show spanning-tree mst 1 no longer lists Po12 as a normal MSTI port, and the port type shows it as a region boundary. show spanning-tree mst configuration on leaf3 shows a different revision than spine1.

Root cause and fix

Name, revision and VLAN-to-instance map must all match for two switches to be in the same MST region. A mismatch makes POD_B its own region; the link becomes a boundary and only the CIST runs across it. Here nothing breaks visibly because all VLANs follow the same topology, but per-instance load sharing and root placement are lost, and in bigger networks this causes surprising blocking. Fix by setting revision 1 on leaf3 and leaf4. Do not rely on config-sanity to catch STP differences between MLAG peers; compare show running-config section spanning-tree on both.

Break / fix 3 · a VLAN missing on one trunk

Inject on spine2: interface Port-Channel12, switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20.

Symptom: host1# ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102 fails intermittently or completely, while VLAN 10 and 20 work. show mlag config-sanity on spine1 reports an interface mismatch.

Root cause and fix

The MLAG port-channel Po12 carries VLAN 30 on spine1 but not on spine2. Flows that the LAG hashes toward spine2 have nowhere to go. Config-sanity compares the MLAG interface settings of the two peers and flags the allowed-VLAN difference. Fix with switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30 on spine2 Po12. In the exam, always compare allowed VLAN lists on both peers of an MLAG.

MST deep dive: regions, instances and the pruning trap

This section follows AW Ch 17 · Multiple Spanning Tree Protocol (pp. 79-87). In the L2LS, MLAG hides almost every loop, so MST has little to do and is hard to see. This part uses a separate three-switch triangle, like the book's, where MST really has to block a port. It runs next to the main lab: sudo containerlab deploy -t labs/topology/mst-triangle.clab.yml (sw1 to sw3 at 172.100.101.61 to .63, admin/admin). On EVE-NG, build the same triangle with three vEOS nodes and paste labs/topology/startup-mst/sw*.cfg.

Key ideas from the chapter. EOS defaults to MST (spanning-tree mode mstp; the other modes are rstp, rapid-pvst, backup and none, p. 79). MST is built on RSTP, so it converges fast, but instead of one tree per VLAN it runs one tree per instance, a group of VLANs (p. 79). Instance 0 (MST0) always exists, runs on every port, and holds every VLAN that is not mapped elsewhere (p. 80). The global spanning-tree root primary and spanning-tree priority commands only affect MST0; other instances use spanning-tree mst N root primary or spanning-tree mst N priority (pp. 80, 84). Two switches are in the same region only if the configuration name, revision and VLAN-to-instance map all match; otherwise the ports between them become P2p Boundary and only MST0 crosses them (pp. 84, 86). Changes in spanning-tree mst configuration take effect when you leave the mode (p. 83). Arista sends MST BPDUs only untagged on the native VLAN, as the standard says; some other vendors send them on every VLAN, so a PVST switch next to an Arista MST switch sees the MST root only on VLAN 1 (pp. 80-81).
TermMeaning (p. 86)
Instance, MSTIA group of VLANs sharing one spanning tree. MSTI means any instance other than 0. The book gives instance numbers 1 to 4096 and up to 16 instances per switch; check spanning-tree mst ? on your release.
RegionSwitches with the same name, revision and instance map. Inside, each instance has its own topology.
ISTThe internal spanning tree: instance 0 inside one region.
CSTThe common spanning tree that connects regions (and non-MST switches) to each other. Each region looks like one big switch to it.
CISTIST plus CST: the single MST0 tree across the whole network.
Boundary portA port facing another region, or a switch running another STP mode (shown as P2p Boundary).

  1. Deploy the triangle. With defaults, find the MST0 root on each switch, the root port on the other two, and the one port that is discarding. Explain why that port, using bridge IDs.

  2. Make sw3 the MST0 root with spanning-tree root primary, check its priority, then set it to 4096 with spanning-tree priority. Which port blocks now? Check that sw2 can ping sw3 on 10.3.0.3 (VLAN 300) and 10.1.0.3 (VLAN 100).

  3. On sw1 only, create instance 1 with VLAN 100 and instance 2 with VLANs 200 and 300. Read show spanning-tree on all three switches and explain the P2p Boundary ports and who claims to be root for MST1 and MST2.

  4. Put the same instance map on sw2 but also give it the name Switch2. What changes? Then remove the name, give sw3 the same map, and confirm there are no boundary ports anywhere.

  5. Load sharing: make sw1 the root for MST1 and sw2 the root for MST2, keeping sw3 as the MST0 root. Show that each instance blocks a different link.

  6. Interop: change sw2 to rapid-pvst. On sw2, which root does it see for VLAN 1 and for VLAN 100? What do sw1 and sw3 show on their ports to sw2? Change sw2 back to mstp.

  7. BPDU guard: on sw1, make Ethernet3 an edge port with BPDU guard and bring it up. Explain what happens, and recover it.

Solution · defaults and moving the MST0 root 
sw1# show spanning-tree
sw2# show spanning-tree
sw3# show spanning-tree
sw3(config)# spanning-tree root primary
sw3# show spanning-tree | include Priority
sw3(config)# spanning-tree priority 4096
sw1# show spanning-tree
sw2# show spanning-tree
sw2# ping 10.3.0.3 repeat 2
sw2# ping 10.1.0.3 repeat 2

With all priorities at 32768, the lowest MAC address wins root. Each other switch has a root port toward it, and on the link between the two non-root switches, the port on the switch with the higher bridge ID is alternate discarding (p. 80). root primary sets 8192 almost at once, and priority 4096 sets it explicitly (p. 80); either way the blocked port moves to the sw1-sw2 link, because sw1 and sw2 now both reach sw3 directly. Every port says P2p with no boundary, because all three switches have the same (empty) MST configuration.

Solution · instances, regions and boundary ports 
sw1(config)# spanning-tree mst configuration
sw1(config-mst)# instance 1 vlan 100
sw1(config-mst)# instance 2 vlan 200,300
sw1(config-mst)# exit
sw1# show spanning-tree
sw1# show spanning-tree mst configuration
sw3# show spanning-tree

sw2(config)# spanning-tree mst configuration
sw2(config-mst)# instance 1 vlan 100
sw2(config-mst)# instance 2 vlan 200,300
sw2(config-mst)# name Switch2
sw2(config-mst)# exit
sw2# show spanning-tree

sw2(config)# spanning-tree mst configuration
sw2(config-mst)# no name Switch2
sw2(config-mst)# exit
sw3(config)# spanning-tree mst configuration
sw3(config-mst)# instance 1 vlan 100
sw3(config-mst)# instance 2 vlan 200,300
sw3(config-mst)# exit
sw1# show spanning-tree mst 1

Once sw1 has a different map, it is its own region. Its ports become P2p Boundary, it is the root of its own MST1 and MST2 (no other switch shares them), and MST0 still elects sw3 across the boundary (pp. 83-84). sw3's port to sw1 is also a boundary port, because the region ends there (p. 84). The book draws this as overlapping circles: MST1 and MST2 exist only on sw1, and MST0 holds two separate regions together (pp. 83-84). Adding the name on sw2 puts sw2 in a third region, so all of its ports are boundary ports even though its instance numbers match sw1's (p. 84). With the same map and no name on all three, every port is plain P2p: one region (p. 85).

Solution · per-instance roots 
sw1(config)# spanning-tree mst 1 root primary
sw2(config)# spanning-tree mst 2 priority 8192
sw1# show spanning-tree mst 1
sw2# show spanning-tree mst 2
sw3# show spanning-tree mst

MST0 blocks on the sw1-sw2 link (root sw3), MST1 on the sw2-sw3 link (root sw1), and MST2 on the sw1-sw3 link (root sw2), so every link forwards for some VLANs. The book's author prefers not to split traffic this way in a data center, where MLAG and VARP do the job better (p. 79), but it is the MST equivalent of PVST load sharing and a likely exam task.

Solution · rapid-pvst next to MST 
sw2(config)# spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst
sw2# show spanning-tree
sw1# show spanning-tree
sw2(config)# spanning-tree mode mstp

sw1 and sw3 show P2p Boundary on their ports to sw2. On sw2, VLAN 1 sees the MST region's root, because Arista MST sends its BPDUs untagged on the native VLAN, which PVST reads as VLAN 1 (pp. 80-81). For VLAN 100, the PVST BPDUs from sw2 pass through the MST switches without being processed, so the PVST switches elect their own roots across the MST region, as if tunneled (p. 81). A Cisco MST switch in the same position behaves differently, which is why replacing a Cisco MST core with Arista can move the root for many VLANs in a PVST network (p. 81). Pin the roots explicitly before any such migration.

Solution · BPDU guard on an edge port 
sw1(config)# interface Ethernet3
sw1(config-if-Et3)# spanning-tree portfast
sw1(config-if-Et3)# spanning-tree bpduguard enable
sw1(config-if-Et3)# no shutdown
sw1# show interfaces status errdisabled
sw1# show logging last 2 minutes | include BPDU|ERRDISABLE
! recover: remove the cause first, then bounce the port
sw1(config)# interface Ethernet3
sw1(config-if-Et3)# shutdown
sw1(config-if-Et3)# no spanning-tree bpduguard
sw1(config-if-Et3)# no spanning-tree portfast

sw2 sends BPDUs on its Ethernet3, so the first BPDU error-disables sw1's port. Without BPDU guard, portfast would have forwarded at once on a port that closes a loop. To bring a port back automatically after the cause is gone, use errdisable recovery cause bpduguard with a recovery interval. Leave Ethernet3 shut down when you finish.

Exam tip. If you see P2p Boundary between two switches that should be in one region, compare show spanning-tree mst configuration on both: name, revision and the full VLAN map, line by line. And remember which commands only touch MST0 (spanning-tree root, spanning-tree priority).
Real world. Keep MST simple (p. 87). For a small network, one instance (MST1) with every VLAN in one region is fine. Use separate regions on purpose, for example one per building or data center, so a bad link between sites only changes the CST and not every topology inside each site; the book describes three dark-fiber data centers that stopped reconverging once each became its own region (pp. 86-87). Give separate sites different instance numbers too, so that if they are ever connected by mistake they form boundary ports instead of silently joining one region (p. 87). Migrating from PVST to MST also moves STP work off the CPU: one set of BPDUs instead of one per VLAN (p. 80).

Break / fix 1 · region mismatch by VLAN map

Start from task 5 (one region, three roots). Inject on sw2: under spanning-tree mst configuration, instance 2 vlan 200 after no instance 2, so VLAN 300 falls back to MST0.

Symptom: sw2's ports and the ports facing it on sw1 and sw3 show P2p Boundary, sw2 claims to be root for MST1, and the load-sharing layout from task 5 is gone. Pings still work.

Root cause and fix

The instance map is part of the region definition, so one VLAN in a different instance makes sw2 its own region (pp. 84, 86). Spot it with show spanning-tree mst configuration on each switch (and show spanning-tree mst configuration digest if your release has it: the digest must match). Fix with instance 2 vlan 200,300 on sw2. Never edit the map on one switch at a time in production without expecting a short reconvergence; push the same map to every switch in the region together.

Break / fix 2 · the pruning trap

Reset to one region with VLANs 100 and 300 both in instance 1 (no instance 2, instance 1 vlan 100,300 on all three), sw3 the root of MST1 (spanning-tree mst 1 priority 4096 on sw3, and remove the other per-instance priorities). Inject: on sw2 Ethernet2 and sw3 Ethernet2, switchport trunk allowed vlan 100.

Symptom: sw2# ping 10.3.0.3 (VLAN 300) fails, while ping 10.1.0.3 (VLAN 100) works. No port is error-disabled, and nothing looks wrong at first glance.

Root cause and fix

MST does not care about VLANs, only instances (p. 87). The loop in MST1 is broken on the sw1-sw2 link, so sw2's only forwarding path to sw3 in MST1 is its direct link, which no longer carries VLAN 300. With PVST, VLAN 300 would have had no loop and no blocked port; with MST it shares VLAN 100's topology and is cut off (p. 87). Prove it with show spanning-tree mst 1 on sw1 and sw2 (Et2 is the root port on both, and one end of the sw1-sw2 link is alternate discarding) and show interfaces trunk (VLAN 300 not allowed on Et2). Fixes (p. 87): stop pruning VLAN 300 from that link (the simple, correct answer when all switches are in one region), or move VLAN 300 to its own instance and make sure that instance's blocked port lands on a link that does not carry VLAN 300 anyway. MST still runs every instance on every trunk regardless of allowed VLANs, so check where the new instance blocks with show spanning-tree mst 2 and steer it with root priority or port cost. Rule: in MST, keep allowed VLAN lists consistent on every link that an instance's tree can use.

4.1 Troubleshooting MLAG: config-sanity, failover, reload delay, dual-primary and upgrades

MLAG faults are the most common Layer 2 problems in an Arista data center, and they rarely look like MLAG faults: they look like intermittent loss for some hosts. This part follows the order you would use on a real pair: consistency, failover behavior, reload delay, split-brain protection, then the upgrade procedure. Page references are to AW Ch 18 · MLAG (written for EOS 4.21; the syntax here is current EOS).

  1. Run show mlag config-sanity on all three domains and confirm there are no global or interface inconsistencies. Make this the first command you run whenever an MLAG misbehaves (AW Ch 18, p. 92-93).

  2. Observe a primary failover on POD_A. Save the config on both leaves (write memory), identify the primary with show mlag detail, then reload the primary. On the secondary, watch show mlag during the reload and after the old primary returns. Record which switch is primary at the end and how long the returning switch keeps its ports down (AW Ch 18, p. 94).

    leaf2# show mlag detail | include State|Primary
leaf1# reload
leaf2# show mlag
leaf1# show mlag detail | grep -i err
leaf1# show interfaces status errdisabled

  3. Add dual-primary detection to POD_A. Use the management network as the heartbeat path (leaf1 peers to 172.100.100.22, leaf2 to 172.100.100.21, default VRF), detect after 10 seconds, and error-disable all interfaces on the switch that detects dual-primary. After the peer link recovers, keep MLAG interfaces down for 60 seconds and non-MLAG interfaces for 0 seconds (AW Ch 18, p. 94 and 96).

  4. Read the reload warnings. On leaf1, type reload and read every warning before you answer no. Then check whether STP can restart without disrupting forwarding (AW Ch 18, p. 97).

    leaf1# reload
leaf1# show spanning-tree bridge detail | include agent
Solution · failover observations

When the primary reloads, the secondary loses its peer and promotes itself; for a short time while the old primary boots, both may report primary. When the old primary comes back it does not take the role back: there is no preemption, so it becomes secondary. That is deliberate, because every role change is a disturbance. The returning switch keeps its MLAG ports error-disabled for reload-delay mlag (300 s) and its other non-peer-link ports for reload-delay non-mlag (330 s in this design). show mlag detail | grep -i err shows the remaining delay and the error-disabled ports. The MLAG system ID does not change, so host1 and the spines never see a new LACP partner.

Why the delays: the switch must relearn MACs, ARP and (in the L3LS) routes over the peer link before it attracts traffic, otherwise it blackholes flows hashed toward it. Non-MLAG ports wait longer so that routed uplinks do not attract traffic before MLAG state is ready. Lowering the timers to 60 and 90 in a lab saves time; keep the defaults in production unless you have measured convergence.

Solution · dual-primary detection on leaf1 (leaf2 uses heartbeat 172.100.100.21) 
leaf1(config)# mlag configuration
leaf1(config-mlag)# peer-address heartbeat 172.100.100.22
leaf1(config-mlag)# dual-primary detection delay 10 action errdisable all-interfaces
leaf1(config-mlag)# dual-primary recovery delay mlag 60 non-mlag 0

Management0 is in the default VRF in this lab. If management lived in a VRF, the command would be peer-address heartbeat 172.100.100.22 vrf MGMT.

Solution · reload warnings and STP restartability

Before a reload, EOS warns about conditions that would make the reload disruptive for an MLAG pair: MLAGs that are only active-partial (the peer cannot carry the traffic alone), a reload delay below the recommended value, error-disabled ports on the peer, and an STP agent that cannot restart gracefully. Read them; each one is a reason to stop. Never use reload now on an MLAG member during maintenance, because it skips exactly these checks. show spanning-tree bridge detail | include agent shows whether the STP agent is restartable.

Verify

leaf1# show mlag config-sanity
No global configuration inconsistencies found.

No per interface configuration inconsistencies found.
leaf1# show mlag detail | include dual|eload|state

Config-sanity is clean on all domains. After the failover, the former secondary is still primary (no preemption). show mlag on leaf1 and leaf2 shows dual-primary detection : Configured and the heartbeat peer address; show mlag detail shows the reload delay and dual-primary values you configured.

Break / fix 4 · config-sanity catches a mismatch

Inject on leaf2: interface Port-Channel5, switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20.

Symptom: host1 has intermittent loss in VLAN 30. show mlag interfaces still says active-full.

Root cause and fix

show mlag config-sanity lists Port-Channel5 with differing switchport trunk allowed vlan values on the two peers. MLAG still comes up because the LAG itself is fine; only the VLAN set differs. The same check catches an access VLAN configured on only one peer's port-channel (AW Ch 18, p. 92). Restore switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30 on leaf2 Po5. Lesson: active-full means the links are up, not that the configuration is consistent.

Break / fix 5 · the peer-link VLAN does not cross

Inject on leaf2: interface Port-Channel10, no switchport trunk group MLAG.

Symptom: show mlag on both leaves shows the peer link Up but negotiation status is not Connected and the state goes inactive or both claim primary. ping 10.255.252.1 from leaf1 fails.

Root cause and fix

VLAN 4094 belongs to trunk group MLAG, so it is only carried on trunks that are members of that group (AW Ch 18, p. 90). Without the trunk group on leaf2 Po10, VLAN 4094 does not cross the peer link, the peers cannot reach each other's local-interface addresses, and the MLAG control session never forms. Restore switchport trunk group MLAG. Debug order for any MLAG that will not form: peer link up, VLAN 4094 on the peer link (show interfaces trunk), ping the peer address, then compare domain-id (case-sensitive), local-interface and peer-address.

Break / fix 6 · peer link down, with and without a heartbeat

Inject on leaf2: interface Ethernet3-4, shutdown. Watch show mlag on both leaves for 15 seconds, then no shutdown. Repeat the experiment after removing the heartbeat and dual-primary lines on both leaves.

Symptom with detection: after about 10 seconds the switch that was secondary becomes primary, sees through the management heartbeat that the old primary is still alive, and shows dual-primary interface errdisabled : True; its non-peer-link ports are error-disabled. Symptom without detection: both leaves act as primary, both keep forwarding, and you may see LACP churn toward host1, MAC flapping and duplicate or lost traffic.

Root cause and fix

When the peer link fails, each peer loses the other on Vlan4094. Without a second path, neither can tell a dead peer from a dead peer link, so both become primary (split brain). The heartbeat over management gives that second path: the switch that sees its peer alive on the heartbeat but not on the peer link knows it is a dual-primary condition and shuts its ports, leaving one clean forwarding switch (AW Ch 18, p. 94 and 96). Fix with no shutdown on leaf2 Eth3-4. With dual-primary recovery delay mlag 60 non-mlag 0, the error-disabled ports return 60 seconds after the peer link is back. Restore the detection config at the end.

Break / fix 7 · the hidden STP priority on the secondary

Inject: find the SPINES secondary with show mlag detail. On that spine only, set spanning-tree mst 0 priority 32768 and spanning-tree mst 1 priority 32768. Check show mlag config-sanity and show spanning-tree mst 1 on a leaf: nothing changes. Now write memory on both spines and reload the SPINES primary.

Symptom: after the failover, both pods lose their gateway and cannot reach each other. On the spines, Po11 and Po12 are blocked by root guard. When the old primary returns, nothing recovers by itself.

Root cause and fix

An MLAG pair runs STP from the primary's configuration. The secondary accepted the new priority silently, and config-sanity does not compare STP priority, so the change was invisible until the secondary became primary (AW Ch 18, p. 97-98). At that moment the spine pair started advertising priority 32768, the leaf pairs (16384) became superior, they claimed root, and root guard on the spine downlinks blocked both pods. Because MLAG does not preempt, the old primary coming back does not restore the old priority. Fix: set spanning-tree mst 0 priority 4096 and spanning-tree mst 1 priority 4096 on the current primary as well as the other spine, so both are identical again. Lesson: keep STP configuration identical on MLAG peers and compare show running-config section spanning-tree on both, because no automatic check will.

Exam tip. When an exam scenario says "MLAG is broken", run in this order: show mlag, show mlag config-sanity, show interfaces trunk (is 4094 on the peer link?), ping the peer address, show running-config section mlag on both peers side by side. Most planted faults are a mismatched peer-address, domain-id, VLAN or trunk group. For STP, diff the spanning-tree sections yourself.
Real world. MLAG upgrade order (AW Ch 18, p. 89 and 96; Lab D1 objective 1.2 practices it): confirm the new image is MLAG ISSU compatible with the running one, check show mlag is Active and config-sanity is clean, resolve every reload warning, upgrade and reload the secondary, wait until it is back and all MLAGs are active-full again, then upgrade the primary, and confirm. Peers should run the same or ISSU-compatible EOS versions at all times. Build the peer link from at least two links on different ASICs, and always configure dual-primary detection.

Checkpoint · L2LS complete

  • Three MLAG domains (SPINES, POD_A, POD_B) are Active, Connected and consistent; all MLAG ports are active-full.
  • The SPINES pair is STP root for MST0 and MST1; nothing is blocked; host ports are Edge with BPDU guard; spine downlinks have root guard.
  • host1 reaches 10.10.10.1, 10.10.10.102, 10.20.20.102 and 10.30.30.102. The gateway MAC is 001c.7300.dc01.
  • You can explain VARP versus VRRP, and why MLAG needs dual-primary detection and reload delay.

4.2 From L2LS to L3LS: why, how, and the reset

The L2LS you built works, but it does not scale: every VLAN exists on every switch, the spines carry all MAC and ARP state, failure domains are as large as the VLAN, and the design is limited to two spines because MLAG pairs two switches. An L3LS routes between leaf and spine. Each link is a point-to-point /31, the underlay protocol provides equal-cost paths across any number of spines, and a failure is contained to one link. Layer 2 services that still need to stretch between racks ride on top as VXLAN, and EVPN is the control plane that tells VTEPs where MACs and IPs live (Domain 6).

QuestionL2LS (MLAG + STP)L3LS (routed underlay)
Spine countTwo (one MLAG pair)2 to many; ECMP across all
Failure domainThe whole VLAN, across all racksOne link or one leaf pair
Control planeSTP (safety net), MLAG, LACPBGP, OSPF or IS-IS; BFD optional
Gateway locationSpines (VARP)Leaves (anycast gateway with VXLAN/EVPN)
MAC/ARP scaleSpines learn everythingSpines learn only loopbacks and /31s
Good fitSmall sites, two-switch cores, simple VLAN stretchAny DC that must grow, multi-tenant, EVPN
Underlay optionStrengthsTrade-offsTypical choice when
eBGP (Arista reference, AVD default)One protocol for underlay and overlay; per-leaf policy; scales; clear failure isolation; ASN shows where a route came fromMore config per device (neighbors, ASNs, route maps); slower defaults unless tuned (BFD)Most new EVPN fabrics
OSPFFamiliar, minimal config, fast convergence, automatic neighbor discoveryOne flooding domain; policy is coarse; overlay still needs BGP for EVPNTeams with strong OSPF operations; smaller fabrics
IS-ISNot tied to IP, scales well, popular with service providers, simple single-level designLess familiar to enterprise teams; NET addressing to planLarge fabrics, SP-style operations

ASN scheme used in this lab (AVD style): all spines share one ASN (65100); each MLAG leaf pair shares an ASN (65101 for POD_A, 65102 for POD_B). Spines sharing an ASN means a leaf can never use a spine as transit to reach another spine (AS-path loop prevention), which avoids suboptimal paths. A leaf pair sharing an ASN means the pair looks like one routing node and needs an iBGP session across the peer link (objective 5.3).

  1. Reset the fabric. On all six fabric switches, restore the day-0 config with configure replace flash:day0.cfg. Then verify that no VLANs, port-channels or MLAG config remain.

    leaf1# configure replace flash:day0.cfg
leaf1# show running-config section mlag
leaf1# show vlan
leaf1# show port-channel

  2. Routed subinterface drill (4.1 "subinterfaces"). The spine1 to spine2 links are unused in the L3LS. On spine1 and spine2 Eth5, create subinterface Eth5.100 with 802.1Q tag 100, using 10.0.100.0/31 on spine1 and 10.0.100.1/31 on spine2. Ping across it, compare it with an SVI, then remove it (and the parent no switchport).

  3. Build the routed underlay links and loopbacks on all six switches exactly as the AVD reference: physical uplinks routed with mtu 1500, the /31 addresses below, Loopback0 as router ID, ip routing, and spanning-tree mode none on the spines.

  4. Rebuild the leaf MLAG pairs for the L3LS (the spines are no longer an MLAG pair). Same POD_A/POD_B domain, Po10 on Eth3-4 and Vlan4094 values as before, plus VLAN 4093 (MLAG_L3, trunk group MLAG) created now for use in 5.3, no spanning-tree vlan-id 4093-4094 and spanning-tree mst 0 priority 16384. Do not build the host port-channel or VLANs 10/20/30 yet; Domain 6 adds them.

DeviceLoopback0Eth1Eth2Eth3Eth4
spine110.255.0.1/3210.255.255.0/31 (leaf1)10.255.255.4/31 (leaf2)10.255.255.8/31 (leaf3)10.255.255.12/31 (leaf4)
spine210.255.0.2/3210.255.255.2/31 (leaf1)10.255.255.6/31 (leaf2)10.255.255.10/31 (leaf3)10.255.255.14/31 (leaf4)
leaf110.255.0.3/3210.255.255.1/31 (spine1)10.255.255.3/31 (spine2)Po10 MLAG peer link

spine1(config-if-Et5.100)# encapsulation dot1q vlan 100

spine1(config-if-Et5.100)# end
spine1# ping 10.0.100.1 repeat 2
spine1# show interfaces Ethernet5.100

! clean up
spine1(config)# no interface Ethernet5.100
spine1(config)# interface Ethernet5
spine1(config-if-Et5)# switchport

An SVI is a routed interface for a VLAN that exists on many switchports; a subinterface is a routed interface for one 802.1Q tag on one routed port. Subinterfaces are how you hand off several VRFs to a firewall or WAN router over one link without creating a Layer 2 domain.

----------------- -------------------- ---------- ------------ ---------- -------
Ethernet1         10.255.255.0/31      up         up              1500

Every spine can ping the leaf end of each /31 (no routing protocol is needed for a directly connected neighbor). ping 10.255.0.3 from spine1 fails: loopbacks are not reachable until you build the underlay in Lab D5. POD_A and POD_B are Active and Connected again.

Exam tip. The exam may give you a half-built fabric with a design document. Before configuring any routing, ping every /31 neighbor. A wrong mask, a swapped address or an interface still in switchport mode is far easier to find now than after BGP refuses to come up.
Real world. configure replace is the cleanest way to get a switch to a known state: it computes the difference and applies only that, without a reboot. In containerlab you can also redeploy the whole lab from the startup files with sudo containerlab deploy -t l5dc.clab.yml --reconfigure, which is the brute-force equivalent.

Break / fix · a /31 that will not ping

Inject on leaf2 Eth1: ip address 10.255.255.4/31 (the spine's address).

Symptom: spine1 cannot ping 10.255.255.5, and leaf2's log reports a duplicate address or ARP conflict.

Root cause and fix

Both ends claim 10.255.255.4. In this design the spine always takes the even (lower) address of each /31 and the leaf takes the odd one. Fix with ip address 10.255.255.5/31 on leaf2 Eth1. A documented, formulaic addressing scheme is what makes this error obvious at a glance.

4.3 Design and best practice: MTU, iBGP vs eBGP, validation habits

MTU and jumbo frames

VXLAN adds 50 bytes to every frame it carries: outer Ethernet 14, outer IPv4 20, UDP 8, VXLAN header 8 (54 if the outer frame is 802.1Q tagged). A host sending full 1500-byte IP packets therefore needs an underlay IP MTU of at least 1550. The production rule is simple: set the underlay links to the platform maximum (mtu 9214 on Arista) so that VXLAN never fragments and hosts can use jumbo frames (9000) if they want. Hosts usually stay at 1500 unless an application benefits from jumbo frames.

The AVD reference for this lab sets p2p_uplinks_mtu: 1500, so every routed link shows mtu 1500. That is a lab compromise, not a recommendation: cEOS-lab forwards in software over Linux veth pairs, jumbo support depends on the container host and the containerlab link settings, and a mismatched MTU silently breaks OSPF adjacencies and large transfers. Keeping 1500 everywhere keeps the lab predictable. The cost is that a full 1500-byte host packet cannot cross the VXLAN overlay in this lab; small pings work, large ones with the DF bit set do not. In the exam environment (vEOS) and in production, use 9214 on underlay links unless the scenario says otherwise.

iBGP versus eBGP in the fabric

DesignHow it worksWatch for
eBGP underlay, eBGP overlay (this lab, AVD default)Each tier has its own ASN; EVPN sessions between loopbacks with ebgp-multihop; spines use next-hop-unchanged for EVPNAS-path loop prevention inside a leaf pair (fixed by the MLAG iBGP session); spines must not rewrite EVPN next hops
IGP underlay (OSPF/IS-IS), iBGP overlayAll switches in one ASN; spines are EVPN route reflectorsRoute reflector config on spines; IGP must carry every loopback
eBGP underlay, iBGP overlayLess common; mixes both modelsTwo sets of ASNs to reason about; usually avoid

Validation habits

  • Layer 1 and 2 first: show interfaces status, show lldp neighbors, show port-channel.
  • Addressing: ping every directly connected /31 before enabling a protocol.
  • Protocol state: show ip bgp summary, show ip ospf neighbor, show isis neighbors, show mlag, show mlag config-sanity.
  • Forwarding: show ip route <loopback> must list every expected ECMP path; ping and traceroute loopback to loopback with source.
  • Change safety: use configuration sessions, show session-config diffs, and commit; keep a file or checkpoint you can configure replace back to.
  • At the end of an exam task: re-run the verification and write memory. Grading looks at the final device state.

Common design and operations failures

FailureSymptomPrevention
MTU mismatch on a linkOSPF stuck in EXSTART/EXCHANGE; BGP up but large updates stall; large pings failOne MTU per link type, set by template; test with ping ... size 1472 df-bit
Loopbacks not advertisedBGP Established but no routes; overlay sessions never come upVerify advertised-routes; prefix-list matches the loopback pools
Single path where ECMP was expectedOnly one next hop; half the uplink capacity idlemaximum-paths set to the number of spines or more
MLAG peers inconsistentIntermittent loss for some VLANs or hostsshow mlag config-sanity after every change
No dual-primary detectionSplit brain when the peer link failsHeartbeat over management plus dual-primary detection
Ping from the wrong source"Fabric broken" when it is not: the /31 source is not advertisedAlways ping loopback to loopback with source

  1. On leaf1, test the path MTU to spine1 with the DF bit set: send 1472 bytes of ICMP data (1472 + 8 ICMP + 20 IP = 1500) and then 1473 bytes. Explain both results.

    leaf1# ping 10.255.255.0 size 1472 df-bit repeat 2
leaf1# ping 10.255.255.0 size 1473 df-bit repeat 2

  2. Fill in the design worksheet below for this fabric before you open the answers. It is the same thinking the exam expects when it hands you a design brief.
