Worksheet answers
Design itemAnswer
Spine ASN65100 on both spines. A shared spine ASN means AS-path loop prevention stops a leaf from ever using one spine to reach another, and it keeps leaf configs uniform (every leaf peers to remote-as 65100).
Leaf ASN schemeOne ASN per MLAG pair: POD_A 65101, POD_B 65102. A single-homed leaf would get its own ASN.
Loopback0 pool10.255.0.0/27: spines .1-.2, leaves .3-.6. Router ID and EVPN peering source.
VTEP Loopback110.255.1.0/27: POD_A 10.255.1.3, POD_B 10.255.1.5. MLAG peers share one VTEP so that remote VTEPs see one logical VTEP and can hash to either peer (anycast VTEP).
P2P pool10.255.255.0/26. 2 spines x 4 leaves = 8 /31s (.0 to .15). The spine takes the even address.
MLAG VLANsVlan4094 peer: 10.255.252.0/31 (POD_A), .4/31 (POD_B). Vlan4093 iBGP: 10.255.251.0/31, .4/31. VRF TENANT-A iBGP on Vlan3001 reuses the 10.255.251.x addresses (Domain 6).
Router IDLoopback0 address, set explicitly with router-id.
maximum-paths4 (two spines today, room to add two more).
MTULab: 1500 (cEOS veth compromise, AVD p2p_uplinks_mtu: 1500). Production: 9214 on underlay links, at least 1550 to carry 1500-byte host packets in VXLAN.
Connected routes into BGPOnly loopbacks: redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP, matching prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY (10.255.0.0/27 eq 32 and, on leaves, 10.255.1.0/27 eq 32). The /31s and MLAG subnets stay out of the routing table of other switches.

Verify

leaf1# ping 10.255.255.0 size 1472 df-bit repeat 2
PING 10.255.255.0 (10.255.255.0) 1472(1500) bytes of data.
1480 bytes from 10.255.255.0: icmp_seq=1 ttl=64 time=0.9 ms
1480 bytes from 10.255.255.0: icmp_seq=2 ttl=64 time=0.7 ms

leaf1# ping 10.255.255.0 size 1473 df-bit repeat 2
PING 10.255.255.0 (10.255.255.0) 1473(1501) bytes of data.
ping: local error: message too long, mtu=1500

1472 bytes of data fits a 1500-byte IP MTU exactly; 1473 does not and, with DF set, cannot be fragmented. On a production underlay at 9214 you would test with size 9186.

Exam tip. Expect at least one task where the configuration is correct but something underneath it is not (MTU, a shut interface, a VLAN missing from a trunk). Verification habits score points: run the checks, do not assume.
Real world. Write the design worksheet before any config, keep it in version control with the AVD inputs, and generate configs from it. That is exactly what labs/avd/group_vars is in this repo: the worksheet as data.

Break / fix · MTU mismatch

Inject on spine1 Eth1: mtu 9214 (leaf1 Eth1 stays at 1500).

Symptom: the link is up and small pings work. From spine1, ping 10.255.255.1 size 2000 df-bit fails. Later, in Lab D5, OSPF on this link would stay stuck in EXSTART while BGP still comes up.

Root cause and fix

The two ends of one link disagree on MTU. IP interfaces do not negotiate MTU; OSPF checks it in its database description packets and refuses to progress, while BGP's TCP session negotiates an MSS that fits and hides the problem until large packets are dropped. Fix by matching both ends (mtu 1500 on spine1 Eth1 for this lab). Check with show interfaces Ethernet1 | include MTU on both sides.

Aristacisms: EOS habits that catch other-vendor engineers

This section follows AW Ch 33 · Aristacisms (pp. 188-193). The chapter collects EOS behaviors that differ from what a Cisco-trained engineer expects. Several of them make good exam traps because the config looks right at a glance. Three of them are tickets T11 to T13 in the C3 ticket queue.

BehaviorWhat EOS doesWhat to do
Speed and duplexThere is no duplex command. Pasted IOS lines like speed 1000 and duplex full are rejected (p. 188).Use speed forced 1000full (or speed auto); check speed ? and speed forced ? for what the port supports.
Comments in configEOS keeps comments inside the config, attached to the section they were typed in. A pasted line that loses its line break can turn the next interface line into a comment, silently merging two interfaces' settings (p. 188).Check what you pasted with show running-config interfaces. Avoid pasting big blocks over a slow console; use configure replace or a session instead.
Protocols start shut downSome routing processes (the book shows RIP) are created in a shutdown state (p. 188).Read the section with show active inside the router mode, then no shutdown.
Trunk groupsOnce a VLAN has a trunk group, it is carried only on trunks that list that group. switchport trunk allowed vlan is not enough, yet show interfaces trunk still lists the VLAN as allowed (pp. 188-190).Look at "allowed and active in management domain" in show interfaces trunk, and at show vlan trunk group.
VRF on an interfaceAssigning, changing or removing an interface's VRF deletes its IP address, with only a one-line notice (p. 190).Set the VRF first, then the address. After any VRF change, check show ip interface brief vrf all.
Routing per VRFEvery VRF needs its own ip routing vrf NAME; without it, show ip route vrf NAME ends with "IP routing not enabled" (p. 191).Add ip routing vrf NAME whenever you create a VRF that routes.
VRF keyword positionThe VRF goes right after the command: ping vrf MGMT 10.0.0.1, ip name-server vrf MGMT ..., not at the end as on IOS (p. 192).Build the habit; ? after the command shows where vrf goes.
Management services in one VRFSome services, such as DNS name servers, can live in only one VRF at a time. Adding one in a second VRF is refused until you remove the first (p. 192).Remove the old entries, then configure them with vrf NAME.

The book creates VRFs with vrf definition and an rd, and notes that route leaking was only just arriving (p. 190). Current EOS uses vrf instance NAME, needs no RD for a plain VRF, and supports leaking between VRFs. RDs and route targets matter only when the VRF is carried in BGP, as TENANT-A is in Lab D6.

  1. On leaf1 Ethernet5, try duplex full, then look at speed ? and speed forced ?. Do not apply a speed: this port carries host1's traffic. (cEOS only creates the Ethernet ports that are wired in the topology, so there is no spare port to experiment on.)

  2. Create VLANs 40 and 41 with trunk group LAB and allow them on host-facing Port-Channel5 with switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40-41. Show that they are listed as allowed but not active. Make them active the EOS way, then remove everything you added.

  3. Create VRF LAB and Loopback99 with 192.0.2.1/32. Then put the loopback in VRF LAB and note what happens to the address. Make ping vrf LAB 192.0.2.1 work, look at show ip route vrf LAB before and after enabling routing in the VRF, and clean up.

Solution · speed 
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet5
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# duplex full
% Invalid input
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# speed ?
leaf1(config-if-Et5)# speed forced ?
! on hardware: speed forced 1000full, and "no speed" to go back to the default

On hardware, speed forced turns off autonegotiation and fixes both speed and duplex in one command (p. 188). cEOS ports are software interfaces, so the list of offered speeds is short; the point is to learn the syntax.

Solution · trunk groups 
leaf1(config)# vlan 40-41
leaf1(config-vlan-40-41)# trunk group LAB
leaf1(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf1(config-if-Po5)# switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40-41
leaf1# show interfaces trunk
leaf1# show vlan 40-41
leaf1# show vlan trunk group

leaf1(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf1(config-if-Po5)# switchport trunk group LAB
leaf1# show vlan 40-41

! clean up
leaf1(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf1(config-if-Po5)# no switchport trunk group LAB
leaf1(config-if-Po5)# switchport trunk allowed vlan remove 40-41
leaf1(config)# no vlan 40-41

Before the trunk group is on the port, show interfaces trunk lists 40-41 under "Vlans allowed" but not under "Vlans allowed and active in management domain", and show vlan shows no ports for them (p. 190). That is exactly how the MLAG peer VLAN is kept off every trunk except the peer link (trunk group MLAG, Lab D4 and AW Ch 18). A VLAN can belong to several trunk groups (p. 190). Do not use trunk groups as a shortcut for a VLAN list; once a VLAN has a group, it can never be carried on a trunk without that group (p. 190).

Solution · VRF on an interface 
leaf1(config)# vrf instance LAB
leaf1(config)# interface Loopback99
leaf1(config-if-Lo99)# ip address 192.0.2.1/32
leaf1(config-if-Lo99)# vrf LAB
! Interface Loopback99 IP address 192.0.2.1 removed due to enabling VRF LAB
leaf1(config-if-Lo99)# ip address 192.0.2.1/32
leaf1# show ip route vrf LAB
! ... ! IP routing not enabled
leaf1(config)# ip routing vrf LAB
leaf1# show ip route vrf LAB
leaf1# ping vrf LAB 192.0.2.1

! clean up
leaf1(config)# no interface Loopback99
leaf1(config)# no ip routing vrf LAB
leaf1(config)# no vrf instance LAB

The notice appears once and is easy to miss in a pasted block (p. 190). The connected route shows up even before routing is enabled, but the VRF does not route until ip routing vrf LAB is present (p. 191). In this fabric, AVD always renders vrf before ip address on SVIs and adds ip routing vrf TENANT-A, which is one reason generated configs avoid these traps.

Exam tip. When a VLAN "should" be on a trunk and is not, check three things in this order: show interfaces trunk (allowed and active), show vlan trunk group, and on an MLAG pair, whether both peers agree. When a tenant subnet stops routing after a "small VRF fix", run show ip interface brief vrf all and show running-config | include ip routing before you touch BGP.
Real world. Most of these bite during migrations, when configs are copied from another vendor. Convert configs with a template or AVD rather than pasting them, and diff the result. The book also mentions some fun hidden commands (show donkeys, show chickens, show farm, p. 192); they are harmless, but don't leave them in scripts.

Checkpoint · Lab D4

  • I can read LLDP detail, control it per port, and explain Cisco LLDP and CDP interop; I can work out oversubscription, incast and buffer drain numbers.
  • On the MST triangle I can move the MST0 root, build instances with their own roots, explain P2p Boundary ports, and fix a region mismatch and the pruning trap.
  • You built and tore down an L2LS with three MLAG domains, MSTP with guards, and VARP gateways, and you can troubleshoot STP and MLAG faults from show output alone.
  • The fabric is now routed at the link level: every /31 pings, Loopback0 exists on all six switches, spines run spanning-tree mode none.
  • POD_A and POD_B MLAG are Active with VLAN 4093 created for the MLAG iBGP session.
  • You can justify L3LS over L2LS, choose between eBGP, OSPF and IS-IS, and explain the 50-byte VXLAN overhead and why this lab uses MTU 1500.
  • Save your work on every switch: write memory.

Domain 5 · Engineering exam

Lab D5 · Underlay protocols

You build the eBGP underlay that the rest of the guide depends on, with the exact peer groups, ASNs, route maps and prefix lists of the AVD reference. You then add ECMP and BFD, try dynamic peering with listen ranges, add the iBGP session across each MLAG pair, and rebuild the same underlay with OSPF and IS-IS before returning to eBGP.

Objectives
5.1, 5.2, 5.3, 5.4
Time
3.5 h
Start state
End of Lab D4: routed /31 links, Loopback0 on all six switches, leaf MLAG pairs Active, VLAN 4093 created. No routing protocol.
Devices
spine1, spine2, leaf1 to leaf4

Read first: AW Ch 2 · Network Designs · AW Ch 18 · MLAG · AW Ch 20 · FlexRoute · ATD lab guides (Data Center: L3LS BGP, L3LS OSPF) · AVD single DC L3LS example

5.1 eBGP underlay: peer groups, ASNs and loopback advertisement

Requirements, as an exam scenario would state them: each switch runs BGP in its design ASN with its Loopback0 address as router ID. Underlay sessions use the directly connected /31 addresses and a peer group named IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS. IPv4 unicast must not be activated by default for every neighbor; activate it explicitly for the peer group. Only loopback addresses may be injected into BGP, by redistributing connected routes through route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP, which matches prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY. Leaves also create Loopback1 (the VTEP address used in Domain 6) now, so that it is advertised from the start.

DeviceASNRouter IDUnderlay neighbors (remote-as)Loopback1Prefix-list entries
spine16510010.255.0.110.255.255.1, .5 (65101); .9, .13 (65102)-seq 10 10.255.0.0/27 eq 32
spine26510010.255.0.210.255.255.3, .7 (65101); .11, .15 (65102)-seq 10
leaf16510110.255.0.310.255.255.0, .2 (65100)10.255.1.3/32seq 10, seq 20 10.255.1.0/27 eq 32
leaf26510110.255.0.410.255.255.4, .6 (65100)10.255.1.3/32seq 10, seq 20
leaf36510210.255.0.510.255.255.8, .10 (65100)10.255.1.5/32seq 10, seq 20
leaf46510210.255.0.610.255.255.12, .14 (65100)10.255.1.5/32seq 10, seq 20

  1. On each leaf, create Loopback1 (description VXLAN_TUNNEL_SOURCE) with the address in the table. leaf1 and leaf2 deliberately share 10.255.1.3.

  2. Create the prefix list and route map on all six switches. The eq 32 keyword matters: it permits only host routes inside the /27, so a /31 that happened to fall in the range would still be refused.

  3. Configure BGP on all six switches with the peer group, neighbors, descriptions in the AVD format (spine1_Ethernet1, leaf1_Ethernet1), send-community and maximum-routes 256000 on the peer group. Do not configure maximum-paths yet; you will add it in 5.2 and see the difference.

  4. Verify sessions and routes, then ping from leaf1 Loopback0 to leaf3 Loopback0. Explain why leaf1 has no route to leaf2's Loopback0 (10.255.0.4) yet.

Solution · spine1 (spine2 follows the table) 
spine1(config)# ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
spine1(config-ip-pfx)# seq 10 permit 10.255.0.0/27 eq 32
spine1(config-ip-pfx)# exit
spine1(config)# route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP permit 10
spine1(config-route-map-RM-CONN-2-BGP)# match ip address prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
spine1(config-route-map-RM-CONN-2-BGP)# exit
spine1(config)# router bgp 65100
spine1(config-router-bgp)# router-id 10.255.0.1
spine1(config-router-bgp)# no bgp default ipv4-unicast
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS peer group
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS send-community
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS maximum-routes 256000
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.1 peer group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.1 remote-as 65101
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.1 description leaf1_Ethernet1
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.5 peer group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.5 remote-as 65101
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.5 description leaf2_Ethernet1
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.9 peer group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.9 remote-as 65102
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.9 description leaf3_Ethernet1
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.13 peer group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.13 remote-as 65102
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.13 description leaf4_Ethernet1
spine1(config-router-bgp)# redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP
spine1(config-router-bgp)# address-family ipv4
spine1(config-router-bgp-af)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS activate

spine2 uses router-id 10.255.0.2 and neighbors 10.255.255.3 (leaf1_Ethernet2), .7 (leaf2_Ethernet2), .11 (leaf3_Ethernet2) and .15 (leaf4_Ethernet2).

Solution · leaf1 (leaf2 to leaf4 follow the table) 
leaf1(config)# interface Loopback1
leaf1(config-if-Lo1)# description VXLAN_TUNNEL_SOURCE
leaf1(config-if-Lo1)# ip address 10.255.1.3/32
leaf1(config-if-Lo1)# exit
leaf1(config)# ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
leaf1(config-ip-pfx)# seq 10 permit 10.255.0.0/27 eq 32
leaf1(config-ip-pfx)# seq 20 permit 10.255.1.0/27 eq 32
leaf1(config-ip-pfx)# exit
leaf1(config)# route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP permit 10
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-CONN-2-BGP)# match ip address prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-CONN-2-BGP)# exit
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# router-id 10.255.0.3
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# no bgp default ipv4-unicast
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS peer group
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS send-community
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS maximum-routes 256000
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.0 peer group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.0 remote-as 65100
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.0 description spine1_Ethernet1
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.2 peer group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.2 remote-as 65100
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.2 description spine2_Ethernet1
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# address-family ipv4
leaf1(config-router-bgp-af)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS activate

The neighbor descriptions name the far-end interface: leaf2 uses spine1_Ethernet2 and spine2_Ethernet2, leaf3 uses Ethernet3, leaf4 uses Ethernet4. In the AVD reference the remote-as sits on each neighbor rather than the peer group; putting remote-as 65100 on a leaf's peer group would also work because both spines share an ASN.

Verify

Outputs are representative and trimmed; your timers, MACs and counters will differ.

spine1# show ip bgp summary
BGP summary information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.1, local AS number 65100
Neighbor Status Codes: m - Under maintenance
  Description      Neighbor      V AS     MsgRcvd MsgSent InQ OutQ  Up/Down State   PfxRcd PfxAcc
  leaf1_Ethernet1  10.255.255.1  4 65101       14      16   0    0 00:05:12 Estab   2      2
  leaf2_Ethernet1  10.255.255.5  4 65101       14      16   0    0 00:05:10 Estab   2      2
  leaf3_Ethernet1  10.255.255.9  4 65102       13      15   0    0 00:05:02 Estab   2      2
  leaf4_Ethernet1  10.255.255.13 4 65102       13      15   0    0 00:04:58 Estab   2      2
leaf1# show ip route bgp
VRF: default
Codes: C - connected, S - static, K - kernel,
       O - OSPF, IA - OSPF inter area, ... B - Other BGP Routes,
       B I - iBGP, B E - eBGP, ...

 B E      10.255.0.1/32 [200/0] via 10.255.255.0, Ethernet1
 B E      10.255.0.2/32 [200/0] via 10.255.255.2, Ethernet2
 B E      10.255.0.5/32 [200/0] via 10.255.255.0, Ethernet1
 B E      10.255.0.6/32 [200/0] via 10.255.255.0, Ethernet1
 B E      10.255.1.5/32 [200/0] via 10.255.255.0, Ethernet1
leaf1# show ip bgp neighbors 10.255.255.0 advertised-routes
leaf1# ping 10.255.0.5 source 10.255.0.3 repeat 2
PING 10.255.0.5 (10.255.0.5) from 10.255.0.3 : 72(100) bytes of data.
80 bytes from 10.255.0.5: icmp_seq=1 ttl=63 time=2.11 ms
80 bytes from 10.255.0.5: icmp_seq=2 ttl=63 time=1.87 ms

All underlay sessions are Established. Each spine accepts two prefixes from each leaf (its Loopback0 and the shared Loopback1). leaf1 learns both spine loopbacks and the POD_B loopbacks. Each POD_B route has only one next hop because maximum-paths defaults to 1. advertised-routes shows only 10.255.0.3/32 and 10.255.1.3/32 from leaf1's own table: no /31s. leaf1 has no route to 10.255.0.4: spine1 advertises it with AS path 65100 65101, leaf1 sees its own ASN 65101 in the path and discards it (eBGP loop prevention). Objective 5.3 fixes that.

Exam tip. no bgp default ipv4-unicast means a neighbor with no activate in address-family ipv4 comes up Established but exchanges nothing (PfxRcd 0). When a session is up but empty, check activation first, then the outbound route map. Also, always ping loopback to loopback with source: a plain ping 10.255.0.5 from leaf1 uses the egress /31 address as source, which is not advertised, so the reply has no route back and the ping fails on a healthy fabric.
Real world. Peer groups keep hundreds of neighbors consistent: one change to the group applies to every member. maximum-routes protects the switch if a neighbor suddenly leaks a full table. Descriptions that name the far-end device and interface make show ip bgp summary a cabling check as well as a routing check.

Break / fix 1 · wrong remote-as

Inject on leaf1: router bgp 65101, neighbor 10.255.255.0 remote-as 65200.

Symptom: show ip bgp summary on leaf1 shows spine1_Ethernet1 cycling between Connect, Active and Idle, never Estab. leaf1 still reaches POD_B through spine2.

Root cause and fix

spine1 sends an OPEN with AS 65100; leaf1 expects 65200, so leaf1 sends a NOTIFICATION (OPEN message error, bad peer AS) and drops the session. show ip bgp neighbors 10.255.255.0 shows the last error, and show logging has the notification. Fix with neighbor 10.255.255.0 remote-as 65100. Note how the fabric kept working over the other spine: redundancy can hide a fault that the exam grader will still find.

Break / fix 2 · a loopback that is not advertised

Inject on leaf3: ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY, no seq 10.

Symptom: sessions on leaf3 stay Established. leaf1 still has 10.255.1.5/32 but has lost 10.255.0.5/32, so ping 10.255.0.5 source 10.255.0.3 fails. spine1 shows PfxRcd 1 from leaf3.

Root cause and fix

The route map only permits what the prefix list matches. With seq 10 gone, Loopback0 (10.255.0.5) no longer matches and is denied by the route map's implicit deny; Loopback1 still matches seq 20. Check with show ip bgp neighbors 10.255.255.8 advertised-routes on leaf3 and show route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP. Fix with seq 10 permit 10.255.0.0/27 eq 32. In Domain 6 this exact fault breaks the EVPN sessions, which peer between Loopback0 addresses.

5.2 ECMP, BFD, route maps, prefix lists and listen ranges

Requirements: traffic from a leaf to any remote loopback must use both spines. Underlay failures that do not bring the link down must be detected in under a second. You will also try dynamic peering on the spines, so that a new leaf in a known address range and ASN range peers without any spine change, and then return to the static neighbors of the reference design.

  1. On all six switches, allow up to four equal-cost BGP paths (maximum-paths 4, as in the AVD reference). Verify that leaf1 now has two next hops to 10.255.0.5 and that the anycast VTEP 10.255.1.3 is reachable from spine1 through both leaf1 and leaf2.

  2. Enable BFD for the underlay peer group on all six switches with 300 ms intervals and a multiplier of 3 (the EOS defaults, configured explicitly under router bfd). The AVD reference does not run BFD on the underlay (only on the multihop EVPN sessions it adds in Domain 6), so treat this as an exercise; you may keep or remove it.

  3. Inspect the policy objects: show ip prefix-list, show route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP, and compare show ip bgp neighbors 10.255.255.0 advertised-routes with show ip bgp neighbors 10.255.255.0 routes on leaf1. Then temporarily remove the route map from the redistribute statement on leaf1 and observe what leaks.

  4. Dynamic peering on spine1: save the config to flash:d5-static.cfg, remove the four static underlay neighbors, and replace them with a listen range for 10.255.255.0/26 bound to IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS and a peer filter PF-LEAF-ASNS that accepts ASNs 65101 to 65199. Verify, then restore the static design with configure replace flash:d5-static.cfg.

Solution · ECMP and BFD (same on all six; spine1 shown) 
spine1(config)# router bgp 65100
spine1(config-router-bgp)# maximum-paths 4
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS bfd
spine1(config-router-bgp)# exit
spine1(config)# router bfd
spine1(config-router-bfd)# interval 300 min-rx 300 multiplier 3

maximum-paths 4 ecmp 4 is also valid; the ecmp keyword limits how many paths are installed in hardware. On leaf1 the commands are the same under router bgp 65101.

Solution · route-map experiment on leaf1 
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# redistribute connected
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# end
spine1# show ip route bgp
! 10.255.255.0/31, .2/31 and 10.255.252.0/31 from leaf1 are now in the spines' tables
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP

Without the route map every connected subnet leaks, including the MLAG peering /31 and the management subnet 172.100.100.0/24. That is why the reference design filters redistribution down to loopbacks.

Solution · listen range on spine1 
spine1# copy running-config flash:d5-static.cfg
spine1# configure
spine1(config)# peer-filter PF-LEAF-ASNS
spine1(config-peer-filter-PF-LEAF-ASNS)# 10 match as-range 65101-65199 result accept
spine1(config-peer-filter-PF-LEAF-ASNS)# exit
spine1(config)# router bgp 65100
spine1(config-router-bgp)# no neighbor 10.255.255.1
spine1(config-router-bgp)# no neighbor 10.255.255.5
spine1(config-router-bgp)# no neighbor 10.255.255.9
spine1(config-router-bgp)# no neighbor 10.255.255.13
spine1(config-router-bgp)# bgp listen range 10.255.255.0/26 peer-group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS peer-filter PF-LEAF-ASNS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# end
spine1# show ip bgp summary
spine1# show peer-filter PF-LEAF-ASNS

! return to the reference design
spine1# configure replace flash:d5-static.cfg

The spine now accepts any session sourced from 10.255.255.0/26 whose OPEN carries an ASN in the filter range. It never initiates; the leaves still have static neighbors and do the connecting. Instead of a peer filter you can pin one ASN: bgp listen range 10.255.255.0/26 peer-group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS remote-as 65101.

Verify

leaf1# show ip route 10.255.0.5
VRF: default
...
 B E      10.255.0.5/32 [200/0] via 10.255.255.0, Ethernet1
                                via 10.255.255.2, Ethernet2
leaf1# show ip bgp 10.255.0.5
BGP routing table information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.3, local AS number 65101
BGP routing table entry for 10.255.0.5/32
 Paths: 2 available
  65100 65102
    10.255.255.0 from 10.255.255.0 (10.255.0.1)
      Origin IGP, metric 0, localpref 100, IGP metric 0, weight 0, tag 0
      Received 00:12:40 ago, valid, external, ECMP head, ECMP, best, ECMP contender
  65100 65102
    10.255.255.2 from 10.255.255.2 (10.255.0.2)
      Origin IGP, metric 0, localpref 100, IGP metric 0, weight 0, tag 0
      Received 00:12:38 ago, valid, external, ECMP, ECMP contender
spine1# show ip route 10.255.1.3
leaf1# show bfd peers
VRF name: default
-----------------
DstAddr          MyDisc     YourDisc   Interface/Transport   Type      LastUp        LastDown  LastDiag       State
---------------- ---------- ---------- --------------------- --------- ------------- --------- -------------- -----
10.255.255.0     1203655214 3911022567 Ethernet1(13)         normal    10/25/26 14:02  NA      No Diagnostic  Up
10.255.255.2     2884197012 1450981237 Ethernet2(14)         normal    10/25/26 14:02  NA      No Diagnostic  Up
leaf1# traceroute 10.255.0.5 source 10.255.0.3
traceroute to 10.255.0.5 (10.255.0.5), 30 hops max, 60 byte packets
 1  10.255.255.0 (10.255.255.0)  1.204 ms  1.101 ms  1.087 ms
 2  10.255.0.5 (10.255.0.5)  2.311 ms  2.148 ms  2.090 ms

Remote loopbacks have two next hops (one per spine), and the two BGP paths are marked ECMP. spine1 reaches the anycast VTEP 10.255.1.3 through both leaf1 (10.255.255.1) and leaf2 (10.255.255.5). BFD sessions are Up on every underlay neighbor. With the listen range, show ip bgp summary on spine1 lists the four leaves as dynamic neighbors in the peer group, and after the configure replace the static neighbors and their descriptions are back.

Exam tip. "Traffic must be load-balanced across all spines" is maximum-paths in BGP (or in OSPF/IS-IS). It has to be on the switch that makes the forwarding decision, so a leaf needs it to use both spines and a spine needs it to reach an anycast VTEP through both leaves of a pair. Listen ranges are a common "make the spine configuration independent of the number of leaves" requirement; remember the peer group and either a remote-as or a peer-filter are mandatory.
Real world. On point-to-point fiber, a link failure usually brings the interface down in milliseconds and BGP reacts immediately, so BFD on directly connected underlay sessions mainly helps with failures that do not drop the link (a stuck line card, a media converter). BFD matters more on multihop sessions such as EVPN, which is why AVD enables it there by default. Listen ranges trade explicit neighbor documentation for less per-leaf spine config; many operators prefer static neighbors generated by automation.

Break / fix 3 · ECMP disappears

Inject on leaf1: router bgp 65101, maximum-paths 1.

Symptom: everything still pings. show ip route 10.255.0.5 on leaf1 lists a single next hop, and show ip bgp 10.255.0.5 shows two valid paths but only one best and no ECMP marking.

Root cause and fix

BGP still learns both paths, but with maximum-paths 1 it installs only the best one (chosen on the lower router ID, here spine1). Half the uplink capacity sits idle and a spine failure costs a reconvergence instead of an instant path removal. Fix with maximum-paths 4. A fault that does not break connectivity is a favorite exam trap: only the forwarding table tells the truth.

Break / fix 4 · the peer filter rejects a pod

Inject on spine1 while the listen range is active: change the peer filter to 10 match as-range 65101-65101 result accept.

Symptom: leaf3 and leaf4 lose their sessions to spine1 and do not come back; leaf1 and leaf2 are unaffected.

Root cause and fix

The peer filter now accepts only AS 65101, so connections from POD_B (AS 65102) are refused. Fix by widening the range to 65101-65199. Then restore the static design with configure replace flash:d5-static.cfg.

5.3 BGP with MLAG: the iBGP session across the peer link

Both leaves of an MLAG pair share an ASN, which causes two problems. First, leaf1 can never learn leaf2's loopback through a spine, because the AS path contains 65101 (you saw this in 5.1). Second, if leaf1 loses both uplinks, it has no routes at all even though its peer, one hop away, has a full table. The fix is an iBGP session between the peers over a dedicated SVI on the peer link (Vlan4093). Two details make it work correctly: next-hop-self and a route map that sets origin incomplete on routes received from the peer.

DeviceVlan4093iBGP neighborNeighbor descriptionPeer-group description
leaf110.255.251.0/3110.255.251.1leaf2_Vlan4093leaf2
leaf210.255.251.1/3110.255.251.0leaf1_Vlan4093leaf1
leaf310.255.251.4/3110.255.251.5leaf4_Vlan4093leaf4
leaf410.255.251.5/3110.255.251.4leaf3_Vlan4093leaf3

  1. On every leaf, create SVI Vlan4093 (description MLAG_L3, mtu 1500) with the address in the table. VLAN 4093 and its trunk group already exist from Lab D4.

  2. Create route map RM-MLAG-PEER-IN (permit 10) that sets origin incomplete, with the description from the reference.

  3. Create peer group MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER with remote-as equal to the local ASN, next-hop-self, the inbound route map, send-community and maximum-routes 256000. Add the peer's Vlan4093 address to it and activate the group in address-family ipv4.

  4. Verify that leaf1 now reaches 10.255.0.4, and that spine1 prefers the direct path to each leaf loopback over the copy re-advertised by its MLAG peer. Then shut both uplinks on leaf2 and confirm that leaf2 still reaches 10.255.0.5 through leaf1. Bring the uplinks back.

Solution · leaf1 (others follow the table) 
leaf1(config)# interface Vlan4093
leaf1(config-if-Vl4093)# description MLAG_L3
leaf1(config-if-Vl4093)# mtu 1500
leaf1(config-if-Vl4093)# ip address 10.255.251.0/31
leaf1(config-if-Vl4093)# exit
leaf1(config)# route-map RM-MLAG-PEER-IN permit 10
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-MLAG-PEER-IN)# description Make routes learned over MLAG Peer-link less preferred on spines to ensure optimal routing
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-MLAG-PEER-IN)# set origin incomplete
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-MLAG-PEER-IN)# exit
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER peer group
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER remote-as 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER next-hop-self
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER description leaf2
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER route-map RM-MLAG-PEER-IN in
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER send-community
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER maximum-routes 256000
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.251.1 peer group MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.251.1 description leaf2_Vlan4093
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# address-family ipv4
leaf1(config-router-bgp-af)# neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER activate

leaf3 and leaf4 use remote-as 65102. The Vlan4093 /31 is not advertised (it is not in the loopback prefix list) and does not need to be: the iBGP session is between directly connected addresses.

Verify

leaf1# show ip bgp summary
BGP summary information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.3, local AS number 65101
  Description      Neighbor      V AS     MsgRcvd MsgSent InQ OutQ  Up/Down State   PfxRcd PfxAcc
  leaf2_Vlan4093   10.255.251.1  4 65101       11      12   0    0 00:02:01 Estab   7      7
  spine1_Ethernet1 10.255.255.0  4 65100       48      45   0    0 00:31:10 Estab   4      4
  spine2_Ethernet1 10.255.255.2  4 65100       48      45   0    0 00:31:08 Estab   4      4
leaf1# show ip route 10.255.0.4
leaf1# ping 10.255.0.4 source 10.255.0.3 repeat 2
spine1# show ip bgp 10.255.0.4
BGP routing table entry for 10.255.0.4/32
 Paths: 2 available
  65101
    10.255.255.5 from 10.255.255.5 (10.255.0.4)
      Origin IGP, metric 0, localpref 100, IGP metric 0, weight 0, tag 0
      Received 00:33:02 ago, valid, external, best
  65101
    10.255.255.1 from 10.255.255.1 (10.255.0.3)
      Origin INCOMPLETE, metric 0, localpref 100, IGP metric 0, weight 0, tag 0
      Received 00:02:00 ago, valid, external
      Not best: Origin
leaf2(config)# interface Ethernet1-2
leaf2(config-if-Et1-2)# shutdown
leaf2# show ip route 10.255.0.5
leaf2# ping 10.255.0.5 source 10.255.0.4 repeat 2
leaf2(config-if-Et1-2)# no shutdown
 B I      10.255.0.5/32 [200/0] via 10.255.251.0, Vlan4093

The iBGP session is Established on both pairs. leaf1 reaches 10.255.0.4 via Vlan4093 as an iBGP route. On spine1, the path to 10.255.0.4 learned directly from leaf2 has origin IGP and wins; the copy re-advertised by leaf1 has origin INCOMPLETE and is not best, so spines do not send leaf2's traffic through leaf1 and the peer link. With leaf2's uplinks down, leaf2 still reaches POD_B through leaf1, with leaf1 as the next hop thanks to next-hop-self.

Deployment variationHowWhen
Dedicated L3 VLAN (this lab, AVD default)Vlan4093 for iBGP, Vlan4094 for MLAG controlDefault: keeps MLAG control traffic and routing separate
Reuse the MLAG VLANiBGP over Vlan4094 (AVD: set the L3 peer VLAN equal to the MLAG VLAN)Saves a VLAN and a subnet; slightly less separation
Per-VRF iBGPOne more SVI per VRF (Vlan3001 for TENANT-A here, Domain 6)Needed so a VRF survives a leaf losing its uplinks
No iBGP, allowas-inLeaves accept their own ASN from spinesFixes peer loopback reachability but not the lost-uplinks case; rarely chosen
Unique ASN per leafEach leaf its own ASN; eBGP across the peer linkSome designs prefer it; changes loop prevention and ASN planning
Exam tip. If an MLAG leaf cannot reach its peer's loopback, or an EVPN session between MLAG peers never forms, check the iBGP session on the peer-link SVI. If the session is up but routes from the peer are not installed, check next-hop-self: without it the peer's eBGP-learned routes arrive with a spine /31 as next hop, which this switch cannot resolve.
Real world. The origin trick is subtle and easy to forget when hand-building. EOS gives redistributed connected routes origin IGP; the inbound route map downgrades only peer-learned copies, so on the spines the direct advertisement always wins and the peer link carries only failure traffic. AVD generates all of this from one mlag setting, which is a good argument for automation.

Break / fix 5 · iBGP without next-hop-self

Inject on leaf1: router bgp 65101, no neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER next-hop-self. Then shut leaf2 Eth1-2.

Symptom: leaf2 keeps leaf1's own loopback but loses every remote route. show ip bgp 10.255.0.5 on leaf2 shows the path from 10.255.251.0 with next hop 10.255.255.0 or 10.255.255.2, and it is not valid or best.

Root cause and fix

iBGP does not change the next hop by default, so leaf1 passes on its eBGP routes with the spine /31 as next hop. leaf2 has no route to leaf1's uplink /31s (they are filtered from BGP on purpose), so the next hop is unresolvable and the path is unusable. While leaf2's own uplinks are up, its eBGP paths hide the fault, which is why you shut them to expose it. Fix by restoring neighbor MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER next-hop-self on leaf1, then no shutdown on leaf2 Eth1-2. Next-hop-self is set on the sender; leaf2 needs it too for the reverse direction.

5.4 IGP underlay alternatives: OSPF and IS-IS

Some exam scenarios and many production fabrics use an IGP underlay with iBGP EVPN on top. You rebuild the underlay twice: first with OSPF (area 0, point-to-point on every /31, loopbacks advertised as passive), then with IS-IS (single level 2, point-to-point). Each protocol must deliver the same result as eBGP: every Loopback0 and Loopback1 reachable with two equal-cost paths. At the end you return to the eBGP reference.

  1. Save the eBGP underlay on all six switches: copy running-config flash:d5-ebgp.cfg. Then remove BGP (no router bgp 65100 on spines, no router bgp 65101 or 65102 on leaves). Confirm that remote loopbacks are gone.

  2. OSPF: configure process 100 on all six switches with router ID Loopback0, passive-interface default, the fabric links (and Vlan4093 on leaves) non-passive, ip ospf network point-to-point on every fabric link, all of these interfaces plus Loopback0 (and Loopback1 on leaves) in area 0.0.0.0, max-lsa 12000 and maximum-paths 4.

  3. Verify OSPF, then remove it (no router ospf 100 and the interface ip ospf lines, or configure replace flash:d5-ebgp.cfg followed by removing BGP again).

  4. IS-IS: configure instance UNDERLAY, level 2 only, NET derived from Loopback0, point-to-point fabric links (and Vlan4093), loopbacks passive, maximum-paths 4 in the IPv4 unicast address family.

  5. Verify IS-IS, then restore the eBGP underlay on all six switches with configure replace flash:d5-ebgp.cfg and confirm the 5.1 to 5.3 verification passes again.

DeviceOSPF router-idOSPF non-passive interfacesIS-IS NETIS-IS interfaces
spine110.255.0.1Eth1-449.0001.0102.5500.0001.00Eth1-4, Lo0 (passive)
spine210.255.0.2Eth1-449.0001.0102.5500.0002.00Eth1-4, Lo0 (passive)
leaf110.255.0.3Eth1-2, Vlan409349.0001.0102.5500.0003.00Eth1-2, Vlan4093, Lo0 and Lo1 (passive)
leaf210.255.0.4Eth1-2, Vlan409349.0001.0102.5500.0004.00same as leaf1
leaf310.255.0.5Eth1-2, Vlan409349.0001.0102.5500.0005.00same as leaf1
leaf410.255.0.6Eth1-2, Vlan409349.0001.0102.5500.0006.00same as leaf1

NET format: area 49.0001, then the system ID built from Loopback0 padded to 12 digits (10.255.0.1 becomes 010.255.000.001, regrouped as 0102.5500.0001), then selector 00.

Solution · OSPF on spine1 
spine1(config)# router ospf 100
spine1(config-router-ospf)# router-id 10.255.0.1
spine1(config-router-ospf)# passive-interface default
spine1(config-router-ospf)# no passive-interface Ethernet1
spine1(config-router-ospf)# no passive-interface Ethernet2
spine1(config-router-ospf)# no passive-interface Ethernet3
spine1(config-router-ospf)# no passive-interface Ethernet4
spine1(config-router-ospf)# max-lsa 12000
spine1(config-router-ospf)# maximum-paths 4
spine1(config-router-ospf)# exit
spine1(config)# interface Ethernet1-4
spine1(config-if-Et1-4)# ip ospf network point-to-point
spine1(config-if-Et1-4)# ip ospf area 0.0.0.0
spine1(config-if-Et1-4)# interface Loopback0
spine1(config-if-Lo0)# ip ospf area 0.0.0.0
Solution · OSPF on leaf1 (other leaves follow the table) 
leaf1(config)# router ospf 100
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# router-id 10.255.0.3
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# passive-interface default
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# no passive-interface Ethernet1
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# no passive-interface Ethernet2
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# no passive-interface Vlan4093
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# max-lsa 12000
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# maximum-paths 4
leaf1(config-router-ospf)# exit
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet1-2
leaf1(config-if-Et1-2)# ip ospf network point-to-point
leaf1(config-if-Et1-2)# ip ospf area 0.0.0.0
leaf1(config-if-Et1-2)# interface Vlan4093
leaf1(config-if-Vl4093)# ip ospf network point-to-point
leaf1(config-if-Vl4093)# ip ospf area 0.0.0.0
leaf1(config-if-Vl4093)# interface Loopback0
leaf1(config-if-Lo0)# ip ospf area 0.0.0.0
leaf1(config-if-Lo0)# interface Loopback1
leaf1(config-if-Lo1)# ip ospf area 0.0.0.0

The classic alternative to interface-level ip ospf area is network 10.255.0.0/16 area 0.0.0.0 under the process. Either is fine; do not mix them on the same interfaces.

Solution · IS-IS on spine1 
spine1(config)# router isis UNDERLAY
spine1(config-router-isis)# net 49.0001.0102.5500.0001.00
spine1(config-router-isis)# is-type level-2
spine1(config-router-isis)# router-id ipv4 10.255.0.1
spine1(config-router-isis)# log-adjacency-changes
spine1(config-router-isis)# address-family ipv4 unicast

spine1(config-if-Et1-4)# isis network point-to-point

spine1(config-if-Lo0)# isis passive

spine1# show isis neighbors
Instance  VRF      System Id        Type Interface          SNPA              State Hold time   Circuit Id
UNDERLAY  default  leaf1            L2   Ethernet1          P2P               UP    28          0E
UNDERLAY  default  leaf2            L2   Ethernet2          P2P               UP    25          0E
UNDERLAY  default  leaf3            L2   Ethernet3          P2P               UP    29          0E
UNDERLAY  default  leaf4            L2   Ethernet4          P2P               UP    27          0E
leaf1# show isis database

leaf1# ping 10.255.0.5 source 10.255.0.3 repeat 2
 I L2     10.255.0.5/32 [115/30] via 10.255.255.0, Ethernet1
                                 via 10.255.255.2, Ethernet2

OSPF: every fabric adjacency is FULL with priority 0 (point-to-point, no DR election), every remote loopback is present, and POD_B loopbacks have two equal-cost paths. Unlike the BGP design, OSPF also carries the /31 link subnets (they are part of the router LSAs); that is normal for an IGP underlay. IS-IS: all adjacencies are L2 UP on P2P circuits, the database holds six LSPs (one per switch), and remote loopbacks show two equal-cost paths. Your OSPF and IS-IS metrics depend on reference bandwidth and default metrics; what matters is that both paths are equal.

Exam tip. For an IGP underlay, the three things graders look for are: adjacencies up on every fabric link, network type point-to-point on the /31s, and loopbacks advertised (passive). If the scenario also needs EVPN, the overlay becomes iBGP between loopbacks with the spines as route reflectors, so the IGP must carry every loopback.
Real world. Point-to-point network type removes DR/BDR election and the type 2 LSAs that go with it, which speeds up convergence and keeps the database small. IS-IS with a single level 2 is the simplest scalable choice; there is no reason to use level 1 inside a DC fabric. Whatever the IGP, keep maximum-paths at least equal to the spine count.

Break / fix 6 · OSPF network type mismatch

Inject on leaf1 while OSPF is running: interface Ethernet1, no ip ospf network point-to-point (it becomes broadcast; spine1 stays point-to-point).

Symptom: the adjacency on spine1 Eth1 may still reach FULL, but leaf1's routes through spine1 disappear or become single-path through spine2, and show ip ospf interface Ethernet1 shows different network types on the two ends.

Root cause and fix

Hello and dead timers are the same for broadcast and point-to-point, so the neighbors can form an adjacency, but they describe the link differently in their router LSAs (a transit network with a DR on one side, a point-to-point link on the other). SPF cannot match the two halves of the link, so it does not use it. Fix by restoring ip ospf network point-to-point on leaf1 Eth1. Compare both ends with show ip ospf interface brief whenever an adjacency is up but routes are missing.

Break / fix 7 · IS-IS level and NET mistakes

Inject A on leaf3: router isis UNDERLAY, is-type level-1. Restore it, then inject B on leaf4: net 49.0001.0102.5500.0005.00 (leaf3's system ID).

Symptom A: leaf3 has no adjacencies with the spines; show isis neighbors on spine1 no longer lists leaf3 and POD_B is reachable only through leaf4. Symptom B: leaf3 and leaf4 log duplicate system ID messages and routes to POD_B flap or disappear.

Root cause and fix

A: a level-1-only router cannot form an adjacency with a level-2-only router; levels must overlap. Fix with is-type level-2. B: the system ID part of the NET must be unique in the domain; two routers with the same system ID overwrite each other's LSPs. Fix with leaf4's own NET 49.0001.0102.5500.0006.00 (remove the wrong NET first with no net ...). Note that an area mismatch alone (for example 49.0002 on one switch) does not break a level-2 adjacency, because level 2 connects areas; it would break a level-1 adjacency.

Route scale drill: thousands of prefixes through the fabric

Real fabrics carry far more than our handful of loopbacks. This optional drill borrows the method from AW Ch 20 · FlexRoute: the author fills a switch with hundreds of thousands of routes using a small eAPI script (p. 106), then checks memory, BGP counters and which routes actually reached the forwarding hardware (p. 108-109). You do the same at lab scale. It needs the finished fabric, including the core1 border peering (load the AVD configs as described in Build the lab; the border sessions are part of them).

Watch out. FlexRoute itself is a hardware feature of R-series platforms (such as the 7280R and 7500R) and does not exist in cEOS or vEOS. The commands the book uses for it (ip hardware fib optimize ... and restarting the forwarding agent) are for reading only here. What carries over to any EOS device is the method: generate routes with eAPI, then prove where they landed.

  1. On core1, confirm eAPI is enabled and the routing model is multi-agent. The book turns on the multi-agent model before FlexRoute (p. 108); your day-0 configs already have it, and EVPN requires it.

    core1# show management api http-commands
core1# show running-config section service routing
core1# show ip route summary | include model

  2. From the containerlab host, run this script. It adds 4,000 static /32 routes to Null0 on core1 over eAPI, in batches, then tells BGP to advertise them to leaf3 and leaf4.

    #!/usr/bin/env python3
# scale.py: push N static /32 routes to core1 over eAPI
import json, ssl, urllib.request, base64

HOST, USER, PWD, COUNT, BATCH = "172.100.100.41", "admin", "admin", 4000, 500
ctx = ssl.create_default_context(); ctx.check_hostname = False; ctx.verify_mode = ssl.CERT_NONE
auth = base64.b64encode(f"{USER}:{PWD}".encode()).decode()

def run(cmds):
    body = json.dumps({"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": 1,
                       "params": {"version": 1, "cmds": cmds, "format": "json"}}).encode()
    req = urllib.request.Request(f"https://{HOST}/command-api", body,
                                 {"Content-Type": "application/json", "Authorization": f"Basic {auth}"})
    reply = json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(req, context=ctx))
    if "error" in reply:
        raise SystemExit(reply["error"])
    return reply["result"]

routes = [f"ip route 11.{1 + i // 65536}.{i // 256 % 256}.{i % 256}/32 Null0" for i in range(COUNT)]
for start in range(0, COUNT, BATCH):
    run(["enable", "configure"] + routes[start:start + BATCH])
    print(f"added {min(start + BATCH, COUNT)} routes")
run(["enable", "configure", "router bgp 65000", "redistribute static"])
print(run(["show ip route summary"])[0]["totalRoutes"], "routes on core1")

    Run it with python3 scale.py. It uses only the Python standard library.

  3. Check the result at each hop: core1, the border leaves, a POD_A leaf, and the spines. Decide before you look whether the spines should hold these routes at all.

    core1# show ip route summary
leaf3# show ip bgp summary vrf TENANT-A
leaf3# show ip route vrf TENANT-A summary
leaf1# show bgp evpn summary
leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A summary
spine1# show ip route summary
leaf1# show processes top once | include Bgp|Rib|ConfigAgent

  4. Find out how many routes are not programmed in hardware. The book counts them by filtering the routing table for lines that start with an asterisk (p. 108). Use the EOS filter, which takes a regular expression.

    leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A | include ^\*
leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A | grep "^\*" | wc -l

    EOS lets you pipe show output into Linux tools such as grep and wc. On cEOS the answer should be zero, because routes go to the Linux kernel instead of an ASIC. On a fixed-configuration switch with a small forwarding table, the same check is how you spot a table that has overflowed.

  5. Clean up and confirm the counts return to normal.

    core1# configure replace flash:startup-config
core1# show ip route summary

    containerlab saved the day-0 file as flash:startup-config, so the replace removes both the static routes and the redistribution in one step, as long as you did not write memory after the script.

Verify

leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A summary
VRF: TENANT-A
   Route Source                                Number Of Routes
------------------------------------- -------------------------
   connected                                                  4
   static (persistent)                                        0
   bgp                                                     4006
     External: 4006 Internal: 0
   ...
   Total Routes                                            4012

   Number of routes per mask-length:
      /0: 1       /24: 4       /31: 1       /32: 4006

leaf1 learns the 4,000 prefixes as EVPN type-5 routes inside TENANT-A, and the spines' default-VRF table does not grow, because the spines only reflect EVPN routes and never install tenant prefixes.

Exam tip. The exam will not ask you to scale a table, but it does expect you to read show ip route summary and BGP prefix counts quickly. "Accepted prefixes" in the BGP summary versus "routes" in the RIB is a fast way to see whether a route-map is filtering.
Real world. Before you put full Internet tables on a border leaf, check the platform's route scale and its FIB profile options in the data sheet. Keep full tables on the border and send only a default route into the fabric. The type-5 table on every leaf grows with whatever the border advertises.

Break / fix

Inject: on leaf3 only, add an inbound route-map on the core1 session that permits only 0.0.0.0/0 and 8.8.8.0/24, and leave leaf4 alone.

Symptom: leaf1 still has all 4,000 routes, but each one now has a single next hop instead of two.

Root cause and fix

leaf4 still advertises the full set as type-5 routes, so leaf1 keeps the prefixes but loses ECMP toward leaf3 for them. Compare show bgp evpn route-type ip-prefix 11.1.0.5/32 detail on leaf1 before and after: only one originating router remains. Policy on a border pair must match on both members. Remove the route-map, or apply the same one on leaf4 if the filter was the intent.

Checkpoint · Lab D5

  • The eBGP underlay is restored from flash:d5-ebgp.cfg and matches the AVD reference: IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS, MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER, RM-CONN-2-BGP, PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY, RM-MLAG-PEER-IN, maximum-paths 4.
  • On every leaf, show ip bgp summary shows three Established sessions (two spines and the MLAG peer); on every spine, four.
  • show ip route 10.255.0.5 on leaf1 shows two ECMP paths, and ping 10.255.0.5 source 10.255.0.3 and ping 10.255.0.4 source 10.255.0.3 succeed.
  • You can build the same underlay with OSPF or IS-IS and explain the network type, passive loopback and NET rules.
  • You decided whether to keep underlay BFD (harmless) or remove it to match the reference exactly. Run write memory on every switch; Lab D6 starts from here.

Domain 6 · Engineering exam

Lab D6 · Overlay protocols: VXLAN, EVPN and multihoming

You turn the routed eBGP underlay from Lab D5 into a VXLAN fabric. You start with a static flood list (head-end replication), replace it with an MP-BGP EVPN control plane, add a symmetric IRB tenant VRF, and finally convert POD_B from MLAG to EVPN all-active multihoming. This is the core of the Engineering exam: almost every scenario ends with "hosts in VLAN X can reach hosts in VLAN Y across the fabric".

Objectives
6.1, 6.2, 6.3

5 to 6 h (6.1: 1.5 h, 6.2: 2.5 h, 6.3: 1.5 h, plus break/fix)

End of Lab D5: eBGP underlay up, MLAG up on both pods, no overlay. See the list below.
Devices
spine1, spine2, leaf1 to leaf4 (you configure); host1, host2 (pre-built test endpoints, read only); core1 (pre-built, used for the type-5 default route)

6.1 VXLAN data plane with static head-end replication

VXLAN wraps the original Ethernet frame in outer Ethernet, IP, UDP (port 4789) and an 8-byte VXLAN header that carries a 24-bit VNI. The VTEP is the logical interface Vxlan1, and its source IP comes from a loopback. Before EVPN, a VTEP has two problems to solve: where to send broadcast, unknown unicast and multicast (BUM) traffic, and how to learn remote MACs. With static head-end replication (HER) you answer the first with a configured flood list, and the switch answers the second by learning MACs from decapsulated traffic (data-plane learning). Building this first makes the data plane visible on its own, which is exactly what you need when you troubleshoot EVPN later.

Warm-up: static HER. Arista Warrior's VXLAN chapter is written for EOS 4.21 and builds VXLAN with a manual control plane (static flood lists), then mentions CVX, and does not cover EVPN. This objective follows the same path on purpose: you build the data plane by hand first, then 6.2 replaces the manual pieces with EVPN using current syntax (AW Ch 21, p. 117).

Terms you must be able to use

TermMeaning in this lab
VXLAN segmentOne stretched Layer 2 domain carried over the routed underlay. Here, VLAN 30 stretched between POD_A and POD_B is one segment (AW Ch 21, p. 110).
VNIThe segment ID in the VXLAN header. It is 24 bits (about 16 million values) where a VLAN ID is 12 bits (4094 usable), which is why VXLAN scales for multi-tenant fabrics (AW Ch 21, p. 110).
VTEPThe device (or MLAG pair) that encapsulates and decapsulates VXLAN. On EOS it is interface Vxlan1 sourced from a loopback (AW Ch 21, p. 110).
VXLAN gatewayA device that bridges between a traditional VLAN and a VNI. Every leaf here is a VXLAN gateway for its host-facing VLANs (AW Ch 21, p. 110).
Underlay / overlayThe underlay is the routed IP fabric from Lab D5 that only needs VTEP-to-VTEP reachability. The overlay is the set of VXLAN segments riding on top of it (AW Ch 21, p. 110).

VXLAN on Arista switches is a hardware forwarding feature, and the book warns that not every platform or software image supports it (AW Ch 21, p. 110). Today vEOS-lab and cEOS-lab both support VXLAN bridging and routing in software, which is why this lab works in containerlab, but check the release notes and platform feature matrix before you assume a given box or image can do it.

A useful mental model from the book: the whole VXLAN overlay behaves like one big Ethernet switch whose "ports" are VTEPs (AW Ch 21, p. 112). A switch floods BUM frames out of every port in the VLAN; a VTEP does the same by sending one unicast copy of each BUM frame to every other VTEP that has the VNI. That is head-end replication, and the flood list is how each VTEP knows which other VTEPs exist (AW Ch 21, p. 112-113). In the same way a MAC table maps a MAC to a port, the VXLAN address table maps a remote MAC to the VTEP behind which it lives (AW Ch 21, p. 113).

VNI numbering: pick a scheme that a human can decode. The book suggests encoding meaning in the digits, for example tenant digits followed by VLAN digits (AW Ch 21, p. 113). This fabric uses 10000 + VLAN for L2 VNIs (10010, 10020, 10030) and 50000 + tenant for the L3 VNI (50001), which follows the same idea and matches the AVD defaults.

Tasks

  1. Create VLANs 10 (APP), 20 (DB) and 30 (L2_STRETCH) on leaf1 to leaf4. Confirm Port-Channel5 already trunks them toward the host and that they are allowed on the MLAG peer link.

    leaf1(config)# vlan 10
leaf1(config-vlan-10)# name APP
leaf1(config-vlan-10)# vlan 20
leaf1(config-vlan-20)# name DB
leaf1(config-vlan-20)# vlan 30
leaf1(config-vlan-30)# name L2_STRETCH

  2. Configure Loopback1 as the VTEP source. The two members of an MLAG pair must use the same address, because they act as one logical VTEP: POD_A uses 10.255.1.3/32, POD_B uses 10.255.1.5/32. Confirm the prefix reaches every other leaf through the underlay (the existing prefix-list already permits 10.255.1.0/27 eq 32).

  3. Configure interface Vxlan1 so that it sources from Loopback1, uses UDP port 4789, maps VLAN 10/20/30 to VNI 10010/10020/10030, and encapsulates routed traffic with the MLAG system ID as the inner source MAC (vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id). You will need that last command once you route in 6.2; adding it now means both MLAG peers present the same router MAC. This is the manual control-plane build the book describes (AW Ch 21, p. 114). You do not have to type vxlan udp-port 4789: EOS adds the default port to the running config by itself (AW Ch 21, p. 115).

  4. Configure a static flood list so that POD_A floods BUM traffic to POD_B's VTEP and POD_B floods to POD_A's VTEP. Use the default (global) list, which applies to every VLAN that has no list of its own (AW Ch 21, p. 114). To add a VTEP to an existing list later, use vxlan flood vtep add <ip> (and remove to take one out): repeating vxlan flood vtep <ip> replaces the whole list instead of appending to it (AW Ch 21, p. 114).

  5. Per-VLAN list: on leaf1 and leaf2 only, configure vxlan vlan 30 flood vtep 10.255.1.5. A per-VLAN list takes precedence over the default list for that VLAN (AW Ch 21, p. 115). Run show vxlan flood vtep and identify which VLANs use the default list (marked with an asterisk) and which use their own list. Remove the per-VLAN list afterwards with no vxlan vlan 30 flood vtep.

  6. Test L2 reachability across the fabric on VLAN 30 (no gateway exists, this is pure bridging): host1# ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102. Also ping on VLAN 10 (ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102). Same subnet, so this works without any SVI.

  7. Explore: find which VTEPs leaf1 knows about, which remote MACs it learned and how, and whether config-sanity is clean on all four leaves. Explain in one sentence why the remote MAC for host2 points at 10.255.1.5 and not at leaf3 or leaf4 individually. Use show vxlan vtep, show vxlan address-table and show vxlan vni (AW Ch 21, p. 116).

  8. MTU: calculate the VXLAN overhead and test whether a full 1500-byte host frame crosses the fabric. Decide what MTU the underlay P2P links should have in production.

  9. When everything works, remove the static flood lists from all four leaves. L2 reachability on VLAN 30 should stop for new flows (clear the ARP entry on host1 to prove it). EVPN in 6.2 restores it.

Solution · leaf1 (full) 
leaf1(config)# vlan 10
leaf1(config-vlan-10)# name APP
leaf1(config-vlan-10)# vlan 20
leaf1(config-vlan-20)# name DB
leaf1(config-vlan-20)# vlan 30
leaf1(config-vlan-30)# name L2_STRETCH
leaf1(config-vlan-30)# exit
leaf1(config)# interface Loopback1
leaf1(config-if-Lo1)# description VXLAN_TUNNEL_SOURCE
leaf1(config-if-Lo1)# ip address 10.255.1.3/32
leaf1(config-if-Lo1)# exit
leaf1(config)# interface Vxlan1
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# description leaf1_VTEP
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan source-interface Loopback1
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan udp-port 4789
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan vlan 10 vni 10010
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan vlan 20 vni 10020
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan vlan 30 vni 10030
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan flood vtep 10.255.1.5
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# end
leaf1# write memory

This is the output while the per-VLAN list for VLAN 30 is configured: VLANs 10 and 20 carry the asterisk (default list), VLAN 30 does not (its own list) (AW Ch 21, p. 115).

Exam tip. show vxlan vtep is not a tunnel status table. With static HER it lists remote VTEPs learned from traffic, so it can be empty right after you configure everything, and entries age out a few minutes after traffic stops (AW Ch 21, p. 116). An empty list does not mean your config is wrong: send a ping between hosts, then look again. With EVPN, VTEPs learned from IMET routes stay as long as the routes do.

How VXLAN behaves with MLAG

  • One logical VTEP per pair. Both peers source from the same Loopback1 (AW Ch 21, p. 118). Remote VTEPs see one tunnel endpoint and the underlay ECMPs packets to either peer. host2's MAC therefore points to 10.255.1.5, not to an individual leaf. Each peer still has its own Loopback0 for BGP router-id and EVPN peering.
  • Identical VXLAN config on both peers. VLAN-to-VNI maps, flood lists and the source loopback must match (AW Ch 21, p. 118). show vxlan config-sanity detail checks "Peer VTEP IP" and "Peer VLAN-VNI" against the MLAG peer.
  • MAC sync. MACs learned from VXLAN on one peer are synced over the peer link, so either peer can forward to the remote VTEP.
  • BUM and the peer link. A frame that arrives from VXLAN is not sent back into VXLAN, and the MLAG peer link does not forward a decapsulated BUM copy onto MLAG ports that are up on both sides. This is the usual MLAG split-horizon rule extended to the tunnel.
  • Router MAC. vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id makes both peers use the MLAG system ID as the inner source MAC (and later as the EVPN router MAC) for routed VXLAN traffic. Without it, a remote VTEP sees two different router MACs behind one VTEP IP.
  • Underlay redundancy. The book calls for a Layer 3 peering between the MLAG peers across the peer link (AW Ch 21, p. 118-119). In this fabric that is the iBGP session on Vlan4093 (MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER), already built in Lab D5.
  • Failure case: one peer loses its uplinks. It still owns the shared VTEP IP and still receives host traffic over its MLAG port. Traffic to and from the underlay is routed across the peer link through the Vlan4093 peering to the other peer, which still has uplinks (AW Ch 21, p. 119). Test it: shut Ethernet1-2 on leaf1, ping host1 to host2 on VLAN 30, then show ip route 10.255.1.5 on leaf1 (next hop 10.255.251.1 via Vlan4093). Bring the uplinks back.
  • Failure case: one peer fails completely. The host's LACP bundle loses one member and rehashes all flows onto the link to the surviving peer, and the underlay stops sending to the dead peer because its routes are withdrawn. The shared VTEP IP stays reachable through the survivor, so remote VTEPs notice nothing (AW Ch 21, p. 119).
Legacy: CVX. Before EVPN was common, Arista offered CloudVision eXchange (CVX) as a controller: VTEPs configured with vxlan controller-client register with CVX, which then distributes flood lists and keeps the VXLAN address tables of all VTEPs in sync, and show vxlan controller status shows the connection (AW Ch 21, p. 117). You may see the "CVX Configuration Check" row in config-sanity because of it. CVX is a legacy, proprietary option and is not built in this lab; EVPN is the open, standards-based alternative and is what 6.2 covers.

MTU and overhead

VXLAN adds 50 bytes to an untagged inner frame: outer Ethernet 14 + outer IPv4 20 + UDP 8 + VXLAN 8 (54 if the outer frame carries an 802.1Q tag). A host sending 1500-byte IP packets therefore needs at least 1550 bytes of IP MTU on every underlay link. The AVD reference uses mtu 1500 on the P2P links because cEOS-lab forwards in software; in production you set the P2P links (and the MLAG peer link) to mtu 9214 and leave hosts at 1500 or 9000.

host1# ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102 size 1472 df-bit repeat 2
leaf1# show interfaces Ethernet1 | include MTU

Size 1472 + 8 ICMP + 20 IP is a 1500-byte packet. On hardware with a 1500-byte underlay this fails silently: the VTEP cannot fragment VXLAN, so the encapsulated packet is dropped. cEOS may not enforce the same limits, so treat this as a reasoning exercise and know the numbers.

Exam tip. show vxlan config-sanity detail is the fastest single check on a VTEP you did not build. Any "FAIL" row tells you which section to fix (loopback IP, VLAN-VNI map, flood list, MLAG peer VTEP IP). Run it on both members of every MLAG pair before you move on, because grading checks the final state of every device.
Real world. Static HER is rare in new builds, but you still meet it in small two-pod designs, in CVX-based fabrics, and in interop with third-party VTEPs that do not speak EVPN. Its weaknesses are operational: every new VTEP means touching every flood list, and data-plane learning means a silent host is unknown until it sends a frame.
Watch out. A Vxlan1 interface with no vxlan source-interface, or a source loopback with no IP, stays down. An MTU problem never shows up in small pings: always test with a large packet and df-bit.

Break / fix 6.1-A · VNI mismatch

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4 (both, so MLAG config-sanity stays clean and the fault is fabric-wide):

leaf3(config)# interface Vxlan1
leaf3(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan vlan 30 vni 10031

Symptom: ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102 from host1 fails, while VLAN 10 still works. leaf1 may still show host2's MAC for a while from earlier traffic.

Root cause and fix

leaf1 encapsulates VLAN 30 with VNI 10030. leaf3 and leaf4 have no VLAN mapped to 10030, so they drop the packet on decapsulation. Their own VLAN 30 traffic leaves with VNI 10031, which leaf1 also drops. The VNI is the only thing that identifies the segment on the wire; VLAN numbers are local. Compare show vxlan vni on both pods (or the "Static VLAN to VNI mapping" line in show interfaces vxlan1), then put back vxlan vlan 30 vni 10030 on leaf3 and leaf4.

Break / fix 6.1-B · MLAG pair with different Loopback1 IPs

Inject on leaf2 only:

leaf2(config)# interface Loopback1
leaf2(config-if-Lo1)# ip address 10.255.1.4/32

Symptom: intermittent loss from host2 to host1. On leaf3, host1's MAC moves between two VTEPs (watch the Moves counter in show vxlan address-table). Config-sanity on leaf1 and leaf2 reports a failure.

Root cause and fix

The MLAG pair no longer looks like one VTEP. host1's LAG hashes some frames to leaf1 (encapsulated from 10.255.1.3) and some to leaf2 (from 10.255.1.4), so remote VTEPs keep re-learning the MAC. The flood list on POD_B only contains 10.255.1.3, so BUM from POD_B never reaches leaf2's VTEP address. show vxlan config-sanity detail shows Peer VTEP IP or MLAG VTEP IP as FAIL. The book's rule is that both MLAG peers must use the same VTEP source IP (AW Ch 21, p. 118). Fix: ip address 10.255.1.3/32 on leaf2 Loopback1.

Break / fix 6.1-C · VTEP loopback not advertised

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4:

leaf3(config)# ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
leaf3(config-ip-pfx)# no seq 20

Symptom: Vxlan1 is up everywhere, the flood list looks right, but no traffic crosses between pods.

Root cause and fix

The route map RM-CONN-2-BGP only redistributes connected prefixes that match the prefix list. Without seq 20, 10.255.1.5/32 is never advertised, so show ip route 10.255.1.5 on leaf1 has no entry and the encapsulated packets have nowhere to go. Always check underlay reachability from VTEP to VTEP: ping 10.255.1.5 source 10.255.1.3. Fix: seq 20 permit 10.255.1.0/27 eq 32.

Break / fix 6.1-D · VNI entered in dotted notation

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4. A colleague enabled dotted notation and then typed what they thought was VNI 10030 the "dotted way":

leaf3(config)# vxlan vni notation dotted
leaf3(config)# interface Vxlan1
leaf3(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan vlan 30 vni 0.100.30

Symptom: VLAN 30 fails between pods, VLAN 10 and 20 work. show interfaces vxlan1 on leaf3 shows VNIs like 0.39.26 that do not look anything like leaf1's 10010.

Root cause and fix

vxlan vni notation dotted makes EOS display and parse a VNI as three octets of its 24-bit value (AW Ch 21, p. 114). The book's example: VNI 10100 displays as 0.39.116 (0x002774), and typing 0.10.100 does not mean 10100 but 10 × 256 + 100 = 2660. Here 0.100.30 is 100 × 256 + 30 = 25630, not 10030, so the VNIs no longer match. Our 10030 in dotted form is 0.39.46. Fix: no vxlan vni notation dotted, then vxlan vlan 30 vni 10030 on leaf3 and leaf4, and compare show vxlan vni across all leaves. Rule: use one notation fabric-wide, and convert before you compare outputs from two switches.

Break / fix 6.1-E · Flood list overwritten instead of extended

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4 (with static flood lists still in place). Someone pre-stages a future pod VTEP 10.255.1.9 by typing a second flood-list line without add:

leaf3(config)# interface Vxlan1
leaf3(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan flood vtep 10.255.1.9

Symptom: after clear arp vrf H30 on both hosts, pings started on host1 still work, but pings started on host2 toward host1 fail until host1 sends something first.

Root cause and fix

Without the add keyword, vxlan flood vtep replaces the list (AW Ch 21, p. 114). POD_B's list now holds only 10.255.1.9, so host2's ARP broadcasts never reach POD_A. host1-initiated traffic still works because host1's ARP request reaches POD_B through POD_A's list, and leaf3 learns host1's MAC from the data plane, so the unicast reply has a destination. show vxlan flood vtep on leaf3 shows the problem immediately. Fix: vxlan flood vtep 10.255.1.3 (or vxlan flood vtep add 10.255.1.3 if you want to keep 10.255.1.9), then remember to remove all static lists before 6.2.

6.2 EVPN control plane, L2 and L3 services

EVPN is an MP-BGP address family (AFI 25, SAFI 70) that replaces the flood list and data-plane learning with BGP routes. Each VTEP advertises a type-3 IMET route per VNI ("send BUM for this VNI to me"), and a type-2 route for every MAC (and MAC-IP binding) it learns locally. For routing between subnets, symmetric IRB adds an L3 VNI per VRF and type-5 routes for prefixes. In this fabric the spines are EVPN route servers: they peer eBGP multihop with every leaf from Loopback0, reflect EVPN routes, and must not change the next hop, because the next hop is the VTEP address that the remote leaf tunnels to.

Part A · EVPN peering

  1. On every leaf, create the peer group EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS that peers with spine1 (10.255.0.1) and spine2 (10.255.0.2) in AS 65100, sourced from Loopback0, eBGP multihop 3, BFD, sending standard and extended communities, with no maximum-routes limit. Activate it only in address-family evpn and make sure it is not active for IPv4.

  2. On both spines, create the same peer group toward all four leaf Loopback0 addresses with the correct remote AS, and add next-hop-unchanged.

  3. Confirm all EVPN sessions are Established before you add any service.

Solution · leaf1 and spine1 (peering) 
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS peer group
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS update-source Loopback0
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS bfd
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS ebgp-multihop 3
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS send-community
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS maximum-routes 0
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.1 peer group EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.1 remote-as 65100
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.1 description spine1_Loopback0
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.2 peer group EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.2 remote-as 65100
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.2 description spine2_Loopback0
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# address-family evpn
leaf1(config-router-bgp-af)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS activate
leaf1(config-router-bgp-af)# exit
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# address-family ipv4
leaf1(config-router-bgp-af)# no neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS activate

spine1(config)# router bgp 65100
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS peer group
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS next-hop-unchanged
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS update-source Loopback0
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS bfd
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS ebgp-multihop 3
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS send-community
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS maximum-routes 0
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.3 peer group EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.3 remote-as 65101
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.3 description leaf1_Loopback0
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.4 peer group EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.4 remote-as 65101
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.4 description leaf2_Loopback0
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.5 peer group EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.5 remote-as 65102
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.5 description leaf3_Loopback0
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.6 peer group EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.6 remote-as 65102
spine1(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.0.6 description leaf4_Loopback0
spine1(config-router-bgp)# address-family evpn
spine1(config-router-bgp-af)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS activate
spine1(config-router-bgp-af)# exit
spine1(config-router-bgp)# address-family ipv4
spine1(config-router-bgp-af)# no neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS activate

leaf2 to leaf4 are identical except the ASN in router bgp (65101 for leaf2, 65102 for leaf3/leaf4). spine2 is identical to spine1. send-community with no keyword sends standard, extended and large communities; send-community extended is the minimum EVPN needs, because route targets and the router MAC are extended communities.

Verify

leaf1# show bgp evpn summary
BGP summary information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.3, local AS number 65101
Neighbor Status Codes: m - Under maintenance
  Description              Neighbor   V AS     MsgRcvd MsgSent InQ OutQ  Up/Down State  PfxRcd PfxAcc
  spine1_Loopback0         10.255.0.1 4 65100       12      11    0    0 00:02:40 Estab  0      0
  spine2_Loopback0         10.255.0.2 4 65100       12      11    0    0 00:02:39 Estab  0      0

Two sessions Established on every leaf, four on every spine. Zero prefixes is correct at this point: no MAC-VRF exists yet. If a session sits in Active or Connect, check ping 10.255.0.1 source 10.255.0.3, the multihop TTL and the update source before anything else.

Part B · L2 EVPN (MAC-VRFs, type 3 and type 2)

  1. On every leaf, create a VLAN-based MAC-VRF for VLANs 10, 20 and 30 with RD <Loopback0>:<VNI>, import and export route target <VNI>:<VNI>, and advertise locally learned MACs.

  2. Confirm the flood list is now learned from EVPN (no static list remains from 6.1).

  3. Ping host2 from host1 on VLAN 30 and VLAN 10. Then find host2's type-2 route on leaf1 and read every field of the detail output: RD, next hop, route targets, VNI, ESI.

Solution · leaf1 (MAC-VRFs) 
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# vlan 10
leaf1(config-macvrf-10)# rd 10.255.0.3:10010
leaf1(config-macvrf-10)# route-target both 10010:10010
leaf1(config-macvrf-10)# redistribute learned
leaf1(config-macvrf-10)# vlan 20
leaf1(config-macvrf-20)# rd 10.255.0.3:10020
leaf1(config-macvrf-20)# route-target both 10020:10020
leaf1(config-macvrf-20)# redistribute learned
leaf1(config-macvrf-20)# vlan 30
leaf1(config-macvrf-30)# rd 10.255.0.3:10030
leaf1(config-macvrf-30)# route-target both 10030:10030
leaf1(config-macvrf-30)# redistribute learned

On leaf2, leaf3 and leaf4 replace 10.255.0.3 with the device's own Loopback0 (10.255.0.4, .5, .6). The RTs are the same everywhere; the RD is unique per device. rd auto (as in the ATD guides) also works and builds the RD from the router-id.

Verify

leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type imet
BGP routing table information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.3, local AS number 65101
Route status codes: * - valid, > - active, S - Stale, E - ECMP head, e - ECMP

Origin codes: i - IGP, e - EGP, ? - incomplete

          Network                Next Hop              Metric  LocPref Weight  Path

                                 -                     -       -       0       i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.5:10010 imet 10.255.1.5
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 *  ec    RD: 10.255.0.5:10010 imet 10.255.1.5
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.6:10010 imet 10.255.1.5

                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
...

Notice that each remote route appears twice (once via each spine) and that the next hop is the VTEP Loopback1, never a spine address. leaf2's routes are missing on purpose: they arrive through the spines with AS path 65100 65101, and eBGP loop prevention drops any path that contains leaf1's own AS. That is harmless here because both MLAG peers share VTEP 10.255.1.3 and sync MACs over the peer link. Keep this behaviour in mind for 6.3, where it stops being harmless.

IMET routes from 10.255.1.5 for all three VNIs, flood list source EVPN, learning via EVPN, host-to-host pings succeed on VLAN 10 and 30, and host2's MAC-IP route carries RT 10010:10010 and VNI 10010 with next hop 10.255.1.5. Both leaf3 and leaf4 advertise it (two RDs, same next hop), because MLAG syncs the MAC and ARP entry.

leaf1# show mac address-table vlan 10
leaf1# show vxlan address-table evpn

          Mac Address Table
