  30  001c.73c2.0102  EVPN      Vx1  10.255.1.5       1       0:02:40 ago
Total Remote Mac Addresses for this criterion: 2

001c.73c2.0102, VLAN 10, seq 1, pref 16, evpnDynamicRemoteMac, source: BGP
   VTEP 10.255.1.5

Type is EVPN (not DYNAMIC as with data-plane learning), and l2rib shows BGP as the source that programmed the MAC. l2rib is the layer between the BGP table and hardware: a MAC in BGP but not in l2rib means an import problem, a MAC in l2rib but not in the address table means a programming problem.

Part C · L3 EVPN with symmetric IRB

In symmetric IRB the ingress VTEP routes the packet into the tenant's L3 VNI, and the egress VTEP routes it again into the destination VLAN. Both VTEPs do a lookup, so each VTEP needs only the VLANs it has hosts on, plus the VRF. Type-2 routes now carry two labels (L2 VNI and L3 VNI) and the router MAC, and each VTEP installs remote hosts as /32 routes in the VRF. Type-5 routes carry prefixes: the subnets, Loopback100, and the default route core1 injects at the border leaves.

  1. Create VRF TENANT-A on all leaves and enable routing in it.

  2. Configure anycast gateways: Vlan10 10.10.10.1/24 and Vlan20 10.20.20.1/24 in TENANT-A on all four leaves using ip address virtual, and the virtual router MAC 00:1c:73:00:dc:01. VLAN 30 stays L2 only.

  3. Map VRF TENANT-A to L3 VNI 50001 on Vxlan1.

  4. Configure the BGP VRF with RD <Loopback0>:50001, EVPN import and export RT 50001:50001, router-id equal to Loopback0, and redistribute connected subnets except the MLAG per-VRF peering /31.

  5. Add per-VRF MLAG iBGP: VLAN 3001 (trunk group MLAG), SVI Vlan3001 in TENANT-A with the same /31 addresses as Vlan4093, and a BGP neighbor in the VRF that reuses the MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER peer group. Explain why this session exists.

  6. Add Loopback100 in TENANT-A (10.255.10.3/.4/.5/.6) and ip address virtual source-nat vrf TENANT-A address <Loopback100> so that pings sourced from the switch inside the VRF use a unique address instead of the shared anycast gateway.

  7. Test: routed ping from host1 VLAN 10 to host2 VLAN 20 (ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102), and from host1 to a prefix behind core1 (ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8 if core1 has 8.8.8.0/24 on a loopback; otherwise check the route only).

Solution · leaf1 (L3 EVPN, full) 
leaf1(config)# vrf instance TENANT-A
leaf1(config-vrf-TENANT-A)# exit
leaf1(config)# ip routing vrf TENANT-A
leaf1(config)# ip virtual-router mac-address 00:1c:73:00:dc:01
leaf1(config)# interface Vlan10
leaf1(config-if-Vl10)# description APP
leaf1(config-if-Vl10)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf1(config-if-Vl10)# ip address virtual 10.10.10.1/24
leaf1(config-if-Vl10)# interface Vlan20
leaf1(config-if-Vl20)# description DB
leaf1(config-if-Vl20)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf1(config-if-Vl20)# ip address virtual 10.20.20.1/24
leaf1(config-if-Vl20)# exit
leaf1(config)# vlan 3001
leaf1(config-vlan-3001)# name MLAG_L3_VRF_TENANT-A
leaf1(config-vlan-3001)# trunk group MLAG
leaf1(config-vlan-3001)# exit
leaf1(config)# interface Vlan3001
leaf1(config-if-Vl3001)# description MLAG_L3_VRF_TENANT-A
leaf1(config-if-Vl3001)# mtu 1500
leaf1(config-if-Vl3001)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf1(config-if-Vl3001)# ip address 10.255.251.0/31
leaf1(config-if-Vl3001)# interface Loopback100
leaf1(config-if-Lo100)# description DIAG_VRF_TENANT-A
leaf1(config-if-Lo100)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf1(config-if-Lo100)# ip address 10.255.10.3/32
leaf1(config-if-Lo100)# exit
leaf1(config)# ip address virtual source-nat vrf TENANT-A address 10.255.10.3
leaf1(config)# interface Vxlan1
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan vrf TENANT-A vni 50001
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# exit
leaf1(config)# ip prefix-list PL-MLAG-PEER-VRFS
leaf1(config-ip-pfx)# seq 10 permit 10.255.251.0/31
leaf1(config-ip-pfx)# exit
leaf1(config)# route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS deny 10
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS)# match ip address prefix-list PL-MLAG-PEER-VRFS
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS)# route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS permit 20
leaf1(config-route-map-RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS)# exit
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# rd 10.255.0.3:50001
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# route-target import evpn 50001:50001
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# route-target export evpn 50001:50001
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# router-id 10.255.0.3
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# neighbor 10.255.251.1 peer group MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# neighbor 10.255.251.1 description leaf2_Vlan3001
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# end
leaf1# write memory
NodeVlan3001 IPVRF neighborPL-MLAG-PEER-VRFSLoopback100 / source-natVRF RD
leaf110.255.251.0/3110.255.251.1 (leaf2_Vlan3001)10.255.251.0/3110.255.10.310.255.0.3:50001
leaf210.255.251.1/3110.255.251.0 (leaf1_Vlan3001)10.255.251.0/3110.255.10.410.255.0.4:50001
leaf310.255.251.4/3110.255.251.5 (leaf4_Vlan3001)10.255.251.4/3110.255.10.510.255.0.5:50001
leaf410.255.251.5/3110.255.251.4 (leaf3_Vlan3001)10.255.251.4/3110.255.10.610.255.0.6:50001

Border leaves leaf3 and leaf4 also peer with core1 inside the VRF (leaf3 to 10.255.254.1, leaf4 to 10.255.254.3, remote-as 65000, activated under address-family ipv4 in the VRF). If Lab D5 or the border lab did not add that yet, copy it from labs/avd/intended/configs/leaf3.cfg. Routes learned from core1 are exported into EVPN as type-5 automatically because they are BGP routes in a VRF with an EVPN export RT.

Why the per-VRF iBGP session: if leaf1 loses both uplinks, routed traffic for TENANT-A that lands on leaf1 still needs a path. The Vlan3001 session gives leaf1 the VRF routes from leaf2, and leaf2 routes via the fabric. It also lets each peer learn the other's non-shared VRF prefixes (Loopback100) without a hairpin through the spines.

Verify

host1# ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102 repeat 3
leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A

10.20.20.101      0:00:39  001c.73c1.0101  Vlan20, Port-Channel5
10.20.20.102            -  001c.73c2.0102  Vlan20, Vxlan1

The MAC-IP route now carries both RTs (MAC-VRF and IP-VRF), the L3 VNI 50001 and the router MAC. Remote ARP entries are learned from EVPN (age shown as -) and point at Vxlan1. Routed pings work in both directions and from both hosts.

Part D · Asymmetric IRB variant (exercise)

In asymmetric IRB the ingress VTEP routes the packet into the destination VLAN locally and then bridges it over that VLAN's L2 VNI. The egress VTEP only bridges. There is no L3 VNI and no type-5. The cost: every VTEP must have every VLAN, VNI and SVI of the tenant, because it must be able to route into any of them. The gain: simpler, and it interoperates with older VTEPs.

  1. Before you change anything, save the current state: show ip route vrf TENANT-A, show ip arp vrf TENANT-A, and the TTL of ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102. Take a checkpoint (configure checkpoint save pre-asym) on each leaf.

  2. Convert the fabric to asymmetric IRB: on every leaf remove the VRF-to-VNI mapping and the EVPN route targets from the BGP VRF. Keep VLANs 10 and 20 with their VNIs and anycast SVIs on every leaf (in our fabric they are already everywhere, which is exactly the asymmetric requirement).

  3. Compare: the routing table, ARP table, TTL and the external default route.

  4. Restore symmetric IRB (configure replace checkpoint:pre-asym or re-add the lines) and confirm the original state.

Solution · asymmetric change on leaf1 and what to expect 
leaf1(config)# interface Vxlan1
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# no vxlan vrf TENANT-A vni 50001
leaf1(config-if-Vx1)# exit
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# no route-target import evpn 50001:50001
leaf1(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# no route-target export evpn 50001:50001
CheckSymmetric IRBAsymmetric IRB
show ip route vrf TENANT-A/32 host routes and type-5 prefixes "via VTEP ... VNI 50001"Only connected subnets (plus Vlan3001 iBGP routes); no "via VTEP" entries
show ip arp vrf TENANT-ARemote hosts in Vlan10 and Vlan20 via Vxlan1Same entries; ARP is now what drives forwarding to remote hosts
Type-2 MAC-IP extended communitiesL2 RT + 50001:50001 + router MAC, L3 VNI 50001L2 RT only, no L3 VNI
TTL seen by host1 on ping vrf H10 10.20.20.10262 (two routing hops)63 (one routing hop, at the ingress leaf)
ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8 from host1Works via type-5 default from leaf3/leaf4Fails: no type-5, so POD_A never learns core1's routes
VLANs/VNIs required per VTEPOnly local VLANs + VRF L3 VNIAll tenant VLANs, VNIs and SVIs on every VTEP

Part E · VLAN-based vs VLAN-aware bundle

So far each VLAN is its own EVPN instance (VLAN-based service): one RD and RT per VLAN, ethernet tag 0 in the routes. A VLAN-aware bundle puts many VLANs into one EVPN instance with one RD and RT, and the routes carry the VNI as the ethernet tag so the receiver can tell the VLANs apart. Bundles cut configuration and route-target count for tenants with many VLANs; VLAN-based gives per-VLAN control of import and export. Both ends must use the same model, because the RTs differ.

  1. Optional, on all four leaves together: replace the three VLAN MAC-VRFs with one bundle TENANT-A, RD <Loopback0>:1, RT 1:1, redistribute learned, VLANs 10,20,30. Verify with show bgp evpn instance and compare a type-2 route before and after. Then revert to VLAN-based, because later labs and the AVD reference use VLAN-based.

Solution · VLAN-aware bundle on leaf1 
leaf1(config)# router bgp 65101
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# no vlan 10
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# no vlan 20
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# no vlan 30
leaf1(config-router-bgp)# vlan-aware-bundle TENANT-A
leaf1(config-macvrf-TENANT-A)# rd 10.255.0.3:1
leaf1(config-macvrf-TENANT-A)# route-target both 1:1
leaf1(config-macvrf-TENANT-A)# redistribute learned
leaf1(config-macvrf-TENANT-A)# vlan 10,20,30

Expected change in show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip: RD: 10.255.0.5:1 mac-ip 10010 001c.73c2.0102 10.10.10.102 (the VNI appears as the ethernet tag) instead of RD: 10.255.0.5:10010 mac-ip 001c.73c2.0102 10.10.10.102. show bgp evpn instance shows "Service interface: VLAN-aware bundle". Revert with no vlan-aware-bundle TENANT-A and re-add the per-VLAN blocks from Part B.

Exam tip. Build EVPN in layers and verify each before the next: EVPN sessions, then IMET per VNI, then MAC-IP, then the VRF routing table. If a scenario hands you a broken fabric, follow the troubleshooting flow at the end of this lab in order; most faults sit in one of the first three layers. Remember that a configuration that looks right in show run is graded on behaviour, so always finish with host-to-host pings both bridged and routed.
Real world. eBGP everywhere with spines as route servers is the AVD default and the most common Arista design. Some shops prefer iBGP overlay with route reflectors on the spines; then next-hop-unchanged is not needed (RRs do not change the next hop) but you need the RR client configuration instead. AVD lets you choose with overlay_routing_protocol.

Break / fix 6.2-A · Route-target mismatch

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4:

leaf3(config)# router bgp 65102
leaf3(config-router-bgp)# vlan 10
leaf3(config-macvrf-10)# no route-target both 10010:10010
leaf3(config-macvrf-10)# route-target both 10011:10011

Symptom: VLAN 10 pings between hosts fail; VLAN 20 and VLAN 30 work. show bgp evpn summary looks normal.

Root cause and fix

leaf1 receives leaf3's IMET and MAC-IP routes for VLAN 10 (spines reflect everything), but they carry RT 10011:10011, which matches no local import RT, so they are not imported. leaf1's VLAN 10 flood list does not include 10.255.1.5 and host2's MAC is missing from show vxlan address-table evpn. Compare show bgp evpn route-type imet vni 10010 on both sides and look at the RT in the detail output, or use show bgp evpn instance. Fix: restore route-target both 10010:10010.

Break / fix 6.2-B · Missing redistribute learned

Inject on leaf3 and leaf4: router bgp 65102 / vlan 20 / no redistribute learned.

Symptom: host1 can still ping 10.20.20.102 from VLAN 20 sometimes, but ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102 (routed) fails.

Root cause and fix

Without redistribute learned POD_B never advertises type-2 routes for VLAN 20, so leaf1 has no MAC-IP route and no /32 for 10.20.20.102. Bridged traffic can still work by flooding unknown unicast to the IMET list (IMET is still sent), which hides the fault. Routed traffic in symmetric IRB needs the host route, so it fails. show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip vni 10020 on leaf1 shows nothing from 10.255.1.5. Fix: redistribute learned in the MAC-VRF.

Break / fix 6.2-C · Communities not sent

Inject on spine1 and spine2: router bgp 65100 / no neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS send-community.

Symptom: all EVPN sessions stay Established and PfxRcd counts look normal, but nothing crosses pods: no remote VTEPs, no remote MACs, no type-5 routes.

Root cause and fix

Spines strip all communities when they reflect, so routes reach the leaves without route targets (and without the tunnel encapsulation and router MAC extended communities). With no RT, the leaves import nothing. show bgp evpn route-type imet detail on leaf1 shows routes from 10.255.1.5 with no "Extended Community" line. Fix: neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS send-community (or at least send-community extended), then clear bgp evpn soft out if routes do not refresh.

Break / fix 6.2-D · Missing next-hop-unchanged on the spines

Inject on spine1 and spine2: no neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS next-hop-unchanged, then clear bgp evpn soft out.

Symptom: sessions up, routes imported, but cross-pod traffic fails. show vxlan vtep on leaf1 lists 10.255.0.1 and 10.255.0.2.

Root cause and fix

eBGP rewrites the next hop to itself by default, so EVPN routes arrive with next hop spine Loopback0 instead of the remote VTEP. leaf1 builds tunnels to the spines, which are not VTEPs and drop the traffic. The next hop column in show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip shows 10.255.0.1/.2. Fix: neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS next-hop-unchanged on both spines.

6.3 EVPN all-active multihoming (ESI LAG)

EVPN multihoming gives a host an active-active LAG to two or more leaves without MLAG: no peer link, no shared VTEP, each leaf keeps its own VTEP IP. The leaves recognise that they share a link by a common Ethernet Segment Identifier (ESI) and coordinate through BGP. Type-4 Ethernet Segment routes let them discover each other and elect a Designated Forwarder (DF) per VLAN, which is the only one allowed to send BUM traffic to the host. Type-1 Ethernet Auto-Discovery routes provide fast mass withdrawal (per ES) and aliasing (per EVI), so a remote VTEP can load-balance to both leaves even if only one of them learned the host's MAC. The host still needs to see one LACP partner, so both leaves present the same LACP system ID.

Tasks

  1. Plan the change. Write down what POD_B loses (MLAG peer link, shared VTEP, MLAG iBGP underlay and per-VRF peering) and what you must add so leaf3 and leaf4 still learn each other's routes through the spines when both are AS 65102. Take a checkpoint on leaf3 and leaf4 (configure checkpoint save pre-esi).

  2. Remove MLAG from leaf3 and leaf4: mlag 5 on Port-Channel5, the mlag configuration block, the peer link Port-Channel10 (shut Ethernet3-4), the SVIs and VLANs 4093, 4094 and 3001, the MLAG iBGP neighbors in the default VRF and in VRF TENANT-A, and vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id.

  3. Give each leaf its own VTEP address: leaf3 Loopback1 stays 10.255.1.5/32, leaf4 Loopback1 becomes 10.255.1.6/32. ASN (65102) and RDs (based on Loopback0) do not change.

  4. Because both leaves are AS 65102 and no longer have a direct iBGP session, allow one occurrence of the local AS in received paths, for both the underlay (so leaf3 learns 10.255.1.6) and the EVPN overlay (so they see each other's type-1 and type-4 routes).

  5. On Port-Channel5 of both leaves configure an all-active Ethernet Segment for host2: identifier 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005, ES-import route target 01:02:01:02:00:05, and LACP system ID 0102.0102.0005.

  6. Verify the ES, the DF election, type-1 and type-4 routes, and aliasing on leaf1 (host2's MAC reachable via both 10.255.1.5 and 10.255.1.6). Ping host2 from host1 bridged and routed.

  7. Optional: make leaf4 the DF for all VLANs with preference-based election (leaf4 preference 200, leaf3 preference 100), and confirm the change in show bgp evpn instance.

  8. Failure test: shut Ethernet5 on leaf3. Watch the type-1 per-ES route from 10.255.1.5 disappear on leaf1 and host2's traffic move to 10.255.1.6. Bring it back.

  9. Revert POD_B to MLAG (configure replace checkpoint:pre-esi on both leaves, or the manual steps in the solution) so the fabric matches the AVD reference for Lab D7.

Solution · leaf3 (full conversion) 
leaf3# configure checkpoint save pre-esi
leaf3# configure session esi
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no mlag configuration
leaf3(config-s-esi)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Po5)# no mlag 5
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Po5)# evpn ethernet-segment
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Po5-evpn-es)# identifier 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Po5-evpn-es)# route-target import 01:02:01:02:00:05
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Po5-evpn-es)# exit
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Po5)# lacp system-id 0102.0102.0005
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Po5)# exit
leaf3(config-s-esi)# interface Ethernet3-4
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Et3-4)# shutdown
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Et3-4)# no channel-group
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Et3-4)# exit
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no interface Port-Channel10
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no interface Vlan4094
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no interface Vlan4093
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no interface Vlan3001
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no vlan 4094
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no vlan 4093
leaf3(config-s-esi)# no vlan 3001
leaf3(config-s-esi)# interface Vxlan1
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Vx1)# no vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id
leaf3(config-s-esi-if-Vx1)# exit
leaf3(config-s-esi)# router bgp 65102
leaf3(config-s-esi-router-bgp)# no neighbor 10.255.251.5
leaf3(config-s-esi-router-bgp)# neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS allowas-in 1
leaf3(config-s-esi-router-bgp)# neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS allowas-in 1
leaf3(config-s-esi-router-bgp)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf3(config-s-esi-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# no neighbor 10.255.251.5
leaf3(config-s-esi-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# exit
leaf3(config-s-esi-router-bgp)# exit
leaf3(config-s-esi)# show session-config diffs
leaf3(config-s-esi)# commit

leaf4 is the same, plus the new VTEP address, and it removes its own MLAG peer (10.255.251.4) instead:

leaf4(config-s-esi)# interface Loopback1
leaf4(config-s-esi-if-Lo1)# ip address 10.255.1.6/32
leaf4(config-s-esi-if-Lo1)# exit
leaf4(config-s-esi)# router bgp 65102
leaf4(config-s-esi-router-bgp)# no neighbor 10.255.251.4
leaf4(config-s-esi-router-bgp)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf4(config-s-esi-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# no neighbor 10.255.251.4
Itemleaf3leaf4
Loopback1 (VTEP)10.255.1.5/32 (unchanged)10.255.1.6/32 (new)
Loopback0 / ASN / RDs10.255.0.5 / 65102 / 10.255.0.5:x (unchanged)10.255.0.6 / 65102 / 10.255.0.6:x (unchanged)
MLAG neighbor removed10.255.251.5 (default VRF and TENANT-A)10.255.251.4 (default VRF and TENANT-A)
ESI / ES-import RT0000:0000:0102:0102:0005 / 01:02:01:02:00:05 (identical on both)
LACP system ID0102.0102.0005 (identical on both)
Optional DF preference100200 (wins)
allowas-in 1IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS and EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS on both

Optional DF preference (higher preference wins):

leaf4(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf4(config-if-Po5)# evpn ethernet-segment
leaf4(config-if-Po5-evpn-es)# designated-forwarder election algorithm preference 200
leaf3(config-if-Po5-evpn-es)# designated-forwarder election algorithm preference 100

Why allowas-in: leaf4's routes reach leaf3 via the spines with AS path 65100 65102. eBGP loop prevention drops any path that contains the receiver's own AS, so without allowas-in 1 leaf3 never learns 10.255.1.6/32 or leaf4's type-1/type-4 routes, and the DF election runs with only one member. With MLAG the iBGP session on Vlan4093 hid this. The alternative is a unique ASN per leaf (common in AVD designs without MLAG), which the task rules out.

Why remove the MLAG router-MAC command: without MLAG there is no MLAG system ID, and each leaf should advertise its own router MAC as an independent VTEP.

Verify

host2# show port-channel 1 detailed | include Active|Partner
leaf3# show lacp interface Port-Channel5 | include System
   Active Ports: Ethernet1 Ethernet2

  Actor System ID: 32768,01-02-01-02-00-05

host2 bundles both links into Po1 because leaf3 and leaf4 advertise the same LACP system ID.

leaf3# show bgp evpn route-type ethernet-segment
          Network                Next Hop              Metric  LocPref Weight  Path
 * >      RD: 10.255.1.5:1 ethernet-segment 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005 10.255.1.5
                                 -                     -       -       0       i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.1.6:1 ethernet-segment 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005 10.255.1.6
                                 10.255.1.6            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 *  ec    RD: 10.255.1.6:1 ethernet-segment 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005 10.255.1.6
                                 10.255.1.6            -       100     0       65100 65102 i

Type-4 routes are imported only by leaves that have the ES-import RT 01:02:01:02:00:05 locally, so leaf1 and leaf2 do not import them. The AS path 65100 65102 is only accepted because of allowas-in.

leaf3# show bgp evpn instance vlan 10
EVPN instance: VLAN 10
  Route distinguisher: 10.255.0.5:10010
  Route target import: Route-Target-AS:10010:10010
  Route target export: Route-Target-AS:10010:10010
  Service interface: VLAN-based
  Local VXLAN IP address: 10.255.1.5
  VXLAN: enabled
  MPLS: disabled
  Local ethernet segment:
    ESI: 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005
      Interface: Port-Channel5
      Mode: all-active
      State: up
      ES-Import RT: 01:02:01:02:00:05
      DF election algorithm: modulus
      Designated forwarder: 10.255.1.5
      Non-Designated forwarder: 10.255.1.6

With the default modulus algorithm the DF for VLAN V is the candidate at index (V mod N) in the list of VTEP IPs sorted ascending. N = 2 and VLANs 10, 20 and 30 are all even, so leaf3 (10.255.1.5, index 0) is DF for all three. After the optional preference change the algorithm shows "preference" and leaf4 becomes DF.

leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type auto-discovery
          Network                Next Hop              Metric  LocPref Weight  Path
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.5:10010 auto-discovery 0 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.6:10010 auto-discovery 0 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005
                                 10.255.1.6            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 ...
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.1.5:1 auto-discovery 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.1.6:1 auto-discovery 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005
                                 10.255.1.6            -       100     0       65100 65102 i

The routes with the MAC-VRF RD and ethernet tag 0 are per-EVI type-1 routes (aliasing). The routes with the auto-generated RD are per-ES type-1 routes (mass withdrawal, and they carry the all-active flag).

host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 repeat 3
host1# ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102 repeat 3
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip 10.10.10.102 detail | include entry|ESI
leaf1# show l2rib output mac 001c.73c2.0102
leaf1# show vxlan address-table evpn
BGP routing table entry for mac-ip 001c.73c2.0102 10.10.10.102, Route Distinguisher: 10.255.0.5:10010
      VNI: 10010 L3 VNI: 50001 ESI: 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005

001c.73c2.0102, VLAN 10, seq 1, pref 16, evpnDynamicRemoteMac, source: BGP
   Remote VTEP list: 10.255.1.5, 10.255.1.6

VLAN  Mac Address     Type      Prt  VTEP             Moves   Last Move
----  -----------     ----      ---  ----             -----   ---------
  10  001c.73c2.0102  EVPN      Vx1  10.255.1.5       1       0:00:44 ago
                                     10.255.1.6

The type-2 route carries the non-zero ESI. Even if only leaf3 learned host2's MAC, leaf1 resolves it to both VTEPs through the per-EVI type-1 routes (aliasing) and ECMPs across them. show vxlan vtep on leaf1 now lists 10.255.1.5 and 10.255.1.6. Pings work bridged and routed, and the VRF routes for host2 show two VTEP next hops.

Revert POD_B to MLAG (manual steps) 
leaf3(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf3(config-if-Po5)# no evpn ethernet-segment
leaf3(config-if-Po5)# no lacp system-id
leaf3(config-if-Po5)# mlag 5
leaf3(config-if-Po5)# router bgp 65102
leaf3(config-router-bgp)# no neighbor IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS allowas-in
leaf3(config-router-bgp)# no neighbor EVPN-OVERLAY-PEERS allowas-in
leaf4(config)# interface Loopback1
leaf4(config-if-Lo1)# ip address 10.255.1.5/32

Then re-add, on both leaves, VLANs 4093/4094/3001 with trunk group MLAG, Port-Channel10 on Ethernet3-4 (and no shutdown), the Vlan4093/4094/3001 SVIs, mlag configuration, the MLAG BGP neighbors in the default VRF and TENANT-A, and vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id, using the values in labs/avd/intended/configs/leaf3.cfg and leaf4.cfg. configure replace checkpoint:pre-esi does all of this in one step and is what you should use on the exam if you saved a checkpoint. Verify with show mlag and show vxlan config-sanity detail.

Exam tip. Three values must match on every leaf of the segment: the ESI, the ES-import route target, and the LACP system ID. The ES-import RT is conventionally the middle 6 bytes of the ESI written as a MAC (0102:0102:0005 becomes 01:02:01:02:00:05). If the task gives you only the ESI, derive the RT that way and say so in your notes. Check the DF with show bgp evpn instance, not by guessing.
Real world. ESI multihoming removes the peer link, MLAG ISSU coordination and the pair-wise failure domain, and it scales beyond two leaves. MLAG remains more common in Arista data centers because it is mature, works for orphan ports and non-EVPN designs, and handles L3 peering at the pair. Many fabrics use MLAG at the top of rack and ESI for specific cases such as border leaves or DCI.
Watch out. If you forget allowas-in with a shared ASN, the ES appears to work (host bundles, local traffic flows) but each leaf thinks it is the only member and elects itself DF, so the host receives duplicate BUM frames.

Break / fix 6.3-A · ESI mismatch

Inject on leaf4: interface Port-Channel5 / evpn ethernet-segment / identifier 0000:0000:0102:0102:0006.

Symptom: host2 still bundles both links (LACP is unchanged), but host2 sees duplicate replies to broadcasts, and leaf1 shows host2's MAC with a single VTEP that changes over time.

Root cause and fix

leaf3 and leaf4 now advertise two different Ethernet Segments. Each one is DF for its own segment, so both forward BUM to host2 (duplicates), and there is no aliasing: the per-EVI type-1 routes for each ESI come from one VTEP only. show bgp evpn route-type ethernet-segment on leaf3 shows only its own ES, and show bgp evpn instance lists no non-DF. Fix: set identifier 0000:0000:0102:0102:0005 on leaf4.

Break / fix 6.3-B · LACP system-ID mismatch

Inject on leaf4: interface Port-Channel5 / lacp system-id 0102.0102.0006.

Symptom: the ES routes look correct, but host2 has only one active member in Port-Channel1 and half the bandwidth.

Root cause and fix

host2 sees two different LACP partners, so it cannot aggregate the two links into one LAG. One link stays active, the other is suspended or individual. host2# show lacp neighbor shows two partner system IDs; show port-channel 1 detailed shows one inactive port. EVPN does not check LACP, which is why the BGP side looks healthy. Fix: lacp system-id 0102.0102.0005 on leaf4.

Break / fix 6.3-C · ES-import RT mismatch

Inject on leaf4: under the ethernet-segment, route-target import 01:02:01:02:00:06.

Symptom: duplicates to host2 again, and each leaf believes it is the only DF.

Root cause and fix

Type-4 routes are filtered by the ES-import RT. leaf3 does not import leaf4's type-4 route and vice versa, so the DF election runs with one candidate on each side. show bgp evpn route-type ethernet-segment on leaf3 shows only its own route. Fix: matching route-target import 01:02:01:02:00:05.

EVPN route-type cheat sheet

TypeNameSent whenKey fieldsUsed forEOS command
1Ethernet Auto-Discovery (EAD)An ESI is configured and up. Per-ES and per-EVI variantsESI, ethernet tag (0 per EVI), MPLS/VNI label, ESI label ext-community with all-active flagMass withdrawal on link failure (per ES), aliasing / load balancing (per EVI), split horizonshow bgp evpn route-type auto-discovery
2MAC/IP AdvertisementA MAC (and its ARP/ND binding) is learned locally and redistribute learned is setMAC, optional IP, L2 VNI, optional L3 VNI, ESI, RTs, router MACRemote MAC learning, ARP suppression, /32 host routes in symmetric IRB, MAC mobility (sequence numbers)show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip
3Inclusive Multicast Ethernet Tag (IMET)A VLAN has a VNI and a MAC-VRFOriginating VTEP IP, VNI, PMSI tunnel attribute (ingress replication)Builds the BUM flood list per VNI (replaces static vxlan flood vtep)show bgp evpn route-type imet
4Ethernet SegmentAn ESI is configuredESI, originating VTEP IP, ES-import RTDiscovery of leaves sharing a segment and DF electionshow bgp evpn route-type ethernet-segment
5IP PrefixA prefix is in a VRF with an EVPN export RT (connected via redistribute, BGP routes automatically)Prefix, L3 VNI, router MAC, RTsSubnets, external routes (default from border), silent hosts reachable by prefixshow bgp evpn route-type ip-prefix ipv4

EVPN troubleshooting flow

Work from the bottom up. Each step depends on the one before it, so the first step that fails is where the fault is. Run it from the ingress leaf (leaf1) toward the destination (host2 behind POD_B).

StepQuestionCommand (on leaf1)Healthy resultTypical faults
1. UnderlayCan my VTEP reach the remote VTEP and the spines' Loopback0?show ip bgp summary, show ip route 10.255.1.5, ping 10.255.1.5 source 10.255.1.3Sessions Established, two ECMP paths, ping worksLoopback not advertised (prefix-list), P2P down, MTU
2. EVPN sessionsIs the overlay control plane up?show bgp evpn summaryEstab to both spines, prefixes receivedWrong update-source, missing ebgp-multihop, AF not activated, wrong remote-as
3. IMETDo I know who to flood to for this VNI?show bgp evpn route-type imet vni 10010, show vxlan flood vtepIMET from 10.255.1.5; flood list source EVPNRT mismatch, VNI not mapped, communities stripped, next hop rewritten
4. MAC-IPDid I receive the host's MAC and IP?show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip 10.10.10.102 detailRoute with right RTs, L2 VNI, L3 VNI, router MACMissing redistribute learned, host silent, RT mismatch
5. l2ribDid BGP hand the MAC to the forwarding layer?show l2rib output mac 001c.73c2.0102Entry with source BGP and the remote VTEPImport failure, local MAC conflict, MAC mobility dampening
6. VXLAN tablesIs it programmed for forwarding?show vxlan address-table evpn, show mac address-table, show ip route vrf TENANT-A, show ip arp vrf TENANT-AMAC behind Vx1 and the right VTEP; /32 via VTEP VNI 50001VRF-to-VNI missing, L3 VNI mismatch, router MAC wrong, VRF RT mismatch
7. Data planeDoes traffic actually pass?host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 and ... 10.20.20.102, large ping with df-bit, show interfaces vxlan1 countersBoth pings succeed, TTL 62 when routed, counters increaseMTU, VNI mismatch on egress, anycast gateway or virtual MAC missing on one leaf

Run show vxlan config-sanity detail at any point; it catches local mapping and MLAG consistency problems quickly but knows nothing about BGP.

Exam-style scenario (60 minutes, timed)

Reset to the start state of this lab (Lab D5 end state) and do the following without looking at the solutions. Grade yourself only by the final checks.

  1. Build an EVPN VXLAN fabric so that host1 and host2 can communicate in VLAN 30 (bridged only), and between VLAN 10 and VLAN 20 (routed, symmetric IRB in VRF TENANT-A with L3 VNI 50001). Use the values in the reference tables. No static flood lists may remain.

  2. host1 must reach 8.8.8.8 (or at least have a type-5 default route via POD_B) without any static route on POD_A.

  3. Your colleague left one fault in the fabric before you started (ask a study partner to inject one break from this lab, or pick one at random). Find and fix it.

Final checks: show bgp evpn summary on all leaves and spines; show vxlan config-sanity detail clean on all leaves; ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102, ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102, ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102, ping vrf H20 10.10.10.102 from host1 all succeed; show running-config | section Vxlan1 has no flood vtep line; configs saved with write memory on every device.

Checkpoint

  • I can configure Vxlan1 from memory: source interface, UDP port, VLAN-to-VNI, VRF-to-VNI, MLAG router-MAC encapsulation, static flood list.
  • I can explain why both MLAG peers share Loopback1 but keep separate Loopback0 addresses and RDs.
  • I can state the VXLAN overhead (50 bytes untagged) and the underlay MTU I would choose.
  • I can build EVPN peering to route-server spines and say why next-hop-unchanged, send-community and ebgp-multihop are needed.
  • I can read a type-2 detail output and name the RD, RTs, L2 VNI, L3 VNI, router MAC and ESI.
  • I can explain symmetric vs asymmetric IRB and show the difference in the VRF routing table and TTL.
  • I can configure a VLAN-aware bundle and explain when I would prefer it.
  • I can convert an MLAG pair to ESI multihoming, including the allowas-in requirement with a shared ASN, and verify DF, type-1 and type-4 routes and aliasing.
  • I can walk the seven-step troubleshooting flow without notes.
  • POD_B is back in MLAG and the fabric matches the AVD reference configs.

Domain 7 · Engineering exam

Lab D7 · Automation and management

You build and change the same L3LS EVPN fabric three ways: with CloudVision configlets (and their "as code" twin, eAPI), with CloudVision Studios, and with Arista Validated Designs (AVD) plus ANTA. The exam grades the result on the switches, not the tool, so the goal is to know each workflow well enough to deliver a correct end state quickly and to prove it. The retired ACE:L5 (Arista Cloud Engineer Level 5) certification was an automation track built on CloudVision, Ansible and Python; this lab covers that ground too.

Objectives
7.1, 7.2, 7.3
Time
5 h (7.1: 1.5 h, 7.2: 1.5 h on CloudVision or 45 min on Path B, 7.3: 2 h)
Start state
7.1 and 7.2 Path A: an Academy, ATD or CVaaS lab with CloudVision. Path B and 7.3: the containerlab fabric at day 0 (startup configs only) or at the end of Lab D6.
Devices
spine1, spine2, leaf1 to leaf4 (you configure); host1, host2, core1 (pre-built); your workstation (Python 3, curl, optionally Ansible)

Read first: AW Ch 15 · CloudVision · AW Ch 30 · eAPI · AW Ch 12 · Bash · AW Ch 31 · Containers · AW Ch 32 · vEOS · ATD: CloudVision Studios Layer 3 Leaf-Spine · ATD: Studios Day 2 Operations · AVD docs · ANTA docs

Start state, workstation setup and the two paths

Objectives 7.1 and 7.2 are CloudVision workflows. CloudVision is not free, so each of them has two paths. Path A is the real thing: use the Arista Academy lab that came with your DCENG or DCOPS course, an Arista Test Drive (ATD) session, or a CVaaS tenant if your employer has one. Path B reaches the same intent without CloudVision, using eAPI, Python and AVD against the free containerlab fabric. Path B teaches the concepts (intended configuration, hierarchy, review before push, validation after push), but it does not replace clicking through CloudVision. The exam lab gives you CloudVision, and the clock punishes anyone who meets the Provisioning screens for the first time on exam day. Do Path A at least once.

Prepare your workstation (any Linux or macOS box that reaches 172.100.100.0/24, usually the containerlab host itself):

$ python3 -m venv ~/l5dc && source ~/l5dc/bin/activate
$ pip install pyeapi requests pyyaml "pyavd==6.4.*" "anta==1.10.*"
$ cd arista-l5-dc-training
$ sudo containerlab deploy -t labs/topology/l5dc.clab.yml
$ curl -sk -u admin:admin https://172.100.100.21/command-api \
    -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"runCmds","params":{"version":1,"cmds":["show hostname"]},"id":1}'

Outputs are representative and trimmed; your timers, MACs and counters will differ.

{"jsonrpc": "2.0", "id": 1, "result": [{"fqdn": "leaf1", "hostname": "leaf1"}]}

If that answer comes back, eAPI works and you are ready. The day-0 startup configs enable management api http-commands (HTTPS) and management api gnmi on every node, so you never need the console after the first boot.

From an Ubuntu automation server with Ansible

If you already run Ansible from a separate Ubuntu server (for example next to EVE-NG), run Lab D7 from there. The repository's AVD inventory already sets the eAPI connection (ansible.netcommon.httpapi, HTTPS, enable as become), so you only need the collections and a route to the management network.

$ python3 -m venv ~/l5dc && source ~/l5dc/bin/activate
$ pip install ansible-core "pyavd==6.4.*" "anta==1.10.*" pyeapi requests
$ ansible-galaxy collection install "arista.avd:>=6.4.0,<6.5.0" arista.eos ansible.netcommon
$ git clone https://github.com/T-Frizz/arista-l5-dc-training.git && cd arista-l5-dc-training/labs/avd

# reachability and a first eAPI call through Ansible
$ ansible -i inventory.yml L5DC_FABRIC -m arista.eos.eos_command -a "commands='show version'"

# the AVD workflow: build, dry run, deploy, validate
$ ansible-playbook -i inventory.yml playbooks/build.yml
$ ansible-playbook -i inventory.yml playbooks/deploy.yml --check --diff
$ ansible-playbook -i inventory.yml playbooks/deploy.yml
$ ansible-playbook -i inventory.yml playbooks/validate.yml

Keep the arista.avd collection on the same release line as pyavd; the collection uses pyavd internally and refuses to run with a mismatched version. arista.eos modules such as eos_command, eos_config and eos_facts are handy for one-off tasks, but in this lab make lasting changes through the AVD data model, as objective 7.3 does. The Python scripts in this lab (eAPI, pyeapi, ANTA) run unchanged from the same server.

7.1 CloudVision engineering workflow with configlets

Before Studios, every CloudVision fabric was built from configlets: blocks of EOS CLI attached to containers (groups of devices) or to single devices. The device's designed configuration is the merge of every configlet from the top container down to the device, and CloudVision pushes the difference between designed and running configuration as a task, usually grouped into a change control. The DCENG labs build L2LS, L3LS and EVPN this way, and the Operations exam expects you to read and fix configlet hierarchies. The engineering skill is choosing what goes where so that one change touches one configlet.

Path A · CloudVision Portal

  1. Build the container hierarchy so that settings shared by the whole fabric, by all leaves, and by one MLAG pod each have exactly one home. Use: Tenant > L5DC > SPINES and LEAVES; under LEAVES create POD_A and POD_B. Move spine1/spine2 into SPINES, leaf1/leaf2 into POD_A, leaf3/leaf4 into POD_B.

    UI: Provisioning > Network Provisioning, right-click the parent container, Add > Container. Drag devices onto containers (or right-click the device, Move). Nothing is pushed yet: moves create pending tasks.

  2. Create a configlet L5DC-BASE with everything identical on all six switches (NTP, DNS, local user, multi-agent model, vlan internal order) and apply it to the L5DC container.

    ! L5DC-BASE (applied to container L5DC)
service routing protocols model multi-agent
vlan internal order ascending range 1006 1199
ip name-server vrf default 1.1.1.1
ntp local-interface Management0
ntp server 0.pool.ntp.org prefer
ip routing
router bfd
   multihop interval 300 min-rx 300 multiplier 3

  3. Create one per-device underlay configlet for each switch (leaf1-UNDERLAY, ...), holding the values that are unique per device: hostname, Management0, Loopback0/Loopback1, P2P interfaces, MLAG peer addressing, and the device's router bgp underlay. Apply each to its device. Copy the values from labs/avd/intended/configs/<device>.cfg.

  4. Create a shared services configlet per pod, POD_A-EVPN-SERVICES and POD_B-EVPN-SERVICES, holding VLANs 10/20/30, VRF TENANT-A, the anycast SVIs, the VXLAN mappings and the MAC-VRF / IP-VRF stanzas. Apply them to the POD_A and POD_B containers. Work out why a single LEAVES-EVPN-SERVICES configlet at the LEAVES level does not work as-is, and how you would make it work.

  5. Sketch a configlet builder that would generate the per-device underlay configlets from a small table, so adding leaf5 means adding one row instead of writing 120 lines.

  6. Review the pending tasks, compare designed and running configuration for leaf1, then group all tasks into one change control with the spines first and one leaf of each pair per stage. Approve and execute it. Take a snapshot before and after.

    UI: Save on Network Provisioning creates tasks. Provisioning > Tasks, select the tasks, Create Change Control. In the change control, arrange stages (series or parallel), add pre/post snapshot actions if your CVP version offers them, then Review and Approve, Approve, Execute Change Control.

Solution · why one shared services configlet fails, and the fix

The services block lives under router bgp <ASN>, and the ASN differs per pod (65101 vs 65102). A configlet that says router bgp 65101 on leaf3 is rejected (EOS answers % BGP is already running with AS number 65102) and the task fails. The route distinguisher also contains the device's Loopback0. Two fixes, both used in production:

  • One services configlet per pod (as in the task), with the pod's ASN, and rd auto so the RD is derived from the router ID on each device (supported on recent EOS; check yours with rd ? under router bgp / vlan 10).
  • A configlet builder that reads the ASN and Loopback0 per device and renders the stanza, so one builder serves every leaf.
! POD_A-EVPN-SERVICES (applied to container POD_A)
vlan 10
   name APP
vlan 20
   name DB
vlan 30
   name L2_STRETCH
!
vrf instance TENANT-A
ip routing vrf TENANT-A
ip virtual-router mac-address 00:1c:73:00:dc:01
!
interface Vlan10
   description APP
   vrf TENANT-A
   ip address virtual 10.10.10.1/24
interface Vlan20
   description DB
   vrf TENANT-A
   ip address virtual 10.20.20.1/24
!
interface Vxlan1
   vxlan source-interface Loopback1
   vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id
   vxlan udp-port 4789
   vxlan vlan 10 vni 10010
   vxlan vlan 20 vni 10020
   vxlan vlan 30 vni 10030
   vxlan vrf TENANT-A vni 50001
!
router bgp 65101
   vlan 10
      rd auto
      route-target both 10010:10010
      redistribute learned
   vlan 20
      rd auto
      route-target both 10020:10020
      redistribute learned
   vlan 30
      rd auto
      route-target both 10030:10030
      redistribute learned
   vrf TENANT-A
      rd auto
      route-target import evpn 50001:50001
      route-target export evpn 50001:50001
      redistribute connected

The AVD reference uses explicit RDs (rd 10.255.0.3:10010 on leaf1). Both are valid; the explicit form keeps the per-device value in the per-device configlet or builder.

Solution · a configlet builder for the per-device underlay

A configlet builder is a Python script stored in CloudVision. When you apply it to a container, CloudVision runs it once per device and stores the printed output as a generated configlet for that device. The script can read the device's identity from the built-in cvplibrary. Library names vary by CVP release, so treat this as the pattern, not copy-paste:

from cvplibrary import CVPGlobalVariables, GlobalVariableNames

mgmt_ip = CVPGlobalVariables.getValue(GlobalVariableNames.CVP_IP)  # also CVP_SERIAL, CVP_MAC
FABRIC = {
    # mgmt IP: (asn, lo0, lo1, [(intf, ip, peer_ip, peer_asn), ...])
    "172.100.100.21": (65101, "10.255.0.3", "10.255.1.3",
              [("Ethernet1", "10.255.255.1/31", "10.255.255.0", 65100),
               ("Ethernet2", "10.255.255.3/31", "10.255.255.2", 65100)]),
    "172.100.100.22": (65101, "10.255.0.4", "10.255.1.3",
              [("Ethernet1", "10.255.255.5/31", "10.255.255.4", 65100),
               ("Ethernet2", "10.255.255.7/31", "10.255.255.6", 65100)]),
}
asn, lo0, lo1, links = FABRIC[mgmt_ip]
print("interface Loopback0\n   ip address %s/32" % lo0)
print("interface Loopback1\n   ip address %s/32" % lo1)
for intf, ip, _, _ in links:
    print("interface %s\n   no switchport\n   mtu 1500\n   ip address %s" % (intf, ip))
print("router bgp %d\n   router-id %s" % (asn, lo0))
for _, _, peer, peer_asn in links:
    print("   neighbor %s peer group IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEERS" % peer)
    print("   neighbor %s remote-as %d" % (peer, peer_asn))

The lookup key here is the management IP CloudVision knows the device by. In practice the serial number or system MAC is more common, because those exist before the device has any configuration (ZTP).

Verify (Path A)

On leaf1, in CloudVision: Provisioning > Network Provisioning, right-click leaf1, Manage > Configlet, then Validate. The validate screen shows the designed config, the running config, and their diff. Then on the switch:

leaf1# show bgp evpn summary
leaf1# show vxlan vni
leaf1# show running-config diffs
BGP summary information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.3, local AS number 65101
Neighbor   V AS     MsgRcvd MsgSent InQ OutQ Up/Down  State  PfxRcd PfxAcc
10.255.0.1 4 65100       58      52   0    0 00:21:07 Estab  9      9
10.255.0.2 4 65100       57      53   0    0 00:21:05 Estab  9      9

Designed and running configuration match (no "Config out of sync" or compliance warning on the device), both EVPN peers are Established on every leaf, and show running-config diffs is empty after CloudVision saved the config.

Path B · The same workflow as code with eAPI

eAPI is the JSON-RPC interface that CloudVision, Ansible and ANTA all sit on (CloudVision mostly streams through TerminAttr now, but configuration push and many tools still use eAPI). If you can drive eAPI by hand, every automation tool becomes understandable.

  1. Confirm eAPI is enabled on leaf1, in which VRF, and with which protocols.

    leaf1# show management api http-commands

  2. From your workstation, run show version on leaf1 with curl and extract the EOS version and serial number with jq. Then run the same command with "format": "text" and compare.

  3. Push a change through eAPI inside a configuration session, review the diff in the same call, and commit it: add ntp server 1.pool.ntp.org on leaf1. Explain why a session is safer than plain configure when a script fails halfway.

  4. Repeat tasks 2 and 3 in Python, once with pyeapi and once with plain requests.

  5. Write a script that collects show bgp evpn summary from all four leaves, parses the JSON, and prints one line per peer with its state and accepted prefix count, exiting non-zero if any peer is not Established. (This is a tiny version of what ANTA does in 7.3.)

Solution · eAPI with curl 
$ cat > showver.json <<'EOF'
{"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": "1",
 "params": {"version": 1, "format": "json", "cmds": ["show version"]}}
EOF
$ curl -sk -u admin:admin -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
       -d @showver.json https://172.100.100.21/command-api \
  | jq -r '.result[0] | "\(.version) \(.serialNumber)"'
4.34.2F 7B9C1E2A4D...

$ cat > ntp.json <<'EOF'
{"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": "2",
 "params": {"version": 1, "format": "text", "cmds": [
   "enable",
   "configure session d7-ntp",
   "ntp server 1.pool.ntp.org",
   "show session-config diffs",
   "commit"]}}
EOF
$ curl -sk -u admin:admin -d @ntp.json https://172.100.100.21/command-api \
  | jq -r '.result[3].output'
--- system:/running-config
+++ session:/d7-ntp-session-config
@@ -48,6 +48,7 @@
 ntp local-interface Management0
 ntp server 0.pool.ntp.org prefer
+ntp server 1.pool.ntp.org

eAPI runs the commands in order and stops at the first error, returning a JSON-RPC error object (code 1002 for invalid commands) with the output of every command up to the failure. In plain configure the commands before the failure are already live: a half-applied change. In a session nothing is live until commit, so a failed call leaves an uncommitted session (visible with show configuration sessions) and the running config untouched. Add commit timer 00:05:00 instead of commit for an automatic rollback unless you confirm with a second configure session d7-ntp / commit.

Solution · pyeapi and requests 
# ~/.eapi.conf
[connection:leaf1]
host: 172.100.100.21
username: admin
password: admin
transport: https
import pyeapi

node = pyeapi.connect_to("leaf1")
ver = node.enable("show version")[0]["result"]
print(ver["version"], ver["serialNumber"])

# pyeapi wraps "configure"; use a session explicitly for an atomic change
node.run_commands([
    "configure session d7-py",
    "ntp server 1.pool.ntp.org",
    "commit",
])
print(node.enable("show running-config section ntp", encoding="text")[0]["result"]["output"])
import requests, urllib3
urllib3.disable_warnings()   # lab only: cEOS uses a self-signed certificate

def eapi(host, cmds, fmt="json"):
    r = requests.post(f"https://{host}/command-api", auth=("admin", "admin"), verify=False,
                      json={"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": 1,
                            "params": {"version": 1, "format": fmt, "cmds": cmds}}, timeout=10)
    body = r.json()
    if "error" in body:
        raise RuntimeError(body["error"]["message"])
    return body["result"]

print(eapi("172.100.100.21", ["show version"])[0]["version"])
Solution · collect show bgp evpn summary from all leaves 
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""evpn_summary.py - EVPN peer health on every leaf, via eAPI."""
import sys
import requests, urllib3

urllib3.disable_warnings()
LEAVES = {"leaf1": "172.100.100.21", "leaf2": "172.100.100.22",
          "leaf3": "172.100.100.23", "leaf4": "172.100.100.24"}

def eapi(host, cmds):
    r = requests.post(f"https://{host}/command-api", auth=("admin", "admin"), verify=False,
                      json={"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": 1,
                            "params": {"version": 1, "format": "json", "cmds": cmds}}, timeout=10)
    r.raise_for_status()
    return r.json()["result"]

bad = 0
for name, ip in LEAVES.items():
    try:
        out = eapi(ip, ["show bgp evpn summary"])[0]
    except Exception as exc:          # unreachable switch counts as a failure
        print(f"{name:6} ERROR {exc}")
        bad += 1
        continue
    peers = out["vrfs"]["default"]["peers"]
    for peer, p in sorted(peers.items()):
        state = p["peerState"]
        print(f"{name:6} {peer:15} AS{p.get('asn', '?'):6} {state:12} pfxAcc={p.get('prefixAccepted', 0)}")
        bad += state != "Established"
sys.exit(1 if bad else 0)
$ python3 evpn_summary.py; echo "exit=$?"
leaf1  10.255.0.1      AS65100  Established  pfxAcc=9
leaf1  10.255.0.2      AS65100  Established  pfxAcc=9
leaf2  10.255.0.1      AS65100  Established  pfxAcc=9
...
leaf4  10.255.0.2      AS65100  Established  pfxAcc=10
exit=0

Find the JSON keys yourself with show bgp evpn summary | json on the switch; never guess them. The exit code matters: it lets a CI pipeline or a change-window runbook stop when the fabric is unhealthy.

Verify (Path B)

leaf1# show configuration sessions detail
leaf1# show running-config section ntp
leaf1# show management api http-commands
Enabled:            Yes
HTTPS server:       running, set to use port 443
HTTP server:        shutdown, set to use port 80
VRFs:               default
Hits:               57
Last hit:           2 seconds ago

No uncommitted sessions are left behind, the NTP line is present, and your script exits 0 on a healthy fabric and 1 when you shut one leaf's uplinks.

Exam tip. In the exam, CloudVision is the provisioning tool and the grader reads the switches. Three habits save points: (1) always check the designed vs running diff before you execute, so an unexpected no ... line from a forgotten configlet does not wipe a working feature; (2) when a task fails, read the task log, which shows the exact EOS error; (3) after a change control finishes, confirm on the CLI. Know the order of configlet merging (top container first, device last, and configlet order within a level) and what "reconcile" does (captures running-only lines into a device configlet so CloudVision stops trying to remove them).
Real world. Teams that stay on configlets long term converge on the same layout you just built: one base configlet per fabric, one per-device configlet produced by a builder or by AVD, and service configlets per pod or per tenant. AVD can even upload its generated configs to CloudVision as configlets (the arista.avd.cv_deploy role), which is a common migration path from hand-written configlets to a data model.

Break / fix

Inject: push a change to leaf1 through eAPI with "configure session d7-bf", "no ntp server 0.pool.ntp.org", "commit timer 00:02:00" and do nothing else.

Symptom: two minutes later the NTP server is back, and a colleague swears the change "applied fine". show logging mentions the session.

Root cause and fix

commit timer applies the change but schedules an automatic rollback unless the commit is confirmed before the timer expires. Nobody confirmed it, so EOS rolled it back. Confirm with a second call: "configure session d7-bf", "commit" within the window. Check pending timers with show configuration sessions detail. This is the EOS equivalent of "commit confirmed" and the right default for any risky remote change (ACLs on management, routing policy) pushed by a script.

Break / fix (Path A)

Inject: in CloudVision, add a second configlet to leaf3 containing router bgp 65101 and one vlan 40 stanza, then save.

Symptom: the leaf3 task fails during execution; other devices succeed.

Root cause and fix

The task log shows % BGP is already running with AS number 65102. A configlet written for POD_A was attached to a POD_B device. Remove it from leaf3 (or correct the ASN), re-validate, and re-run the task. Prevention: attach service configlets at the pod container only, and validate one device per container before saving.

eAPI beyond the basics

This drill follows AW Ch 30 · eAPI (pp. 162-173) and adds what Path B of objective 7.1 does not cover: the Explorer, the eAPI transport options, privilege and interactive commands, a fabric-wide inventory loop, text-mode config backups, certificates, and eAPI in a VRF. The book's scripts use Python 2 and jsonrpclib; everything here is Python 3 with requests, or plain curl.

Key ideas from the chapter. Before APIs, automation meant expect scripting (send a command, wait for a prompt) and screen scraping (cut fields out of text), and both break as soon as a prompt or an output line changes (pp. 162-164). eAPI returns JSON, so you read response[0]["version"] by key, not by position (pp. 164-166). show ... | json on the CLI shows you the same keys, and key names are case-sensitive camelCase (p. 170). eAPI is configured under management api http-commands: HTTPS by default, with http, http localhost (port 8080) and unix-socket as other transports; the local transports need no username (pp. 166-167). The result is a list with one entry per command, in order. Interactive commands are not allowed, which is why scripts use reload now rather than reload, and enable can take its password as {"cmd": "enable", "input": "..."} (pp. 171-172). If the management interface is in a VRF, eAPI must be enabled in that VRF too, or you get "connection refused" (p. 167). CloudVision Portal also talks to switches through eAPI (p. 173).

  1. Open the eAPI Explorer on leaf1 in a browser (https://172.100.100.21/explorer). Run show version in the Simple Request tab and compare the Request Viewer with your showver.json from 7.1. Then run the "print switch address" example in the Script Editor and open the Command Documentation tab.

  2. On leaf1, read show management api http-commands and note the enabled transports, the VRFs, the URLs, and the per-user hit counters.

  3. Write inventory.py: one eAPI call per node for all nine EOS nodes (spines, leaves, hosts, core1), printing a table of name, model, system MAC and EOS version, and marking any node that does not answer.

  4. Send ["configure", "interface Ethernet5", "description set-by-eapi"] to leaf2 without enable. Read the error, then fix the call. Also explain why reload cannot be sent through eAPI while reload now can (do not run either).

  5. Write backup.py: save every switch's running config as text to backups/<name>-<timestamp>.cfg.

  6. On leaf1, enable the local transports and call eAPI from bash on the switch with no username: once over the Unix socket and once over http localhost. Then remove them again.

  7. Run the inventory script without verify=False, read the certificate error, and list three ways to fix it.

Solution · Explorer and status

Log in with admin/admin and accept the self-signed certificate warning. The Simple Request tab builds the same JSON-RPC body you wrote by hand ("method": "runCmds", "version": 1, "format": "json", "cmds": [...]), and the Response Viewer shows the result list. The Explorer sends real requests, so a configuration command typed there changes the switch (p. 168). The Script Editor runs JavaScript against the same API, and Command Documentation is the built-in reference for every command's JSON model, which is the fastest way to find a key name (p. 168).

leaf1# show management api http-commands
Enabled:            Yes
HTTPS server:       running, set to use port 443
HTTP server:        shutdown, set to use port 80
Local HTTP server:  shutdown, no authentication, set to use port 8080
Unix Socket server: shutdown, no authentication
VRFs:               default
Hits:               143
...
   User        Requests       Bytes in       Bytes out    Last hit
----------- -------------- -------------- --------------- ------------
   admin        143            21844          1904322     4 seconds ago

URLs
-----------------------------------------
Management0 : https://172.100.100.21:443

Representative output. The per-user table is a quick way to see which account a script or tool is really using.

Solution · inventory.py 
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""inventory.py - model, MAC and EOS version of every node, via eAPI."""
import requests, urllib3

urllib3.disable_warnings()          # lab only: self-signed certificates
NODES = {"spine1": "172.100.100.11", "spine2": "172.100.100.12",
         "leaf1": "172.100.100.21", "leaf2": "172.100.100.22",
         "leaf3": "172.100.100.23", "leaf4": "172.100.100.24",
         "host1": "172.100.100.31", "host2": "172.100.100.32",
         "core1": "172.100.100.41"}

def eapi(host, cmds, fmt="json"):
    body = {"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": 1,
            "params": {"version": 1, "format": fmt, "cmds": cmds}}
    r = requests.post(f"https://{host}/command-api", json=body,
                      auth=("admin", "admin"), verify=False, timeout=5)
    reply = r.json()
    if "error" in reply:
        raise RuntimeError(reply["error"]["message"])
    return reply["result"]

print(f"{'Node':8} {'Model':12} {'System MAC':19} Version")
print("-" * 50)
for name, ip in NODES.items():
    try:
        v = eapi(ip, ["show version"])[0]
    except Exception as exc:
        print(f"{name:8} UNREACHABLE  {exc.__class__.__name__}")
        continue
    print(f"{name:8} {v['modelName']:12} {v['systemMacAddress']:19} {v['version']}")
Node     Model        System MAC          Version
--------------------------------------------------
spine1   cEOSLab      00:1c:73:11:00:00   4.34.2F
spine2   cEOSLab      00:1c:73:12:00:00   4.34.2F
leaf1    cEOSLab      00:1c:73:21:00:00   4.34.2F
...
core1    cEOSLab      00:1c:73:41:00:00   4.34.2F

This is the book's ten-switch loop (pp. 170-171) in Python 3: a dictionary of nodes instead of building IP strings, f-strings instead of ljust, and an exception handler so one dead switch does not stop the report. Add show hostname to the same call if you want the switch to name itself; then read result[1]["hostname"].

Solution · privilege and interactive commands 
>>> eapi("172.100.100.22", ["configure", "interface Ethernet5", "description set-by-eapi"])
RuntimeError: CLI command 1 of 3 'configure' failed: invalid command
>>> eapi("172.100.100.22", ["enable", "configure", "interface Ethernet5", "description set-by-eapi"])
[{}, {}, {}, {}]
! with an enable secret configured:
>>> eapi("172.100.100.22", [{"cmd": "enable", "input": "EnableSecret"}, "configure", ...])

An eAPI session starts in unprivileged exec mode, where configure does not exist, so EOS calls it an invalid command (error code 1002) (p. 171). Put enable first. Commands that print nothing return an empty dictionary each, so four commands give [{}, {}, {}, {}], and a warning comes back in a messages key without failing the call (p. 172). eAPI cannot answer prompts: reload asks for confirmation and would hang, while reload now does not ask. Avoid top, watch and anything else that waits for input (p. 172). Undo the description afterwards, or better, make the change in a configuration session as in 7.1.

Solution · backup.py 
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""backup.py - text running-config of every switch, timestamped."""
from datetime import datetime
from pathlib import Path
from inventory import NODES, eapi     # reuse the helper and the node list

out = Path("backups"); out.mkdir(exist_ok=True)
stamp = datetime.now().strftime("%Y%m%d-%H%M%S")
for name, ip in NODES.items():
    try:
        res = eapi(ip, ["enable", "show running-config"], fmt="text")
    except Exception as exc:
        print(f"{name}: FAILED {exc}")
        continue
    path = out / f"{name}-{stamp}.cfg"
    path.write_text(res[1]["output"])
    print(f"{name}: {path} ({len(res[1]['output'])} bytes)")

Move the table-printing loop in inventory.py under if __name__ == "__main__": first, so importing it does not print the table. In text mode every result is still a dictionary in a list, with the text under the output key, and the config is in element 1 because enable is element 0 (p. 172). Text is the right format for a backup because you can feed it straight back with configure replace; the JSON form of the running config cannot be pushed back (p. 172).

Solution · on-box transports 
leaf1(config)# management api http-commands
leaf1(config-mgmt-api-http-cmds)# protocol unix-socket
leaf1(config-mgmt-api-http-cmds)# protocol http localhost
leaf1(config-mgmt-api-http-cmds)# exit
leaf1# show management api http-commands | include Local|Unix

leaf1# bash
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ BODY='{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"runCmds","id":1,"params":{"version":1,"cmds":["show version"]}}'
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ curl -s --unix-socket /var/run/command-api.sock -d "$BODY" http://localhost/command-api | head -c 300; echo
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ curl -s -d "$BODY" http://localhost:8080/command-api | head -c 300; echo
[admin@leaf1 ~]$ exit

leaf1(config)# management api http-commands
leaf1(config-mgmt-api-http-cmds)# no protocol unix-socket
leaf1(config-mgmt-api-http-cmds)# no protocol http localhost

Neither call needs a username, because only code already running on the switch can reach them (p. 167). That is what on-box scripts and event handler actions should use: no stored password, and no certificate problems. The socket path matches the unix:/var/run/command-api.sock URL that show management api http-commands lists (p. 167). The last two lines put leaf1 back to its designed config.

Solution · certificate errors

Without verify=False, requests raises an SSLError ending in CERTIFICATE_VERIFY_FAILED, because EOS uses a self-signed certificate by default. The book hit the same error when the Python on EOS started checking certificates (p. 168). The fixes, from best to worst:

  • Give eAPI a certificate signed by a CA your scripts trust: an SSL profile under management security, attached with protocol https ssl profile NAME under management api http-commands (check the exact syntax with ? on your release). Then pass the CA bundle with verify="/path/ca.pem".
  • For scripts that run on the switch itself, use the Unix socket or http localhost, which skip TLS entirely.
  • Turn off verification (verify=False, or ssl._create_unverified_context() in plain Python). Fine in this lab, but on a real network it lets anyone in the path read the password.
Exam tip. When a script fails, read the JSON-RPC error object before anything else: it names the command number and the EOS error ("CLI command 1 of 3 'configure' failed: invalid command"). Errors 1002 and 1003 usually mean a missing enable, a typo, or a command that does not exist on that platform. Run the same command on the CLI with | json to check it and to see the key names.
Real world. The book warns that an open eAPI with a well-known username lets anyone with a five-line script reboot your switches (p. 171). Use per-person accounts or AAA (eAPI honours TACACS+ authorization and accounting), put eAPI in the management VRF only, and add an ACL under vrf MGMT in management api http-commands so only your automation hosts can reach it (p. 167). Libraries such as pyeapi, Ansible's arista.eos collection, ANTA and AVD's eos_config_deploy_eapi all sit on this same API.

Break / fix · eAPI in a VRF

Inject (host1, from docker exec -it clab-l5dc-host1 Cli, since you will lose SSH):

host1(config)# vrf instance MGMT
host1(config)# interface Management0
host1(config-if-Ma0)# vrf MGMT
host1(config-if-Ma0)# ip address 172.100.100.31/24
host1(config)# no ip route 0.0.0.0/0 172.100.100.1
host1(config)# ip route vrf MGMT 0.0.0.0/0 172.100.100.1

Symptom: ping 172.100.100.31 works from your workstation, but inventory.py reports host1 as unreachable with a connection refused error, and curl -k https://172.100.100.31/command-api says "Connection refused".

Root cause and fix

Moving Management0 into a VRF moves the address, but eAPI is still only listening in VRF default, so nothing answers on port 443 in VRF MGMT (p. 167). show management api http-commands shows VRFs: default. Fix:

host1(config)# management api http-commands
host1(config-mgmt-api-http-cmds)# vrf MGMT
host1(config-mgmt-api-http-cmds-vrf-MGMT)# no shutdown

Re-run inventory.py. Remember that SSH, NTP, DNS and TerminAttr all need the same VRF treatment (AVD does this with mgmt_interface_vrf). Undo the change afterwards with configure replace flash:startup-config from the container console.

7.2 CloudVision Studios engineering: the L3LS EVPN fabric

Studios replace hand-written configlets with forms. You describe the fabric (which devices, which roles, which pools and ASNs), the services (tenants, VRFs, VLANs) and the host ports; the studios render the EOS configuration. All edits happen inside a workspace, which you build, review and submit; submitting creates a change control. The fabric studio is built on the same data model as AVD (7.3), which is why the inputs feel familiar once you have done 7.3: Studios are a UI over a design data model, not a configlet editor.

Studio names differ slightly across CloudVision releases. Recent CVP and CVaaS show Inventory and Topology, L3 Leaf-Spine Fabric (in some releases Campus Fabric (L2/L3/EVPN) covers campus and the same logic), EVPN Services, Interface Configuration (connectivity / host ports) and Static Configuration. The ATD guide uses exactly these names, so practice there if you can.

Path A · CloudVision Studios

Start from an onboarded but unconfigured set of switches (ATD: run the studiosl3ls preparation from the console menu; Academy lab: the Studios lab start state). The steps below use our fabric's names and values; the ATD lab uses its own (s1-leaf1, VLANs 112/134), which is fine.

  1. Onboard the devices into Studios. Provisioning > Studios, enable Active Studios, Create Workspace named inventory, open Inventory and Topology, go to Network Updates, select all, Accept Updates, then Review Workspace and Submit Workspace. The studio learns the devices and their LLDP links; every later studio uses this topology.

  2. In a new workspace L3LS-Fabric, open L3 Leaf-Spine Fabric and build the hierarchy with tags: data center DC:L5DC, pod DC-Pod:1, leaf domains Leaf-Domain:POD_A (leaf1, leaf2) and Leaf-Domain:POD_B (leaf3, leaf4). Set Role (Spine or Leaf) and NodeId (spines 1-2, leaves 1-4) for each device. Type a new tag value, click Create under the tag input, then click elsewhere to commit it.

  3. Set the fabric parameters so the rendered config matches our reference: spine ASN 65100, leaf ASNs 65101 (POD_A) and 65102 (POD_B), router-ID / Loopback0 pool 10.255.0.0/27, VTEP Loopback1 pool 10.255.1.0/27, leaf transit uplink pool 10.255.255.0/26, MLAG peer pools 10.255.252.0/24 and 10.255.251.0/24, virtual router MAC 00:1c:73:00:dc:01, underlay eBGP, overlay EVPN over eBGP. The field names are under Underlay Routing, BGP Configuration and the MLAG / VXLAN sections of the pod and leaf-domain pages.

  4. Open EVPN Services. Add Tenant TENANT_A, Add VRF TENANT-A with VNI 50001. Add Vlan 10 (name APP, VRF TENANT-A, SVI virtual IP 10.10.10.1/24), 20 (DB, 10.20.20.1/24) and 30 (L2_STRETCH, no VRF, no SVI). For each VLAN, Add VTEP with the tag query DC:L5DC AND Role:Leaf, then open the right arrow to confirm the four leaves matched.

  5. Open Interface Configuration. Add Profile HOST_TRUNK: switchport mode trunk, allowed VLANs 10,20,30, port-channel group 5, Enable MLAG yes. Under Devices, set Ethernet5 on all four leaves to Enabled with profile HOST_TRUNK.

  6. External network: leaf3 and leaf4 need eBGP to core1 in VRF TENANT-A (Ethernet6, 10.255.254.0/31 and 10.255.254.2/31, peer AS 65000). Find where your CloudVision release models this: some releases expose external / border connectivity per VRF in EVPN Services or the fabric studio; where they do not, use the Static Configuration studio to add a configlet-style block to leaf3 and leaf4 only. Either way it stays inside the same workspace and change control.

  7. Review Workspace. Read the build output and the Proposed Configuration Changes for leaf1 and leaf3. Fix any validation error (Rebuild after each fix). Then Submit Workspace, View Change Control, Review and Approve, Approve, Execute Change Control.

  8. Day 2, in a new workspace day2-vlan40: add VLAN 40 (WEB, 10.40.40.1/24, VRF TENANT-A) to the same VTEP query and allow it on HOST_TRUNK. Before submitting, predict exactly which lines change on which devices, then compare with the proposed changes. (ATD's Day 2 guide does a bigger version: re-addressing the uplink pool, changing ASNs and renaming the VRF in one workspace.)

Solution · the external network block (Static Configuration fallback), leaf3 
interface Ethernet6
   description P2P_core1
   no switchport
   vrf TENANT-A
   ip address 10.255.254.0/31
!
router bgp 65102
   vrf TENANT-A
      neighbor 10.255.254.1 remote-as 65000
      neighbor 10.255.254.1 description core1
      address-family ipv4
         neighbor 10.255.254.1 activate

leaf4 is the same with 10.255.254.2/31 and neighbor 10.255.254.3. Static Configuration must not repeat lines the fabric studio already renders (for example the whole router bgp block); only add what is missing, or the studios fight over the same lines and the build fails or flaps.

Solution · predicted Day-2 change for VLAN 40

Only the four leaves change; the spines are untouched because they are pure EVPN route servers with no VLAN state. On each leaf: vlan 40, interface Vlan40 with ip address virtual 10.40.40.1/24 in VRF TENANT-A, vxlan vlan 40 vni 10040, a router bgp / vlan 40 stanza with RD and RT 10040:10040, and the Port-Channel5 allowed list grows to 10,20,30,40. That is exactly the diff AVD produces in 7.3 (task 8), which is a useful cross-check.

Verify

In CloudVision: the change control shows every task Completed; Devices > Inventory > leaf1 > BGP lists the peers; the device's configuration timeline shows the change. On the switches:

leaf1# show bgp evpn summary
leaf1# show vxlan vni
leaf3# show bgp summary vrf TENANT-A
host1# ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102

                                  Vxlan1          30

Workspace submitted with no validation warnings, change control fully executed, EVPN peers Established, the core1 session in VRF TENANT-A is Established on leaf3 and leaf4, and host1 reaches 10.20.20.102.
