Useful variations:

$ anta nrfu ... table --hide success                  # only what needs attention
$ anta nrfu ... -d leaf3 -t VerifyBGPPeerSession table # one device, one test
$ anta nrfu ... md-report --md-output reports/nrfu.md  # a report to attach to a change ticket
$ anta nrfu ... json                                   # for a script or CI gate

Some tests can fail on a lab for reasons unrelated to the design. VerifyNTP fails if the containerlab host has no internet path to pool.ntp.org (fix the lab or accept it knowingly). VerifyRunningConfigDiffs fails until the config is saved (write memory). VerifyZeroTouch fails if ZTP is still enabled on a cEOS image. Know why each failure is there; never get used to a red line.

ANTA checks the control plane and the switches. Prove the data plane from the endpoints as well:

host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 repeat 3
host1# ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102 repeat 3
host1# ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102 repeat 3
host1# ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8 repeat 3
Solution · task 7: AVD's validation role

AVD ships a role that builds an ANTA catalog from the structured configs, runs it against the inventory and writes CSV and Markdown reports: arista.avd.anta_runner in current AVD (it replaced the older eos_validate_state role, which also used ANTA under the hood). labs/avd/playbooks/validate.yml runs it:

$ ansible-playbook -i inventory.yml playbooks/validate.yml
$ ls ../avd/reports/

What it adds over hand-run ANTA: the catalog is derived from the design automatically every time (new VLAN, new peer, new test), the run is part of the same playbook flow as build and deploy, and the reports are standard artifacts for change records. build.py reproduces the catalog part with pyavd.get_device_test_catalog; that is what is in intended/test_catalogs/. Settings in the data model (for example per-interface validate_state / validate_lldp) switch individual checks off for ports that are expected to be down.

Solution · task 8: Day 2, add VLAN 40 
$ git switch -c day2-vlan40
# group_vars/L5DC_NETWORK_SERVICES.yml, under vrfs[TENANT-A].svis:
          - id: 40
            name: WEB
            enabled: true
            ip_address_virtual: 10.40.40.1/24
# group_vars/L5DC_CONNECTED_ENDPOINTS.yml, both servers:
        vlans: "10,20,30,40"
$ python3 build.py
$ git diff --stat intended/configs
 intended/configs/leaf1.cfg | 17 ++++++++++++++++-
 intended/configs/leaf2.cfg | 17 ++++++++++++++++-
 intended/configs/leaf3.cfg | 17 ++++++++++++++++-
 intended/configs/leaf4.cfg | 17 ++++++++++++++++-
$ git diff intended/configs/leaf1.cfg
+vlan 40
+   name WEB
+!
 interface Port-Channel5
    description SERVER_host1
-   switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30
+   switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30,40
+interface Vlan40
+   description WEB
+   no shutdown
+   vrf TENANT-A
+   ip address virtual 10.40.40.1/24
+   vxlan vlan 40 vni 10040
+   vlan 40
+      rd 10.255.0.3:10040
+      route-target both 10040:10040
+      redistribute learned

The spines do not change. The VNI is derived (mac_vrf_vni_base 10000 + 40), and the RD uses each leaf's Loopback0. Review this diff the way you would review a Studios workspace: if it touched the spines or removed a line you did not expect, stop. Then deploy (task 5, Way 1 or Way 2 with --dry-run first), rebuild the merged catalog, and rerun ANTA.

Put VLAN 40 on the hosts (lab-only change to the pre-built hosts, same pattern as their other VLANs):

host1(config)# vlan 40
host1(config)# vrf instance H40
host1(config)# ip routing vrf H40
host1(config)# interface Port-Channel1
host1(config-if-Po1)# switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40
host1(config-if-Po1)# interface Vlan40
host1(config-if-Vl40)# vrf H40
host1(config-if-Vl40)# ip address 10.40.40.101/24
host1(config-if-Vl40)# exit
host1(config)# ip route vrf H40 0.0.0.0/0 10.40.40.1

host2 is the same with 10.40.40.102.

Verify

leaf1# show vxlan vni 10040
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type imet vni 10040
host1# ping vrf H40 10.40.40.102 repeat 3
host1# ping vrf H40 10.10.10.102 repeat 3
BGP routing table information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.3, local AS number 65101
          Network                Next Hop              Metric  LocPref Weight  Path
 * >      RD: 10.255.0.3:10040 imet 10.255.1.3
                                 -                     -       -       0       i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.5:10040 imet 10.255.1.5
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 *  ec    RD: 10.255.0.5:10040 imet 10.255.1.5
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.6:10040 imet 10.255.1.5
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 i
 ...

python3 build.py runs clean, the diff touches only the four leaves, ANTA shows no new failures, each leaf sees IMET routes for VNI 10040 from both VTEPs (10.255.1.3 and 10.255.1.5), and host1 reaches 10.40.40.102 (bridged) and 10.10.10.102 (routed in TENANT-A).

Exam tip. You will not run AVD in the exam lab, but two habits carry over directly. First, read the Studios fields as AVD keys (the table in 7.2): pools, node ids and tag-based placement behave the same. Second, end every task with your own validation pass (the ANTA test list is a good mental checklist: interfaces, LLDP, MLAG, BGP peers, VXLAN config sanity, reachability), because the grader checks final state, not effort.
Real world. A mature AVD pipeline looks like this: a pull request changes YAML; CI runs the build and posts the config diff; a reviewer approves; the deploy job pushes (to CloudVision with cv_deploy, which creates a workspace and change control, or straight to eAPI); ANTA runs after the push and fails the pipeline on regressions. The repo you are using is a small version of that.
Watch out. AVD manages the whole config. Anything configured by hand on a switch is removed by the next full-replace deploy. If something has to stay, put it in the data model (or a custom_structured_configuration_ key), never only on the device.

Break / fix

Inject: in L5DC_NETWORK_SERVICES.yml, change VLAN 20's ip_address_virtual to 10.20.20.1 (no mask) and indent l2vlans two spaces too far. Run python3 build.py.

Symptom: the build stops with a validation or YAML error and writes nothing.

Root cause and fix

The YAML parser fails first on the indentation (a yaml.parser.ParserError with a line number). Fix it and the schema check (pyavd.validate_inputs) catches the next problem, reporting the path of the bad key (for example tenants[0].vrfs[0].svis[1].ip_address_virtual) and why. This is the value of a data model: a broken input fails at build time on your laptop, not at 2 a.m. on the switch.

Break / fix

Inject: on leaf2, by hand, add interface Ethernet7 / description TEMP-TEST / no shutdown and ip route vrf TENANT-A 192.0.2.0/24 Null0. Then run the deploy dry run (Way 2 --dry-run, or Ansible --check --diff).

Symptom: the dry run for leaf2 is not empty even though nobody changed the YAML.

Root cause and fix

That is configuration drift: running config no longer equals intent. The diff shows the two lines being removed. Decide: either the change is wanted (add it to the data model, rebuild, deploy) or it is not (deploy as is, which removes it). A scheduled dry run against all switches is a cheap drift detector; ANTA's VerifyRunningConfigDiffs catches the related case of unsaved changes.

Checkpoint

  • I can explain designed vs running configuration, configlet merge order, reconcile, tasks and change control, and I have pushed a change through a change control at least once (Path A).
  • I can lay out a configlet hierarchy so that a pod-level or tenant-level change touches exactly one configlet, and I know why per-pod ASNs break a fabric-wide services configlet.
  • I can call eAPI with curl and Python, parse the JSON, and push an atomic change with a configuration session (and commit timer).
  • I can loop a script over every node, send privileged commands with enable, back up configs in text mode, use the on-box transports, and fix eAPI when the management interface moves into a VRF.
  • I have built the fabric in the L3 Leaf-Spine Fabric, EVPN Services and Interface Configuration studios, or I can describe each input and its AVD equivalent.
  • I can build the fabric with pyavd or Ansible, deploy it with a full replace, run ANTA with the generated catalogs, and make a Day-2 change only through the data model.

All domains · Professional exam practice

Real-world capstones

Five scenario labs that mix the domains the way the exam and real projects do. C1 is a timed mock exam; C2 to C5 are the jobs you get after go-live: border connectivity and a new tenant, a ticket queue of injected faults, a zero-impact maintenance, and monitoring. Each starts with a brief and acceptance criteria; hints and solutions are collapsed. Try each one cold first.

Objectives
All (each capstone lists its own)
Time
C1 4 h, C2 2 h, C3 3 h, C4 2 h, C5 2.5 h
Start state
C1: day-0 startup configs. C2 to C5: the AVD end state (labs/avd/intended/configs deployed, Lab D7 7.3), clean ANTA run.
Devices
Full topology: spine1-2, leaf1-4, host1-2, core1

Read first: Arista exam datasheet · Data Center · AW Ch 29 · Troubleshooting · AW Ch 27 · Event Manager · AW Ch 28 · Event Monitor · AW Ch 24 · Scheduler · AW Ch 10 · Upgrading EOS · ANTA docs

C1 Mock exam: greenfield L3LS EVPN fabric in 4 hours

Exercises: 1.1 · 4.1 · 4.2 · 4.3 · 5.1 · 5.2 · 5.3 · 6.1 · 6.2 · 7.1 (or 7.2) · 3.4

This is the closest thing to an Engineering exam sitting you can run at home. You get a written brief, a fixed clock and a grader that only looks at final state. Set a 4-hour timer, close this guide's solutions, and work from the brief alone. The exam is open book, so the EOS manual and your own notes are allowed; AVD-generated configs are not (pasting them defeats the purpose). Configure by CLI, or by configlets / Studios if you have CloudVision.

The brief

"Acme has racked two spines and two MLAG leaf pairs. Management is reachable (172.100.100.0/24, admin/admin) and nothing else is configured. Build a routed leaf-spine fabric with an EVPN VXLAN overlay that gives the application servers (host1 in POD_A, host2 in POD_B) layer 2 and layer 3 connectivity in one tenant, and internet access through the border pair. Values are fixed by the design document below. At the end of the window we test from the hosts and inspect the switches."

ReqRequirementObjectives
R1P2P links leaf-to-spine as routed /31s from 10.255.255.0/26 (spine takes the even address, see the cabling plan), MTU 1500. Loopback0 router IDs 10.255.0.1-.6.4.2, 5.1
R2eBGP underlay: spines AS 65100, POD_A AS 65101, POD_B AS 65102. Use a peer group for underlay neighbors. Up to 4 equal-cost paths. Only loopbacks (10.255.0.0/27 and 10.255.1.0/27, /32 only) are advertised into the underlay.5.1, 5.2
R3MLAG on each leaf pair: domain POD_A / POD_B, peer link Port-Channel10 on Ethernet3-4, peer VLAN 4094 (10.255.252.0/31, .4/31), peer VLAN in trunk group MLAG and excluded from STP.4.1
R4iBGP between MLAG peers over VLAN 4093 (10.255.251.0/31, .4/31) for the underlay, so a leaf that loses both uplinks still forwards via its peer.5.3
R5Spanning tree MSTP on leaves with priority 16384; spines run no spanning tree.4.1, 4.3
R6VTEP: Loopback1 shared per pair (10.255.1.3 POD_A, 10.255.1.5 POD_B), Vxlan1 sourced from it, UDP 4789.6.1
R7EVPN overlay: eBGP multihop between leaf and spine loopbacks; spines do not change the next hop; BFD on overlay sessions.6.2
R8VLANs 10 APP, 20 DB, 30 L2_STRETCH mapped to VNIs 10010, 10020, 10030. MAC-VRF per VLAN: RD Loopback0:VNI, RT VNI:VNI.6.1, 6.2
R9VRF TENANT-A, symmetric IRB with L3 VNI 50001 (RD Loopback0:50001, RT 50001:50001). Anycast gateways 10.10.10.1/24 and 10.20.20.1/24 on all four leaves with virtual MAC 00:1c:73:00:dc:01. VLAN 30 has no gateway.6.2, 4.1
R10Host ports: Ethernet5 on each leaf in Port-Channel5 (LACP active), MLAG 5, trunk 10,20,30.4.1
R11Border: leaf3 Ethernet6 10.255.254.0/31 and leaf4 Ethernet6 10.255.254.2/31 in VRF TENANT-A, eBGP to core1 (AS 65000). Default route and 8.8.8.0/24 reach every leaf. Per-VRF iBGP between border peers on VLAN 3001.6.2, 5.3
R12All configs saved. No pending configuration sessions. Hostnames and management unchanged.1.1
R13Leave evidence: a snapshot (show commands or ANTA run) of the final state saved on your workstation.3.4, 7.3

Acceptance tests (run from the hosts): host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102, ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102, ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102, ping vrf H20 10.10.10.102, ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8, and the same from host2 towards host1.

Scoring rubric

AreaHow it is checkedPoints
Underlay links and eBGP (R1, R2)ANTA VerifyReachability, VerifyBGPPeerSession (underlay peers), VerifyLLDPNeighbors, VerifyInterfacesStatus15
MLAG and MLAG iBGP (R3, R4)ANTA VerifyMlagStatus, VerifyMlagInterfaces, VerifyMlagConfigSanity, BGP session to the peer on Vlan409315
STP and port-channels (R5, R10)ANTA VerifySTPCounters, VerifyPortChannels, VerifyIllegalLACP; show spanning-tree priority5
VTEP and EVPN overlay (R6, R7)ANTA VerifyVxlan1Interface, VerifyVxlanConfigSanity, VerifyVxlanVtep, EVPN peers (VerifyBGPPeersHealth)15
L2 services (R8)VerifyVxlanVniBinding; host pings H10 to .102 and H30 to .10215
L3 services (R9)VNI 50001 binding; host pings across subnets (H10 to 10.20.20.102, H20 to 10.10.10.102)15
Border (R11)VRF peer to core1 Established; default and 8.8.8.0/24 in VRF TENANT-A on every leaf; host ping to 8.8.8.815
Hygiene (R12, R13)VerifyRunningConfigDiffs (saved), no sessions; snapshot file exists5
Total (pass mark you set yourself: aim for 85+)100

Suggested time plan

ClockWork
0:00-0:15Read the whole brief. Write a one-page plan: per-device values table (interfaces, IPs, ASNs). Decide CLI vs configlets.
0:15-1:15Underlay (R1, R2, R5) on all six switches. Verify BGP and loopback reachability before moving on.
1:15-1:50MLAG and MLAG iBGP (R3, R4), host port-channels (R10). Verify MLAG active and Po5 up.
1:50-2:50VTEP, overlay, L2 then L3 services (R6-R9). Verify after each step with host pings.
2:50-3:20Border (R11).
3:20-4:00Full validation sweep, fix, save, snapshot (R12, R13). Never start a new feature in the last 20 minutes.

Hints

  • Build one leaf completely, verify it, then use it as a template for the others (change only the per-device values from your plan). Configuration sessions let you paste a whole block and review it before commit.
  • Most "nothing works" moments in this build come from four places: no bgp default ipv4-unicast without activating the underlay group in address-family ipv4; forgetting send-community (or extended) on the EVPN peer group; the spine changing the EVPN next hop; and the VTEP Loopback1 not being advertised.
  • The MLAG pair must use the same Loopback1 address and vxlan virtual-router encapsulation mac-address mlag-system-id; anycast gateways need ip virtual-router mac-address.

Automated grade

When the clock stops, grade with the AVD-generated catalogs (they encode the design, so they work against a hand-built fabric as long as your values match), plus an extra catalog for the services and border, plus host pings.

  1. Save the extra catalog as labs/avd/c1-extra.yml:

    anta.tests.vxlan:
  - VerifyVxlan1Interface:
      filters: {tags: [leaf]}
  - VerifyVxlanVniBinding:
      filters: {tags: [leaf]}
      bindings:
        10010: 10
        10020: 20
        10030: 30
        50001: TENANT-A
  - VerifyVxlanVtep:
      filters: {tags: [POD_A]}
      vteps: [10.255.1.5]
  - VerifyVxlanVtep:
      filters: {tags: [POD_B]}
      vteps: [10.255.1.3]
anta.tests.routing.bgp:
  - VerifyBGPPeersHealth:
      address_families:
        - afi: evpn
        - afi: ipv4
          safi: unicast
          vrf: default
  - VerifyBGPPeerSession:
      filters: {tags: [border]}
      bgp_peers:
        - peer_address: 10.255.254.1
          vrf: TENANT-A
  - VerifyEVPNType2Route:
      filters: {tags: [leaf]}
      vxlan_endpoints:
        - address: 10.10.10.101
          vni: 10010
        - address: 10.10.10.102
          vni: 10010
anta.tests.routing.generic:
  - VerifyRoutingTableEntry:
      filters: {tags: [leaf]}
      vrf: TENANT-A
      routes: [0.0.0.0, 8.8.8.0]
anta.tests.connectivity:
  - VerifyReachability:
      filters: {tags: [leaf1]}
      hosts:
        - source: Loopback100
          destination: 10.255.10.5
          vrf: TENANT-A
          repeat: 2

    (The VerifyBGPPeerSession border test only lists leaf3's core1 address; on leaf4 it reports the peer as not found. Split it into two tests filtered by leaf3 and leaf4 with 10.255.254.1 and 10.255.254.3 if you want leaf4 graded too: a good first exercise in writing your own catalog.)

  2. Merge and run, saving JSON for the score:

    $ cd labs/avd
$ python3 -c "import glob; from anta.catalog import AntaCatalog as C; \
print(C.merge_catalogs([C.parse(f) for f in sorted(glob.glob('intended/test_catalogs/*-catalog.yml')) + ['c1-extra.yml']]).dump().yaml())" \
  > c1-catalog.yml
$ anta nrfu -u admin -p admin -i anta-inventory.yml -c c1-catalog.yml --ignore-status table --hide success
$ anta nrfu -u admin -p admin -i anta-inventory.yml -c c1-catalog.yml --ignore-status json -o c1-anta.json

  3. Run the host pings and compute a score (save as labs/avd/c1_grade.py):

    #!/usr/bin/env python3
"""c1_grade.py - host pings plus ANTA results to a 100-point score."""
import json, requests, urllib3
urllib3.disable_warnings()

def eapi(ip, cmd):
    r = requests.post(f"https://{ip}/command-api", auth=("admin", "admin"), verify=False, timeout=30,
                      json={"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": 1,
                            "params": {"version": 1, "format": "text", "cmds": [cmd]}})
    return r.json().get("result", [{}])[0].get("output", "")

def ping(host_ip, vrf, dst):
    out = eapi(host_ip, f"ping vrf {vrf} {dst} repeat 3")
    return " 0% packet loss" in out or ", 0% packet loss" in out

H1, H2 = "172.100.100.31", "172.100.100.32"
PINGS = {   # name: (host, vrf, destination, rubric area)
    "h1 L2 VLAN10":   (H1, "H10", "10.10.10.102", "l2"),
    "h1 L2 VLAN30":   (H1, "H30", "10.30.30.102", "l2"),
    "h2 L2 VLAN10":   (H2, "H10", "10.10.10.101", "l2"),
    "h1 routed 10-20": (H1, "H10", "10.20.20.102", "l3"),
    "h1 routed 20-10": (H1, "H20", "10.10.10.102", "l3"),
    "h2 routed 10-20": (H2, "H10", "10.20.20.101", "l3"),
    "h1 internet":    (H1, "H10", "8.8.8.8", "border"),
    "h2 internet":    (H2, "H20", "8.8.8.8", "border"),
}
AREAS = {   # area: (points, ANTA tests that belong to it)
    "underlay": (15, {"VerifyReachability", "VerifyLLDPNeighbors", "VerifyInterfacesStatus", "VerifyBGPPeerSession"}),
    "mlag":     (15, {"VerifyMlagStatus", "VerifyMlagInterfaces", "VerifyMlagConfigSanity"}),
    "stp_po":   (5,  {"VerifySTPCounters", "VerifyPortChannels", "VerifyIllegalLACP"}),
    "overlay":  (15, {"VerifyVxlan1Interface", "VerifyVxlanConfigSanity", "VerifyVxlanVtep", "VerifyBGPPeersHealth"}),
    "l2":       (15, {"VerifyVxlanVniBinding", "VerifyEVPNType2Route"}),
    "l3":       (15, set()),
    "border":   (15, {"VerifyRoutingTableEntry"}),
    "hygiene":  (5,  {"VerifyRunningConfigDiffs"}),
}
results = json.load(open("c1-anta.json"))
checks = {a: [] for a in AREAS}
for r in results:
    for area, (_, tests) in AREAS.items():
        if r["test"] in tests:
            checks[area].append((f'{r["name"]}:{r["test"]}', r["result"] == "success"))
for name, (h, vrf, dst, area) in PINGS.items():
    checks[area].append((name, ping(h, vrf, dst)))

total = 0
for area, (pts, _) in AREAS.items():
    ok = sum(1 for _, p in checks[area] if p)
    n = len(checks[area]) or 1
    score = round(pts * ok / n, 1)
    total += score
    print(f"{area:9} {score:5}/{pts:<3} ({ok}/{n} checks)")
    for name, p in checks[area]:
        if not p:
            print(f"          FAIL {name}")
print(f"TOTAL     {round(total)}/100")

    Outputs are representative and trimmed; your timers, MACs and counters will differ.

    $ python3 c1_grade.py
underlay   15.0/15  (24/24 checks)
mlag       15.0/15  (12/12 checks)
stp_po      5.0/5   (12/12 checks)
overlay    13.6/15  (11/12 checks)
          FAIL leaf4:VerifyBGPPeersHealth
l2         15.0/15  (13/13 checks)
l3         15.0/15  (3/3 checks)
border     15.0/15  (6/6 checks)
hygiene     3.3/5   (4/6 checks)
          FAIL spine1:VerifyRunningConfigDiffs
          FAIL spine2:VerifyRunningConfigDiffs
TOTAL     97/100
Solution · reference end state

The reference answer is the AVD output: labs/avd/intended/configs/<device>.cfg. Diff your saved configs against it to see what you did differently. Differences in names (peer group names, descriptions) do not matter to the grader; differences in values and features do.

$ for n in spine1 spine2 leaf1 leaf2 leaf3 leaf4; do
    ssh admin@clab-l5dc-$n "show running-config" > mine/$n.cfg
    diff -u labs/avd/intended/configs/$n.cfg mine/$n.cfg | less
  done

Labs D4 to D6 build each piece step by step with explanations; go back to the lab for any rubric area that scored under full marks.

Exam tip. The real exam gives you a scenario and a CloudVision-managed lab instead of a blank fabric, but the scoring logic is the same: automated checks on final state. Partial credit exists for completed sections, so finish and verify each section before starting the next; a fully working underlay and MLAG with no overlay scores better than everything half-configured.
Real world. The rubric and the grade script are a real acceptance test. Teams hand the same kind of catalog plus endpoint tests to the customer at handover, and re-run it after every change.

C2 Border and external connectivity, plus a new tenant

Exercises: 6.2 · 5.3 · 5.2 · 4.3 · 7.3 (optional AVD path)

"The application in TENANT-A now needs internet access through the WAN router core1, which connects to the border pair leaf3/leaf4. Separately, a new web application must live in its own routing domain, TENANT-B, on VLAN 40, isolated from TENANT-A except for the shared internet path, which the security team will decide on later."

core1 is pre-built: AS 65000, Ethernet1 10.255.254.1/31 to leaf3, Ethernet2 10.255.254.3/31 to leaf4, and it originates 0.0.0.0/0 and 8.8.8.0/24 (its Loopback1 is 8.8.8.8). The AVD end state already contains the border; to practice it, remove it first:

leaf3(config)# default interface Ethernet6
leaf3(config)# router bgp 65102
leaf3(config-router-bgp)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf3(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# no neighbor 10.255.254.1

(leaf4 the same with 10.255.254.3.) If you did the Day-2 VLAN 40 change in Lab D7, revert it first so VLAN 40 is free.

Part 1 · Border

  1. Configure leaf3 and leaf4 so that each has an eBGP session to core1 inside VRF TENANT-A over Ethernet6.

  2. Make sure every leaf in both pods has the default route and 8.8.8.0/24 in VRF TENANT-A, learned as EVPN type-5 routes, and that core1 can route back to 10.10.10.0/24 and 10.20.20.0/24.

  3. Explain the role of the per-VRF iBGP peering on VLAN 3001 between leaf3 and leaf4, and prove it by shutting leaf3 Ethernet6.

  4. Real-world hardening: advertise only the tenant subnets (not /32 host routes, not the P2P /31) to core1, and accept only 0.0.0.0/0 and 8.8.8.0/24 from it.

Acceptance: host1# ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8 and host2# ping vrf H20 8.8.8.8 succeed; with leaf3 Ethernet6 shut, both still succeed; core1 receives only 10.10.10.0/24 and 10.20.20.0/24 from the fabric after task 4.

Hints
  • Routes learned by BGP in a VRF that has route-target export evpn are exported as type-5 automatically; connected routes need redistribute connected.
  • leaf3 and leaf4 share VTEP 10.255.1.5. A type-5 route from leaf4 has next hop 10.255.1.5, which is leaf3's own address, so leaf3 cannot use it. That is the gap the VLAN 3001 peering fills.
Solution · leaf3 (leaf4 analogous) 
leaf3(config)# interface Ethernet6
leaf3(config-if-Et6)# description P2P_core1
leaf3(config-if-Et6)# no switchport
leaf3(config-if-Et6)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf3(config-if-Et6)# ip address 10.255.254.0/31
leaf3(config-if-Et6)# no shutdown
leaf3(config-if-Et6)# router bgp 65102
leaf3(config-router-bgp)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf3(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# neighbor 10.255.254.1 remote-as 65000
leaf3(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# neighbor 10.255.254.1 description core1
leaf3(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# address-family ipv4
leaf3(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A-af)# neighbor 10.255.254.1 activate

The rest is already in place from the AVD build: redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS (which hides the MLAG /31 from the VRF), the VLAN 3001 iBGP peer (neighbor 10.255.251.5 peer group MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER in the VRF), and the EVPN RTs.

Hardening (task 4):

leaf3(config)# ip prefix-list PL-TENANT-A-OUT seq 10 permit 10.10.10.0/24
leaf3(config)# ip prefix-list PL-TENANT-A-OUT seq 20 permit 10.20.20.0/24
leaf3(config)# ip prefix-list PL-CORE1-IN seq 10 permit 0.0.0.0/0
leaf3(config)# ip prefix-list PL-CORE1-IN seq 20 permit 8.8.8.0/24
leaf3(config)# route-map RM-CORE1-OUT permit 10
leaf3(config-route-map-RM-CORE1-OUT)# match ip address prefix-list PL-TENANT-A-OUT
leaf3(config-route-map-RM-CORE1-OUT)# route-map RM-CORE1-IN permit 10
leaf3(config-route-map-RM-CORE1-IN)# match ip address prefix-list PL-CORE1-IN
leaf3(config-route-map-RM-CORE1-IN)# router bgp 65102
leaf3(config-router-bgp)# vrf TENANT-A
leaf3(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# neighbor 10.255.254.1 route-map RM-CORE1-OUT out
leaf3(config-router-bgp-vrf-TENANT-A)# neighbor 10.255.254.1 route-map RM-CORE1-IN in

In AVD the same is route_map_out / route_map_in on the bgp_peers entry plus the route-maps and prefix-lists under custom_structured_configuration_ or structured_config.

Verify

leaf3# show bgp summary vrf TENANT-A
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type ip-prefix 0.0.0.0/0
leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A 0.0.0.0/0
core1# show ip bgp
host1# ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type ip-prefix 0.0.0.0/0
BGP routing table information for VRF default
Router identifier 10.255.0.3, local AS number 65101
          Network                Next Hop              Metric  LocPref Weight  Path
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.5:50001 ip-prefix 0.0.0.0/0
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 65000 i
 *  ec    RD: 10.255.0.5:50001 ip-prefix 0.0.0.0/0
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 65000 i
 * >Ec    RD: 10.255.0.6:50001 ip-prefix 0.0.0.0/0
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 65000 i
 *  ec    RD: 10.255.0.6:50001 ip-prefix 0.0.0.0/0
                                 10.255.1.5            -       100     0       65100 65102 65000 i

leaf1# show ip route vrf TENANT-A 0.0.0.0/0
 B E      0.0.0.0/0 [200/0] via VTEP 10.255.1.5 VNI 50001 router-mac 02:1c:73:xx:xx:xx local-interface Vxlan1

host1# ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8
PING 8.8.8.8 (8.8.8.8) 72(100) bytes of data.
80 bytes from 8.8.8.8: icmp_seq=1 ttl=62 time=9.81 ms
...
5 packets transmitted, 5 received, 0% packet loss

Both border sessions Established; leaf1/leaf2 have 0.0.0.0/0 and 8.8.8.0/24 via VTEP 10.255.1.5 VNI 50001; core1 has 10.10.10.0/24 and 10.20.20.0/24 with two paths; hosts reach 8.8.8.8, also with leaf3 Ethernet6 shut (leaf3 then reaches core1 through leaf4 over VLAN 3001).

Part 2 · New tenant TENANT-B

  1. Create VRF TENANT-B on all four leaves: L3 VNI 50002 (RD Loopback0:50002, RT 50002:50002), VLAN 40 WEB mapped to VNI 10040 with an anycast gateway 10.40.40.1/24, MLAG per-VRF iBGP on VLAN 3002, and a diagnostic Loopback101 (10.255.11.3-.6). Allow VLAN 40 on the host port-channels. Do it through the AVD data model if you can (then deploy), or by CLI.

  2. Add VLAN 40 to host1 and host2 in a new host VRF H40 (10.40.40.101 and .102, default route to 10.40.40.1), as in Lab D7 task 8.

  3. Prove isolation: H40 reaches the other H40 host but not 10.10.10.102 and not 8.8.8.8.

  4. Discussion: the security team asks for TENANT-B to reach the internet (8.8.8.0/24 and the default route) but never TENANT-A. Describe three ways to leak routes between VRFs on this fabric and recommend one.

Solution · AVD data model and resulting CLI 
# append to tenants: in group_vars/L5DC_NETWORK_SERVICES.yml
  - name: TENANT_B
    mac_vrf_vni_base: 10000
    vrfs:
      - name: TENANT-B
        vrf_vni: 50002
        mlag_ibgp_peering_vlan: 3002
        vtep_diagnostic:
          loopback: 101
          loopback_ip_range: 10.255.11.0/27
        svis:
          - id: 40
            name: WEB
            enabled: true
            ip_address_virtual: 10.40.40.1/24
# and vlans: "10,20,30,40" for both servers in L5DC_CONNECTED_ENDPOINTS.yml

The rebuild adds these lines on leaf3 (other leaves analogous):

vlan 40
   name WEB
vlan 3002
   name MLAG_L3_VRF_TENANT-B
   trunk group MLAG
vrf instance TENANT-B
interface Port-Channel5
   switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30,40
interface Loopback101
   description DIAG_VRF_TENANT-B
   vrf TENANT-B
   ip address 10.255.11.5/32
interface Vlan40
   description WEB
   vrf TENANT-B
   ip address virtual 10.40.40.1/24
interface Vlan3002
   description MLAG_L3_VRF_TENANT-B
   mtu 1500
   vrf TENANT-B
   ip address 10.255.251.4/31
interface Vxlan1
   vxlan vlan 40 vni 10040
   vxlan vrf TENANT-B vni 50002
ip address virtual source-nat vrf TENANT-B address 10.255.11.5
ip routing vrf TENANT-B
router bgp 65102
   vlan 40
      rd 10.255.0.5:10040
      route-target both 10040:10040
      redistribute learned
   vrf TENANT-B
      rd 10.255.0.5:50002
      route-target import evpn 50002:50002
      route-target export evpn 50002:50002
      router-id 10.255.0.5
      neighbor 10.255.251.5 peer group MLAG-IPv4-UNDERLAY-PEER
      neighbor 10.255.251.5 description leaf4_Vlan3002
      redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP-VRFS

Note the reuse of 10.255.251.4/31 on Vlan3002: the per-VRF peering addresses can repeat because each lives in its own VRF. One thing AVD does not add for you: the PL-MLAG-PEER-VRFS filter already matches 10.255.251.4/31, so the transit subnet stays out of EVPN in both VRFs.

Verify

leaf1# show vrf
leaf1# show vxlan vni
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type ip-prefix ipv4 vni 50002
host1# ping vrf H40 10.40.40.102
host1# ping vrf H40 10.10.10.102
host1# ping vrf H40 8.8.8.8

VNI 50002 is bound to TENANT-B on all leaves; host1 H40 reaches 10.40.40.102; the pings to 10.10.10.102 and 8.8.8.8 fail (no route in TENANT-B), which is the required isolation.

Solution · route leaking options (task 4)
OptionHowTrade-off
1. Leak at the edge (recommended)Give TENANT-B its own border sessions to core1 (or, in production, to a firewall with one interface or subinterface per VRF). core1 or the firewall routes between them and enforces policy.Clean and auditable: inter-VRF traffic always crosses a policy point. Costs an extra P2P link or subinterface (for example Ethernet6.40) per VRF on the border leaves.
2. EVPN route-target importOn the leaves, add route-target import evpn 50001:50001 to VRF TENANT-B (with an import route-map that only permits 0.0.0.0/0 and 8.8.8.0/24) and the matching reverse import for return traffic.Distributed and fast, no hairpin, but policy lives in route-maps on every leaf, and the reverse import must be filtered too or TENANT-A becomes reachable from TENANT-B. Easy to get subtly wrong.
3. Local VRF leaking on the borderEOS router general / vrf TENANT-B / leak routes source-vrf TENANT-A subscribe-policy RM-LEAK on leaf3/leaf4, then redistribute the leaked routes into EVPN for TENANT-B.Keeps leaking at one pair, but behaviour (re-advertisement of leaked routes, ECMP) varies by EOS release. Lab it before you trust it.

For an exam answer and for a security team, option 1 is the defensible choice. Option 2 is worth trying in the lab to see how RT import works, but put the reverse-direction filter in place first.

Exam tip. "Hosts cannot reach the internet" in a border scenario usually comes down to one of three checks, in this order: is the VRF eBGP session up and receiving the default; is the default exported as type-5 and imported by the other leaves (RT 50001:50001); does the outside know the way back (tenant subnets advertised to core1).
Real world. Border leaves are where most outages start: a leaked full table, a missing outbound filter, a default route that disappears when one link fails. Inbound and outbound prefix filters on every external session are standard practice, as is a test that pulls one border link during commissioning.

Break / fix

Inject: on leaf3 and leaf4, under router bgp 65102 / vrf TENANT-A, replace route-target export evpn 50001:50001 with route-target export evpn 50001:50011.

Symptom: host2 still reaches 8.8.8.8; host1 does not. leaf1 has no default route in TENANT-A, and some routed traffic between the pods may fail too.

Root cause and fix

POD_B exports its type-5 routes (and its type-2 MAC-IP routes' IP-VRF RT) with 50001:50011, which POD_A does not import. POD_B itself still has the default from core1 directly. show bgp evpn route-type ip-prefix 0.0.0.0/0 detail on leaf1 shows nothing; on a spine the route exists with the wrong extended community. Restore the export RT. Lesson: compare RTs at the source (exporting leaf) and in transit (spine), not only on the receiver.

C3 Troubleshooting ticket queue

Exercises: 3.4 · 4.1 · 4.3 · 5.1 · 5.3 · 6.1 · 6.2 · 6.3 · 1.1 · 7.3

A script breaks the fabric; you get user complaints, not fault descriptions. This mirrors the Operations exam and every on-call shift. The script is labs/capstone/inject-faults.sh (usage in labs/capstone/README.md). It talks to the switches with docker exec clab-l5dc-<node> Cli -p 15 -c $'configure\n...\nend' by default (Cli -p 15 starts at privilege 15 and -c runs each newline-separated line as a command), or over eAPI with MODE=eapi. Faults are made in running-config only.

$ cd labs/capstone
$ ./inject-faults.sh list            # the complaints
$ DOCKER="sudo docker" ./inject-faults.sh 3
$ ./inject-faults.sh random 3        # three unknown tickets, numbers saved in .injected
$ ./inject-faults.sh all             # hard mode: every fault at once
$ ./inject-faults.sh restore         # configure replace back to the AVD intended config

Acceptance for each ticket: you name the root cause in one sentence, fix it with the smallest correct change, and prove the fix with the verification listed (plus a clean ANTA run at the end). Target: 15 minutes per ticket, about 3 hours for all 13 one by one, then a final run with all.

Method (use it on every ticket)

  1. Reproduce the complaint from the endpoint (host ping in the right VRF).
  2. Locate by layer, bottom up along the path: physical and port-channel, MLAG, underlay (BGP, loopback reachability), overlay (EVPN routes, VTEPs, VNIs), services (gateway, VRF), policy.
  3. Compare a broken element with its healthy twin (leaf3 vs leaf1, VLAN 20 vs VLAN 10). Differences are the fastest clue.
  4. Fix, then re-run the exact test that failed and one broader test (ANTA).
Exam tip. show running-config diffs (running vs startup) instantly reveals an unsaved change, and ANTA's VerifyRunningConfigDiffs flags the same. Both work here because the script never saves. In the exam the faults are usually saved, so practise finding them from symptoms first and use diffs only to confirm.
TicketComplaint
T1Server team: host1 Port-Channel1 shows only one member bundled, and leaf2 monitoring says "MLAG inactive".
T2NOC: BGP alarm on spine2. One underlay neighbor is not Established. POD_B lost a path.
T3DB team: the DB cluster heartbeat between 10.20.20.101 (host1) and 10.20.20.102 (host2) is down, but the app tier in 10.10.10.0/24 can still reach both DB servers.
T4Platform team: the L2_STRETCH cluster (VLAN 30) is split. 10.30.30.101 cannot reach 10.30.30.102. Nothing else seems broken.
T5App team: host2 can reach host1 inside its own subnet, but nothing off-subnet. Its gateway 10.10.10.1 does not answer from host2. host1 is fine.
T6Backup team: nightly backups from host1 to host2 stall. Small pings work. A colleague "tidied up interface settings" on leaf3 and the spines yesterday.
T7Major incident: since last night's change, host1 cannot reach anything on POD_B (10.x.x.102). All BGP and EVPN sessions show Established.
T8Compliance audit: leaf3 advertises EVPN type-1 and type-4 routes. POD_B is supposed to be MLAG-only. Remove the drift and explain the risk.
T9Monitoring: leaf2 Port-Channel5 / Ethernet5 is err-disabled. host1 is running on one link.
T10NOC: spine1 and spine2 each lost one underlay BGP neighbor and one EVPN neighbor. Users report no impact yet.
T11DB team: some DB flows from host1 (10.20.20.101) fail and others work. VLAN 10 is fine. The on-call engineer says show interfaces trunk on leaf1 lists VLAN 20 as allowed on Port-Channel5.
T12App team: hosts on POD_B can reach other hosts in their own VLAN, but nothing routed, in any subnet. A change on leaf3 and leaf4 last night was about "tidying up the routing config".
T13App team: about half of host2's off-subnet traffic in VLAN 10 fails. A colleague "fixed the VRF" on leaf4 Vlan10 this morning; the running config still shows vrf TENANT-A on it.
T1 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf2# show mlag
leaf2# show mlag detail | include peer-address|State|Negotiation
leaf2# show running-config section mlag
MLAG Configuration:
domain-id                          :               POD_A
local-interface                    :            Vlan4094
peer-address                       :        10.255.252.9
peer-link                          :      Port-Channel10

MLAG Status:
state                              :            Inactive
negotiation status                 :          Connecting
peer-link status                   :                  Up

Root cause: leaf2's MLAG peer-address is 10.255.252.9; leaf1's Vlan4094 is 10.255.252.0. The peer link is up but the MLAG control session never forms, so each switch uses its own LACP system ID and host1 can bundle only one member.

leaf2(config)# mlag configuration
leaf2(config-mlag)# peer-address 10.255.252.0

Proof: show mlag on both shows state Active, negotiation Connected; show mlag interfaces shows mlag 5 active-full; host1# show port-channel 1 detailed lists Ethernet1 and Ethernet2 both bundled.

T2 · root cause, fix, proof 
spine2# show bgp summary
spine2# show bgp neighbors 10.255.255.11 | include BGP state|Last error|notification
BGP state is Active
Last sent notification:  OPEN Message Error/Bad Peer AS

Root cause: spine2 expects AS 65103 from leaf3 (10.255.255.11); leaf3 is AS 65102. The OPEN is rejected.

spine2(config)# router bgp 65100
spine2(config-router-bgp)# neighbor 10.255.255.11 remote-as 65102

Proof: show bgp summary on spine2 shows 10.255.255.11 Established; leaf3# show ip route 10.255.0.2 shows two ECMP paths again (via both spines through the underlay).

T3 · root cause, fix, proof 
host1# ping vrf H20 10.20.20.102          ! fails
host1# ping vrf H10 10.20.20.102          ! works (routed)
spine1# show bgp evpn route-type mac-ip 10.20.20.102 detail | include Route-Target|RD
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type imet vni 10020
leaf3# show running-config section router bgp | section vlan 20
  BGP routing table entry for mac-ip 5000.00ab.cd02 10.20.20.102, Route Distinguisher: 10.255.0.5:10020
      Extended Community: Route-Target-AS:10020:12020 Route-Target-AS:50001:50001 TunnelEncap:tunnelTypeVxlan

Root cause: POD_B's MAC-VRF for VLAN 20 uses RT 10020:12020; POD_A imports 10020:10020. POD_A drops POD_B's VLAN 20 type-2 and type-3 routes for the MAC-VRF, so bridging in VLAN 20 between pods fails (no flood list entry, no MAC). Routing still works because the same type-2 routes also carry the IP-VRF RT 50001:50001, which POD_A imports into TENANT-A: symmetric IRB routes on the /32 host route.

leaf3(config)# router bgp 65102
leaf3(config-router-bgp)# vlan 20
leaf3(config-macvrf-20)# no route-target both 10020:12020
leaf3(config-macvrf-20)# route-target both 10020:10020

(Same on leaf4.) Proof: leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type imet vni 10020 lists 10.255.1.5; leaf1# show vxlan flood vtep vlan 20 includes 10.255.1.5; host1# ping vrf H20 10.20.20.102 works.

T4 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf3# show vxlan vni
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type imet vni 10030
leaf1# show vxlan flood vtep vlan 30
leaf3# show running-config interfaces Vxlan1

Root cause: vxlan vlan 30 vni 10030 is missing on leaf3 and leaf4. Without a VNI, POD_B advertises no IMET or MAC routes for VLAN 30 and does not decapsulate VNI 10030; VLAN 30 is local to each pod. VLANs 10 and 20 are unaffected.

leaf3(config)# interface Vxlan1
leaf3(config-if-Vx1)# vxlan vlan 30 vni 10030

(Same on leaf4.) Proof: show vxlan vni on leaf3 lists 10030; the IMET table on leaf1 for VNI 10030 shows 10.255.1.5; host1# ping vrf H30 10.30.30.102 works; ANTA VerifyVxlanConfigSanity passes (it flags VLAN/VNI inconsistencies between MLAG peers and the MAC-VRF config).

T5 · root cause, fix, proof 
host2# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.1            ! fails
host2# show arp vrf H10                   ! no entry for 10.10.10.1
leaf3# show ip interface Vlan10
leaf3# show running-config | include virtual-router
leaf1# show running-config | include virtual-router   ! healthy twin

Root cause: ip virtual-router mac-address 00:1c:73:00:dc:01 was removed on leaf3 and leaf4. ip address virtual (the anycast gateway) needs the virtual router MAC; without it the gateway address does not answer ARP, so host2 has no gateway. Traffic arriving from other VTEPs still works because symmetric IRB uses the router MAC of the L3 VNI, not the virtual MAC; that is why host1 is fine and host2 can still be reached by bridging.

leaf3(config)# ip virtual-router mac-address 00:1c:73:00:dc:01

(Same on leaf4.) Proof: host2# show arp vrf H10 shows 10.10.10.1 at 001c.7300.dc01; host2# ping vrf H10 10.20.20.101 and ping vrf H10 8.8.8.8 work. The MAC must be identical on every leaf so a host that moves between pods keeps a valid ARP entry.

T6 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf3# show interfaces Ethernet1-2 | include line protocol|MTU
leaf1# show interfaces Ethernet1-2 | include line protocol|MTU     ! healthy twin
leaf1# ping 10.255.1.5 source 10.255.1.3 size 1450 df-bit repeat 3   ! fails
leaf1# ping 10.255.1.5 source 10.255.1.3 size 1350 df-bit repeat 3   ! works
host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 size 1400 df-bit repeat 3          ! fails
Ethernet1 is up, line protocol is up (connected)
  IP MTU 1400 bytes, BW 1000000 kbit

Root cause: leaf3's uplinks and the spine ports toward leaf3 were set to MTU 1400. VXLAN adds 50 bytes, so any host packet over 1350 bytes (plus encapsulation) no longer fits on the path to POD_B. BGP stays up because its packets are small. Note that even the intended 1500 fabric MTU cannot carry a full 1500-byte host packet once encapsulated; production fabrics run 9214 on the underlay for exactly this reason (Lab D4, objective 4.3).

leaf3(config)# interface Ethernet1-2
leaf3(config-if-Et1-2)# mtu 1500
spine1(config)# interface Ethernet3
spine1(config-if-Et3)# mtu 1500

(And spine2 Ethernet3.) Proof: the 1450-byte underlay ping and the 1400-byte host ping with df-bit succeed. cEOS forwards in software, so the exact drop point may differ from hardware; the interface MTU comparison and the df-bit ping between VTEP loopbacks are the reliable evidence.

T7 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf3# show ip route 10.255.1.3
leaf3# ping 10.255.1.3 source Loopback1
leaf3# show vxlan config-sanity
leaf1# show ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
spine1# show ip bgp 10.255.1.3
leaf1# show ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY
      seq 10 permit 10.255.0.0/27 eq 32

Root cause: seq 20 (10.255.1.0/27 eq 32) was removed from the prefix-list on leaf1 and leaf2, so redistribute connected route-map RM-CONN-2-BGP no longer advertises the VTEP Loopback1 (10.255.1.3). The overlay sessions use Loopback0, so they stay Established and EVPN routes still arrive with next hop 10.255.1.3, but nobody can route to that next hop: the data plane to POD_A is dead in both directions.

leaf1(config)# ip prefix-list PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY seq 20 permit 10.255.1.0/27 eq 32

(Same on leaf2; with only one leaf fixed it already works, but the redundancy is gone.) Proof: spine1# show ip bgp 10.255.1.3 shows two paths (leaf1 and leaf2); leaf3# ping 10.255.1.3 source Loopback1 works; all host pings across pods work. Lesson: "sessions Established" says nothing about next-hop reachability.

T8 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type ethernet-segment
leaf1# show bgp evpn route-type auto-discovery
leaf3# show running-config interfaces Port-Channel5
leaf4# show running-config interfaces Port-Channel5    ! healthy twin
interface Port-Channel5
   description SERVER_host2
   switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30
   switchport mode trunk
   mlag 5
   evpn ethernet-segment
      identifier 0000:0000:0000:0000:0505
      route-target import 00:00:00:00:05:05

Root cause: an EVPN ethernet segment was configured on leaf3's MLAG port-channel only. leaf3 now originates type-4 (ES) and type-1 (A-D) routes for a segment that has no second ESI member (leaf4 runs it as plain MLAG). Risk: MLAG and EVPN multihoming are two different answers to the same problem; mixing them on one segment gives remote VTEPs conflicting information about who serves host2 (aliasing and mass-withdraw via an ESI that only one leaf has, DF election with one member), which can cause blackholing or duplicates when a link fails. Choose one model per segment (Lab D6, 6.3 converts POD_B to all-active ESI properly, with MLAG removed).

leaf3(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf3(config-if-Po5)# no evpn ethernet-segment

Proof: show bgp evpn route-type ethernet-segment and auto-discovery are empty fabric-wide; show mlag config-sanity shows no interface inconsistency for Po5. If your EOS release rejects an ethernet segment on an MLAG port-channel, the injection does nothing visible; note that as a finding in itself (the platform protects you).

T9 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf2# show interfaces status errdisabled
leaf2# show logging | include BPDU|errdisable
leaf2# show spanning-tree interface Port-Channel5 detail
leaf2# show mlag config-sanity
Port       Name                     Status       Reason
---------- ------------------------ ------------ ---------
Et5        SERVER_host1_Ethernet2   errdisabled  bpduguard

Root cause: BPDU guard was enabled on leaf2's host port-channel. host1 is an EOS box running MSTP and sends BPDUs, so the port err-disabled on the first BPDU. MLAG config-sanity also flags the per-interface mismatch with leaf1.

leaf2(config)# interface Port-Channel5
leaf2(config-if-Po5)# no spanning-tree bpduguard
leaf2(config-if-Po5)# interface Ethernet5
leaf2(config-if-Et5)# shutdown
leaf2(config-if-Et5)# no shutdown

Proof: show interfaces status shows Et5 connected; show mlag interfaces shows mlag 5 active-full; host1 has both members bundled. Real world: BPDU guard on edge ports is good practice, but only where the attached device must never bridge; for hypervisors and appliances that speak STP, use spanning-tree portfast plus root guard instead, and configure it identically on both MLAG peers. errdisable recovery cause bpduguard with an interval avoids manual bounces after a one-off event.

T10 · root cause, fix, proof 
spine1# show bgp summary
leaf4# show interfaces status
leaf4# show logging | include Ethernet1|Ethernet2
leaf4# show ip route 10.255.0.1
Port       Name                     Status       Vlan     Duplex Speed  Type
Et1        P2P_spine1_Ethernet4     disabled     routed   full   1G     EbraTestPhyPort
Et2        P2P_spine2_Ethernet4     disabled     routed   full   1G     EbraTestPhyPort

Root cause: leaf4 Ethernet1 and Ethernet2 are administratively shut. No user impact because of the design: leaf4 still reaches the fabric over the MLAG iBGP session on Vlan4093 through leaf3 (requirement R4 in C1), and the shared VTEP 10.255.1.5 is still advertised by leaf3. This is exactly the redundancy you lose silently if nobody watches BGP alarms.

leaf4(config)# interface Ethernet1-2
leaf4(config-if-Et1-2)# no shutdown

Proof: spines show leaf4's underlay and EVPN peers Established; leaf4# show ip route 10.255.0.1 is via Ethernet1 again (not via Vlan4093). Ask who shut the ports and why (show logging, AAA accounting): an unexplained shutdown is a process issue as well as a config one.

T11 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf1# show interfaces trunk
leaf1# show vlan 20
leaf1# show vlan trunk group
leaf2# show vlan trunk group

Root cause: VLAN 20 on leaf1 was given trunk group DB-ONLY. From then on it is carried only on trunks that list that group, so leaf1 prunes it from Port-Channel5 and from the peer link, even though switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30 is unchanged. show interfaces trunk lists VLAN 20 as allowed but not as "allowed and active"; show vlan 20 on leaf1 shows no ports. Flows that host1 hashes to leaf2 still work, which is why only some fail (AW Ch 33, pp. 188-190).

leaf1(config)# vlan 20
leaf1(config-vlan-20)# no trunk group DB-ONLY

Proof: show vlan 20 on leaf1 lists Po5, Po10 and Vx1 again, and a long ping vrf H20 10.20.20.102 from host1 has no loss. Compare show vlan trunk group on both MLAG peers whenever one VLAN misbehaves on one peer only.

T12 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf3# show ip route vrf TENANT-A
leaf3# show running-config | include ip routing
leaf3# show bgp evpn route-type ip-prefix ipv4

Root cause: ip routing vrf TENANT-A was removed on leaf3 and leaf4. show ip route vrf TENANT-A ends with "IP routing not enabled", so the anycast gateways on POD_B may still answer ARP but do not route, and POD_B stops importing routed (type-5 and host) routes into the VRF. Bridged traffic in the same VLAN, including over VXLAN, still works. In EOS every VRF needs its own routing statement (AW Ch 33, p. 191).

leaf3(config)# ip routing vrf TENANT-A
leaf4(config)# ip routing vrf TENANT-A

Proof: show ip route vrf TENANT-A on leaf3 shows the connected SVIs, the type-5 routes from POD_A and the default route from core1; host2# ping vrf H20 10.10.10.101 works.

T13 · root cause, fix, proof 
leaf4# show ip interface brief vrf all | include Vlan10
leaf4# show running-config interfaces Vlan10
leaf3# show running-config interfaces Vlan10

Root cause: the VRF on leaf4 Vlan10 was removed and set again. EOS deletes an interface's IP addresses whenever its VRF changes, so Vlan10 on leaf4 is in TENANT-A but has no ip address virtual 10.10.10.1/24. host2's flows that hash to leaf4 have no gateway, the ones that hash to leaf3 work. The running config "looks right" if you only check the VRF line (AW Ch 33, p. 190).

leaf4(config)# interface Vlan10
leaf4(config-if-Vl10)# ip address virtual 10.10.10.1/24

Proof: the two peers' show running-config interfaces Vlan10 match, show ip interface brief vrf all lists Vlan10 with 10.10.10.1/24 on leaf4, and a long ping from host2 VRF H10 to 10.20.20.101 has no loss. If no vrf is rejected on your release, inject the same fault by changing the VRF to another one and back.

Real world. Write each solved ticket up in three lines: symptom, root cause, fix plus proof. A queue of those notes is the best revision material for the Operations exam and the start of your team's runbook. C5 has an incident report template.
Watch out. With all, faults mask each other (T7 hides T3 and T4 completely; T1 changes what T9 looks like). Fix in path order: physical and MLAG first, then underlay, then overlay, then services, and re-test after each fix.

C4 Day-2 operations: upgrade an MLAG leaf with zero host impact

Exercises: 1.1 · 1.2 · 3.4 · 4.1 · 5.3 · 7.3 · (2.2 snapshots and change control on Path A)

"leaf4 must be upgraded tonight. host2 and the internet path through the border pair must not notice. Produce the runbook, run it, and attach evidence that the fabric after the change is the same as before."

cEOS cannot change its image in place (the image is the container), so in the lab the "upgrade" is a reload, which exercises everything that matters: drain, MLAG reload delay, re-convergence and verification. On hardware, the same runbook wraps install source or boot system plus reload; on CloudVision it is an image bundle applied through a change control (Lab D1, objective 1.2).

Acceptance criteria

  • A continuous ping from host1 to host2 (ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102) and from host2 to 8.8.8.8 loses at most 2 packets during the whole procedure (target zero; cEOS timing is less precise than hardware).
  • Pre and post snapshots exist and their state summaries are identical (except uptime-type fields).
  • ANTA is clean after the change, including VerifyMaintenance (proves you exited maintenance mode) and VerifyReloadCause.
  • A written rollback plan that you could execute under pressure.

  1. Write a pre/post snapshot tool that saves a state summary (MLAG state, BGP and EVPN peer states, route counts per VRF, interface link states) for all six switches as JSON, plus raw text of key show commands, and diffs two snapshots.

  2. Pre-checks: snapshot pre, clean ANTA run, MLAG health and ISSU warnings on leaf3/leaf4, a configuration checkpoint on leaf4, and confirm leaf3 alone can carry the load (its uplinks, its core1 session, its MLAG ports).

  3. Start the continuous pings on host1 and host2.

  4. Drain leaf4: put it into maintenance mode so BGP peers stop preferring it, then move host and border traffic off it.

  5. Reload leaf4 ("upgrade"). While it boots, watch MLAG reload delay on leaf4 and the peer state on leaf3.

  6. Restore: wait for reload delay to finish, bring the ports back, exit maintenance mode.

  7. Post-checks: snapshot post, diff with pre, ANTA, stop the pings and count losses.

  8. Write the rollback plan and fill in the change-window runbook template below.

Solution · task 1: snapshot.py 
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""snapshot.py TAG  |  snapshot.py diff PRE POST
Saves state summaries and raw show output for the fabric under snapshots/TAG/."""
import json, sys, pathlib, requests, urllib3
urllib3.disable_warnings()
HOSTS = {"spine1": "172.100.100.11", "spine2": "172.100.100.12",
         "leaf1": "172.100.100.21", "leaf2": "172.100.100.22",
         "leaf3": "172.100.100.23", "leaf4": "172.100.100.24"}
RAW = ["show version", "show mlag", "show mlag interfaces", "show bgp summary vrf all",
       "show bgp evpn summary", "show vxlan vtep", "show ip route vrf all summary",
       "show interfaces status", "show port-channel dense"]

def eapi(ip, cmds, fmt="json"):
    r = requests.post(f"https://{ip}/command-api", auth=("admin", "admin"), verify=False, timeout=30,
                      json={"jsonrpc": "2.0", "method": "runCmds", "id": 1,
                            "params": {"version": 1, "format": fmt, "cmds": cmds}})
    return r.json().get("result", [])

def summary(ip):
    s = {}
    mlag, bgp, evpn, routes, intf = eapi(ip, ["show mlag", "show ip bgp summary vrf all",
                                             "show bgp evpn summary", "show ip route vrf all summary",
                                             "show interfaces status"])
    s["mlag"] = {k: mlag.get(k) for k in ("state", "negStatus", "peerLinkStatus", "mlagPorts")}
    s["bgp"] = {f"{vrf}:{peer}": p.get("peerState")
                for vrf, v in bgp.get("vrfs", {}).items() for peer, p in v.get("peers", {}).items()}
    s["evpn"] = {peer: p.get("peerState")
                 for peer, p in evpn.get("vrfs", {}).get("default", {}).get("peers", {}).items()}
    s["routes"] = {vrf: v.get("totalRoutes") for vrf, v in routes.get("vrfs", {}).items()}
    s["links"] = {i: v.get("linkStatus") for i, v in intf.get("interfaceStatuses", {}).items()}
    return s

def take(tag):
    base = pathlib.Path("snapshots") / tag
    for name, ip in HOSTS.items():
        d = base / name
        d.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        (d / "summary.json").write_text(json.dumps(summary(ip), indent=1, sort_keys=True))
        for cmd, out in zip(RAW, eapi(ip, RAW, fmt="text")):
            (d / (cmd.replace(" ", "_") + ".txt")).write_text(out.get("output", ""))
        print("saved", d)

def diff(pre, post):
    changed = 0
    for name in HOSTS:
        a = json.loads((pathlib.Path("snapshots") / pre / name / "summary.json").read_text())
        b = json.loads((pathlib.Path("snapshots") / post / name / "summary.json").read_text())
        for section in a:
            for key in sorted(set(a[section]) | set(b[section])) if isinstance(a[section], dict) else [None]:
                va = a[section].get(key) if key else a[section]
                vb = b[section].get(key) if key else b[section]
                if va != vb:
                    changed += 1
                    print(f"{name:7} {section}:{key}  {va} -> {vb}")
    print("NO STATE CHANGES" if not changed else f"{changed} differences")
    return changed

if sys.argv[1] == "diff":
    sys.exit(1 if diff(sys.argv[2], sys.argv[3]) else 0)
take(sys.argv[1])

Check the JSON key names on your EOS release with show mlag | json and friends; the .get() calls keep the script from crashing if a key differs, but a None in the summary means you should fix the key. Raw text files are for humans; the summary is for the diff, because counters and timers make raw output differ every time.

Solution · tasks 2 to 7: the procedure

Pre-checks

$ python3 snapshot.py pre
$ anta nrfu -u admin -p admin -i labs/avd/anta-inventory.yml -c labs/avd/fabric-catalog.yml table --hide success
leaf4# show mlag
leaf4# show mlag config-sanity
leaf4# show mlag issu warnings
leaf4# show mlag detail | include reload-delay
leaf4# configure checkpoint save pre-upgrade
leaf4# show version | include image|Software
leaf3# show bgp summary vrf TENANT-A          ! leaf3's own core1 session is up
leaf3# show interfaces Ethernet1-2,5-6 status

Continuous pings (one terminal each):

host1# ping vrf H10 10.10.10.102 repeat 10000 interval 0.2
host2# ping vrf H20 8.8.8.8 repeat 10000 interval 0.2

Drain: maintenance mode first, so the routing layer moves traffic away gracefully (BGP peers receive the routes with a lower preference / graceful-shutdown community and choose leaf3), then take the host and border ports down so host2's LACP bundle runs on leaf3 only.

leaf4(config)# maintenance
leaf4(config-maintenance)# unit System
leaf4(config-unit-System)# quiesce
leaf4(config-unit-System)# end
leaf4# show maintenance
spine1# show ip bgp 10.255.1.5                 ! best path now via leaf3
leaf4(config)# interface Ethernet5-6
leaf4(config-if-Et5-6)# shutdown
leaf4(config-if-Et5-6)# end
leaf4# show interfaces counters rates | include Et1|Et2|Po10   ! near zero except peer link
leaf4# show maintenance
Unit Name        Status           Bgp Groups  Interface Groups
---------------- ---------------- ----------- ----------------
System           underMaintenance           1                1

Reload (save first so the post-reload config still has maintenance mode and the shut ports; that keeps the box out of the path until you decide):

leaf4# write memory
leaf4# reload now
leaf3# show mlag                                ! peer down, leaf3 carries everything
leaf4# show mlag detail | include reload        ! after boot: reload delay running
leaf4# show interfaces status errdisabled

After boot, MLAG keeps leaf4's MLAG ports in err-disabled for reload-delay mlag 300 seconds (and other ports for reload-delay non-mlag 330) so the peer link, MAC tables and routing can sync before traffic returns. That built-in delay is what makes an MLAG member reload hitless; do not shorten it in production to save time.

Restore:

leaf4# show mlag                                ! state active, reload delay expired
leaf4(config)# interface Ethernet5-6
leaf4(config-if-Et5-6)# no shutdown
leaf4(config)# maintenance
leaf4(config-maintenance)# unit System
leaf4(config-unit-System)# no quiesce
leaf4(config-unit-System)# end
leaf4# write memory

Post-checks:

$ python3 snapshot.py post
$ python3 snapshot.py diff pre post
NO STATE CHANGES
$ anta nrfu -u admin -p admin -i labs/avd/anta-inventory.yml -c labs/avd/fabric-catalog.yml table --hide success

Stop the pings (Ctrl-C) and read the summary line, for example 9873 packets transmitted, 9873 received, 0% packet loss. If you lost packets, find the step: losses at the drain point to a missing maintenance step; losses when ports came back point to returning before reload delay finished or before BGP converged.

Solution · task 8: rollback plan
TriggerAction
leaf4 does not boot the new image, or boots with errors (agents crashing, show logging errors)Console in, boot system flash:<previous>.swi, write memory, reload. leaf4 stays drained, so there is no hurry.
Config lost or changed after bootconfigure replace checkpoint:pre-upgrade (or the AVD intended config).
MLAG does not come back (ISSU incompatibility)Keep leaf4 drained, roll back the image. Do not touch leaf3. Check show mlag issu warnings before the next attempt.
Host loss above threshold during drainStop. no quiesce, no shutdown on Eth5-6, verify recovery, investigate before retrying.
Window overrunLeave leaf4 in maintenance (traffic is safe on leaf3), close the window, finish next window.

Change-window runbook template

FieldContent
Change ID / titleCHG-____ · Upgrade leaf4 EOS x to y
Owner / approver / on-call peer
Window and durationStart, end, time reserved for rollback (at least one third of the window)
Risk and impact statementExpected impact: none (MLAG peer carries all traffic). Worst case: POD_B single-homed for the window.
Pre-checks (with expected result)Snapshot pre; ANTA clean; MLAG active and config-sanity clean; ISSU warnings none; checkpoint saved; image MD5/SHA verified; peer leaf healthy.
Go / no-goAll pre-checks pass and the peer is healthy. Otherwise stop.
StepsNumbered, each with the command, the expected result, and an abort criterion.
Verification after each stepContinuous ping counts; show maintenance; spine best path for the shared VTEP.
Rollback planTable above, with who decides and the time limit for deciding.
Post-checksSnapshot post, diff, ANTA (including VerifyMaintenance), host tests, image version.
CommunicationsStart, completion and abort messages; who is told.
Evidence attachedSnapshot diff, ANTA report (md-report), ping summary.
Sign-offName, time, result (successful / rolled back / partial).
Exam tip. Upgrade questions focus on the order and the safety features: one MLAG peer at a time, reload delay, ISSU compatibility, SSU for single-homed switches where supported, and on CloudVision: image bundles, snapshots and change control stages. Know where each fits.
Real world. The snapshot diff plus ANTA report is what turns "I think it went fine" into evidence. Teams that automate exactly these two artifacts get changes approved faster, because reviewers trust the result.

Break / fix

Inject: skip the restore step's no quiesce, save, and walk away.

Symptom: everything pings, but next week leaf3's reload causes a POD_B outage.

Root cause and fix

leaf4 stayed in maintenance, so the spines never preferred it and its BGP routes kept a low preference; it looked healthy but was not carrying traffic as designed. When leaf3 later went down, the fabric had to fail over to a drained switch. ANTA VerifyMaintenance in the post-checks catches this immediately. Fix: no quiesce, and make the post-check list mandatory.

C5 Operations and observability

Exercises: 3.1 · 3.2 · 3.3 · 3.4 · 7.1 (eAPI) · 7.3

"Operations wants to know about fabric problems before users do, and wants evidence collected automatically when something breaks. Build on-box automation, polling and streaming telemetry, and an incident report format." Use leaf1 for the on-box parts, then roll them out to all switches (by hand, configlet, or AVD structured_config). The collector for SNMP, sFlow and gNMI is your containerlab host, reachable from the switches at 172.100.100.1.

Acceptance criteria

  • Shutting a BGP-facing interface on leaf1 creates a timestamped evidence file in leaf1's flash and a syslog line, within 10 seconds, without anyone logging in.
  • A CLI scheduler job saves an EVPN snapshot every 5 minutes and keeps at most 24 files.
  • You can answer "when did 8.8.8.0/24 last change and from where?" with event-monitor.
  • snmpwalk returns interface status; sFlow samples reach the collector (where cEOS supports it).
  • gnmic receives an on-change update within seconds when leaf1 Ethernet1 goes down and when a BGP session changes state.
  • An incident report for a fault from C3 using the template below.

  1. Configure an event handler on leaf1 that fires when any BGP session leaves Established, saves BGP, EVPN, interface and log state to /mnt/flash/evidence/ with a timestamp, and logs a syslog message. Test it with shutdown on Ethernet1.

  2. Use event-monitor to show the history of route, MAC and ARP changes, then answer the 8.8.8.0/24 question.

  3. Schedule a periodic EVPN snapshot with the CLI scheduler.

  4. Configure SNMP (read-only community restricted by ACL, traps to 172.100.100.1) and sFlow export to 172.100.100.1, and test both from the host.

  5. Stream telemetry with gNMI: list capabilities, get interface counters, and subscribe on-change to interface oper-status and BGP neighbor state.

  6. Write an incident report for C3 ticket T7 using the template.

Solution · task 1: event handler 
leaf1# bash mkdir -p /mnt/flash/evidence
leaf1(config)# event-handler BGP-DOWN
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN)# trigger on-logging
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN-trigger-on-logging)# regex BGP-5-ADJCHANGE.*old state Established
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN-trigger-on-logging)# exit
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN)# action bash F=/mnt/flash/evidence/bgp-$(date +%Y%m%d-%H%M%S).txt; for c in "show bgp summary vrf all" "show bgp evpn summary" "show interfaces status" "show logging last 5 minutes"; do echo "=== $c" >> $F; FastCli -p 15 -c "$c" >> $F; done; logger -p local4.warning "EVENT-HANDLER BGP-DOWN evidence saved to $F"
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN)# delay 5
leaf1(config-handler-BGP-DOWN)# asynchronous
leaf1(config)# interface Ethernet1
leaf1(config-if-Et1)# shutdown
leaf1# show event-handler BGP-DOWN
leaf1# bash ls -l /mnt/flash/evidence/
leaf1# show logging last 2 minutes | include EVENT-HANDLER|ADJCHANGE
leaf1(config-if-Et1)# no shutdown
%BGP-5-ADJCHANGE: peer 10.255.255.0 (VRF default AS 65100) old state Established event Stop new state Idle
%BGP-5-ADJCHANGE: peer 10.255.0.1 (VRF default AS 65100) old state Established event BfdDown new state Idle
leaf1 logger: EVENT-HANDLER BGP-DOWN evidence saved to /mnt/flash/evidence/bgp-20261025-101502.txt

delay 5 lets the state settle before collecting; asynchronous stops a slow action from blocking the next trigger. The regex matches the EOS log text, so check the exact message on your release with show logging | include ADJCHANGE. Other useful triggers: on-intf Ethernet1 operstatus, on-counters (for example error counters with a threshold), on-boot, and on-maintenance. Real world: cap the evidence folder size (a flapping peer can fill flash) by adding a cleanup in the action or a scheduler job.

Solution · task 2: event-monitor 
leaf1# show event-monitor route
leaf1# show event-monitor mac
leaf1# show event-monitor arp
leaf1# bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db "select * from route limit 20;"
2026-10-25 10:15:02.118 | 10.255.255.0/31 | ... | removed
2026-10-25 10:15:03.902 | 8.8.8.0/24       | ... | updated
2026-10-25 10:16:41.233 | 10.255.255.0/31 | ... | added

Event monitor keeps a rolling SQLite log of route, MAC, ARP and other table changes. Since EOS 4.21 it is off by default, so if show event-monitor says it is not running, enable it with event-monitor in config mode (and note that history only starts from then). On current releases the show event-monitor sqlite form no longer works, so query the database from bash (AW Ch 28, p. 150); first run bash sqlite3 /var/log/eventMon.db ".schema route" to see the column names on your release, then filter (for example a where clause on the prefix column for 8.8.8.0/24). To keep history across reloads, store the database on flash (event-monitor backup path em.log). Note the VRF: tenant routes live in VRF TENANT-A, so look for entries tagged with that VRF.

Solution · task 3: CLI scheduler 
leaf1(config)# schedule EVPN-SNAP interval 5 max-log-files 24 command show bgp evpn summary
leaf1# show schedule summary
leaf1# bash ls /mnt/flash/schedule/EVPN-SNAP/
Maximum concurrent jobs  1
Prepend host name to logfile: No
Name           At Time       Last        Interval  Timeout   Max     Log   Last
                             Time        (mins)    (mins)    Log     Files Status
                                                             Files
-------------- ------------- ----------- --------- --------- ------- ----- ------
EVPN-SNAP      now           10:20       5         30        24      3     Success
tech-support   now           10:02       60        30        100     1     Success

Each run writes a compressed log file in /mnt/flash/schedule/EVPN-SNAP/. To capture several commands, schedule a bash command that calls FastCli several times, or point it at a script stored on flash. The built-in tech-support schedule is a real lifesaver for post-mortems; do not delete it.

Solution · task 4: SNMP and sFlow 
leaf1(config)# ip access-list standard ACL-SNMP
leaf1(config-std-acl-ACL-SNMP)# permit host 172.100.100.1
leaf1(config-std-acl-ACL-SNMP)# exit
leaf1(config)# snmp-server community l5dc-ro ro ACL-SNMP
leaf1(config)# snmp-server location containerlab-l5dc
leaf1(config)# snmp-server host 172.100.100.1 version 2c l5dc-ro
leaf1(config)# snmp-server enable traps
leaf1(config)# sflow sample 1024
leaf1(config)# sflow destination 172.100.100.1
leaf1(config)# sflow source-interface Management0
leaf1(config)# sflow run
$ snmpwalk -v2c -c l5dc-ro 172.100.100.21 IF-MIB::ifOperStatus
IF-MIB::ifOperStatus.1 = INTEGER: up(1)
IF-MIB::ifOperStatus.2 = INTEGER: up(1)
...
$ sudo tcpdump -ni l5dc-mgmt udp port 6343 or udp port 162   # sFlow datagrams and traps
leaf1# show sflow

sFlow sample rates below the hardware default may need sflow sample dangerous on some platforms; in the lab, a low rate makes samples visible with little traffic. cEOS sFlow support depends on the release; if show sflow reports it running but no datagrams leave, treat this part as a configuration exercise. In production use SNMPv3 (snmp-server group / snmp-server user ... v3 auth sha ... priv aes ...) instead of v2c communities.

Solution · task 5: gNMI with gnmic

The startup configs already enable management api gnmi with transport grpc default (port 6030, no TLS), and the AVD configs keep it. Install gnmic on the containerlab host:

$ bash -c "$(curl -sL https://get-gnmic.openconfig.net)"
$ G="gnmic -a 172.100.100.21:6030 -u admin -p admin --insecure"
$ $G capabilities | head
$ $G get --path "/interfaces/interface[name=Ethernet1]/state/counters"
$ $G subscribe --path "/interfaces/interface/state/oper-status" --stream-mode on-change
$ $G subscribe --stream-mode on-change \
    --path "/network-instances/network-instance[name=default]/protocols/protocol[identifier=BGP][name=BGP]/bgp/neighbors/neighbor/state/session-state"
$ $G subscribe --path "/interfaces/interface[name=Ethernet1]/state/counters" \
    --stream-mode sample --sample-interval 10s
{
  "source": "172.100.100.21:6030",
  "subscription-name": "default-1729851302",
  "timestamp": 1729851321842117000,
  "time": "2026-10-25T10:15:21.842117+00:00",
  "updates": [
    {
      "Path": "interfaces/interface[name=Ethernet1]/state/oper-status",
      "values": {"interfaces/interface/state/oper-status": "DOWN"}
    }
  ]
}

Shut and unshut leaf1 Ethernet1 while the subscriptions run: the oper-status and BGP session-state updates arrive within a second or two, without polling. --insecure means plain gRPC without TLS, which matches the lab config; production uses a TLS profile (transport grpc default / ssl profile ...). For EOS-native paths beyond OpenConfig, add provider eos-native under management api gnmi and use the eos_native: origin. gnmic can also write to Prometheus or InfluxDB (--output config), which is the usual way to build dashboards from this. CloudVision itself receives similar streaming state through TerminAttr, which is what makes its dashboards and events (objective 3.2) possible.

Verify

leaf1# show event-handler
leaf1# show schedule summary
leaf1# show snmp
leaf1# show sflow
leaf1# show management api gnmi

The event handler shows a trigger count above zero and the last run time of your test; the scheduler shows the job with Success; SNMP shows packets in and out; gNMI shows the server enabled and running on port 6030 with active RPCs while gnmic is subscribed.

Incident report template (one page)

SectionWhat to write
Title, ID, severityOne line that a manager understands. Severity by impact, not by effort.
Timeline (UTC)Detected, acknowledged, mitigated, resolved, with the source of each timestamp (syslog, event-handler file, gNMI log, ticket).
ImpactWho and what was affected, for how long, measured (for example: all POD_A to POD_B traffic, 42 minutes).
DetectionHow we found out (alarm, user, telemetry). If a user found it first, say so; that is an action item.
Root causeThe technical cause in two or three sentences, plus the contributing cause (process, review, tooling).
ResolutionExact change made, by whom, and the verification that proved it.
EvidenceEvent-handler files, snapshots, ANTA report, relevant show output (trimmed).
What went well / what did notShort bullets, blameless.
Action itemsEach with an owner and a date: for example "add VerifyRoutingTableEntry for both VTEP loopbacks to the ANTA catalog", "require prefix-list changes to go through AVD review".
Solution · task 6: example report for T7 (abridged)

INC-0007 · Sev 1 · POD_A unreachable from POD_B after prefix-list change. Timeline: 22:10 change applied on leaf1/leaf2 (AAA accounting); 22:11 event-monitor shows 10.255.1.3/32 removed on spine1; 07:05 user report; 07:31 cause found; 07:33 fixed. Impact: all inter-pod traffic for TENANT-A, about 9 hours (overnight). Detection: user, because BGP sessions stayed Established and no alarm watched next-hop reachability. Root cause: seq 20 of PL-LOOPBACKS-EVPN-OVERLAY removed, so the VTEP loopback was no longer advertised; contributing cause: manual change outside the AVD workflow, no post-change validation. Resolution: re-added seq 20 on leaf1 and leaf2; verified with show ip bgp 10.255.1.3 on the spines and host pings. Actions: ANTA reachability test between VTEP loopbacks after every change (owner: network team, 1 week); gNMI alert on route-count drop in the default VRF (owner: ops, 2 weeks).

Exam tip. For Domain 3 know the three on-box automation tools by purpose: event-handler (react to a trigger, run an action), event-monitor (history of table changes, query with SQL), CLI scheduler (run a command periodically, keep N files). Expect to configure one from a requirement and prove it fired.
Real world. Polling (SNMP) answers "what is the value now", flow sampling (sFlow) answers "who is talking", and streaming telemetry (gNMI, or CloudVision's TerminAttr) answers "what changed, and when", at sub-second resolution. Mature operations use all three, plus on-box evidence collection so the first responder already has data.

Break / fix

Inject: change the event handler regex to BGP-5-ADJCHANGE.*new state Established and remove asynchronous.

Symptom: evidence files appear only when sessions come back up, and when a peer flaps several times in a row only the first event is captured.

Root cause and fix

The regex now matches the recovery message instead of the failure, so you collect state after the problem has gone. Without asynchronous, each action blocks the handler, and triggers that arrive during the run are not handled. Restore the regex to old state Established and add back asynchronous. Always test a handler by causing the real event, not by reading the config.

Checkpoint

  • C1: I built the full fabric from the brief inside 4 hours and scored 85 or more with the grader.
  • C2: hosts reach 8.8.8.8 through the border, also with one border link down; TENANT-B is isolated, and I can argue for a leaking design.
  • C3: I solved all 13 tickets one by one, then all at once, each with a one-sentence root cause and a proof.
  • C4: I reloaded an MLAG leaf with no more than 2 lost pings and attached a clean snapshot diff and ANTA report.
  • C5: my event handler, scheduler, SNMP and gNMI subscriptions all fired on a real event, and I wrote an incident report.
